Ben-Now (seed)

What the Dreaming Became

Here is one book where there were several. The three stories you read in September and the plan you read in October are told again in it, in one voice and in type your eyes will not have to fight, and set in the order the place keeps them ...

The first pages are the whole picture as it stood in September, on one page, so you can hold it in your hand before anything else. Then the sport itself, told plainly for anybody who has never stood at the rail, so a reader who has not can walk in beside you. Five short writings follow it, about the lift at the rail, the trumpet, the blanket, the field and the oldest carts there are ...

Then comes the dark night at the track, a day and a night walked from the lot to the barns to the hotel window, half the length it was and speaking in one voice now. After it comes the last night of the meet, from the last race to first light and on into the hallway. Say Go is in here under a new name, After It There Is No Recall, and its last pages are still headed for the wall ...

Then the wishes, grown from lines into prose, with the best of the lines kept whole. Some of them will sound familiar by then, because the two nights before are where they were found ...

Then the plan, the plan you know, with its questions for you still standing at the end. After the plan comes the film, where it comes from and how we would make it. The hallway in the story of the last night is the one we dreamed first, two lit panels and a rail. The plan is the one we are bringing you ...

The last one, When the Sandhill Goes Flying, is a short telling about the cranes, who come through at the end of this month ...

None of it is set in stone. These pages are where the work stands this fall, and they will keep changing as the hallway takes shape with you ...

Every paragraph in here ends on its dots, and every one of them can stand alone under a lamp. That is what they were made for. Read it a writing at a time, and tell us anything that is wrong about the track or the horses. You know this ground, and we are still learning it ...

This Wall Posts the Wishes

The whole picture on one page

Here is the whole picture of the work, set down on one page so you can hold it in your hand. The call can then be given over to your questions, all of them ...

The end wall holds one work, a large screen in a frame of solid white oak, hung the way a framed painting is hung. The screen is a commercial 4K display, and its face is matte, which is to say dull and not shiny, so light does not sit on it as glare. One silent film plays on it, made of our hand-drawn images and sentences. They surface and slowly dissolve at the speed of breathing, and when the film reaches its end it begins again. The whole of it reads as a painting that happens to be moving, very slowly. The light behind the work was the one practical thing you floated, and it is the heart of the whole design. Nothing like it exists that we know of ...

On the walk toward the work the noise of the casino falls away. The end of the hallway becomes one of the quietest spots in your building. A person stands before one slow light and is given a still and meditative minute. The board in the infield posts the odds. This wall posts the wishes ...

The wall is made from the writing, three finished stories out of your grounds. We will come out to look at the wall again and to photograph your horses and your track, and the marks are made from that visit. The visuals are settled last, after we have looked at the wall together with you ...

The film lives on a small memory card inside a simple player. When the power of your building comes on, the film starts itself. Nobody ever has to operate it. Nothing waits to be scheduled and nothing waits to be updated ...

The work is about seven feet wide and four feet tall. Your guests see it from far down the hall, before they reach it. A wide shape like that reads at such a distance, and up close it stays calm ...

From your building the work needs one ordinary outlet and a wall sound enough to carry a framed work of this size. The whole list ends there ...

Nothing is ordered until a sample has proven the look, to you and to us both. A piece of the film runs on a test screen, we stand with you where your guests will stand, and the judging is done first with our own eyes ...

The right builders are your own screen people and your own woodwork shop. The equipment is ordinary and commercial, the carpentry is honest, and it is work they already know ...

Around The Dream the still pieces gather and become the surround. The Wish is among them, the still one, a single steady light with a horse, a sulky and a driver in our strokes. Harness racing keeps two gaits, raced separately and never mixed, and so one work trots and one paces. That part is designed after you have stood at the wall with us ...

Only you can answer what remains. We ask who supplies the screens or the signage for your building, and who does your woodwork. We ask whether that wall has power, and whether about seven feet by four suits the hallway. We ask last whether your building has any rules we should know ...

One part of the story touches the names the Dakota and the Ojibwe have for this ground and for the horse. That part stays a draft until it has been reviewed with tribal representation, and before that review nothing goes on the wall ...

The call comes next, then the visit, then the proposal, which carries what we work out together with you. You asked for a heavenly dream and left the dreaming to us ...

Nobody Sits on the Horse, Here

For anybody who has never stood at the rail for this one ... it is horse racing ... the same as the other kind ... with one difference that changes everything ... nobody sits on the horse ... The horse pulls a cart ... two wheels, one seat, and no more cart than that ... The cart is called a sulky ... an old word for somebody who would rather sit alone ... The driver holds the lines ~ lines is what this sport calls reins ~ and the horse's mouth is at the other end of them ...

The rule that makes it a different sport is the gait ... A horse here is allowed two ways of hurrying ... the trot and the pace ... and no third ... In the trot the legs move on the diagonal ... front left with back right, front right with back left ... In the pace the two legs on one side swing together ... then the two on the other ... so the horse rolls a little ... side to side ... like somebody walking fast with a full tray ... Pacers are the faster of the two ... Most of them wear hopples ... leather loops from front leg to back leg on each side ... to hold the stride together at speed ... A pacer going without them is called free-legged ... and keeps the gait on breeding and training alone ...

The gallop is what every horse wants to do when it is asked to go fast ... Here the gallop is the fault ... The sport's word for it is a break ... A horse that breaks has to be pulled to the outside and brought back down to its gait before it can race on ... and if it gained ground while galloping it is set back in the placings ... The training of a young horse is mostly this ... a mile at a time ... until the gait is the thing the horse reaches for when it is tired and crowded ...

.

The grandfather of this sport is the chariot ... Kings in the old Assyrian carvings ride behind horses in harness ... The Greeks raced chariots at their games ... with prizes ... and with spills ... The movies have kept the chariot race alive for anybody who never read the history ... so none of us has to be a scholar to picture it ... Those kingdoms fell and the horses stayed harnessed ... The cart got smaller over the centuries until it was two wheels and a seat ... and the sport we have now is what was left ...

.

In North America every horse that races here is a Standardbred ... The name is plain bookkeeping ... To get into the breed's first register a horse had to trot a mile in two minutes and thirty seconds ... That was the standard ... the horses that met it were the Standardbreds ... and the name stuck ... Beside a Thoroughbred they are a little shorter in the leg, longer in the body, heavier through the shoulder, and a good deal calmer in the head ... which is what we want in an animal asked to hold one gait for a mile with a cart behind it and other carts all around it ...

.

The track is an oval ... nearly always ... a mile around or a half or something between ... The footing is packed stone dust or clay ... harrowed smooth between races ... with a handful of tracks on a made surface ... A mile is the standard race ... In this country the start is a car ... It drives ahead of the field with two long gates folded out from its sides like wings ... the horses trot up into them ... and at the line the wings fold in and the car pulls away and the horses chase it ... It may be the only sport where the car gets a head start on the horses ... Elsewhere they line up and go from a standstill ...

.

Most of the races on a card are for pacers ... They are the faster horses and the steadier ones ... and a pacer seldom breaks ... Trotters break more often ... the gait is harder to hold ... and a horse holding it at full speed is being asked to hurry without ever quite running ... Trotting races are fewer ... and to some eyes lovelier ...

This is a slower sport than the other kind of horse racing ... and a closer one ... Speed matters less than where the driver puts the horse ... Tucked in behind the leader ... out of the wind ... is called the pocket ... Out in the second lane with nothing in front is called parked ... and a horse parked the whole way is a tired horse at the top of the stretch ... The driver's whole art is the moment ... when to sit ... when to pull out ... when to let the horse go ... It is easy to follow from the rail because the field stays together and the moves can be seen ... anybody can watch the one that pulls out on the last turn and wonder whether it was too early ...

.

The betting is the ordinary kind ... a horse to win ... or to come first or second ... or to land anywhere in the first three ... then the harder bets ... naming the first two in order ... or the first three ... which pay well because they are hard ... All of it goes into one pool and the people betting are betting against each other ... with the house taking a fixed slice off the top and no stake at all in which horse wins ...

.

Some of the people at the rail here were brought by their grandparents and are now the grandparents ... The driver and the horse have been a pair since the chariot ... longer than almost any other working pair we have ... and the people watching know what they are looking at when they see one ... For anybody who wants to sit in the cart ... there are beginner courses ... At the end of them is a license ... and before the license come the qualifying miles ... driven at a trot or a pace in front of a judge ... with nobody allowed to bet on them ...

First or Last Horse

That's where you'll find us

All Four Feet Off the Ground

The wanting was at the rail before any window opened, a little room kept empty in every chest for lift. Most weeks nothing comes to fill it ...

The body keeps a quick fire in two small glands riding the kidneys, and it has never once asked what struck the match. A deer stepping out on the county road will do it, and so will a bay mare three wide at the head of the stretch with our two dollars on her ...

Lucid is how the stretch looks from inside it. The eye lets seven horses go and keeps the one, the clock goes soft, and the legs come plain, one at a time, which they never do at thirty or so miles an hour. Wild is the voice that comes up out of grown people who have not raised it since they were nine, calling a name none of us knew an hour ago. Nobody on the apron turns around ...

For the length of the stretch the ticket is leave to holler, two dollars so a careful person has a reason to make that much noise in public. Down the whole rail heels come up off the ground, the nearest any of us gets to leaving it ...

The driver has the lines, and the rest of us hitch on by voice. What we hitch to is full of life in the plain sense, a third of its red blood held back in the spleen while it stood in the stall, sent out within seconds once the fire came. Every stride of the mile has an instant with all four feet off the ground ...

One of us had nothing down and yelled for the gray because it was gray, heels up with the rest. The redwings go the whole day that way, over the wetland ...

The fire in us is spent in minutes. The horses are pulled up and turned, the hands come down, and at the window somebody's fingers are still unsteady on the ticket. The horse takes about an hour to put the blood back in the spleen, walked in circles for most of it ...

Sulky Racing and the Sound of Most Wonderous Trumpet Music

A trumpet has never kept a thing to itself, and the sulky is said to be named for keeping to oneself, one seat and no room for company ...

Racing took its trumpet from the cavalry, a stand-by call, and it turns us all one way before there is a horse on the track. The call has thirty-four notes, no sharps and no flats, and when it sounds the next race is about ten minutes off ...

The oldest trumpet music was in this marsh country before any track. A swan named for the instrument carries close to a yard of windpipe looped inside the breastbone, and what comes out of that coil crosses two miles of open marsh. The swans were gone from this state for the better part of a century. They came home as eggs, flown down from Alaska in first class, which is more than most of us can say. There were about fifty thousand at the last count, some of them on the water of this same town ...

In the old tellings a trumpet is kept for the last morning, the one the sleepers get up to. We have watched this place get up already, more than once, the grass after the fire, the trout by the truckload, the swans by the egg ...

The trumpet goes over the mile the way a tune goes over a pulse. The mile brings the pulse, hooves down the stretch in twos and two wheels turning. Our chests keep the same time at the rail ...

Wish Upon a Blanket

A star takes a wish from a long way off, and a blanket takes it at arm's length, warm, smelling of horse ...

Flat on our backs in the grass, with wool between us and the ground, we have the whole sky for a board. Star-wishing was always done from a blanket. This ground had the grass and the singing before it had a rail ...

On a cold night we go out to meet the horses with a blanket over the arm. The harness comes off and the cooler goes over the steaming back, first horse and last horse alike. A wish laid there is the whole load. There is no rider in this sport, and the harness is back on its peg by then ...

When a field comes home so close that one blanket could cover every horse in it, the old hands call it a blanket finish. We have come home in that bunch all our lives. The photo sorts the noses a minute later ...

More Than One Address

Everything entered in one race stands together as one field, the program's word for the horses themselves. Near it is the paddock fence, the walk out, the rail at the gap, anywhere we can hear harness being buckled. The state keeps a number on how big a field may grow, ten horses on a track of five-eighths of a mile ...

Every horse in the field wears a pad colored for its post, no two alike in the same race. Lined up for the start they make a bow of colors walking, and something always rides in front of it, the starting car with its wings spread across the track and the field nosing up behind. At the start the wings fold in and the car pulls away ahead of the horses ...

A rainbow only stands where the sun is at our backs. So whoever is in front of one is facing the low light with the rain behind, squinting, held still for somebody's picture. Mornings on the backside the horses out together get that. They jog the oval the wrong way round, half a lap facing the bow and half a lap carrying it, and not one head turns. Horses in morning training go clockwise, against the way they race ...

We keep more than one address on these grounds, the blanket, the field and the rainbow behind us, and whoever comes looking checks all three. The nearest field of all is the infield, with the oval drawn around it and the tote board standing in it ...

One Horse Wearing Eight Legs

The cart came first. It had two wheels, a light body and a driver standing behind the horse, which is the shape we hitch to on a race night. Six of them came out of one tomb in the Valley of the Kings in 1922, every one found taken down to its parts, parade carts and hunting carts and one built lighter and faster than the rest for war. They stand together again at the museum beside the pyramids, the sixth one carried over in boxes from a military museum in the Cairo Citadel ...

On a plateau in southern Algeria somebody painted a two-horse cart at full stretch across the wall of a rock shelter, in red, with the driver holding four reins and a whip of two strands. The second horse ran hidden behind the first, so the painter gave the one horse the legs of both. It is one horse wearing eight legs, all eight off the ground in one red line, speed drawn the only way a flat wall allows. The plateau carries more than fifteen thousand paintings and engravings, and the cart painters came late in that count, after the cattle and before the camels ...

A breeder in the old port of Sousse had his best horses laid in stone in his own house, each name set over its back, Adorandus and Crinitus facing a palm between them, Amor and Dominator in the next circle. Every horse is branded on the hindquarter with the first four letters of the man's name and on the shoulder with three more, a mark that stayed after the horse was gone, same as our fastest miles in the books. The names stayed in the floor, and the floor lies in the archaeological museum at Sousse. In another floor from the same town four named horses pull for the four colors, blue and white and green and red ...

A floor from Carthage, kept at the Bardo in Tunis, holds the finish. It shows the circus from outside and inside at once, the race in its last strides and the Blues' driver in the moment of winning. A rider goes alongside hollering the teams on, and a man runs with a jar and a whip. The floor does not say what is in the jar. The circus at Carthage ran more than four hundred and seventy meters end to end and seated about forty-five thousand ...

A floor in the inland town of Gafsa shows the whole oval from above, the long barrier down the middle set with obelisks and lions, and four carts running two to a side, the Greens and the Blues the two best preserved. The starting gate is drawn as four arches with runners inside them, and the crowd is done as heads only, under arches of their own. The pavement lies in the town's archaeological museum, across from the old Roman pools ...

The sport as we know it is barely on the African continent. It made a start in the Free State with seven standardbreds, six off a plane from Sweden and one from Australia, and the first formal harness races in South Africa were run at Senekal with no betting on them. The carts and the harness came as help from the Swedish trotting people, who beam their races in free for the South African windows. The chairman said jog carts were likely to be built locally soon, and that there was no government money in it. Senekal is a town in the Free State ...

The One Light at the Barns

The people in this book are made up. The place is real.

We come in at five in the afternoon by the house's own directions, since the house says the map services get this road wrong. The way is plain enough once somebody tells it. Just south of Forest Lake, before the interstate splits, we take the exit, turn right at the top of the ramp, and go west three-quarters of a mile on a road that used to have a different name, a new name laid on an old bed. Somebody had to write that warning about the maps. A person wrote it, at a desk, after the tenth phone call from the top of the ramp, and we would like her name as well, though not to print it ...

Then the road runs out and the ground opens. A long building stands there with windows down to the floor, more window than wall, and beside it a hotel five stories tall in wood siding and tall windows, which has the look of a cabin somebody kept adding floors to. An enclosed walkway joins the two, and nobody has to cross the lot to get from one to the other. The lot was a lawn once. Now it is a parking lot, empty of people at this hour but for us, and at one end of it stands a drive-in movie screen with nothing on it ...

Past the long building and the hotel lies the racetrack, an oval five-eighths of a mile around, made of lime and stone dust and ringed by wetland. It keeps benches, a rail and an apron on the near side, and it has no grandstand at all. Tonight there is no racing and the lights over the oval are off, which leaves a circle of nothing with a fence around it. The barns stand beyond the far turn and the pond lies off to one side. From the grass all the way around something is calling, frogs or something that sounds like frogs, and the last thing the approach gives us is a smell of water ...

We get here between five and six and we stand in the lot a good while, longer than people stand in lots. There is a door to go through, the door of the long building, where a man checks licenses, and everyone inside it is eighteen or older. Out on the apron on race nights no such age is asked. We let the door wait. We came tonight because there is no racing tonight, and we learn a track on a dark night the way we learn a man when he is not talking ...

At half past five a pickup comes in pulling a two-horse trailer. It passes the door and keeps on toward the barns without slowing, and when something inside the trailer shifts its weight, the whole trailer shifts along with it. Somebody is shipping in for tomorrow night, which is to say a horse is arriving a day ahead. Out past the far turn a single light is burning in a barn. We wonder who is at the wheel at this hour, the trainer, or the trainer's wife, or a groom who will see to the horse's supper before his own. Whoever it is, that barn light is on because somebody is at the barn, and somebody always is ...

With the lights off, the oval can still be read. It is five-eighths of a mile, and a mile race has to go around it one and three-fifths times. That makes more turns than a mile track asks for and fewer than a half-mile track, and the turns are where the horses drawn to the outside give up ground, having the longer way around. On a night like this the track is only ground, ground somebody spread lime on so it would hold a hoof and then let go of it ...

All around the oval there is water standing in the grass, and the calling comes from there. We do not know the names of the things that call and we are not going to look them up. A name is a thing a person owns, and the swamp is not ours to own. Whatever is calling was here before the fish farm that once stood on this ground, and it has gone on calling ...

A stage with its lights off is still a stage, and this one has its set up and somebody already in the barn. Tomorrow night the light will fall on the far turn, on eight horses, and nobody at the rail will look at anything else. Tonight the only light belongs to the barn, one window's worth, and one window is enough for us. We can stand in a parking lot for an hour and look at it ...

At a quarter past six the sun goes down behind the barns. The lights come on inside the long building before the evening is dark enough to need them, and from the lot we see the room through the glass, the tables, the dealers in their white shirts, the red of the chips, and people sitting, not one of them looking out. Beyond the tables hangs a wall of screens. On one of those screens is a track somewhere where it is still light, and a horse is running there right now for somebody's two dollars ...

The hotel lights its windows one at a time. It has a hundred and sixteen rooms on its five stories, and the windows look at the oval. Standing in the lot we ask who makes a hundred and sixteen beds by noon. Somebody does, and we mean to find out who. Down at the end of the lot the drive-in screen stays blank ...

By half past six we are at the door. The man who checks licenses is young, and he has been on his feet since three. He scans a license and hands it back turned around, with the photo facing its owner, the way a thing is handed back when it belongs to somebody. We ask how long his shift runs. Three until eleven, he says, and he tells us he is twenty-two ...

He checks everyone, the young man at the door says, the regulars too, and among the regulars is a man who has come nearly every night for eleven years and does not mind being checked. Eleven years of nights comes to around four thousand. What those nights cost is somebody else's business. What they amount to is a man with somewhere to go, and that is more than most people standing at a door can say. We ask whether anyone here knows the man's name, and the young man says everybody knows his first name. It is a first name at a door, and we keep that one ...

The door itself is a scanner, a haircut, and a rope strung between two brass posts that is not stopping anybody. The carpet begins exactly at the door, which makes the lot the last ground under us tonight that is not trying to be anything. Our licenses are scanned and handed back, each with its photo facing its owner. Outside, the lot holds our cars and the marks the trailer left, the drive-in screen at the end of it is blank, the oval is dark, and the one light at the barns stays on ...

At three in the morning we are in the card room, which was drawn for fifty tables the year the track opened. The cards had to wait for the horses. The state would not let a first card be dealt until the house had run fifty days of them, so every table in here stands on a season of racing ...

The windows go to the floor, and at this hour they are black and hold the room in them. A dealer at one table shuffles real cards, and every round she deals, every hand, goes out by screen to eleven seats. The poker room does not close. The tavern is open too, under the name of the fish farm that was on this ground before the track. Out past the glass, where we cannot see it, the bubbler in the pond is running in the dark ...

The room is cards in shoes, the boxes they are dealt out of, and chips in racks, and coffee in paper cups with the lids left on. It is eleven chairs bolted to the floor, facing eleven small screens and a woman. It is a shoe with six decks in it. It is a window where a wall should be, and past the window a pond we cannot see, with a machine in it keeping fish alive at fifty-five degrees. That is the room ...

At ten to four a supervisor comes to the table with the eleven screens and touches the woman on the shoulder. She plays the hand out. Then she spreads her fingers, turns both palms up for the camera in the ceiling, and steps away from the table. A man steps in, and the hands on the eleven screens are his now. She goes the length of the windows with her arms folded and on through the tavern door, and inside, at the counter, a cook in his whites is holding a paper cup ...

We go over and ask. She is twenty-nine, she tells us, with five years of dealing behind her and two of the five at the screens. We ask what eight hours of six decks does to a pair of hands, and she opens hers and closes them to show us, and says the wrists go first ...

The cook came on at eleven last night. At three he feeds the poker room and whoever comes over the walkway from the hotel in socks. There are a hundred and sixteen rooms over there, and we wonder how many of them are awake and hungry at three, and how far a person will walk in socks for a plate of something from this counter ...

At half past four the dealer with the wrists comes back. Her palms go up to the camera once more, and the table is hers again. The camera in the ceiling sees those palms every time she comes and goes, and we wonder whether it has ever noticed the wrists ...

At ten past six the black in the windows turns grey. After that the room comes back through the glass a little less each minute, until the parking lot is out there instead of the tables. The cook walks out of the tavern and stands at the glass as long as a cup of coffee lasts, and then he returns to the counter ...

By eleven in the morning we are at the pond. This ground went into business first as a fish farm. The farm had ponds, more than twenty of them, and a restaurant where three hundred could sit down, and on the grass it held company picnics three thousand strong, and out beside the interstate it kept a giant fish made of fiberglass. A quarter of a century has passed since it shut. People kept the name in their heads all the same, and when the next house came along it put that name above its tavern door ...

One of those ponds is open again. The house dug it ten years ago in the place where the twenty had been, and it keeps rainbow trout in it, a fresh load twice a week, in water a bubbler holds at fifty-five degrees. The pond is open some days and shut others, and today a Scout is handing out the poles. The rule here is that a caught fish is a kept fish, and no one is allowed to throw one back. The price goes by the pound. After that the kitchen will clean a trout and cook it, or it can go home in a bag of ice. When there is a wedding the pond can seat a hundred and twenty ...

The near bank belongs this morning to a father and two children, who have three poles among them. Over on the far side, not moving at all, is a gray heron in the reeds ...

Everybody who puts a line in comes out ahead by one fish. Of the three on the bank only the little one is keeping still, and holding still is the whole of fishing and about half of horses ...

The pond is the one place on the grounds where the customer knows to the ounce, and pays it standing up, and smiles ...

Somebody has to clean the fish, and the somebody is a cook. The kitchen is the one that fed the poker room at three this morning, and by noon a cook there has put down the smoked dip and picked up the knife ...

The pond is a pole and a bobber, and a bucket holding an inch of water. It is a post with a scale on it and a hook below the scale. It is a sign with a rule on it and no please. It is a machine under the water pushing air, and no pond has one of those unless a person gave it one. It is reeds. It is one bird, gray, the size of a coat rack, that fishes here for nothing and is the only creature on the property allowed to throw one back, and it never has. And it is a count, which was twenty ponds once, then nineteen filled in, then one dug again where they lay ...

The fish farm left the new house a single prop. It is a hole in the ground, filled with cold water and with fish that cannot be thrown back ...

At half past eleven the service road brings down a truck carrying a tank, and the truck backs to the edge of the pond. Its driver lays a hose from tank to water and opens a valve, and for two minutes the hose runs silver. Both children quit fishing to watch. When the tank is empty the driver coils up his hose, marks a sheet on his clipboard, and heads off toward the barns ...

The hose gives two minutes of silver and then runs clear, and that much is a truck's worth ...

A track would say the field was just put in the gate, meaning all the starters are lined up at once. Each has the same chance as the next. Not one has a past performance, the record a horse carries from its earlier races, and so this is the fairest card we have ever seen on a racetrack, and it is the one nobody bets. The little one's line has not moved ...

The truck comes twice a week. Every fish off that truck will leave the pond in a bag or in a bird, and the house keeps a sheet for only one of the two. We would like to know the ratio. It is the one count on the property where both columns are fish ...

We wanted to know where the fish come from, and we put it to the driver before he left, while the hose was still being coiled. The town he named was one we did not know. Two hours, he said. He hauls for four or five places, and of them all only this one has a racetrack around it. He has driven fish for eleven years. He said the fish do not mind the truck, it is the pouring they mind, and they get over it. He has a daughter who is about as old as the little one, and we asked whether she had been here with him. Not yet, he told us, maybe next summer, depending on the schedule ...

Had we been asked to place the players, we could not have blocked it better. The cast arrives in a tank, and the villain is already upstage in the reeds ...

At noon the smallest child's bobber goes under. Her father reaches for the rod, touches it, and takes his hand away. She brings the fish in herself, slowly, the way she was shown, and the Scout gets a net under it. It is a rainbow trout and looks to be about a pound. At the post the Scout hangs it from the hook, and the scale makes it a pound and two ounces. Her father pays. She will not have it cleaned, so the Scout packs it in a bag of ice and hands her the bag, and she carries it with both arms ...

We ask what she will do with it, and she says eat it. We ask who will cook it, and she says her father. Her father says he has not cooked a trout once in his life and will know how by tonight. The kitchen is not getting that fish. It is hers, she says. Nobody in that family uses the word bought ...

So there is a girl carrying a fish with both arms because it is heavier than a fish. We will make nothing of that. We will set it in the row and let it stay, and the row is pole, bucket, scale, sign, bird, hose, bag, girl, fish ...

Half past twelve brings a woman with a folder along the far bank, and a couple with her. She points to the water, then the grass, then the sky, in that order. She paces off a distance, writes, and paces again. The heron takes one step and stops ...

At six the light will come low off the far turn and stand behind the couple, and for about eleven minutes it will go copper on the pond. If she times the vows to it, nobody will remember a word they said and everybody will remember the water ...

We would guess that the people who get married at a racetrack are people who met at a racetrack ...

The same kitchen serves the hundred and twenty at the pond, and the next morning whoever is on folds the hundred and twenty chairs ...

The wedding will be a hundred and twenty chairs set on grass, beside a pond nobody can lose at, and the only guest eating for nothing will be the one without an invitation, the bird in the reeds ...

At one in the afternoon the poles go back to the Scout in the father's hands, and the bag goes with the girl. The woman with the folder walks with her couple toward the hotel. The Scout puts something down in a notebook, and then he takes a pole for himself and sits on the bank. The heron lifts, slowly, and goes over the reeds toward the wetland around the oval, and does not come back while anyone is watching. The bubbler runs ...

At dusk we are out on the apron, and it costs nothing to stand here and watch the racing. Families have brought blankets and lawn chairs and settled on the grass behind the benches, and children are loose on the apron, because the age the card room asks at its door is not asked out here. Nobody built a grandstand. What the place gives us is a rail, some benches, and a board that shows the odds in red numbers. The finish line is here too, and anyone in the tavern can watch it through the glass without getting up ...

The horses tonight are Standardbreds. Each one pulls a driver on a race bike of about twenty-five pounds. Some of them trot and the others pace, and either way the gait, which is the manner of going, has two beats to it. The pacers wear hopples, straps that join the legs on each side, and the straps help a horse hold its pace. When a horse drops its gait and runs, the driver has to steer it to the outside and give up ground, and should the running gain it anything, it is set back in the order. Every race is a mile. On this oval a mile is one and three-fifths times around, and so the horses come by the apron twice ...

A race goes off about every twenty-five minutes, and a voice calls each one from a booth above the rail and behind it. The house takes a fixed share of all the money bet, which is called the pool, and so it does not care which horse gets home first. Now the horses of the first race come out behind a car that has wings on it, wings that fold. At the rail stands a man with eleven years of nights behind him, nearly every one of them spent here, and he is holding a program, a pencil, and two dollars ...

We know where we want to be. It is at the rail, about forty yards before the wire, which is the finish line. From there we can watch the horses come out of the last turn and tell how much each one has left ...

The man beside us at the rail is the same man we heard of at the door, the one with eleven years of nights. Everybody knows his first name, and the young man at the door kept it to himself, which we respected. So we ask the man himself, and he hands it over in about a second, and it will not be said here. He is sixty-eight years old. His wife came with him the first four years and has not since. He does not tell us why, and we leave it alone. He bets on a single race each night, two dollars, to win, and the horse is one he has chosen before he arrives. Then he stays at the rail for every race on the card ...

The race is eight horses with thirty-two legs among them, and sixteen wheels. It is eight men sitting on nothing, their feet stuck out ahead of them. It is a car whose wings fold, and a pencil tucked behind an ear. It is lime dust, because the ground here is crushed rock the color of a cigarette. It is straps on the pacers' legs, so the two legs on one side move together, which is a thing a man decided a horse should do. And it is a voice above us and behind us. We mean to listen to that voice, because nobody else on the property is paid to say what is happening while it is happening ...

The car rolls down the stretch with both wings spread, and eight horses keep their noses right up on the wings. Then the wings fold, the car pulls off, and the curtain is up, and it was the curtain that was moving, not the stage ...

With the wings folded and the car gone, the horses come by the apron for the first time strung out in a line, the four horse on the outside, and they bend into the turn. The call tumbles down from the booth, names and numbers climbing as they come. Along the backstretch, the far straight side of the oval, the favorite, who wears the number one, falls out of its gait and runs. Its driver pulls it wide and stands up in the bike, and the rest go past. Out of the last turn the six is in front and hanging on, with the four second and closing. At the wire the six still has it, and the four is a length behind. The man with the pencil does not move. His horse was the one ...

The one broke, which is the sport's word for a horse leaving its gait. The best horse in the race went faster than every other and lost by doing it, and its driver stood up in the bike like a man who has been told bad news on the phone ...

The man with the pencil says nothing. He writes something in his program, and when we ask what it is, he turns the page toward us. He has put down the time the one broke, and he does not mean the time of the race, he means the time of night. He keeps a list of them. It holds eleven years of the nights his horse broke, each one by the clock. This, he says, is the single thing about the sport he has ever wanted to know, and since nobody will tell him, he is finding it out himself. He has eleven years of them by now, and no pattern yet ...

The breaks over the nights, at one horse a night, come out to a rate. We could work it, and we will not say it. He is finding it out for himself, and to hand him the number would be to take the one thing he comes to this rail for ...

So there is a man writing the time of night in the margin of a program, and we will keep him. The row is pencil, program, the time of night, and eleven years of them. A person could build the whole sport out of what that man has written down and nobody would believe a word of it, and every word would be true ...

For the second race the lights over the oval come on, though the sky has light in it yet, and for a time the two burn together. Up on the board the odds shift every few seconds. The man with the pencil walks to the betting window and walks back with no ticket, since his one race is already run ...

He bets once and watches eight. The whole evening costs him a coffee, and on all the apron he alone is watching the races and not his money. We would not call him wise. He is a man with a pencil who wants to learn when horses break, and the only way he has found is to stand at this rail for the rest of his life ...

We ask who drives the four. A woman of forty-one drives him, we are told, and she is his trainer as well. A third of him is hers, and she shares him with two people who come down from up north each time he starts. Those two sit on the far bench, since being near the rail when he goes is more than they can stand. Then we ask who holds the horse afterward. A groom does, a boy. He will walk the horse forty minutes to cool him, and have him in the barn again before the fourth race. We will find that boy tomorrow morning ...

The breed takes its name from a standard. Once, a horse earned its place in the book, the registry of the breed, by trotting a mile inside a set time. That time was the standard, and the name still says so. Pedigree, the record of a horse's parents, took the standard's place long ago, and today a Standardbred is a Standardbred because its parents were, like every other aristocrat. So the sport goes by a name that remembers a rule it has stopped keeping. We set that down as fact, not complaint, and the horses go on pulling the name around the oval ...

The second race is run. Its six horse goes wire to wire, which is to say it leads from the start to the finish, and it pays short, a small return on a winning ticket. Back in the crowd behind the benches a child is asleep on a blanket and does not wake for the call. At the rail two men who have never met are explaining the third race, each one to the other and both at once, and neither walks away. The man with the pencil hears them both out. He says nothing and he writes nothing ...

The rail is two men explaining one race at once, each to the other. It is a child asleep through a horse race, which is the loudest quiet we know of. It is the board, which changes its mind every four seconds. It is a boy behind the barn leading a wet horse in circles under one light. It is the only spot on the property where nothing is being sold to us. It is horses, and men who will tell us what they think for free ...

The third race goes off. The man with the pencil speaks, to no one, or to us, since we are the ones beside him, and what he says is, living is a crazy wild night at the track. Then he goes back to watching the horses, who are into the first turn ...

That is a man who has stood at this rail eleven years, telling what he found out ...

We wonder who he was saying it to. It was not us. It was the horses going into the turn. And we notice that he said living and not life. Life is a noun. Living is what he is doing ...

So we have a sentence. A man with a pencil behind his ear said it at a rail, to eight horses going into a turn. It goes in the row just as he said it, with nothing trimmed. It sits there beside the pencil, the program and the time of night ...

He will say something else. Men like that always do ...

The third race ends under the lights, with the sky gone fully dark, and the man with the pencil has nothing more to say. The fourth field follows the car out. At the rail the two men have gotten as far as the fifth race. The child sleeps on the blanket. And behind the barn the boy keeps the four horse walking in circles under the one light, until the horse is dry ...

By five in the morning we are among the barns. They sit out past the far turn, and whoever built them made room for three hundred stalls. The horses are here for the meet, which is the season of racing. It opens in the middle of May and closes on the third Saturday of September, three nights each week and fifty-five nights altogether, and it has two weeks to go ...

The grooms get here ahead of the feed, and the feed gets here ahead of the light. When the morning is bright enough to see by, the horses are taken out to jog, their slow work, and they do it clockwise, which on this track is the wrong way. The carts behind them weigh more than the race bikes. Fast miles are saved for later in the day and are gone the right way around. First, though, a tractor pulls a drag over the surface and leaves it smooth ...

A night of racing here pays out around a hundred and ten thousand dollars in purses, the prize money. Some of that comes from the card room, since the law has a share of the room's take paid over to the purses, so the horses are fed in part by the cards. When a horse is finished racing, a foundation the house created works with a farm where it is taught another life, and most of them get one ...

The barn has a tack shop in it, a store for the gear a horse wears. Right behind the shedrows, the long rows of stalls, the wetland begins. Fog is lying on the shallows by the far turn this morning, and the heron from the pond is out in it, standing on one leg ...

We promised to find him this morning, and he is here. He is the boy who walked the four horse last night. He got in at half past four and has reached his third stall. His age is twenty-three, and this season is his third. The house does not pay him. His trainer does, by the week and by the horse, and home is a place in town he splits with two more grooms and a single bathroom. The meet is over in two weeks. After that he drives to Ohio, to a track that goes on racing all winter, and does this same work in the cold until May brings him back. In his three seasons he has never stood at the rail while a race was run, since a race always finds him back here, with a horse that just ran or one about to. Every race of his working life has reached him on a tack room screen, or has not reached him. We ask him what the finish line looks like from the rail. He says he heard it was nice ...

The barn is straw. It is manure, warm and steaming. It is a wheelbarrow gone soft in its one tire. It is liniment, the rub for sore legs, and we could find it blind. It is a radio in the tack room with a city station on, kept so low that the sound reaches us and the words do not. It is a cat on a bale, who has the only job on the property with tenure. It is hopples hung on a nail like a pair of trousers somebody stepped out of. It is a hoof pick. It is a bucket of water wearing a skin of hay. That is the room. Everything in it is for something ...

A horse is not a number before eight in the morning. No board has him, no line, no program, and nobody has him at five to two. He is only a horse, eating in a stall. An hour from now he goes out the wrong way with a kid in a parka sitting behind him, and in his whole week that is the only time nobody has money on him ...

We are backstage. Every show has a backstage and no audience has ever seen one, and this one is three hundred doors long, with its crew working in the dark. The cast is the horses and the crew is the grooms. The stage manager is the trainer, who is due in ten minutes with coffee. She will go down every leg with her hands, like a woman checking the set for a loose board. When the audience goes home happy nobody claps for the man who swept the stage ...

At half past five the trainer walks down the shedrow carrying two coffees, and the boy gets one. She lets herself into the stall of the four horse, slides a hand down all four legs, front ones and back ones, and straightens. Whatever she tells the boy, he nods at it. The feed cart can be heard by now, and up and down the row the horses are calling for it. Then it comes, and eight horses eating turn out to make a single sound. It is a sound like rain on a roof if the roof were made of something soft ...

He came out of it fine, the trainer told the boy. That was all of it. He nodded, she went along to the next stall, and last night's second-place finish had its whole review, from the one person on the property whose opinion the horse would care about. We ask what she would have told him if the leg had been hot. She says, less. The boy would have found it first anyway, she says, since he is the one with his hands on that horse eleven hours a day. She pays him for his hands and gets his eyes for free ...

Six brings the first light. Fog covers the wetland and comes up as high as the top of the rail. The tractor is finished, and it rolls off the track trailing its drag and parks. Now the first horses are sent out to jog. The fog hides them, and our ears get them before our eyes do, as two beats on the lime, the roll of a cart, and a man's voice saying nothing in particular. Then a horse and a hooded driver come out of the white at a trot, going the wrong way, and go back into it ...

Sound arrives ahead of sight. We get two beats, a wheel and a voice. After that comes a horse made out of fog, and then no horse ...

They are jogged the wrong way to let the horse feel the difference. Right way means go. Wrong way means what day is it. The horse can tell one from the other. So can a man who has been around horses, and from the far side of a fog he can tell by the sound which way they are going ...

We ask who does the driving in the morning. Mostly, we learn, it is not the people who drive at night. It is grooms, and the trainer, and a boy whose license is good for mornings and no good for nights. That boy has had the four horse around this track the wrong way some two hundred mornings. He has never had him around it the right way with money on it, not once. He says he would like to, one time. Then he says probably not, they do not give the night to the morning kids, and he returns to his stall ...

At a quarter past seven the farrier, who shoes the horses, backs a truck up to the end of the shedrow. Somebody leads a horse out and holds it. An old shoe is pulled, the foot is trimmed, a new shoe is fitted, and the hammer rings on the anvil all the way down the row and out across the fog. A horse van has come in two barns away. Toward it walks an old horse with gray in his face, wearing a halter and no hopples. Somebody other than the boy is walking him, but the boy has stopped to watch ...

We ask who the old horse is. He is a pacer of fourteen, we are told, who won more than he cost twice over, and last week his trainer called it enough. The foundation the house made has a farm, and on the farm is a program, and he is going to it this morning to learn to be ridden. Should it take, he has fifteen more years ahead as somebody's horse, with no starting gate in any of them. The woman at his halter has walked him for nine years. She is not crying. She says she will cry in the truck, on the way back ...

He is fourteen years old and leaving on his feet ...

This morning, in a tack room, his hopples came off him for the last time. They are on a nail now and they still have his shape. Some other horse will wear them, and the shape will change ...

Those hopples are a costume. When a show closes, the costume is taken off on the last night and put on a hanger. Then the actor walks out the stage door in his own clothes, and nobody in the alley knows who he was. That horse is walking to the van in his own clothes. He does not know he was anybody ...

We ask when a horse is done. It is the trainer who decides. She does it on mornings like this, she tells us, with a hand on a leg. The horses that break down are not the hard ones, since they make the decision for her. The hard ones are fine and slow. He was fine and slow. She kept him racing a season longer than she ought to have, she says, because he liked it. Then she says she does not know how she knew he liked it. And then she says she knew ...

Up goes the ramp of the van. The woman who has walked the horse nine years climbs into her own truck, and she sits in the cab and does not start it. The van leaves by the service road and swings onto the road with the new name laid on the old bed. By now the fog is thin, and we can watch the van the whole way to the turn. The farrier's hammer starts again. The boy goes back to his fourth stall ...

So a horse leaves on his feet in the morning, and there is somebody sorry to see him go ...

The row is straw, manure, liniment, a radio and a cat. It is a pair of hopples with a shape in them. It is a boy on his fourth stall. We would rather leave all of it where it is than take one thing ...

By half past eight the jog carts are coming in. The horses get a wash and a walk and are put up, meaning put back in their stalls, and the tractor returns to the track to drag it for the afternoon. The door of the tack shop is unlocked. The shallows have no heron in them now. In the tack room somebody has turned the radio up, and the words reach us at last. They are about the weather, which is going to be clear ...

At two in the afternoon we are in the hotel. It stands five stories high in wood siding and tall windows, and the enclosed walkway ties it to the card room. There are a hundred and sixteen rooms, and the ones along one side face the oval. Every room has a large television, and what the television shows is the feed the track sends out itself ...

The lobby keeps a fireplace and a wide corridor. In the front lounge sits a coffee table that holds a bed of sand under a sheet of glass. A steel ball travels through the sand and leaves a pattern behind it, and then it draws over the pattern it made ...

The hotel opened its doors in March of twenty twenty. It had five days. Then the state shut down for the pandemic, and the building stood dark for months ...

There is no racing today. We are on the fifth floor, and one window up here holds the whole property. It holds the oval, where the tractor is pulling its drag, and the wetland that rings it, and the barns and the pond. It holds the lot and the blank screen at the lot's end, and the road that once went by another name. Along the corridor a housekeeper is coming, room by room, with a cart ...

Seen in one window, the property is a model. A designer brings such a thing to the first meeting in a box, little trees and all, and somebody stands over it deciding where the light should go. Up here everything sits closer together than it feels when we are down among it. From down there the card room and the barn are different countries ...

From this window the whole ground can be said in one breath. The ground is an oval. It is a tractor, and a rake behind the tractor. It is water in the grass all the way around. It is barns, three hundred doors of them. It is one pond where twenty were. It is a movie screen in a parking lot. It is a road with the wrong name. That is all of it ...

A horse watched from a bed is a number on a screen and a horse watched from a rail is a horse ...

The woman with the cart is in the corridor, and at the door we put to her what we have been asking since the parking lot, which is who makes a hundred and sixteen beds by noon. She does. On a full day two others make them with her, and on a slow day one. A shift is fourteen rooms, and it is more when the whole hotel checks out together. She is fifty-four. She has been here from the hotel's opening, which puts her here for the five days. For five days, she tells us, she made up rooms for guests. After that the manager told her to leave it ready, and she made up every one of the hundred and sixteen rooms for no one. She was home four months. The building she returned to had stood ready the whole time. The beds were fine, she says, and there was dust on the glass. It was the strangest work of her life, and she thinks of it each time she opens a room and it is already made ...

At half past two the cart comes to a stop outside the next room. The housekeeper carries an armful of linen in and leaves the door open behind her, and we can see the room through it. The bed has been stripped and the curtain is open. The window has the oval in it, and the television is dark. Out in the corridor, light from the window at the end is lying along the carpet ...

The fifth floor is a room with its bed stripped and the oval in its window. It is a cart with towels in three sizes. It is a woman who made a hundred and sixteen beds for nobody ...

The bed looks at a window that holds the whole track, and next to the window the television holds the race. So a man can lie here and check the screen against the world and never leave the pillow. The room argues with itself. The window is winning. We ask the woman with the cart, and she tells us that she always finds the curtains open ...

She has one more thing to tell. During the five days, before the state closed, a couple had this very room, the one whose door is standing open. They were the first guests she ever made up a room for. She remembers them because they came for a race and there was no race. It was March and the meet had not begun. The two of them spent an hour at that window, looking out at an empty oval with snow on its turns. Then the state closed, everybody went home, and she has not seen them since ...

So two people stood at a window for an hour looking at an empty stage, and then the theater closed for four months. A theater that goes dark after five nights and reopens is the only kind that knows what it is for. Nobody who was in this building for the five days has any doubt what the building is for. The woman with the cart has none. She made the beds for nobody, and then she made them again ...

At three we are downstairs in the front lounge, and the sand table is going. The steel ball is working inward under the glass on a curving line, and behind it the pattern it drew an hour ago is being crossed out and covered. Nobody has lit the fireplace. At the desk a man in a suit is checking in, with a rolling bag. The clerk asks if he wants a room on the track side, and he says whichever is quieter ...

The table is a steel ball in sand under glass, and the ball draws a line and covers the one before. The tractor does that same thing to the track at dawn. It is ball, sand and glass, and a pattern half gone, and a line arriving. The sand table is the only machine on the property that makes nothing and takes nothing ...

The ball goes around and it is never the same pattern and there is no wire, there is nothing to pick ...

Somebody has to dust the glass, and the housekeeper is the one. It is the first thing she does each morning, ahead of the rooms, because the owner likes it, and because guests lay their hands on the table and by nine the glass has prints on it. Once, she says, she stopped and watched the ball a while in an empty lobby. That was in the four months, when she had come in to look over the building, and the table was running with nobody there. To her it was the saddest thing she had ever seen, a table drawing for nobody. Then she thought it was the opposite. She still does not know which ...

The ball does not mind ...

The man at the desk wanted the quieter side. Of all the guests we have heard about, he is the first to come here and not want the horses in his window. We asked, and he is here for a funeral, his father's, up the road, tomorrow. He did not want a window on anything ...

At four somebody from the staff comes out behind the banquet rooms, onto the patio, and lights the fire pit, though no one is out there and the evening is warm. Out on the oval the tractor completes its lap, and the track lies flat behind the drag. Up on the fifth floor the housekeeper shuts the last door and rolls her cart to the elevator. And in the lounge the steel ball gets to the center of the sand and stops. Then it starts outward again, along a new line ...

At seven in the evening we are in the lot again, and the lot was a lawn once. Back in the eighties the fish farm turned its grass into an amphitheater. Nobody put in seats. There was the slope and there was a stage, and the biggest acts of that day came and played, southern rock, heavy metal, and a fiddler. His best known song is about a bet with the devil, and in the song the human wins. The property's own account is that the night the fiddler played drew more people onto this grass than the ground has held before or since, and that they sang the story of the devil losing ...

Tonight the lawn is a parking lot with a drive-in screen at one end. On certain nights a film plays on that screen, and the sound travels by car radio. This is one of those nights, and there is no racing. Cars turn in from the road with the old name and line up in rows, every one facing the screen. The oval behind the lot is dark, and at the barns the light is on. One edge of the lot still has a strip of the old grass. A woman of sixty or so has parked beside it and set up a lawn chair on the grass ...

The lot is windshields, a hundred of them, all tilted the same way. It is radio dials. It is two kids on a mattress in the bed of a pickup. It is a screen as big as a barn wall, still empty, with a steel back turned to the road. It is exhaust where the grass was. It is a strip of grass left at the edge like the crust off a sandwich. It is a lawn chair with a woman in it, and she is not watching the screen. Forty years ago thousands of people stood here on grass with nothing between them. Tonight a hundred people sit in a hundred boxes with the windows up, and we are not going to say which is better. The grass held them closer ...

This ground had a stage before it had a track. Before anybody thought of horses, somebody stood on that slope and thought, a crowd could stand here and look that way ...

The woman in the lawn chair was here. We do not mean tonight, we mean the night of the fiddler. She was nineteen then and is sixty-one now, and she stood beside her sister, about where the third row of cars is parked. She tells us she remembers the grass better than the song. The grass was wet, and she remembers how it smelled, and the fish farm's lights burning across the pond. When the devil lost, she says, everybody around her screamed like it was a horse race. She had not been to one yet. She has gone to a hundred since, every one of them here. Then she gives us what we came for. The morning after, she came back with her sister, and the fish farm paid them five dollars apiece to pick cups up off this grass, and they did it all day. She had never seen so many cups in one place. And she has never forgotten how a field looks the morning after thousands of people have been happy on it. Somebody had to clean up after the devil lost, and it was two girls for five dollars ...

So there are cups on wet grass the morning after, and two girls, and five dollars ...

At half past seven the light goes off the face of the screen and the lot fills up. A truck gets the last space in the second row, waved into it by somebody from the staff with a flashlight. At the entrance a sign gives the radio frequency. One car has its windows down, and out of it comes the sound of a family arguing over the volume ...

While it was still light we walked the second row to see who had come. A man is by himself with a dog. Two teenagers would rather be alone, and they have a pickup between them and their parents' car, which is one row back. A family of five came with a cooler and a baby. Three women in their seventies have been coming every summer since the screen went up, and in all that time they have not gone inside the building once, and they do not plan to. And the boy from the barns is here, the boy. The trainer told him to come and see a picture, so he is sitting in the passenger seat of her truck. He has never been to one of these either. It is the first time, he says, that he has sat facing the lot and not the track. Who is in the cars is a town ...

By eight it is dark enough. The screen comes on white. Then the house gets its thirty seconds, its own picture, with its name and its horses and its line about staying and playing, the letters twenty feet tall. After that the film starts. The sound comes from a hundred radios at a hundred volumes and lies over the lot in pieces ...

Every car has the same picture and the same words, but the words cross the field in a hundred pieces at a hundred loudnesses. Standing on the grass, we hear a sentence start in one car and finish in another ...

The row is the sound in the fog, the eight horses eating, the sand under the glass and the hundred radios. Every one of them lasts as long as it is happening and not a second after ...

The woman in the lawn chair has one thing more to tell us, and she tells it as the picture starts. That night, she says, was the only time she has ever been in a crowd that big and felt safe in it. She has spent forty years trying to work out why. She thinks it is because everybody in it wanted the same man to win and he was not any of them ...

At ten the film is over. The screen goes white and then dark, and the engines start. Headlights come on a row at a time and swing toward the exit. The woman folds her chair into the trunk and leaves with everybody else. Nothing from the night the devil lost is on the strip of grass, not a cup, not a stake, not the mark of a stage. It has been mowed every week of every summer for forty years. Once the last car is out, the lot is empty and the screen is a pale wall. Tomorrow there is racing. We walk across the lot to the door of the long building ...

Forty years of mowing is grass, and nothing ...

The screen at the end of the lot is pale. The oval is dark. The one light at the barns is on, and it will be on at half past four, when the boy comes to his first stall. Tomorrow there is racing, and post time is seven ...

After It There Is No Recall

The people in this book are made up. The place is real.

It is the last night of the meet, a Saturday, the nineteenth of September, twenty twenty-six. The racing began on a Sunday halfway through May and has filled fifty-five nights, the Tuesdays starting at seven and the Thursdays and Sundays at six. This final week was shuffled. Thursday went dark, which means no racing, and Friday and Saturday got the two Night of Champions cards, a card being a night of races. Both were made around the races for horses sired in Minnesota. A year ago the closing card handed out seven hundred and eighty thousand dollars in purses, the prize money, and matched the record. An average night comes to about a hundred and ten thousand. Post time tonight was six, and we have had a race about every twenty-five minutes since ...

Between the building and the rail lies a paved strip called the apron. It has benches and no roof. The plan for the track had a grandstand in it, and the money was gone before one could go up. For the first summer bleachers stood on the grass next to the apron, and a year later they were gone, since the liquor license let beer be sold on the apron and nowhere on the grass. People carry out lawn chairs and blankets and take the grass anyway, without the beer. The windows of the building reach down to the floor. Through that glass the tavern looks at the finish line, and it is named for the fish farm that had this ground before there was a track. The room where the races of other tracks play on screens all year has the same view, and the hotel turns its windows to the oval. In the infield, the ground inside the oval, stands a video board. It gives the odds up to the moment the gate car, the car that starts the field, draws off and folds its wings. After the wire, which is the finish line, it gives the replay, the order of finish and the prices. One camera there photographs that line and nothing else. When two horses reach it together the judges separate them from that picture, and until they have, the board says PHOTO ...

A ticket is paper, a slip from a window or a machine. Printed on it are the track, the date, the race, the kind of bet, the numbers of the horses, the amount and the time of sale, then a barcode, then a number beneath it. Whoever hands it in is paid. The odds are not on it. By the rule of the state a torn ticket goes unpaid, unless there is another way to verify the bet. A ticket that lost is worth its paper. Most get torn and some get dropped. The tickets do not go into the cans at the doors, and by the end of a card the apron is white with them ...

We have never torn one. We have folded a few and kept them, and the coats they were kept in are gone ...

The last race of the night is the twelfth. The horses jog up behind the car, the car draws away with its wings folding, the odds on the board hold still, and the race is called down to us from the booth. People rise from the benches for the stretch and lay their forearms along the rail. The four is on the rail and the seven is outside it, and they reach the wire as one. The board says PHOTO ...

Nearly everybody on the apron keeps standing. One man does not stay, a man in a canvas work jacket at the fourth bench. He reads his ticket and then the board, and tears the ticket across. The halves drop by his feet. He heads for the doors with his hands in his pockets and is inside before any numbers show. The picture gives it to the seven by a nose. The board says OFFICIAL, with the prices below. Two dollars to win on the seven is worth nine dollars and forty cents ...

We have torn a thing across before the picture was up, more than once, and walked in the same way, hands in the pockets. The picture never hurried for us either ...

Any other night, half the apron would be gone by now. Not tonight. With the numbers up, people keep to the rail and look at a track that has nothing on it. A woman photographs the board and its prices. A man does not leave his program on the bench. He folds it in half and slips it inside his coat. A girl of about eight walks past the last bench with her father. Her ticket is still whole in her hand, two dollars to show on the eight, and the eight came in sixth. A show ticket pays when the horse is in the first three. Her father has his program under his arm and is not looking at the ticket ...

The winner's circle has plenty going on. The driver of the seven is down off the bike, the light two-wheeled cart a driver sits on. The horse stands under the lights in harness, the sweat dark along its neck, and across its back lies a blanket with the name of the race on it. People in good coats have been placed around the horse for the picture. It breathes hard and looks at nothing. After the picture it is led through the gap in the rail toward the paddock, the long building where horses wait before they race. At the gap the blanket comes off, because the blanket is for the picture ...

A man with a canvas bag has had the last bench since the tenth race, the bench closest to the mouth of the hallway. Most race nights find him here. He bets early, quits at the sixth race and only watches after that. Once the last race is official and the apron begins to empty, he gets up to walk it, bending. The sport calls such a man a stooper, and so does he. He has a rule for when to begin. After the last race the windows go on cashing, and he gives them ten minutes before he bends for anything, because people come back for what they dropped ...

We would not have thought of the ten minutes. It is a kindness to people he will never see, and he keeps it by the watch ...

The board changes. It runs the replay once more and stops, the prices stay up, and along the bottom a line of type gives thanks to everyone for the season. The caller gives us the same thanks from the booth, then good night, and the speakers click. Behind the tavern glass the last tables still have people at them, and a server is turning chairs up onto the empty ones. The shutter is down on the beer stand at the far end. Two people run it, and on any other night they would restock for Tuesday. Tonight the tubs go out onto the grass and the ice is tipped. One rolls the kegs indoors on a hand truck. The other padlocks the shutter and tugs the lock twice ...

Two people come back inside the ten minutes. A man gets his program off the second bench. A woman goes over the ground beneath the third bench and by the rail below it. She does not find what she came for, and she leaves the scarf and goes. When his watch says ten minutes, the man with the bag stands ...

He works the apron in the order it filled, starting at the mouth of the hallway and going toward the beer stand, the benches first and the rail after. He carries no stick. He bends at the knees, and with two fingers he turns every paper over, reads it and lets it go. He leaves each one face down. Close to the rail a film of dust lies on the paving, and he takes it for lime from the track. Paper there has gone grey on its upward side ...

The apron holds halves of tickets, whole tickets folded once, whole tickets flat, fifty-cent tickets from the last two races, programs with every race marked in pen but the last one, a child's shoe, plastic cups, and a scarf on the third bench. He lets the scarf lie, because a scarf gets come back for. Nearly every ticket was sold at the windows here. A few are on other tracks, printed by the machines inside in the afternoon and carried out and dropped with the rest. Most are for two dollars and some for twenty. One is two hundred dollars to win on the one horse in the ninth, and that horse finished fourth. He reads it twice before it goes face down ...

At the fourth bench he finds the ticket the man in the canvas jacket tore. Its halves are where they fell, one on the bench and one beneath. He joins them on his knee. It reads race twelve, the seven, two dollars to win, and the tear runs through the barcode. He says to nobody that this is the nine forty. Both halves go into his shirt, in the breast pocket, apart from the bag ...

The bag is for whole tickets, the ones bound for the window. By the beer stand it holds two small ones. One is a show ticket, and its horse won the eleventh. The other is a place ticket, which pays for first or second, and its horse ran second in the eighth. The result of one more ticket he cannot read. It is another from the machines inside, an exacta for two dollars, a bet on the first two horses in order, on a California race that will not start until past midnight here. He holds it under the light for a while. Then it joins the halves in the shirt pocket ...

One window is still cashing. The clerk there has counted this drawer since June and does not look at the keys. Four people stand at the window, then two, then only him. Before the bag is open she asks whether it is two, and he says it is. He lays the tickets down. The reader beeps twice as she passes them under it, and she counts out five dollars and eighty cents with her eyes off the drawer ...

Then he lays down the halves. She joins them as he did, though on the counter, one finger to each half. The reader cannot take a torn code, so she leaves it alone. She turns the halves over to find the number printed below the code, and types it. The terminal finds the ticket. It is a live winner, uncashed, nine dollars and forty cents. She says somebody tore it before the photo. He tells her the fourth bench, the fourth or the fifth ...

From under the counter she takes an envelope, and from a clip a form. On the form go the ticket number, the amount, and the reason, which is torn. The halves go into the envelope, which she leaves unsealed. She tells him the manager has to sign for a torn one and is counting the night, and it will be a few minutes. He says he has a few minutes. She asks whether he will wait or wants it mailed, and he says he will wait ...

She nods and goes back to her drawer. He stands off to one side under the light, the bag on his shoulder, and has the California ticket out to read again. He asks whether the race book is open tomorrow. She is counting twenties and says it is, but she will not be, tonight being her last. He says he knows it and puts the ticket back in the pocket ...

A little after eleven the sweeper comes through the doors. She has a push broom, a dustpan on a stick, and a cart with a roll of clear bags on it. Each summer since the track opened she has swept here, the first one under the bleachers. She is not tall. She begins by the beer stand at the far end, the place where he finished. Nobody ever put the arrangement on paper. He gets fifteen minutes ...

She does not read any of it. Her strokes are long, toward the rail and then back along it, and the paper gathers into a ridge. The ridge goes into the pan, the pan goes into a bag, the bag is tied and set on the cart. One cart is enough on a Tuesday. Tonight it is full at the fourth bench. She goes in for another. She lifts the scarf, hangs it on the back of the bench, then sweeps beneath ...

She is at the second bench when the lights of the track go off. They go in banks, first the backstretch, the straight on the far side, next the far turn, and the stretch last. What is left of the oval is a dark shape, and behind it the wetland. The wetland is loud. A diesel starts up somewhere past the track and idles, and a second one follows. On the apron the lights stay on, since their switch is separate and indoors ...

The manager arrives at the window from inside, carrying a clipboard. The name of the house is on his shirt. He went into the money room when the twelfth went official and has been there since. He looks at the envelope, joins the halves on the counter for himself and signs the form. Then the clerk counts out nine dollars and forty cents. The man with the bag puts it into his pants pocket beside the five eighty and gives his thanks ...

The manager tells him to have a good winter, and then tells her he will see her in May. She says they will see, and the manager goes back in. The man with the bag says fifteen twenty. She says fifteen twenty back and pulls the drawer. From the inside, the shutter comes down across the window ...

The infield board goes dark, the prices first. The line about the season lasts a moment more, then it goes too. Now the infield is as dark as the track. We can see screens still lit through the glass, six or seven, in the room behind the tavern. One shows horses walking under lights in a paddock, somewhere two hours earlier than here. A few hotel windows turned to the oval have light in them ...

The man with the bag takes the last bench again, at the mouth of the hallway, and sits for as long as the sweeper needs to finish. She does not hurry for him. She has thought before that he stays to see it bare, and she has never asked. She says twelve as she passes his bench with the broom. He nods. She sweeps as far as the doors. Then she returns to the third bench for the scarf, lifts it off the back, folds it once and lays it with the bags on the cart.
