When the cart has the last bag, the apron is bare from rail to building, as bare as it is on an April morning before the meet opens. He gets to his feet. He does not go down the hallway. He leaves the way everyone else did, along the building and out toward the lot. He has the bag, empty now, and in the shirt pocket a ticket on a race that has not been run ...

We have stayed to see a place bare after everyone else has gone, and never once had a reason we could have given ...

The sweeper goes in through the doors, pushing the cart. She has been in a few minutes when the apron lights go off. The card room behind the glass has its lights on. The building is open twenty-four hours a day, every day of the year ...

The barns, the same hour

From the building the barns are far across the grounds, and the gate to them has a guard. No horse passes without three papers, a health certificate less than ten days old, a negative Coggins test less than a year old, and proof of a vaccination against the equine herpes virus within six months. In the spring each trainer applied for stalls and was given a block. Every barn has a diagram posted at its end showing who has which. This ground is called the backstretch too, and its opening day was the second of May. The trainers hire the barn workers and pay them. Some workers live the summer in trailers on the grounds, assigned as the stalls are, and the house arranges motel rooms for others ...

A horse that has raced is not put away. It leaves the track with the harness still on. The test barn comes first for the winner and for any other the judges pick. There the state takes blood and urine, and the horse does not go home before that. A horse will not urinate on command. The test barn people whistle one long note at it, again and again, since horses learn to tie the whistle to urinating, and most do in the end. Next comes the walking. The harness is taken off. A pacer, a horse that moves both legs on a side together, loses its hopples, the straps that hold that step. Hosed and scraped, the horse is walked in the shedrow, the aisle along the stalls, under a cooler, until its breathing comes down and the coat is dry, twenty minutes when the night is warm, longer when it is cold. It drinks a few swallows at once. Then the legs are done up, and the horse goes into the stall with hay ...

A horse sleeps mostly standing. For deep sleep it must lie flat, and it lies down only where it feels safe. It needs only a little, in short pieces. Now and then one lies down too near a wall and cannot get its legs under it to rise. That horse is said to be cast. A horse also cannot vomit, so trouble in the gut has one direction to go. For those two things a night watch walks the barns ...

The twelfth race horses leave the track at ten to eleven. They walk through the gap in the rail, and the drivers are still on the bikes. The seven heads for the test barn. A groom is at its head, and a man from the state walks behind. The four lost the photo. It goes down the row to its own barn, and beside it walks a woman with one hand flat on its neck ...

The four is a pacer. The woman unhooks the bike, and a man has it by the shafts and takes it away. She takes the hopples off and hangs them on the nail beside the stall. Off come the harness, the hood and the shadow roll. The wash stall is at the end of the row, and she leads the horse there. The water steams a little. She hoses the horse from the neck back and scrapes it in long pulls, and the water flies to the concrete. The horse takes it standing, head down, the way they do. The cooler she puts on it is wool and dark green, with the initials of the trainer on the corner. Then the two walk off down the shedrow. At the far end they leave the light, and we see them turn and come back into it ...

The trainer is stripping stalls. This barn has six of his horses. Four leave tonight, and those four stalls are emptied down to the floor. Shavings go into a cart by the forkful, and the cart goes out to the pile. Each water bucket is tipped and stacked in the aisle upside down. Hay nets come down and are rolled. The forking is the work of a boy of about seventeen, who has worked for the trainer since June. Each door still has its stall card with the name of the horse. When a stall is finished the trainer takes the card down and puts it into his shirt pocket. He says to leave the two on the end. He says they are not, then to leave those two, then that they are not going until Tuesday ...

In the paddock the house has its own crew, finishing the year. The house puts a numbered saddle pad on every horse in every race. The pads come in from the stalls, are counted against a list and go into a canvas hamper for the laundry. The stalls of the paddock are hosed. A woman wearing a house shirt takes a broom the length of the building and pushes the water toward the drains. The paddock judge has an office at the end, and its blinds are down and its light is off ...

A trailer has been backed to the barn end. Its ramp is down and it is lit inside, a six-horse, the dressing room in its nose, and the truck hitched to it is running. Two horses already stand in it with hay nets before them, heads to the front. The third comes from its stall wearing a cooler and a shipping halter. At the foot of the ramp it stops ...

The boy tells it to walk on, then to walk on again, then to come on. It does not. The lead rope tightens and the head lifts. The front feet keep to the gravel, an inch short of the rubber. The boy waits, and so does the horse. He leads it in a circle then, off from the ramp and around to it again, and walks on once more. One front foot goes on the ramp, and the horse takes it off again ...

The trainer is inside the barn and does not look out. He says not to pull on him, to let him look. The boy does not pull. With the rope slack he stands by the shoulder of the horse and looks up the ramp too. In a while a foot is on the rubber, then the second foot, and two strides carry the horse up into its slot. The boy ties it there, and the partition swings shut. The fourth horse follows and loads without a stop. The ramp rises on its springs and the boy pins it ...

We have stood at the foot of a ramp ourselves with somebody saying walk on. What got us up it was the slack ...

The trainer comes out of the barn with a tack trunk on a dolly and runs it up into the dressing room. He goes back for the bikes, two of them. With the wheels off they go on a rack on the trailer roof. He has an envelope in his back pocket. He gives it to the boy and says nothing about it, and the boy does not look inside. It goes into his jacket. Then the boy gets in on the far side of the truck ...

The truck moves. From the next barn over we hear a horse call after it. One in the trailer answers, then a third from somewhere down the row, and that is all. The truck is slow going up the gravel to the gate, and behind it the trailer rocks. It stops there. Out of the booth comes the guard, clipboard in hand. The driver hands a paper down. The guard looks at it, writes, says something short, and the truck is out on the road ...

The seven stands in a stall of the test barn, and the groom has hold of it. The man whistling at it wears a state jacket. He holds one note, then holds it again. The horse has been there a long time. Over the stall door lies the blanket from the picture, folded. The people in good coats who stood with the horse for that picture are in the tavern. The groom has nothing to sit on. He shifts his weight and stays on his feet. The whistling goes on, and the horse keeps its ears turned back toward the sound ...

The woman brings the four to a stop where the light is. The back of her hand goes flat to its chest, between the front legs, and she holds it. Under her hand the heart has come down. She tries the flank the same way, reaching under the cooler, and takes the cooler off. The coat beneath is dry. Where the scraper went it stands up in places. The stall is second from the end, one of the two that are staying, and she leads the horse there. It goes in, and its head goes straight into the hay ...

She does the legs sitting on an upturned bucket in the doorway, a wet cotton under a bandage on each front leg, wound from the knee down, the bandage pinned. The horse eats a flake of hay in the time this takes her. She gets up, takes the bucket and stays a moment more in the doorway. She turns the stall light off and walks down the row past the four bare doors. In the tack room a chair and a coffee maker are left and not much else, and there she sits down. Outside is her trailer, unhitched. She stays until Tuesday, with the two on the end ...

We have kept a chair like that one, beside the thing that was not leaving yet ...

An hour passes before the seven leaves the test barn, the man from the state behind it. Another barn and a different groom see to the washing and the walking, and the horse is put up. The blanket goes over a rail ...

The night watch has come on. He is sixty or so and wears a canvas coat. For this trainer he has walked these barns the whole meet, starting at eleven at night and ending when the first grooms come in. A flashlight goes with him, and he leaves it off unless he needs it, the shedrow lights being enough. A light in the face wakes a horse. At each stall he looks in as he passes. Under his breath he says the number of the stall and not the name of the horse. The end of the row has the diagram on its wall, and the stalls of the trainer are marked on it in pencil. He stops and draws a line through four of the stalls ...

That leaves two horses on this side of the barn, the side this trainer has. Across on the other side are nine, and their trainer has a truck that does not leave until morning. The night watch walks that side too. All nine are eating. A barn at this hour sounds of hay pulled from a net and ground, of a bucket knocked, of a hoof shifted in shavings. The shavings pile outside is high. Light catches on the buckets stacked in the aisle ...

Around midnight one more truck heads for the gate, the last of the night, a pickup pulling a two-horse trailer. The guard comes out and writes, and it goes. No engine runs after that. We hear the wetland, as loud here as it was behind the track ...

Some time later, in the second stall from the end, the horse lies down. Its front end goes first, down onto the knees. Then it goes over on its side and lies flat, legs straight out, neck along the shavings, head on the ground. Each breath moves the shavings by its nose. The bandages show white on the front legs against the shavings. The night watch is coming down the row. Through the stall gate he sees the legs and stops. He leaves the flashlight off. A stall away, he stands long enough to see the ribs rise and fall three times, and then he goes on. He looks at the diagram again at the end of the row and does not write anything ...

He stood a stall away so that he would not be the thing the horse woke to. We have been watched that way once or twice in our lives, and found out about it later ...

Two stall cards are left on the doors, and each says the name of a horse. On that side the other four doors are bare. The woman has fallen asleep with her coat on, in the chair in the tack room. All night the shedrow lights stay on. The barns are offered for events in the off-season. The listing says two barns with a hundred and fifty stalls and more than forty-six thousand square feet, and a seven-thousand-square-foot paddock for musical guests, food and beverage outlets, or meeting locations, with a farmhouse feel ...

The tavern, after midnight

The tavern is on the ground floor of the building, and its glass faces the finish line. The fish farm that had this ground before the track gave it its name. The farm wrote it with a hyphen, and over the door of the tavern the sign has it as two words. In the sixties a family dug ponds here, more than twenty, for raising rainbow trout. The trout were sold to restaurants, and later the public was let in to catch their own. A picnic ground was there for cleaning and grilling the catch. A restaurant came in the seventies and a stage on the lawn in the eighties, and the end of the nineties found all of it closed. The track has dug one pond back. A bubbler aerates it, the water stays near fifty-five degrees, and the fish come from a local farm. Nothing caught is let go. A fish out of the pond is paid for by the pound. For a fee it can be cleaned, and for a fee cooked. On the menu of the tavern are a smoked trout dip and a baked rainbow trout ...

Neither the tavern nor the card room it opens off ever closes. The building is smoke-free everywhere, and inside the doors it is eighteen and over. The glass looks on the wire on a race night. The rest of the year, and under snow, it looks on a track where nothing is running ...

At eleven we hear nothing in the building louder than the long table by the glass. Eight sit there in the good coats from the winner's circle. The last race of the meet went to their horse by a nose, and it paid nine forty. The board behind them still shows the prices and the track still has its lights, and none of them has looked at the glass since sitting down. The server is young and came on at four. He has carried two rounds to the table and is bringing a third. One of the eight is telling what he said to the teller, not to watch the photo, that nobody can tell a thing from it, and tells it again, not to watch it. Somebody says the teller watched it. The teller says so. Somebody says everybody watched it ...

At twenty past the driver comes in. His hair is wet, and he has changed into a shirt and put a jacket over it. He does not sit. One hand on the back of a chair, he stands at the table end and lets them tell him what he did. The horse did it, he says, and the four had him at the eighth pole and he was not sure. One of the women gets up from the table and holds his face in both hands. What she says to him the server does not hear, coming with the third round. Five percent of a purse is the share of a driver and five of a trainer, and the rest is for the owners. He has to get back, he says, and he goes. The server sets the third round down where he was standing ...

The cook has come on in the kitchen. She has worked the overnight since before there was a hotel. The table of the owners has asked for eight of the trout. The name of the place is why most of them wanted trout, and two of the eight do not like fish. Six are in the walk-in. She sends those and has the server tell the table. He does. The two order something else, and two trays take out the eight plates ...

The track lights go off in banks at half past eleven, and the long table looks at the glass. The track is dark now, and what the glass gives back is the room, the eight coats, the plates, the server standing back with the tray on his hip. One man gets up from the table and goes to the window. With his hand flat on the glass he looks out through the shape of it. He sees the board in the infield, still lit, and then the board is dark too. He sees himself in the glass and says well, there he is, and sits down ...

We have put a hand flat to a window at that hour and found ourselves on the other side of it, looking in ...

The owners are gone by half past twelve, up to the hotel or out to the lot. Their table, stripped and reset, stands beside the glass with settings on it and no one at it. Above it the glass has the print of the hand the man put there, all of it, fingers up. The room is again as it is at that hour. At the bar four men out of the card room eat in their jackets. A woman sits in the corner with a book and a coffee, and since the tenth race she has had that coffee. The bartender is wiping down. We hear the card room through the archway, the same as at noon, the chips, the shuffle, a voice on the speaker calling a seat open on a table ...

The bartender asks the server whether that was the last one, meaning the race. The server says that was it. The bartender asks whether they settled. The server says the one in the red coat did, in cash. He puts a glass up. At the bar the four men talk about a hand. One had a pair and another a better one. The man with the better pair tells it, and the man who lost eats and says yep at the right places. What they are eating is a burger and three of the fish sandwich ...

The cook is filtering the fryer oil in the kitchen. A pump sends it through the filter and back. The crumb stays in the paper. It is done at this hour each night of the year. With a rag folded to a pad she wipes the flattop. The dish machine runs its cycle out and stops. The clipboard comes off the door of the walk-in, and she goes in, counts, comes out and writes down what the morning will need ...

At one the server has his break, shoes off, in the chair beside the walk-in. From that chair he can see the length of the room to the window. The room is in the window, and in the room is the woman with the book. He came on at four in the afternoon. The field for the first race was then warming up in the glass, going the wrong way of the track. Thursday is his next shift, and on Thursday nothing is on the other side of the glass. All summer he has known that. Now he finds it strange. The shoes go back on. Without taking it all the way out he counts what his apron holds, and it comes to more than a Tuesday ...

It took him all summer and one dark track to find that strange. We are the same, and have never been quicker about it ...

A dealer on her break comes through the archway from the card room at half past one, in the vest of the house. She stands at the bar end to eat a sandwich. She asks the bartender for a coffee to take back, and goes through the archway again with it ...

A man from the hotel comes down at two, in a hooded sweatshirt and the pants he meant to sleep in. He takes a seat at the glass and orders a beer and the dip. To get by his own reflection he tips his head, and with a hand up he sees the shape of the rail, with nothing past it. He is on the fifth floor, he tells the server, with the curtains open. Last night the track was lit and he went to sleep. Tonight it is dark and he could not, so he has come down to see. He goes back up with the dip eaten and half the beer left. The server wipes the table, and the bowl and the glass go back to the bar with him ...

At three the cook carries her own plate from the kitchen to the bar end. Her stool is the one nearest the kitchen door, and her back is to the window while she eats. Some nights she has cooked with the room full to the glass, and some with only the bartender and herself in it. Tonight it is the four men, finishing, and the woman with the book, who has closed it, and the server, polishing a glass. The trout is gone, she tells the bartender, all six. He writes it for the morning on the pad by the register ...

The loser of the hand pays for the four of them before they go. At the empty tables the server turns the chairs up, so that the floor under them can be done, and the bartender runs the machine. The woman with the book goes out through the card room, and the bill she leaves is under her cup ...

The far turn, first light

Five-eighths of a mile is the distance around the track. Its surface is agricultural lime and stone dust. No part has a roof over it, and summer is when the racing is done here. A mile race brings the field twice through the far turn. Beneath the surface the ground is the Anoka Sand Plain, glacial outwash, sand over water, a lowland that would not take a crop. A family made trout ponds of it by digging. When the ponds were gone a wetland was put back, and it is this the track circles. Next door the state has its wildlife management area, about twenty-five thousand acres, marsh, lakes and swamp. A company in Saint Paul wove rugs from wire-grass a hundred years ago, and the marsh was cut for it. The state had lost the trumpeter swan, and there it was brought back. There the sandhill crane nests. Both pass over the track, coming and going ...

The twentieth of September has its sunrise a little before seven, and first light about half an hour ahead of that ...

At six the building is lit. So are the lot lights, and on the upper floors of the hotel two windows. Beyond all of that the oval is dark, though its darkness differs slightly in quality from the darkness around, the lime being pale and holding what light there is. The infield board is off. Under it all we hear the interstate, and the hour does not stop it. Over that is water, or the things in it, a frog, and another, spaced far apart now that the nights have gone cold, and a duck somewhere shifting ...

A rattle comes from the wetland with a rise in it, once, and then an answer. It is the cranes. They spend the night standing in the water as a group, where it is a few inches deep. They speak first. They go on speaking a long time before light comes to see them by. From farther off, in the marsh next door, more cranes answer ...

By twenty past six there is light enough to see the fog against, and the fog shows. It lies on the water, the low grass and the track, and it is thickest over the water. It moves a little and in no direction. Out of it comes the rail, first the top rail, a line, then the posts beneath. The surface of the track comes next, pale and combed in fine lines, the pass of the crew after the twelfth ...

Next the infield comes up, the grass, the water in the low places, the board on its stand with its screen the grey of a screen that is off. The cranes have not stopped calling. Now we can make out their shapes, thirty or forty standing tall in the shallows. A heron at their edge does not move. Something the height of a dog walks the outside rail in the fog. It is a fox, and it follows the rail on into the fog ...

At twenty to seven there is color in the light. The fog goes from the high ground first, and the track comes clear, all of the oval, a pale ring. Inside the ring is the wetland and beyond it the marsh. On the far side are the building, the apron and its benches, and the glass of the tavern lit from inside. The two lit windows in the hotel are dark now, and three others have their curtains open ...

A few at a time the cranes go up. They run through the shallow water, then lift. The legs hang at first, then straighten out behind. The whole time they are calling. Low over the far turn they make one circle, so near the surface that fog moves beneath them. They cross the rail and the marsh, and the calling goes on for a while behind them. In a quarter of an hour the water is nearly empty of them. Two walk the shallows with their heads down ...

We came out for the horses and stood a quarter of an hour for the cranes. They were calling before there was light to see them by, and nobody had asked them to ...

The sun comes up. Nothing in the infield is taller than the board, so the sun hits it first. Its screen turns from grey to white and stays that way while the sun is low. Then the sun is on the top rail, all the way around. The surface is next, and its grey goes to something nearer white. The combed lines show plain in it now, the whole way round. No fog is left on the track. Next door it still lies on the marsh in a layer ...

A pickup comes onto the track at ten past seven. It comes in by the paddock, through the gap in the rail, and drives once around the oval on the inside, slowly, its windows up. At the far turn it stops, then goes on. Where it came on it comes off, and its tires show across the lines of the surface behind it. The lot can take twelve hundred cars. It has a few dozen, near the doors. Geese in a line go low over the lot, calling ...

Most curtains in the hotel are open. Some windows have people standing in them, looking at the track. The two cranes have not left the shallows. The water at the far end of the infield has a pair of swans on it, and with the sun on them they are hard to look at ...

Four thousand and fifty of the twenty-five thousand acres next door are sanctuary, and the whole year they are closed to people ...

The fifth floor, the morning after

The hotel stands five stories and holds a hundred and sixteen rooms, and the track-side rooms have their windows on the oval. Ground was broken in November of twenty eighteen. The cost was more than eighteen million dollars, and it opened in March of twenty twenty. It describes itself as expansive corridors, high ceilings and the largest rooms in the category ...

The corridor on the fifth floor runs as long as the building. Track-side rooms are on one hand of it and lot-side rooms on the other, and it is wide enough that two carts can pass. In the morning each housekeeper is handed a list. Its rooms are of two kinds. A room whose guest is leaving gets stripped, then done from the bare mattress up. A room whose guest is staying gets made, wiped and left. When the desk calls up, the list changes. A room is done the same way every time it is done, down to the folded point at the end of the paper roll and the way the towels hang ...

At nine the carts stand by the ice machine at the far end. Two women work the fifth floor, and they have divided the list, the track side to one and the lot side to the other. They talk low across the corridor, and we hear one laugh. Coffee from the breakfast room comes up the stairwell, and the corridor smells of it ...

In the first track-side room the curtains stand open, as they have all night, and the bed was slept in. The window holds the whole oval at once, pale, with the sun on it and nothing moving on it, the far turn, the infield with the water in it, the benches on the apron below. A program on the nightstand has been folded back to the twelfth race, and a whole ticket lies under it. Here the guest stays another night. The wastebasket holds four torn tickets and a coffee cup, and the woman with the track side empties it. She makes the bed. Around the program and the ticket she wipes, and she does not move them ...

The owners had the corner room. Glasses out of the tavern sit on the desk, the dresser, the windowsill. The wastebasket has a bottle in it. The beds were sat on more than they were slept in. A copy of the program lies on the bed, the twelfth race circled in pen. A number is written in its margin, and under that a second number, much larger, followed by a question mark. The desk called up, and this room is a checkout. She strips the room to the mattress. Into the bag with the rest goes the program. The glasses go onto the cart, on the tray beneath the top shelf ...

We would have kept the page with the two numbers on it. It went into the bag with the rest, and the question mark went with it ...

Two doors have the sign on them. She goes by those rooms and returns to them later. At ten a man in a T-shirt, barefoot, opens one of the doors. He says sorry twice and asks the time, and she tells it to him. He will be out by eleven, he says. She says that is fine and takes the cart down one. The door closes ...

What the lot-side rooms show is the lot. Thirty cars are in it, and beyond it are a lawn, the road and trees. A family has gone early from one of these rooms and left the television on. It shows a game with the sound down. The woman with that side uses the remote to turn it off, then lays the remote by the phone on the nightstand ...

The supervisor is walking the corridor with the list when the radio on her belt says a room number, then says late check. She crosses a room off in one column and writes it into the other. The hotel first opened in March of twenty twenty, and she has been here since. She was here through its five open days, before it closed along with the rest of the state, and she was here when it opened once more. Next Saturday already has its list, and it is on her clipboard under this one. That list is a wedding block, and it takes most of the fifth floor, track side and lot side ...

At half past ten the woman with the track side is in a room the guest has left, and she is running the vacuum. She turns the vacuum off, and she stands at the window. A window on the fifth floor fits the whole oval in. The apron and its benches lie directly below, and across the water is the far turn. From here she has watched races, standing in rooms not her own, and the vacuum stayed off for the two minutes of the race. The track has nothing on it. The vacuum goes back on, and along the window she does the floor ...

The door of the stairwell opens at the corridor end. We see a man in the whites of the kitchen come through, carrying a case of water in the small bottles. He leaves the case beside the ice machine, then goes down again ...

The middle of the corridor is where the two carts meet, by eleven. The women stop a minute with the carts nose to nose. They drink from the small bottles of water and talk. One takes a glove off and shows a cut on her thumb to the other, who looks at it. Then they go on ...

We would have missed the cut on the thumb. The other one did not ...

The corner room is done. The bed is as tight as every bed on the floor, the glasses are gone, the wastebasket has a new liner, and the curtains are open, the way track-side rooms are left. The supervisor radios down that it is ready ...

By half past eleven the man who had the sign on his door has gone. That makes his room a checkout, and the woman with the track side goes in. His curtains are open. The side of the bed nearest the window has been slept in, and the pillow from the other side was moved over to it. She strips the bed and makes it ...

The breakfast room is downstairs, and the mural on its wall has in it the fish farm, the card tables, the horses and the skyline of Minneapolis ...

The hallway

From the card room the hallway takes the long building to its far end. It is high, and so wide that people pass in it and do not turn. It is brightest where it begins, lit from the windows of the room by day and from the tables by night. As it goes it darkens, and the one light at the far end comes from what hangs there. Paragraphs go down one wall, at the height where a person reads. They are short and plain, set one after another the length of the hallway. They are about the sport and the ground. Under them runs a band of the surface of the track itself, lime and stone dust, combed. It is behind glass, as high as a hand let down at the side, and it goes from end to end ...

Two lit panels stand side by side where the hallway ends. One holds a single dark figure. It was drawn in one line with the pen never lifting, and the light of that panel does not change. The other holds a band of handwriting as wide as the wall. Its light does change, too slowly for a person standing before it to see. A sentence comes up in it now and then. It stays long enough to be read twice, and goes. Near the panels one line of writing is set so small it must be read from ten inches. A wooden rail stands before the panels, a standing distance off, as high as the rails outside. The hallway has no speaker in it. The lamps are behind the panels, and they are hidden from every place a person can stand ...

It is Sunday, the twentieth of September, half past two in the afternoon. Any Sunday afternoon finds the card room this full and this loud. Its windows show an empty track and a dark board in the infield. Two dealers come on shift by the far door of the room. From the hotel side a woman comes through the card room. She wears the uniform of the fifth floor, her shift is over, and her bag is over her shoulder. She goes by the tables and turns in at the mouth of the hallway ...

Some days she walks it, a long shift behind her or a short one, and in the card room nobody looks up. The room sounds all around her for the first few steps, and after those the sound is behind her ...

The writing on the wall passes at her shoulder. Some of it she has read on other days, one paragraph at a time. She knows where those paragraphs are and goes by them. Where the carpet ends the floor is harder. The light from the card room end stops reaching the floor ahead of her, and the far light has not reached it yet. She can hear the floor she walks on, for a few steps, and cannot see it. Then it has the far light on it, low and getting stronger. As she comes on, the two panels grow at the end ...

An empty paper cup stands against the wall on the floor. She picks it up and keeps walking ...

She stops at the rail. The bag goes down between her feet. Her forearms go onto the rail, where the wood is worn smooth under them. She leans forward, and the weight goes onto the wood ...

The left-hand panel has the figure, dark against the light, and it is one line. That line goes around and returns to where it started. Her eyes follow it, lose it and find it again. The right-hand panel has a different color now than when she came into the far light, if it has. If there is a change, it is too slow to catch. In it the band of handwriting goes from edge to edge, and seen from the rail it is a band ...

In the right-hand panel a sentence comes up. Before it goes she reads it twice ...

She takes her forearms from the rail and goes closer, to the small line. Hands behind her back, she bends from the waist to it. At ten inches she reads it, and she straightens up ...

Her fingertip goes onto the figure in the left-hand panel, where the line is raised, and she follows it. The line has a tremble in places. All the way around she follows it, back to where the finger went down, and her hand comes off it ...

The tremble in the line is ours. We did not steady it, and she found it with a finger ...

Behind her, a long way off, the card room is going on, and between is the empty hallway ...

She picks up the bag and the cup. Back up the hallway she walks, toward the sound. On her back is the far light, and then it is not. Now the paragraphs pass at her other shoulder. The carpet begins. Light from the room reaches the floor ahead of her, and then she is in the room. We hear a man laughing at something at the first table. She passes him, crosses the room and goes out by the door she came in by, and by that door she puts the cup in the can ...

For a while the hallway is empty. Then we see two people come into its mouth from the room, talking. The far light lies on the floor a long way ahead of them ...

The wall

When a horse leaves the track on three legs, the groom runs toward it. When a horse stands on three legs in its stall, a hind foot cocked, no weight on that foot, it is resting, and the groom walks past ...

The depth of a harrow is set, and it never gets down to the base. That base is packed hard beneath the cushion, and it carries every race. Between races the few inches on top are combed smooth, in thousands of fine parallel lines. They run the way the tractor went and the horses will go, and the next race is run across them ...

Most weeks the sport holds a race that has nothing on it. It goes in the morning, the windows shut and the apron empty. It is a qualifier, a mile against the clock with no purse and no line. A horse that broke stride too often or was away too long must go it without breaking before the judges let it back into a race that pays ...

Hands on this property are mostly a measure, each four inches, counted up from the ground to the withers. A Standardbred stands fifteen or sixteen hands. It cannot hold a ticket. At the window it is bet on by program number ...

Next door is the wetland where the state brought back its swans. Eggs were collected in Alaska over three summers of the eighties, flown down and hatched there. The young were raised there and released at two years old. Swans pair for life, cranes too, and the geese come in lines ...

The handicapper sets odds called the morning line, and the program prints them before any ticket is sold. All night the caller and the crowd measure the board against that line. The board moves its line. The program keeps its line as printed ...

When the wetland sends fog onto the backstretch, the call of a race comes in pieces. The start is called and the first turn. Quiet follows, the field being a sound. Then one at a time the horses are picked up, coming out of the fog into the lights of the stretch ...

What a driver looks for is cover, another horse to sit behind that breaks the wind. First over is the hard trip, parked out with nobody ahead. With cover a horse sits and the driver waits, pulling when the cover tires ...

The post positions on this oval are drawn, and the draw matters at five-eighths of a mile. A horse has a short way around from the rail and from the two hole. From the outside posts the driver must decide in the first eighth whether to leave hard or tuck in and wait. A different post gives the same horse a different race to run. The program prints win percentage by post ...

The board shows us first the order the horses made at the wire, then the order made by the judges and, under it, the rule. A horse still breaking at the wire may be placed, should the judges choose, behind any horse that was on its gait and lapped on it there. One word follows, official, and on it the tellers pay. A later ruling can move the purse. The tickets were paid at official ...

The two interstates of the two cities are one road again just south of the house, and the first exit after they join is for the house. The property gives it in its own directions and warns that the map services have it wrong. They say left at the top of the ramp, then a mile west. Miss it, and a driver is carried on by the one road to the next exit, Forest Lake ...

A trot or a pace is a gait held on purpose. The gallop is the gait a horse is born with, and one that breaks into it must be taken back until it is on stride again. Coming back loses lengths, and those are usually the race. In the record the program keeps, an x marks a break ...

Mornings, the horses circle this oval the wrong way. Most of the work of a horse is jog miles, slow and untimed, and they go clockwise. To train or race, a horse turns and goes counterclockwise, the right way of the track ...

Some races here are claiming races. Every horse in such a race is for sale, and the race is named for the price. A claim goes on a slip, the slip goes into a locked box before the race, and there is no taking it back. From the moment the gate car pulls away the new owner has the horse. What it earns in that race goes to the owner who sold it ...

A rule gave the breed its name. When the registry opened, a Standardbred was a horse able to trot a mile inside a standard time. Pedigree has replaced the standard since. A Standardbred now is a horse whose sire and dam were, and so back through the registry to the horses that made the time ...

Hopples are what most pacers race in. On each side a strap loops the front leg together with the hind leg behind. It is long enough that the two swing as a pair, which is the pace, and too short to let them swing apart. Its length is measured in inches to the horse. A horse able to hold the gait on its own races without them, free-legged ...

Whatever size the oval is, a race in this sport is nearly always a mile. The oval decides what shape the mile has. A mile track makes it one lap with two turns. This five-eighths makes it a lap and part of another with three turns, and a half-mile track two laps with four. A horse has its time for the mile read against the size of the track where it was run ...

A car starts the field. The starter rides in the back and faces the horses. The gate has two wings, and the field comes up behind them. Until the starter gives the word the horses are his. At first he brings them up slowly, and then at speed. The mile begins at a point on the track, and there he says go. The wings fold, and the car pulls away. Before the word he can call the field back, for a horse in trouble behind the gate or for a gate that fails. The field then goes around and comes again. From the word, each horse in it is a starter. After it there is no recall ...

Whatever Burned Here Came Back Kinder

The place is named Running Aces, for the fastest card in the deck. It was set down on the one ground where a horse that runs has gone off stride, meaning it has broken out of the gait the race is asking for. We laugh every time we say the name, and then we say it again, Running Aces ...

Whatever burned here came back kinder. The prairie has been doing this sum for a long time, and it is old arithmetic. A fire takes a field down to dark ashes in one afternoon, and then the grass that pushes up through the black is the sweetest grass a horse ever put a mouth to. Lightning bought this whole property once and paid for it in fire, and when the deed came back it was written in clover ...

Before every race the track is harrowed smooth, which means it is dragged over with a wide rake of teeth, and the ground goes on remembering every race anyway. That is the ground's memory. The teeth only comb the top layer of what the ground knows ...

The oval is a shape with the wetland behind it. Graders made the oval, the machines that scrape ground level, and it goes quiet by ten. The wetland is the older establishment of the two, and it stays open all night. We count it as a grandstand, full of frogs and blackbirds calling odds on nothing, and every seat was taken before the first shovel went in ...

When the sun comes up it hits the board first, because the board is the tallest thing in the infield, the ground inside the oval. The board is the tote, the big sign that shows the odds and what a bet paid. So the first light of the day is spent on yesterday's numbers, and it lays gold on a tote nobody has bet ...

A line of geese goes over the lot, low and calling. We take them for the other harness racers, hitched nose to tail in the oldest silks there are, which is to say the oldest racing colors. The card is the list of races, and the geese want nothing off it but south ...

A stroke has hung in the air over the wetland ever since. We are not told ever since what, and we do not ask. What left one hand never touched ground again. The redwings carry it now, one feather each ...

The trout get a resurrection of their own, and theirs comes by road. Cold water takes its miracles plain. A truck backs down to the pond, the gate lifts, and everything that was gone is swimming again ...

The house nearly died once itself. It faced the bank one winter, and out of that winter it learned to pension its horses. Its trotters are the horses that race at the trot, and none of them ever will face what the house faced. Each one has hay coming for every January left in it ...

One mark stayed dead, and now it flies somewhere past the edge of the picture. The comebacks all stand inside the frame. Whatever races here goes out wearing silks, under a number, at a post time, which is the minute its race is set to start. The one that kept its death got the flying kind, no silks, no number, no post time ...

The lot is the last ground that is not trying to be anything. Past the gravel everything has a job. The oval performs and the wetland testifies. The lot just holds cars the way a field holds cows, off the clock ...

Whatever is calling was here before the fish farm. The farm raised trout on that water and took itself for the founder. The calling knew better and went on calling. It had been paying rent on that water since the glacier's back was still turned ...

People stood up beside the wetland and spoke, and not a word of it will last in anybody's memory. The water will. It lay behind the speakers like a curtain, and the curtain did the moving while the stage stood still. All that speaking sank the same as the rain, and what the body kept was the shine off the wetland ...

The grass held them closer. The rail is the fence along the track, and all it ever did was watch them go by. It was the infield that kept a green grip, ground holding whatever it has decided to raise ...

This ground had a stage before it had a track. Boards were put up here for singing before a single horse drew a number. Whatever dream this building is, the water is its oldest memory. Under both runs the first tenant, still holding the lease and remembering the place from before it meant anything ...

Resurrection is the habit of this whole neighborhood, and the building cannot claim it as a private miracle. Everything on this section has died once and thought little of it. What was drained came back too. The oval is a ring drawn around water. The track drew its circle where any settlement draws one, around the well ...

Everything in a trotter at speed is asking to gallop, to lay itself out flat and go, and the horse keeps to the gait all the same. The whole discipline is a promise kept at thirty or so miles an hour ...

Sometimes the race is only a mile and a clock. Nobody goes alongside and no purse is waiting, the purse being the prize money a race pays out. The horse is up against the oldest opponent on the grounds, the one that has never lost and never once gloated ...

Out on the track a driver goes looking for cover, which means the back of another horse to tuck in behind while that horse takes the wind. Drafting is the sport's plainest confession. Somebody has to go into the weather first, even here, so that somebody behind can keep a run saved up for the stretch, the last straight run to the finish ...

The post is the lane a horse starts from, and the same horse from a different post runs a different race. The posts are handed out by a draw, eight lanes of luck, and half the story is settled before the gate car rolls, the car that carries the starting gate. Then come the weather and the cover and the stride. At the end of it one word goes up, official, and the tellers, the clerks at the betting windows, pay on it. The whole afternoon comes down to a single lit word that turns opinions into money ...

The trot and the pace are gaits held on purpose. Neither one comes natural at that speed. What we are watching is a horse full of plain run agreeing to carry itself in time, and that is what makes it a discipline and not an escape ...

A pacer, a horse that races at the pace and not the trot, wears hopples, which are straps around its legs. They give the two legs enough strap to swing as a pair and too little to swing apart. The hopples measure a covenant in inches, room to keep the rhythm and none to lose it. Some horses hold the gait with nothing on, and those are raced free legged. The rig comes off once the rhythm has moved out of the leather and into the animal, and from then on the pace is kept on honor alone ...

A race starts on a word. Once it is said every horse in the field is a starter, and nobody is called back to try the beginning again. Some of us said it once in a kitchen, quietly, and that field is running still ...

He did not lose. He went off stride. A mile has eight furlongs in it, and some are worse than others to come apart in. The gallop was in them first, and some evenings the legs remember it at the worst possible furlong ...

The finest horse on the track outran every other one, and the outrunning is what cost it the race. We have kept ledgers like that, the finest number in the whole book standing in a losing column ...

One gait is kept by a strap and the other by character. The whole breed is the refusal to break. The strap comes off in the paddock after the race, and what stays on has no buckle to it ...

In this game our horse loses when it runs. Every open field would break its heart over a rule like that. The whole animal is built for it, and it is the one thing forbidden ...

A long race is not won by speed but by not breaking. Some of us have been in one since the eighties and were never once fast, and we were still on stride at the kitchen table this morning ...

We hold the pace we can hold, past the place where holding it stops feeling like anything. Past feeling is where the stride goes quiet and the ground starts doing half the work ...

A horse is held at the top of what a body can do and asked not to break. The top is the narrowest place, with room for four hooves and no room for wanting more ...

There is a hope that has no stake under it, the stake being the money put down on a bet. We call it A WISH WITH NOTHING RIDING ON IT. We have made those all our lives, whole shelves of them, wished without a wager and still paying out ...

The numbers were up and the people stayed at the rail anyway, looking out at a track with nothing on it. The whole crowd still faced the same way, nobody willing to be the first spine that turns ...

When a picker, somebody who picks a horse to win, keeps a wish felt and never says it out loud, there is nothing for two dollars to come due on. The unsaid wish is the only bet a picker cannot lose, and some of us have grown old winning it ...

A fourth race is run at this track, and it never gets a line on the tote board. No betting window sells it, no prize money waits on it, and no horse is named its winner. All of us are in it anyway. Nobody drew us a lane, since the starting gate swung open on the day each of us came. The race on the board is the fast one, and it posts odds. This fourth one is slow, and what it posts is wishes. Two boards over the same track, and only one of them ever changes its numbers ...

They bet once and they watch eight, and the difference is a wish with nothing riding on it. It comes to one ticket in the pocket and eight horses in the eye, seven of them carried for free ...

Out on the apron, the standing ground between the building and the rail, one customer is watching the races. All the others are watching their money. The track runs truest in the one gaze it never billed ...

The rail is the one place on the property where nobody is selling anything. It is free to stand at and free to lean on, the last plain wood in a building of windows ...

They were not talking about beating the house. They were talking about the night. The house closes its ledger at the last race, and the night stays open past every window ...

A picture of a horse has had one job longer than any other, and that job is to carry a wish. It is work older than the wagering and as old as cave walls, the animal drawn mid-stride so the wanting has somewhere to run ...

A grandstand, when it comes down to it, is a platform full of people wishing on horses. It is a whole architecture raised for wanting, tiered so that every wish can see over the wish in front of it ...

The wish is older and kinder than the wager. A wager is no good unless its horse finishes first. The wish only needs it to be beautiful somewhere in the run ...

The house edge on a wish is zero. The edge is the share of every bet the house keeps for itself, and on a wish there is none. It is the one number no house has ever moved, posted nowhere and honored at every window ...

A story, a stillness, and a wish with nothing riding on it are three fares for the same evening, and the gate takes them all without making change ...

The crowd has the horses to watch, and the board, and its own money. That is a great deal to look at, and so far not one head has turned the other way. The truest things on the property go unnoticed, kept safe by the crowd looking elsewhere ...

This is for the horses, and for the others who have never won anything with money on it. A dedication that wide has room in it for most of the standing world, us among them ...

A favorite is the horse most of the money is on, and at the rail the title goes to the first of us to get an elbow on the wood. Every leaner is rated alike, at even money, the bet that pays back just what was put down. The money a picker holds is hoping, counted out in bills. It gets folded twice so the hope keeps its shape ...

The wire is the sport's name for the finish line, and no real wire hangs over it. Nobody strung one. Still their noses reach for it coming home, and it is green light all the way home. Ours is the porch lamp, lit before we left, and we crossed under it anyway, all our lives ...

The fish that will bite tomorrow are already swimming tonight, every one of them. They are circling a hook we have not tied and a morning we have not reached ...

A ticket has two halves, and every wish gathered here was on the losing one. The winning halves went through the window and came back as money. The losing halves stayed wishes, kept in the coat all winter ...

Hope can be offered wanting nothing back. Wanting without needing is the most stable ground there is. It takes our full weight and gives back nothing but the holding, and nobody has driven a stake anywhere in it ...

Thanks run both ways where neither one outranks the other. Ours is a palm laid on a neck gone gray with dust. Theirs came first, a whole mile handed over only a moment ago. Neither of us wipes it off, and the dust stays on the palm the whole walk back ...

The odds are posted on the board in the infield, and the wishes are posted on this wall. The board we read from across the track. The wall we read from arm's length, close enough to add ours in pencil ...

Somebody is in the barn before first light, and somebody is still there after the last window closes. We have stopped looking for the hour when nobody is. It is presence in the stalls the way heat is in a body. The racing keeps dark days on its calendar, when nothing runs. The feeding has never heard of one ...

Somebody in the barn has fifteen minutes coming to him, and no paper anywhere says so. It is an understanding among the people who feed. What the fifteen minutes are for was never passed along to us. Not a day goes by without his quarter hour showing up at its proper time, kept in the same heads that never once forgot a feeding ...

She has watched races from here on her feet, in rooms that were not hers. For the two minutes a race takes she switches the vacuum off and leaves the cord in the wall. Those are two minutes lifted off the shift, and the carpet none the wiser ...

When a horse comes off the track on three legs, the groom, the one who looks after that horse every day, runs toward it. Everybody else's afternoon stops still at the rail. Theirs breaks into a run before the announcer finds a word for it ...

Down the shed row, the long line of stalls, a horse is standing on three legs, one hind foot cocked with no weight on it. That one is only resting, and the groom walks past. The same three legs get read twice. Walking past is the deeper fluency, knowing rest from wreck at one glance down the row ...

Every barn anywhere has a kid in it gripping the handle of something. The wood was worn slick before any of them showed up. Every grip that learned the chore before them is kept in the grain ...

The spirit keeps stalls of its own, and tonight each of them is a LAMP THAT CANNOT BE SEEN. The lamps burn behind dark windows, and not an animal in the barn is standing cold ...

He is used to falling asleep while the track is lit. Tonight it was dark and sleep would not come, so he walked down for a look. We have gone down to look at less. The dark let itself be looked over and never stirred. It was sound all the way through, which took the walk down to know ...

No dream comes to a horse still on its feet. It holds off until the legs have folded and the body is down. It wants the whole animal or nothing ...

The barn goes quiet after the last race, with one lamp left burning in the aisle. Straw settles under everything that has gone flat. Then somewhere down the row a dream starts running under a closed eye, the first race of the night card ...

On a dark night she learns a track by walking it, and she learns him the same way, in the hours when he says nothing. We could not say which takes longer. Neither ground has cost her a turned ankle, the dark and the not-talking both walked sure-footed by now ...

First there is a horse made out of fog, and then no horse. By morning the whole shape has breathed back into the wetland. There was more animal in it than most fields hold ...

A ledger is built to hold one sort of thing, and some things are another sort, which is the whole reason they are missing from it. A lamp that cannot be seen shines through them from the back, and not one has been let go. The older set of books holds them, the ones with no columns, where every entry is made by that same lamp ...

The building is dreaming too. It has not been off its feet since the boards went up, so it does its dreaming standing, over every dreamer that had to go flat first ...

Slowness is also thrilling. All it asks is a room quiet enough to feel it in, and the room here is a whole wetland turned up to the volume of frogs. We would not have called that quiet either, until we stood in it. It is quiet the way deep water is quiet, loud all night and holding still underneath ...

There is a race we keep running again, at the speed of memory. It was run once at thirty miles an hour or so, and it goes slower with every rerun. By now the stretch alone takes most of an evening. An evening is coming when the whole race holds mid-stride, every hoof off the ground at once, and us at the rail of it till morning ...

The loudest room has one light in it that counts the minutes left before the next race. Nothing else lit in there goes so slowly, with the bulbs on every side of it flashing at a dead run, and the minutes walking down to post on a held gait, refusing to break ...

Heaven is set at ground level. A pair of barn boots will walk a person straight in, and whoever stands up in it finds there is sky enough. We had it figured for somewhere farther up and harder to get into. The gate of it is a stall latch, opened all day long, and nobody is made to die first ...

Not one date stands on the whole reckoning. This is TIME MEASURED BY WHAT CONTINUES, and the counting is done in feed and in foals. The feedings count off the days and the foalings count off the years ...

Come a night of racing, the glass has one thing to show, and that is the wire. Every face along it, ours among them, leans toward one lit stripe of finish, and the whole pane is spent on a single second ...

Under snow the track keeps its oval quiet, and we walk the row remembering where the rail runs ...

Swans pair for life, and so do the cranes, and the geese come in lines. They cross in those lines over the backstretch, the far straight side of the oval. Down here we harness two by two and call it a morning ...

After a horse is gone its mark is still standing in the books, the mark being its fastest winning mile. We read them, two minutes standing for a whole life, and somewhere the oats still rattle ...

On a cold morning every breath in the barn comes out as a cloud shaped like a horse's head. A second herd gathers above the first. We end up counting horses two times over, the warm ones in the stalls and the others, which we are too polite to finish naming. That herd goes up past the barn lights with the steam while we see to the frozen buckets. We crack the ice with the mallet, water under it all along, and the horses drink the temperature ...

Living is a crazy wild night at the track. Somebody said so at the rail, a person who has been coming back for eleven years or so. Race nights will teach that. We lean on the same rail they lean on, believing every word of it ...

The cranes were over this place before the ice that made the sand. We rake the footing, the surface the horses go on, and it is ground the cranes crossed while the glacier lay down. Their lines still know the route ...

The ages have hardly put a cut on the crane, over ground that writes everything down each night and rubs it out again. A fresh program comes out, the printed sheet for the night's races, and the chalk on the board gets rinsed away. Up above, the wings have never needed a correction, feathers from before the sand. Under them the tickets are torn nightly, confetti for the long memory ...

The newest picture medium on earth was born at this sport's gait. There was a wager on the trot, and cameras settled it midair. Every movie since is horses coming home ...

The most widely sung secular song on this planet is a harness racing song. Camptown ladies sing it, doo-dah, doo-dah. Goin' to run all night, Goin' to run all day, Bet my money on a bob-tailed nag, Somebody bet on the gray. That puts five miles of track in everybody's mouth, and nobody remembers the bay. We hum it mucking stalls, and the race runs unfinished a hundred and seventy years on, nag and gray still nose and nose ...

The cranes cleared out ahead of the winter, and now they are back. They are entered again, the way a horse is put down in the book for a race, and their entry is worn thin from use. Same book, same line, and we take the entry every spring, form unbroken since the sand was ice ...

A thing that is truly scarce needs nobody to stage it. The gate holds fifty-five nights a year and no more, and we never once painted the moon scarce ...

This work spent three decades in the drawers, and we can only say the drawers were patient. It was private all that time, and this is the first wall it has met in public. The lamps come up on it where the bettors pass, unhurried at last ...

The sounds here come out of a chest, and it is full of HEARTS and SPADES and DIAMONDS and CLUBS, every one of them beating. We shuffle and the whole deck walks, four suits to the stride, red and black taking turns at the rail ...

At this hour in the barn, hay is pulled from a net and ground between teeth, a bucket gets knocked, and a hoof shifts in the shavings. We stop with the wheelbarrow to listen, as if the work had been waiting on us. It all keeps going anyway, jaw and bucket and hoof, the barn talking in its sleep ...

They speak first, and they have been at it a long while by the time any light comes. The nickers, the soft low sounds a horse makes down in the chest, go down the row before anybody has touched a switch. We walk in late to a conversation the dark had already started ...

The field is passing the eighth pole, the marker an eighth of a mile from the finish, and the whole apron is roaring. A child is asleep through all of it. Against a shoulder the small breathing keeps its own program, all favorites ...

Then again, eight horses eating do not make eight sounds. Once they start they make one. It begins with one jaw, and then the row falls in. We stand in the middle of a single animal chewing ...

It is a sound like rain on a roof if the roof were made of something soft. Hay-rain comes down steady over the stalls, and we stop sweeping to stand under it, dry as bones and getting rained on ...

The sport's gait is a two-beat, and so is the heart's. A person is hearing their own two-beat again. We walk the shed row and the pacer's stride hangs level, lub-dub down the rail, and we keep the entry ...

A person walking the shed row is hearing their own smell of horse manure again. We carried ours around for years without once checking on it ...

Harness racing is the only racing whose sound is a heartbeat. A gallop scatters to a tumble. The pace comes down the stretch in twos, and every chest at the rail answers it ...

Snow takes the sound out of a sleigh, and that is why a trotting harness wears bells. The runners went quiet on the bleached road, so the law hung music on the horse, and winter learned to hear itself coming ...

The sounds get here ahead of the birds. Buckets and bolts are going, and out of the dark comes the first nickering whin. The sparrows wake into a morning already made ...

A very old record gives away its room before it gives any music. For one second ahead of the first note, the ear can already tell that the place it was recorded in was a very large one. We do not know the room and we know its size. The needle finds its groove and a vast room clears its throat, and we wait small inside it for the band ...

Everybody has a two-beat gait of their own, and the piece gives it back. It happens as they walk away from the wall, and somewhere past the parking lot each stride finds its old level pair again ...

The MARK and the LINE both shake a little, and nobody is going to steady them. A steady line is easy to come by and tells us nothing. The pen crossed living ground, and we keep the tremble in, proof the hand was breathing when it wrote ...

The top of the rail shows before anything else does, one stroke drawn across nothing. The rest of the fence is still owed to us, and it comes when it comes. Fog giving the track back one piece at a time, and we watch the line remember the whole oval ...

The tractor leaves the track ruled like notebook paper, thousands of thin lines lying side by side. They all point the way it drove, and the horses will be sent the same way. Then the next race goes straight over the top of every one of them. The harrow writes its marks and the field signs over them in hooves, all night the same handwriting twice ...

Overnight nobody touches the sand, and it keeps every line the rake gave it. It can stay that neat only as long as the horses stay in bed. Then morning sends the first one out at a jog. The grooming holds under the lamps, and one warm-up mile ends it, first signature on a clean page ...

A deer came over from the wetland, and at first light a crane walked the turn. Both left their tracks in the combed sand, and the crew finds them waiting when it comes to work, the one page of the morning that the rake had no hand in. In an hour the horses go out. Until then the ground stays theirs ...

The writing has turned animal by now, and it gets no desk and no drawer. It is kept the way the barn keeps anything that breathes, stabled with the rest then fed at the same time, startled at things the barn never hears ...

No machine can sew the strongest seam that leather knows. A hand has to make it, and a hand takes its time, and the time is the reason the seam outlasts the others. Somebody opens each hole with an awl, which is a pointed spike, and pulls the thread through after it. Every bridle in this barn came through somebody's lap, an awl and an evening at a time ...

One old harness hangs from its peg still bent to the body it was buckled around, as if it had gone on wearing the horse. Rules in a barn seldom get written down, and this one never was. Nobody hangs anything else on that peg, and nobody in the shed row ever said why ...

Anyone who keeps house folds a certain way. A made room is supposed to look as if nobody made it, and the fold is where the maker's hand held out and stayed in sight. Out in the barn the towels sit on the shelf with the tack, which is the gear a horse wears, and they are folded as wide as one hand. The barn knows whose ...

The horses go out in groups the barns call sets. Hooves write in lime, a chalky white powder, and they put it down on whatever they cross and never read it back. Rain takes most of it by noon, and the afternoon set walks out and makes more ...

The wall is made from the writing. Where the early sun falls on it the horses rest their weight against it. The boards keep that as well, tails swat flies against it all July, and that goes into the grain with everything else ...

At the door of a stall a groom is standing with empty hands and no chore, which is harder work than it looks. LET THEM LOOK. Nobody calls the horse and it walks over all the same, and neither of them is rushed. The saying in the barn is to never pull on him. Whoever said it first is long gone, and it still gets said every spring to somebody new ...

A lovely horse is stretched out full length in the straw at noon. A horse will only lie down in a place it trusts, so the barn has been paid a compliment. The whole shed row drops to a whisper going past ...

A girl comes down the row carrying a fish, and she needs both arms for it. The fish weighs more than a horse lick, the block of salt a horse works at with its tongue. The news gets ahead of her, and the barn cats have it before she has walked half the row. Not one of them looks at the horses ...

After the mile a horse is walked in slow circles until the heat has gone out of it, which the barns call cooling out. We have never seen a stopwatch come along. The race gets clocked to a fifth of a second, and the forty minutes of circles after it, never. Nobody posts the circles on the board, and nobody takes a bet on how they come out. The walk goes on all afternoon either way, nobody's business but the horse's ...

The horse walks out with the noise of the card room on every side of her, and a few steps later all of that noise is behind her. A bath out of a bucket is waiting where the barn begins, and a net stuffed with hay. Whoever leads her hums or does not ...

The horse is far from the card room by now, and the card room is still dealing. Nothing is for sale where her walk ends, and a horse carries no money anyway. The paper on a horse is the tickets people have bet on it, and she never saw one of hers. Steam off her back in the cold and the paper on her has not changed a thing ...

A corner post can be checked, because it stands in the same place every time anybody looks. We hear talk of a way to the light, and we would want it marked with posts like that one. A horse is walked in circles on the shank, which is the lead rope, and somebody has to keep count of the circles. A boy holds the shank and counts laps by the same corner post his father used ...

A stakes day is a day of the richest races, and a horse comes down from it exactly as it comes down from a Tuesday, and the walk is never told which one it was. Everything that is left over is walking. Forty minutes of nothing to see, and every good barn on the grounds does it in full ...

The old ones stay put and the rest of the world makes the trip to them. On these grounds the staying put is done on four legs at the gap, the opening in the rail where horses go on and off the track, and the callers are green, meaning young and new to all of it. The oldest pony on the grounds stands flat-footed at the gap, and every green two-year-old gets walked past him twice ...

A heart going too fast is stood in front of the old pony, and the heart slows down, softly, and nobody is charged. He has calmed forty springs of them without a sound, and the barn keeps him in cheap peppermints for it ...

What is on offer is a spot that does not move, and some respect for whoever is standing in it. We have paid more for less. Nobody young understands what the old pony is for, and nobody old explains it ...

We count a groom and a horse going down the gravel together, two legs beside four. Neither is hauling the other, and at every step the pair of them agree again to get there. The place to watch is the throatlatch, where the head joins the neck. A good groom walks level with the throatlatch, never ahead, and six feet hit the gravel in one rhythm ...

The slot machines stand in rows called banks, and the noise carries on a little way after the rows give out. We have looked for the place ourselves and walked right over it. Somewhere past the last slot bank the ear lets go of the noise, and nobody ever marks the spot ...

At the gap there is a way everybody turns, and we have all been new to one somewhere. Somebody new turns left at the gap, and the whole line just swings wide around them ...

Mornings, the horses are sent the wrong way around this oval. Come racing it all turns back around, the horses treat both directions as somebody else's idea ...

A horse pointed the right way around knows it is time to go. Turned about, it knows less. Wrong way means what day is it ...

Fishing is nothing but keeping still, start to finish. With horses, keeping still gets the job about halfway done. The other half is knowing which mornings not to, and no one writes down which is which ...

A racetrack will do for a wedding when a racetrack is where the two of them met. Ask around the grounds, that is most of the marriages here, and nobody finds it strange except the caterer ...

There is a pole and a bucket, a scale and a sign, then a bird, a hose, a bag, a girl and a fish. Nine things standing in plain sight of each other on a Wednesday ...

We keep clear of the tellers, and this reached us all the same. The house's biggest night was a bet the house lost and everybody went home happy, the payout took two tellers and a supervisor, and the line behind clapped every bundle across the counter ...

The sky above the track is our canvas, a clear light blue, and we are glad to be standing under it. Oh, for sure. Forty degrees and sunny gets called a beauty out here, and nobody is wrong about it ...

We remember the shovel that lifted the horse manure when the lifting was ours to do. Still leaning in the same corner it always did, and every new kid gets handed it the first morning ...

Of all the casinos we have stood in, this is the one that lets us know when night has come. Its windows keep saying so the whole evening long, and when the final race is over everybody walks out beneath real stars. The card room can keep its forever noon, the barns run on the sun like always ...

The crowd believes the silks are put on for the crowd, and the crowd is right. Our opinion was not sought. A horse would race as bare as it was born, and the people would stand three rows thick along the rail all the same. The colors get pressed and hung the night before anyway, the way things get done for a wedding ...

The winning horse stands for his photograph, and that is over in a minute. Then the shank is unclipped in the paddock, the yard where horses are made ready to race, as on any other night. Letting go is victory's own gesture, and it is done with dignity. The flowers wilt in a bucket by the tack room door, and the horse gets his bath in the dark ...

Somebody keeps what the crowd leaves behind. The lost-and-found drawer runs deep in reading glasses, and the window envelope stays a window envelope ...

When the one broke, they wrote the hour down. They did not mean how fast the race went, they meant the hour on the clock that night. Every race gets a chart, the printed record of who finished where and how fast, and whoever was minding the clock understood which of the two the barn would hold on to. The chart keeps the other one ...

A horse that is leaving goes out to the van dressed in nothing borrowed. The blanket belongs to him, and the halter, and the shipping wraps, which are the padding wound on his legs for the ride. Nobody hurries him, and the van sits until he has gone aboard calmly. Before the evening meal his stall is cleaned down to the floor, behind the webbing, the wide strap that hangs across the door for a gate. The name stays on the webbing till somebody needs the webbing ...

A race is what happens after one line turns loose and before another line shows up. The first is in the keeping of the gate crew, the people who send the horses off, and the second is the wire. Nothing in the middle has an owner. This property keeps a count of every last thing on it, and then for two minutes at a time nobody is in charge. The crowd knows it in their feet ...

A compass carried along the shed row loses its mind politely. Every horse stands on four iron shoes, and iron is the one thing a compass cannot ignore. Storms do not turn that needle and neither does a grandstand, yet it swings toward each stall as it goes past. Anywhere else in the world it has only the one north to be true to. This aisle is a barn full of norths, and the needle, honest about it, greets every one ...

The barn has exactly one love song, and the song lasts for one note. It is the nicker, and a horse saves it for the handful of people it knows. It starts when the right footsteps come over the gravel, well ahead of any face coming into view. We had it by heart the first time we heard it. Same verse before first light, same verse after the last race, answered in grain and a hand ...

A good groom walks a horse on a rope that droops, with about a hand of empty air from the fist to the halter. The horse weighs a thousand pounds, and it honors that loose rope as if it were written in a statute book. The slack is how the row measures kindness. A tight shank anywhere down the row draws the second look, the loose ones pass unremarked, which is how a barn says grace ...

A claiming horse travels more of the country than most of the people who come to watch ever will. It is a horse entered in races where it can be bought for a posted price, and it goes from one state to the next one race at a time, with half the map packed into four legs. We have gone across half the world ourselves to find out what our own soul has in it. When the door of the van comes down at one more barn it has never seen, the horse wants two things before anything else, a deep drink of water and a good roll in clean straw. The people of the barns watch for the roll. The old ones price that, a hundred dollars more every time a horse rolls clear over ...

Love does not die, and we are asked to take that on trust. It paces, at a marvelous clip, and the clip is how fast a breath comes. The night check, the walk through the barn after dark, is done by listening. No light is turned on. Whoever is making the rounds goes from one door to the next, and a horse sleeping upright takes twelve breaths or so in a minute. Somebody stands at every door till the count arrives, thirty years of rounds and never a page to them ...

A Hallway Lit Like a Barn at Night

The end wall, and the hallway that carries people to it

In August you walked us through your whole building, and your family made us feel at home, and then you showed us a wall. Then you did the rarest thing that can be done for an artist and left the dreaming to us. What the dreaming became is in these pages ...

Our two families are turning into one, and the work ought to be worthy of that as well. It comes out of true stories about your ground, your people and your horses, and we mean it to be a thing your guests return to for years ...

The pages go through the whole picture in plain words, one thing at a time. That leaves the call for your questions, all of them ...

The whole of it fits in one breath. A hallway lit like a barn at night leads to a picture made of light, and the picture is slow and quiet and concerns the horses, the people and the water of this place. Down that hallway there is nothing to buy. Whoever goes in comes out calmer ...

The casino is at a guest's back, as loud and as bright as casinos always are, and the hallway takes some of that away at every step, a little of the noise and a little of the light. Along the wall small warm lights are glowing, and they look like the windows of a barn seen at night. Each has a few lines beneath it for reading, and a rail of smooth wood is there for leaning. The picture hangs at the far end, large and slow and made of light. Drawings and short sentences about this place come up in it, wait until they have been read, and fade, and others are always on the way. Nothing more is asked of anyone. Most people will walk out feeling better than when they walked in, and the hallway is made for that. They stand, they look, they read, they walk back ...

Casinos everywhere are built so that it always feels like noon. We know of nothing like this hallway, and people will make the drive only to stand in it. It hands the night back, the barn after the last race, the horses asleep, one lamp left on ...
