The hallway is called The Night Check, after the last look down the barn aisle before bed. Twelve small warm lamps sit in the wall behind glass, one after another down the length of the hall. They are kept low, so that the far wall stays the only bright thing. Every lamp breathes. It brightens and dims slowly, twelve breaths to the minute, which is the rate of a horse asleep on its feet. When a guest walks by, the nearest lamp glows gently brighter and the glow is handed down the row, one lamp and then the next, and then it stops. One horse calls in the dark that way, and another answers from down the aisle ...

A few lines of the writing are etched into the glass under each lamp, and they are read in the pool of light that lamp makes. Below the lines sits a small brass plate, engraved with the name and the record of a real Running Aces horse, one that is pensioned, which is to say retired, or one that is gone ...

The rail is oak, rounded and smooth, and it runs the full length of the hall at the height of the hip so that a guest can lean. People will touch, as people at a rail always have, and so the rail is the thing given them to touch. Twenty years of hands will only make it finer. There may be old wood on your property already, a retired stall front or a length of fence, and the rail can be made from it. Wood like that would arrive with the history of the place already in it ...

The end wall is called The Dream. A single large screen hangs there the way a framed painting is hung, in a frame of solid white oak, and it measures about seven feet across and four feet high. It is a commercial 4K display. Its face is matte, which is to say dull and not shiny, and glare never gets a place to sit on it. The film it plays is silent and is made of images and sentences drawn by our own hand. They rise and dissolve as slowly as breathing, and each time the film ends it begins again, forever. What a guest sees is a painting that happens to be moving, very slowly. Behind the work there is light. You floated that one practical thing when we stood at the wall together, and the whole design has it for a heart ...

Inside The Dream, the field comes around. We first dreamed that part as lanterns and running legs, and learned afterward that the two are old companions. People once sat in dark rooms, long before any movie, and watched painted light thrown by a device called a magic lantern. Then a harness racing question needed settling, the legs of a trotter were photographed to settle it, and those photographs were the first moving pictures ever made. The movies were born at this sport's gait, and the wall remembers ...

The field comes around once in each cycle of the film, the field being the track's word for all the horses in a race. The white gives up legs, a great many of them running, some large and some small, some sharp and some soft, and they are seen from every side at once, which is how a dream shows things. Their running is slow, as slow as a race becomes when we remember it years later. No machine draws them. They are real shadows. We will cut each leg from card, give it joints, and work it by hand behind a cloth screen while a lantern burns behind it, and the camera will film only what that lantern throws. Every shadow will carry the small flicker of the flame. Hands move them, light casts them, a camera keeps them ...

A faint tremble of two beats goes through the wooden rail while the field is passing. A hand resting on the wood is feeling hooves, the same way the ground gives word of horses before the ears have any. Felt, never heard. The hallway keeps its quiet. When the field has gone by, the running legs come loose into strokes and sentences again and the wall goes on breathing. The field will be around again, as it always is at this track ...

Beside The Dream is The Wish, the still one. One unbroken line draws a horse, a sulky and a driver as a single figure, the sulky being the light two-wheeled cart the driver sits on. One small light is on it, steady, and that light never changes. Harness racing keeps two gaits, raced separately and never mixed, and so one of our works moves and one holds still. What stands around the two works is the surround, and it waits to be designed until you have stood at the wall with us ...

The wall lives by the law of the racetrack, which is that nothing stays written, and that is why people come back. Each night there is a newly printed program. The chalk on the board washes off. The harrow combs the sand smooth, and every night the horses write on it again ...

A vault stands behind the work. It holds hundreds of finished lines and passages about this place, and they are kept in twelve named stalls, among them the memory of the ground, the law of the sport, the workers and the sound. The wall shows only a little at a time, and the rest stands in its stall and waits its turn ...

A child could be told how the turn works. The lamps number twelve, and so do the stalls, and so do the months. One stall has the turn each month, and for that month its sentences are the ones that surface on the wall, while its own lamp out in the hallway burns a shade warmer. Someone who visits in January and again in March finds that the wall has changed. The regulars will learn to ask which stall is up ...

The film has four seasons, the seasons of your track, and each one carries the stall of the month inside it. Winter is sleigh bells and clouds of breath. Spring is the foals, July is the grandstand, and September is the cranes going over ...

The brass plates are a row that grows. Horses of your house will retire, and horses of your house will pass, and each name joins the row. Someone will come back to look for a horse they knew ...

None of the changing ever reaches your staff. The film lives on a memory card, one card changes a season, and we are the ones who bring it. You do nothing and pay nothing to have the work refreshed, since the vault is written already and runs deep enough for years. The changes are kept small and seasonal, and they never make a show of themselves ...

A fixed artwork is seen once. This one turns into a habit, and gives a person twelve reasons a year to return, for years ...

Of the five senses, the eyes get the slowest light in the building, in a house of fast ones. The ears get a quiet that had to be built. Soft materials line the hallway, so the roar of the casino really does drop away one step at a time, and there are no speakers. The silence is the music. The hands get the rail, oiled oak, smooth, made to be leaned on and touched. The nose gets no perfume and nothing piped in, only the smell of what is really there, oak and oil and leather ...

Taste is the fifth, and your menu has the story on it already. A guest can order the resurrection, which is the trout at the Trout Air Tavern. Two small ideas go with it, costing almost nothing, for you to take or leave. A dish of wrapped peppermints could sit where the hallway begins, free and unexplained, peppermints being the candy of the barn. Your bar could pour a drink with no alcohol in it and call it A Wish with Nothing Riding on It ...

The work is built for a casino and not for a museum. Nothing hangs and nothing sticks out, because the lamps sit flush in the wall, which also satisfies the accessibility code for corridors. The glazing is the material hockey rinks use, so a thrown drink wipes off and nothing shatters. The light is low-voltage and runs cool. There is no paper anywhere, only oak, metal and glass. The electronics live in a locked room, the sensors sit in the ceiling, and the fasteners take a special driver, the kind that turns a screw and not the kind that sits a sulky. Your cameras already watch this hallway around the clock. The one thing a guest can touch is the rail, and touching improves it ...

The work runs itself. The film lives on a memory card in a simple player, and the lamps run from one small controller. When the power of the building comes on, everything starts itself, and nobody operates it. Your staff have nothing to schedule and nothing to update. When a season changes, we bring the new card ...

Your own people build it, your screen supplier, your woodwork shop and your electrician. The equipment is ordinary and commercial, the carpentry is honest, and it is work they already know ...

From your building the work asks for power in the hallway wall and at the end wall, and for a wall sound enough to carry a framed work of this size. It asks for the house lighting of the hallway to be stepped down toward the end. If the building allows it, the hallway would also be kept a degree cooler than the casino floor. That is night air, and the body feels it before the eyes do ...

Nothing is ordered until a sample has proven the look, to you and to us both. A piece of the film runs on a test screen and one test lamp is lit. We stand with you where your guests will stand, and the judging is done first with our own eyes ...

One part of the story touches the names the Dakota and the Ojibwe have for this ground and for the horse. That part stays in draft until it has been reviewed with tribal representation, and nothing goes on the wall before that review ...

What remains are questions for the call, and only you can answer them. We ask who supplies screens or signage for your building, who does your woodwork, and who your glazier is, the one who sets the glass. We ask whether that wall has power, whether the hallway does, and whether about seven feet by four suits the end wall. We ask how long the hallway is, because twelve lamps is the design and the hall decides the spacing ...

We ask whether the house lighting of the hallway may be stepped down toward the end, and whether your property has old wood of its own for the rail, a retired stall front or a length of fence. We ask whether there are horses of your house that you would want remembered on the brass plates, and which season the wall should open in. We ask last whether your building has any rules we should know ...

You asked for a heavenly dream and left the dreaming to us ...

The Stride will become a Slow Walk combined with Standing Still

Where our film and dissolve animation for Running Aces comes from, and how we would like to create it

Our proposal is a hallway. Twelve warm lamps are set low along one wall, a station at each, an oak rail runs the whole length at hip height for the hand, and on the end wall hangs one screen, where the film plays. The proposal calls that end wall the dream ...

Twelve frames of a trotter and sulky make the spine of our film. They show a harness horse from the side at racing speed, going a mile in two minutes and twenty-four seconds, which is about twenty-five miles an hour. Twelve frames match twelve stations, so one frame could stand at each, and the walk down the hallway would itself become the stride. The frames share one card, and the back of the card is scanned too, where each frame is described in words. Each frame is under two inches wide and cannot be enlarged as a photograph on a big screen, and up there the horse has to be drawn ...

The sulky's wheel fired the twelve cameras. It struck wires laid across the track and tripped each camera in turn, so the horse and cart drew their own picture. The backdrop behind them carried lines twenty-one inches apart, which is a ready measure for spacing marks or stations ...

After the twelve frames come trotting hooves, hooves alone in the trot with no body, the closest relative we have to an artist who works in marks. They are our reference for how little of the horse is needed. The one who filmed them wanted motion to leave one continuous trace, a good kind of insane, and that wanting is the ground under our pen marks. We stay hungry. Our film returns the horse to the line. The sound of hoofbeats deserves a look too, haha ...

What stands for the finish line? Nobody has to get esoteric about it. On a jar with a race painted on it, a white marker does, and THE JAR WAS ITSELF THE PRIZE. The jar is ancient and anyone can draw from it. We do not just closely reproduce a photograph with an artistic technique. We draw it out for its own different thing, where the legs can multiply and the line can be offered a shape to pass through as the leg swings. Legs alone can hold attention. A slow-motion horse set against darkness holds up at great size ...

We give the hoof sound meaning equal to the moving picture. In one museum a slow-motion horse runs with the sound of its hooves on a small screen, around four by six and a half feet, and its gait is a gallop, so it is a reference for the staying power of sound, more so than just a look. We introduce the Mongolian horse-headed fiddle and its sound, which imitates pounding hooves, while the long slide up the neck of the instrument sounds like a whinny. The bow is strung with horsehair, so the horse is physically in the sound. The rhythm is the three-beat gallop. Running Aces would need the even two beats of the trot, so we just borrow the method and not the rhythm ...

The line does the horse, the driver and the sulky, and a thousand or more soft marks of color are the air around the race ...

Legs were running along walls long before any of this. Cave painters gave their animals extra legs some thirty-two to thirty-five thousand years ago. Fifty-three figures in twelve caves were drawn as two or more pictures laid one over another, and the bison with eight legs was meant to be trotting or running. It has its full effect when a lamp or a torch is carried along the wall. A hallway is a wall that people walk along. Drawings with many legs at each station, under our warm lantern light, would come alive as the visitor walks, by the oldest method known, and the walking does the work. The legs we promised for the screen are close kin to this, cut from card, jointed, worked by hand before a lantern, and filmed as the shadows they throw. There were small discs too, an animal in one pose on each side, spun so the eye flips between the two. Two pictures, turn and turn about, are our two beats made into a thing. A disc like that would make a fine picture for a station, if it sits where hands cannot spin it ...

Then horse legs became handwriting, thousands of years ago. In the written character for horse, 馬, there are three strokes for the mane, four dots for the four feet and a tail hooking down, and in the simplified form, 马, the four feet are drawn as a single stroke. They are marks that are legs and writing at once. The simplified character came out of the fast cursive hand, and it was the speed of the writing that reshaped the horse. The line loosening into handwriting has happened once already, out in the world, to that very word. The word belongs to living languages and our marks belong to none, so it teaches us and stays off the wall ...

The old world also left a small bronze horse with all its speed balanced on one hoof, and that hoof resting on a bird. Speed is told there by how little of the horse is touching anything. Our trot has an instant when no hoof touches the ground. We could let the film rest on that frame a good while ...

Then the wheel struck its wires. The card of the twelve frames of the trotter Abe Edgington is a hundred and forty-eight years old and in the public domain, which is to say it belongs to everybody now. The library that keeps it puts it in its own careful words, "No known restrictions on publication." A drawing made from it by our hand is new work, and belongs to the hand that made it. We take the largest file and keep a dated copy of those words beside it ...

The same horse and the same driver were photographed going slow as well, about seven and a half miles an hour against twenty-five on the fast card, with eight frames on the slow card where the fast one has twelve. One animal at a slow trot and at a racing trot gives the film two tempos from one set of legs, an easy stretch and a fast one, and the beat can slow and quicken with them, as a heart does. Edgington comes around again in a later print, the very horse our film is built on, though this print's listing calls the cart a chariot and does not say the gait. We open the picture and see for ourselves whether it is a trot before we lean on it. If it is, we have our main horse in a larger print ...

The same horse is there once more with one stride told in eighteen moments, smoother than the twelve, and kinder when the film needs the places in between. But twelve is the count of our stations. Twelve for the hallway and eighteen for the screen would let both be right, and that is not yet decided. The later plates come from Animals in Motion, the old book of motion plates, free to download and marked public domain. Its chapter on the trot is still to be read, for the order the feet fall in ...

In those same years white papers, each a different shape, were glued to the joints of a dark horse, with lampblack dulling the shine of the coat, and the camera kept only the paths those papers traveled. The horse was made to vanish and the going stayed. Our line comes down from that one. Each joint could keep its own kind of mark ...

More horses went past the cameras after that, and they are free to come along. Lizzie M. trots in a sulky, free to download and everybody's, the same gait in another animal's way of going. The legs can come back around in the dream and not be the same legs twice. A second plate of the same mare gives two runs of one horse, and they let the film show that no two strides are the same, which suits a hand that keeps its tremble. Katydid trots from the side and from the front, free to all. The bottom row shows the same strides head on, the legs coming toward whoever is looking. Our visitor walking down the hallway toward the screen has that very view, and the dream can turn and come to meet them. Dercum, a different horse, gives the same two views, public domain as well. Between Katydid and Dercum the eye will choose whichever reads clearer when it is small ...

Nellie Rose trots with the sulky wheels caught turning, free as the others. That makes five trotters with names to draw from, Abe Edgington, Lizzie M., Katydid, Dercum and Nellie Rose, a full enough barn. The bay horse Reuben is another trotter, free for any use, one more horse in the barn and no more than that. If the string needs trimming, Reuben can be turned out first, with our thanks ...

The brown horse Pronto paces in a sulky, and that sets the pace beside the trot. A good many of the horses our visitors watch are likely going this way, the two legs on one side swinging together, and Taro will know how many. The pace counts in twos as well. A film about this track should probably show both gaits, and Pronto is free for any use. One more plate shows, line by line, the feet falling one after another through a stride. It hints that the footfalls were being written down as a score, a page our sound could one day be played from ...

Painters had their turn with the horse. One built a racehorse at the gallop from bright planes laid one over another, and we could stack several frames of one stride on the screen that way, as sheets of color, the silks all crowding through the turn at once. Another broke a horse and rider into pieces to show their energy more than their bulk. The breaking melts them into what stands around them, a horse joined to its place. The legs in our film could take in the rail, the lights and the track as they go by, and a little of the wetland behind it. A third made marks that stand where our line loosens into handwriting, somewhere between writing and a body. Sixty-four of those drawings once sat beside a single poem, picture and words in one work, and ours are stabled together too ...

A sculptor had a turn, and joined horse and machine into one form. The sculptor had studied the old photographs of motion and had spent time in the cavalry, so the work comes from pictures and from knowing horses. Harness racing is already a horse joined to a machine, the sulky and its wheel, and that joining belongs to this sport more than to any other kind of racing. It gives our film a place to go when the legs gather back from handwriting, toward wheel, spoke and shaft as well as bone ...

Old film of harness racing from 1954 has close shots of horses' legs and sulkies going past the camera, sulky wheels, a mane blowing, and shots that travel along beside the horses. It would sit in the dream as a layer of memory. No price and no terms are posted, so the National Film Board of Canada gets asked, with the use named. The old scan will go soft on a screen as fine as ours, and it plays small, or layered, or drawn over, and not as the main image. Lightning in Harness is a silent black-and-white film made for the sport's own club about training trotters, with several stretches in slow motion, sulkies on the track, mares with foals and a farrier shoeing a horse. Trotters in slow motion, already silent, come near to what the dream is asking for. The archive keeps the reel, and keeping a reel is one thing and owning the rights another. We write to Northeast Historic Film and say plainly where it will be shown ...

A dream is already on film, fifty-three seconds of trotters racing off the turf, out across the tide flats and through the surf, in black and white. Horses in water suit a place that called its water back for the trout. A high-definition copy can be bought. The seller calls it public domain and gives no reason, and we find the reason before we trust it. Its narration and music stay behind, and the shots will be wide, with few close looks at the legs ...

About then somebody else scratched marks by hand into black film and timed them to a drum, a white line on black keeping time. A moving line and a drum are enough, with no photograph underneath. We will have our own sound made, by somebody we can thank by name, and our drum is the trot's two beats. Our proposal kept the film silent and the hallway quiet, and gave those two beats to the rail, to be felt in the hand. Whether they come to be heard as well is not yet decided ...

In our own time an artist draws, rubs out and draws again on one sheet, and what was rubbed out stays behind as a light gray trail. The trail keeps the place the leg has just left, slow motion done by hand, and it keeps every tremble too. Our track knows this work. Hooves write, the harrow rubs out, and the next race is written over what is left. Ink will not rub out. The pen can only add and let the earlier lines stay ...

A computer has pulled trails of movement out of old sports film, smooth as a machine makes them, and we keep the tremble in. A horse of lights was stripped down to the few lines anybody needs to know a horse, one even, clean line at the gallop, a long way from our hand. It stood outdoors for years in a public park, and our hallway will not have a gallery's protections either. In another work, eighteen points of light, and a viewer sees a life-size horse at the gallop. The eye asks for very little. The line can wander a long way off into handwriting and the horse will still be there, so long as a few points keep their time ...

Writing has gone the other way and become a creature. It was done once in real space, written characters starting fixed to the floor, lifting, and turning into birds as they climbed toward the ceiling. Our twelve stations could do the turning in steps along the walk, the marks a little more leg at each one, and the change completed on the screen ...

One short film comes closest to a dream. It sets the old photographed horse down inside a mother's daydreams about time passing, and we watch it to learn how it crosses between the real horse and the dream. Every frame of it belongs to somebody else, and watching is all we take. It closes on a canon, one line of music chasing itself, legs following legs around the oval ...

Our film has open ground ahead of it, as far as we can see. One writer walked through the harness horse in art, from an old Dutch print up to the illustrators of now, and every work along the way is a portrait or a race scene. It is one writer's short walk, and we say it carefully. There is nothing like it that we know of ...

The stock film houses are out. The Getty license holds the clips it marks editorial away from any commercial use, and it names gambling, betting and gaming in the same clause. So we ask every owner in writing, and we put the word casino right in the question ...

What we film ourselves is ours outright. The 2026 meet is over, and filming at the rail waits for 2027. The racing ran three nights a week, starting at six or seven in the evening, and slow motion is hungry for light, more than an evening gives. Taro will know the 2027 dates before anybody, and whether horses can be filmed training by daylight sooner than that. The daytime qualifiers are our window, and there is no line at this one. They are run in full daylight with no crowd, with all the light a fast camera wants and a clear look at the horse from the side. Last season they went at noon on May 7 and May 12, and the horses were on the grounds two weeks before the meet opened, which gives us more days we could ask for ...

A fast camera can be rented, 4K, at up to eight hundred frames a second. At that speed a stride that takes under half a second in life lasts about fifteen seconds on the screen, one stride at the speed of memory. The shutter takes the whole frame at once, so legs and wheel spokes will not bend. Filming still wants written permission from the track and from each horse's owner and driver ...

A free tool follows one chosen point through a film, frame by frame. It is the old trace, taken without laying a hand on the horse. We mark a hoof, a hock and a knee, and it gives back the true curves those joints travel. The curves only guide the hand. The pen draws every one of them over again, and the work stays made by hand ...

If the permissions come, a horse of this track goes past the camera in daylight with the sulky's wheel turning, and the horse and cart draw their own picture once more. Twelve frames of a trotter and sulky, one frame at each station, and the walk down the hallway itself becomes the stride ...

When the Sandhill Goes Flying

We hear them before we see them, and some days we never see them at all. The call is throaty and sounds older than any of us, and a few of us are getting up there. When a sandhill crane crosses over, we stop where we stand. The ones who cannot find it in all that sky hold still with the rest, faces turned up, listening ...

Through the fall the calling grows louder, as more of them come in from nesting grounds a long way off and the crowd builds. The bird people have a word for it, staging, which is to say getting everybody together before the long trip. The days go to feasting on what the harvest left in the fields, and on wild seed. At dark they fly back to refuge ground to roost for the night. Nobody has to tell them twice. That goes on until they are ready to go south ...

Fourteen thousand of them come through here between the end of October and the first days of November ...

Formed and Shaped Truths

THE CART CAME FIRST ... Two wheels, a light body, a driver standing behind the horse, which is the shape we hitch to on a Friday ... Six of them came out of one tomb in the Valley of the Kings in 1922, every one found taken down to its parts ... parade carts and hunting carts and one built lighter and faster than the rest for war ... They stand together again at the museum beside the pyramids, the sixth one carried over in boxes from a military museum in the Cairo Citadel ...

ONE HORSE WEARING EIGHT LEGS ... On a plateau in southern Algeria somebody painted a two-horse cart at full stretch across the wall of a rock shelter, in red, the driver holding four reins and a whip of two strands ... The second horse ran hidden behind the first, so the painter gave the one horse the legs of both ... Eight legs off the ground in one red line, speed drawn the only way a flat wall allows ... The plateau carries more than fifteen thousand paintings and engravings, and the cart painters came late in that count, after the cattle and before the camels ...

THE NAMES STAYED IN THE FLOOR ... A breeder in the old port of Sousse had his best horses laid in stone in his own house, each name set over its back ... Adorandus and Crinitus facing a palm between them, Amor and Dominator in the next circle, every horse branded on the hindquarter with the first four letters of the man's name and on the shoulder with three more ... A mark that stayed after the horse was gone, same as our fastest miles in the books ... The floor lies in the archaeological museum at Sousse, and in another floor from the same town four named horses pull for the four colors, blue and white and green and red ...

THE FINISH ... A floor from Carthage, kept at the Bardo in Tunis, shows the circus from outside and inside at once, the race in its last strides and the Blues' driver in the moment of winning ... A rider goes alongside hollering the teams on, and a man runs with a jar and a whip ... The circus at Carthage ran more than four hundred and seventy meters end to end and seated about forty-five thousand ...

FOUR CARTS AT GAFSA ... A floor in the inland town shows the whole oval from above, the long barrier down the middle set with obelisks and lions, four carts running two to a side, the Greens and the Blues kept best ... The starting gate drawn as four arches with runners inside them, and the crowd done as heads only, under arches of their own ... The pavement lies in the town's archaeological museum, across from the old Roman pools ...

A START IN THE FREE STATE ... The sport as we know it is barely on the continent ... Seven standardbreds, six off a plane from Sweden and one from Australia, and the first formal harness races in South Africa run at Senekal with no betting on them ... The carts and the harness came as help from the Swedish trotting people, who beam their races in free for the South African windows ... Jog carts likely to be built locally soon, the chairman said, and no government money in it ... Senekal is a town in the Free State ...

Plain reading. I found no harness racing industry anywhere in Africa. The one documented start is Harness Racing South Africa: its chairman reported six newly arrived Standardbreds from Sweden and one from Australia, and the first formalized non-betting harness races held at Senekal in the Free State, with Swedish trotting interests supplying equipment and free live simulcasts. The article's date was not in what I pulled, and whether the group still races is unchecked.

The pictures and objects are all ancient and all from the north. For motion, the Tassili painting is the find: a faded red two-horse chariot galloping right, the charioteer holding four reins and a short two-strand whip, and the convention of drawing one horse in profile with eight legs to stand for the second horse hidden beside it. That is the same problem Muybridge's cameras solved three thousand years later, how to put legs at speed on a flat surface. For objects, six chariots were found in the tomb, four in the antechamber and two in a store room, all dismantled, and the sixth, the light war chariot, was moved from the military museum to the Grand Egyptian Museum so all six could be displayed together for the first time. They were never racing carts, but the shape is the sulky's. For the race itself in pictures, the Tunisian floors: Sorothus, a rich stock breeder at ancient Hadrumetum, had his winning horses Adorandus and Crinitus set in mosaic with victory palms, named and branded; another Sousse floor names four horses by faction color, Pupillus blue, Cupido white, Amator green, Aura red; the Carthage mosaic at the Bardo shows the circus from outside and inside at once, a race ending in the moment of victory for the Blues; and the Gafsa pavement shows four chariots from a bird's-eye view, the spina with obelisks and lions, the starting gates as four arches, spectators drawn as heads.

For your film, the eight-legged horse and the man with the jar are the two with motion in them. On legal footing: the works are ancient, the photographs are not. The World History Encyclopedia photos of the Sousse floors are CC BY-SA, usable with credit. The University of Saskatchewan photos of Gafsa and the Sorothus floor are CC BY-NC, which rules out commercial use, and a casino wall likely counts as commercial. The British Museum photo's terms I did not check. Drawing from any of them in your own hand is a different matter from showing them.

Facts note.
- Checked: the six chariots, their finding, the 2018 move, the sixth from the Citadel; the museum director's sorting of them as ceremony, hunting, and one for war; the Tassili painting's details and the eight-legs convention; more than 15,000 paintings and engravings at Tassili; the horse tradition coming after the cattle tradition and before the camel tradition; the Sorothus names and brands (SORO on the hindquarter, THI on the shoulder); the four faction horses; the Carthage mosaic's two views and the Blues winning; the Carthage circus running more than 470 meters and holding about 45,000; the Gafsa details; the Roman pools opposite the Gafsa museum; Senekal in the Free State.
- Dates conflict across sources and I used none in the ore: the Tassili horse period is given as from about 2000 BC by the Met and about 1200 BC elsewhere; the Carthage mosaic is dated first to second century by one source and early third by another; the Gafsa pavement is called sixth-century Byzantine by one record and second to fourth century by a stock agency.
- Not checked, general knowledge: that the running man's jar was for wetting the horses (the record says only a jar and a whip); that the Egyptian chariot was the lightest of the ancient chariots; that Tutankhamun's painted chest shows his horses at the flying gallop. The sulky-ancestor line is my reading of the shape, not a sourced lineage.
- Not done: any survey of Morocco, present-day Egypt, Kenya, Zimbabwe, or Mauritius for harness racing. The answer "barely on the continent" rests on one South African source.

NEAR A FIELD ... The program uses the word for the horses themselves, everything entered in one race standing together as one field ... Near it is anywhere we can hear harness being buckled, the paddock fence, the walk out, the rail at the gap ... The state keeps a number on how big a field may grow, ten horses on a track of five-eighths of a mile ...

Every horse in the field wears a pad colored for its post, no two alike in the same race ... Lined up for the start they make a bow of colors walking, and something always rides in front of it ... the starting car, wings spread across the track, the field nosing up behind ... At the start the wings fold in and the car pulls away ahead of the horses ...

A rainbow only stands where the sun is at our backs ... So whoever is in front of one is facing the low light with the rain behind, squinting, held still for somebody's picture ... Mornings on the backside the whole set gets that ... they jog the oval the wrong way round, half a lap facing the bow and half a lap carrying it, and not one head turns ... Horses in morning training go clockwise, against the way they race ...

ALSO ... We keep more than one address on these grounds ... the blanket, the field, the bow behind us, and whoever comes looking checks all three ... The nearest field of all is the infield, the oval drawn around it, the tote board standing in the middle of it ...

Plain reading. The line adds to the blanket line. "That's where you'll find us" now has a second address. On a racetrack "field" means two things: open ground, and the horses entered in one race. So "near a field" can mean near the horses. "In front of a rainbow" reads two ways. One is the posed picture: a person in front of a rainbow is facing the sun after rain. The other is native to the track. The starting car drives in front of the horses, and the horses wear different colored saddle pads by post, so the car leads a moving band of color. The "also" says the "we" is happy in more than one place: wishing, close to the horses, and standing in the light after rain.

Facts note.
- Checked: "field" as the horses in a race, and the cap on field size. Minnesota's harness rules set the maximum for overnight events at ten horses on a five-eighth mile track. That is the 2008 version on the state Revisor's site; the current rule was not checked.
- Partly checked: pads differing by post. Pennsylvania's harness rules require saddle pad colors to differ for each race or post position. Minnesota's own rule on pad colors was not checked, and no specific colors went into the passages.
- Not checked, from general knowledge: that Running Aces is a five-eighths-mile track (this comes from the project's own record); that a rainbow appears only with the sun behind the viewer; that the starting car's wings fold and the car pulls away at the start; that horses jog clockwise in morning training; that the tote board stands in the infield (this one comes from the locked book).
- This line has no "you," but it rides on the blanket line, so your open ruling on "you'll" still covers it.

Wish Upon a Blanket. That's where you'll find us. ... A star takes a wish from a long way off ... A blanket takes it at arm's length, warm, smelling of horse ...

Flat on the back in the grass, wool between us and the ground, the whole sky for a board ... Star-wishing was always done from a blanket ... This ground had the grass and the singing before it had a rail ...

On a cold night we go out to meet them with one over the arm ... The harness comes off, the cooler goes over the steaming back, first horse and last horse alike ... A wish laid there is the whole load ... No rider in this sport ... The harness is back on its peg by then ...

When a field comes home so close that one blanket could cover every horse in it, the old hands call it a blanket finish ... We have come home in that bunch all our lives ... The photo sorts the noses a minute later ...

Plain reading, outside the voice. I take the line as an address. It moves the wish from the star, which is far off and chooses nobody, down to the blanket, which is near and covers whatever is under it. The blanket turns up in three places on this ground: on the grass where people lie back to look up, over the horse after the mile whether it won or lost, and at the wire when the whole field arrives together. "That's where you'll find us" says the people of this book live in those three places, among the cared-for and the uncounted. It also lands on your title: a harness horse under a blanket has nothing riding on it.

One flag. The second sentence says "you'll," and the standing law for the elder's voice is "we," never "you." My recommendation is that it stands as a said thing, like a note left on a door, the way "You betcha" stands in the locked book. That is your ruling.

Facts. Two rest on general knowledge of the sport and were not checked today: "blanket finish" as the term for a very close finish, and "cooler" as the blanket put on a hot horse after work. The grass, the singing before the track, and the harness on its peg come from the locked book.

This chat is at a middle weight now: fine for this throw and a few more, and I will say so before it turns heavy. Your line is in italics; the rest is trial ore for your carve.

Sulky Racing and the Sound of Most Wonderous Trumpet Music ... A trumpet has never kept a thing to itself ... The cart is said to be named for keeping to oneself, one seat and no room for company ...

Racing took its trumpet from the cavalry, a stand-by call ... It turns us all one way before there is a horse on the track ... Thirty-four notes, no sharps, no flats, and the next race about ten minutes off ...

The oldest of that music was in this marsh country before any track ... A swan named for the instrument carries close to a yard of windpipe looped inside the breastbone, and what comes out of that coil crosses two miles of open marsh ... They were gone from this state for the better part of a century ... They came home as eggs, flown down from Alaska in first class ... About fifty thousand at the last count, some of them on the water of this same township ...

In the old tellings a trumpet is kept for the last morning, the one the sleepers get up to ... We have watched this place get up already, more than once ... the grass after the fire, the trout by the truckload, the swans by the egg ...

The trumpet goes over the mile the way a tune goes over a pulse ... The mile brings the pulse, hooves down the stretch in twos, two wheels turning ... Our chests keep the same time at the rail ...

Plain reading, outside the voice. I take the phrase as a title that sets the most solitary seat on the grounds beside the one instrument whose whole job is to announce. The trumpet says something is about to arrive, and near this track its truest players are the trumpeter swans, which were lost to Minnesota and brought back. That is the same coming-back story the book already tells. For the film, it points to a sound made of breath through a coiled tube, laid over the two beats of the trot.

We want to be free as a gleeful bird flying swift and fast* ... The wanting was at the rail before any window opened ... a little room kept empty in every chest for lift ... Most weeks nothing comes to fill it ...

Can adrenaline get us to that state or lucid wild sense? ... The body keeps a quick fire in two small glands riding the kidneys, and it has never once asked what struck the match ... A deer stepping out on the county road will do it ... so will a bay mare three wide at the head of the stretch with our two dollars on her ...

Lucid is how the stretch looks from inside it ... the eye lets seven horses go and keeps the one, the clock goes soft, and the legs come plain, one at a time, which they never do at thirty or so ... Wild is the voice ... up out of grown people who have not raised it since they were nine, a name none of us knew an hour ago ... Nobody on the apron turns around ...

Can the adrenaline stirred up from cheering for the horse that we bet on provide us that full of life feeling? ... For the length of the stretch, yes, all of it ... Heels come up off the ground down the whole rail, the nearest any of us gets to leaving it ... The ticket is leave to holler ... two dollars so a careful person has a reason to make that much noise in public ...

The driver has the lines ... the rest of us hitch on by voice ... What we hitch to is full of life in the plain sense, a third of its red blood held back in the spleen while it stood in the stall, sent out within seconds once the fire came ... Every stride of the mile has an instant with all four feet off the ground ...

One of us had nothing down and yelled for the gray because it was gray ... heels up with the rest ... The redwings go the whole day that way, over the wetland ...

The fire in us is spent in minutes ... The horses are pulled up and turned, the hands come down, and at the window somebody's fingers are still unsteady on the ticket ... The horse takes about an hour to put the blood back, walked in circles for most of it ...

Plain reading, outside the voice. I take the fragment to be asking whether the thrill of the stretch run is the real feeling of flight or a rented copy of it. My answer is that it is real while it lasts, the bet is mostly what gives a person permission to shout, and the horse is the one who truly has the feeling. That also gives the film its reason: slow motion on the screen is what the body already does to time in the stretch, offered at the end of the hallway with no ticket needed.

THE FIVE SENSES AT RUNNING ACES
A harvest of every sensory vein in the project's documents, with the physical anchors — ruled and proposed
October 8, 2026. Nothing new here is ruled; what stands ruled elsewhere is marked so.

THE GOVERNING LAW — ALREADY IN THE CARVE

From the Paraploo Goal document, Reach's own hand:

- "Surfacing text will speak the senses — language doing the conjuring ... The viewer's own imagination supplies the pull."
- "Smell enters through harness leather ... cut hay ... cold loam after rain."
- "A person fishes in flat true sentences ... the omitted weight is what makes the story tremble ... Say the eighth ... submerge seven-eighths."

The law: the senses are conjured, not installed. Each sense gets ONE real, physical anchor in the hallway — true, checkable, ordinary — and the writing does the conjuring above it. No machine fakes what a material can be.

SIGHT — ruled; the core of the work

What stands in the pages:
- "A painting that happens to be moving, very slowly" / "the slowest light in the loudest room" / "in a building of lights that lie, the slowest light tells the truth" (telling; harvest)
- "The light changes at the speed of breathing, or slower" — the tempo law over everything (wall file; carve)
- "Strata with light depth the visitor can look into ... the picture we love most here also implies infinite space" (carve)
- The darkhouse window — the ice-fishing darkhouse as the backlit box's oldest local form; "the floodlit oval is a ring of light standing in darkness" (carve)
- Cold-night racing, the Minnesota gift — "every horse's breath comes out as cloud spirit, made visible" (carve); "every breath a horse head shaped cloud" (telling)
- "A horse made out of fog, and then no horse" (telling)
- The gradient: "it darkens as it goes, and the one light at the far end is the light of what hangs there" (harvest)

Physical anchor (ruled): The Dream's breathing backlit field; the Night Check lamps barn-amber behind glass; the hallway's house light stepped down toward the end.

SOUND — the envelope is translated into light; the quiet is built

What stands in the pages:
- "Harness racing is the only racing on earth whose sound is a heartbeat ... a field of eight horses is a chest full of heartbeats held at maximum speed" (carve; telling)
- The race-sound envelope, from the carve: "Far drumming, felt before it is heard ... hooves, the hiss of forty wheels, the announcer climbing, the rail trembling into the chest ... then the break, whoosh of passage ... then gone ... Approach, break, recede is the envelope the dissolve should wear." — THE SOUND LIVES IN THE LIGHT'S BEHAVIOR. The film is ruled silent; the race's sound-shape is what the dissolve wears.
- "Quiet while the field is a sound" — the fog call (wall file)
- "The biggest word of the night is the one nobody hears" (Dream sentence, ruled)
- "Nobody has counted the steps from the roar to the quiet" (bank)
- "The casino noise falls away on the walk toward it" (The Work in Plain Words)
- "The sound of eight horses eating, when it starts, is one sound" / hay-rain on a soft roof / the old record's room-tone / bells on a trotting harness because a sleigh in snow is nearly silent / "the sounds arrive before the birds" (telling)
- "A child asleep through a horse race, which is the loudest quiet I know of" (harvest)

Physical anchor (proposed): BUILD THE QUIET. Acoustic absorption in the hallway — soft panels behind fabric above the niches, a carpet or runner underfoot — so the casino's roar measurably falls away step by step. The decrescendo is the composition; the walk is the volume knob. No speakers. If the sample night ever proves the quiet needs grain, the reserve option is a near-threshold sound floor in the last third — hay pulled from a net, a bucket, a hoof in shavings — but the ruling instinct is silence, and the Dream's cycle already carries the race's sound-shape in light.

TOUCH — ruled anchor already standing

What stands in the pages:
- THE RAIL, ruled in the wall file: "Worn-smooth wood. No instruction. White oak, the barn's hardwood ... ask whether the property has wood of its own — a retired stall front, a length of old fence — because the hallway says the wood is worn, and the property's wood makes that true on the first night. No tooled wear; oiled; the hands do the rest."
- "Worn-smooth wood, the most touched surface in racing" / "the wood under them is worn smooth, and she leans, and the weight goes forward onto the wood" / "a wall to lean on" (telling; harvest)
- "A palm can be laid on the ground the horses run on" (harvest)
- "Every barn kid on earth has a hand on a handle ... the wood already smooth when their hands arrived" (telling)
- Fishing rod and reins as one gesture: "taut line reading the trout ... the driver reads the horse's mouth through the leather ... both know this activity by tension alone ... the pen line is the third practice of the same gesture — hand, tension, mark" (carve)
- The whisper at ten inches — "the one lamp left on in the aisle" — readable by one person at a time (ruled): leaning in is a body act; the piece makes the visitor bow close.

Physical anchors: the oak rail (ruled — and the September 19 question about the property's own wood, a retired stall front or fence length, folds into the next visit if that one hasn't happened). Proposed additions: letters v-carved or etched where fingertips can read them; a change of footing in the last few steps before the wall — the shed row underfoot, felt through shoes, "the way to the light paved with things that can be checked" made literal.

SMELL — carried by real materials, never piped

What stands in the pages:
- "Smell enters through harness leather, cut hay, cold loam after rain" (carve — the conjuring law applied to scent)
- "An old harness on its peg still holds the shape of the horse that wore it" (telling)
- "The strongest seam in leatherwork is the one only a hand can make" (telling)

Physical anchor (proposed): the materials themselves. The rail is ruled oiled — oiled white oak has a smell, faint and true, strongest where hands warm it. If any leather enters the hallway (a strap, a keeper, the real harness behind glass in one niche), it is real leather, oiled. NOTHING DIFFUSED, NOTHING PIPED: the casino floor perfumes its air the way it lights its noon — this hallway smells only of what is actually in it. That contrast is the point, and it is checkable with a nose. The conjured layer — cut hay, cold loam, steam off a hot horse in winter air — rides in the etched lines where the writing does that work.

TASTE — already in the work, and the house already owns the anchor

What stands in the pages:
- The trout at the tavern, wall-file reserve (25): "He ordered the trout because the book had told him to and because he wanted to see what a fish tasted like that had been called back from the dead, and he said it tasted like trout, which he seemed to find satisfactory." — THE RESURRECTION CAN BE ORDERED OFF A MENU. The Trout Air Tavern is the taste chamber of the work; the piece quietly sends people there.
- The old pony's wages: "the barn keeps him in cheap peppermints for it" (telling) — the peppermint is the barn's universal candy, every horse person on earth knows it.

Physical anchors (proposed, both cheap and ordinary):
1. A small dish of wrapped red-and-white peppermints where the hallway meets the floor — the pony's candy, free, unexplained. "The one thing on this property that's never asked anybody for a dime" gets a taste. Staff refills it on ordinary rounds; wrapped candy in a casino is as normal as a host desk.
2. Offered to Taro as hospitality kin, not part of the artwork: a zero-proof drink at the bar named A WISH WITH NOTHING RIDING ON IT — hope without a stake; nothing riding on it; house edge zero. The artwork hands his bar a house drink, and the menu line markets the wall.

THE BODY — the sixth sense the pages keep reaching for

What stands in the pages:
- "The piece gives each person back their own two-beat gait ... as they leave the wall walking, and somewhere past the parking lot each stride finds its old level pair again" (telling)
- The dwell law, ruled: each Dream sentence legible long enough to be read twice AT A WALKING PACE — the piece is already timed to the human walk.
- "Every step is the viewer agreeing to arrive" / "nobody has counted the steps from the roar to the quiet" (harvest; bank)
- The lean: weight onto the wood. The whisper: one person bowing close. (ruled elements)
- The cold as Minnesota's gift: cold-night racing, breath made visible (carve).

Physical anchors (proposed): space the twelve lamps at a stride-related interval, so walking the hall paces the visitor without their knowing why. And ask the building for ONE DEGREE: the hallway's HVAC zone set a touch cooler than the casino floor — barn air, night air, felt on the skin the moment they step in. Ordinary equipment, no cost, and the body knows it has left the forever noon before the eyes finish adjusting.

THE SHORT TABLE

- SIGHT — the breathing light (ruled)
- SOUND — the built quiet; the race's sound-shape worn by the dissolve (ruled in the carve; quiet proposed)
- TOUCH — the oiled oak rail, the property's own wood if it exists (ruled); carved letters, the footing change (proposed)
- SMELL — real oak, real oil, real leather; nothing piped (proposed)
- TASTE — the trout at the tavern (standing); the pony's peppermints; the zero-proof Wish (proposed)
- THE BODY — walking-pace dwell (ruled); stride-spaced lamps, the cooler degree (proposed)

One law over all of it: every sense is answered by something true. Nothing performs a sensation the place does not actually have.

WHATEVER BURNED HERE CAME BACK KINDER ... The old prairie arithmetic ... fire takes a field down to dark ashes in one afternoon, and what comes up through is the sweetest grass a horse ever put a mouth to ...

The ground's memory ... harrowed smooth before every race and remembering every race anyway ... the teeth only comb the top layer of what the ground knows ...

The oval is a shape with the wetland behind it ... Made with graders and gone quiet by ten, and behind it the older establishment, open all night ... The wetland is a grandstand of frogs and blackbirds calling odds on nothing, every seat taken since before the first shovel ...

The sun comes up and hits the board first, because the board is the tallest thing in the infield ... First light spent on yesterday's numbers, gilding a tote nobody has bet ...

A line of geese goes over the lot low and calling ... The other harness racers, hitched nose to tail in the oldest silks there are, wanting nothing off the card but south ...

The stroke has been in the air over the wetland ever since ... What left one hand never touched ground again ... the redwings carry it now, one feather each ...

Resurrection of the trout ... Cold water takes its miracles plain ... a truck backs down to the pond, the gate lifts, and everything that was gone is swimming again ...

Whatever burned here came back kinder ... The lightning bought the whole property once, paid in fire, and the deed came back written in clover ...

A house that nearly died learned to pension its horses ... Faced the bank itself one winter, and none of its trotters ever will ... hay for every January left in them ...

The one mark that stayed dead flies now somewhere off the edge of the picture ... The comebacks all stand inside the frame ... the one that kept its death got the flying kind, no silks, no number, no post time ...

The lot is the last ground that isn't trying to be anything ... Everything past the gravel has a job ... the oval performs, the wetland testifies, and the lot just holds cars the way a field holds cows, off the clock ...

Whatever's calling was here before the fish farm ... The farm raised trout on it and thought itself the founder ... the calling had been paying rent on that water since the glacier's back was still turned ...

Nobody will remember a word they said and everybody will remember the water ... It was the curtain that was moving, not the stage ... All that speaking sank same as the rain, and what the body kept was the shine off the wetland ...

The grass held them closer ... The rail only ever watched them go by ... it was the infield that kept a green grip, ground holding whatever it has decided to raise ...

This ground had a stage before it had a track ... Whatever dream this building is, the water is its oldest memory ... The boards went up for singing before any horse drew a number here ... and under both runs the first tenant, holding the lease, remembering the place before it meant anything ...

Resurrection is the neighborhood's habit, not the building's private miracle ... The oval is a ring drawn around water ... Everything on this section has died once and thought little of it ... the burned came back, the drained came back, the trout came back by the truckload ... so the track drew its circle the way any settlement does, around the well ...

A trotter at speed wanting nothing more than to run flat out, and holding the gait anyway ... the whole discipline is a promise kept at thirty or so miles an hour ...

A mile against the clock ... No horse beside them, no purse, just the oldest opponent on the grounds ... the one that has never lost and never once gloated ...

A driver looks for cover ... another horse to sit behind and let break the wind ... Drafting is the sport's plainest confession ... even here, somebody has to go first into the weather so somebody else can save their run for the stretch ...

The same horse from a different post runs a different race ... Then one word, official, and the tellers pay on it ... The draw decides half the story before the gate car ever rolls ... eight lanes of luck ... and after all that weather and cover and stride, the whole afternoon comes down to a single lit word that turns opinions into money ...

The trot and the pace are gaits held on purpose ... Neither one comes natural at that speed ... a horse full of plain run agreeing to carry itself in time, which is what makes it a discipline and not an escape ...

Long enough to let the two swing as a pair, and short enough that they cannot swing apart ... The hopples measure a covenant in inches ... room to keep the rhythm, none to lose it ... A horse that holds the gait on its own is raced without them, free-legged ... The rig comes off when the rhythm has moved from the leather into the animal ... the pace kept now on honor alone ...

From the word every horse in the field is a starter, and there is no recall after it ... And some of us said it once in a kitchen, quietly, and that field is running still ...

He didn't lose ... He went off stride ... The gallop was in them first, and some evenings the legs remember it at the worst possible furlong ...

The best horse in the race went faster than all of them and lost by it ... We have kept ledgers like that, the finest number in the whole book standing in a losing column ...

One gait is kept by a strap, the other by character ... The whole breed is the refusal to break ... The strap comes off in the paddock, and what stays on has no buckle to it ...

A long race is not won by speed but by not breaking ... Some of us have been in one since the eighties, never once fast, still on stride at the kitchen table this morning ...

Hold the pace we can hold, past the place where holding it stops feeling like anything ... Past feeling is where the stride goes quiet and the ground starts doing half the work ...

In this game our horse loses when it runs ... A rule to break the heart of every open field, the whole animal built for it and the one thing forbidden ...

Held at the top of what a body can do and asked not to break ... The top is the narrowest place, room for four hooves and no room for wanting more ...

Hope without a stake ... A WISH WITH NOTHING RIDING ON IT ... We have made those all our lives, whole shelves of them, wished un-wagered and still paying out ...

People stay at the rail after the numbers are up and look at the track with nothing on it ... And the whole crowd still faced the same way, nobody willing to be the first spine that turns ...

A wish that stays felt is never said out loud, so no two dollars ever come due on it ... The unsaid wish is the only bet a picker cannot lose, and some of us have grown old winning it ...

Then there is a fourth race ... It has no line on the board, no window, no purse, and no winner ... And every one of us is entered in it, no draw needed, the gate opened the day we arrived ... The fast one posts the odds, the slow one posts the wishes ... Two boards over the same track, and only one of them ever changes its numbers ...

A wish with nothing riding on it, they bet once and watch eight ... One ticket in the pocket and eight horses in the eye, seven of them carried for free ...

The only customer on the apron who is watching the races instead of their money ... And the track runs truest in the one gaze it never billed ...

The rail is the one place on the property where nobody's selling anything ... Free to stand at, free to lean on, the last plain wood in a building of windows ...

They weren't talking about beating the house ... They were talking about the night ... The house closes its ledger at the last race, and the night stays open past every window ...

The oldest work a horse picture has is to carry a wish ... Older than the wagering, old as cave walls, the animal drawn mid-stride so the wanting has somewhere to run ...

Grandstand is basically a platform full of people wishing on horses ... A whole architecture raised for wanting, tiered so every wish can see over the wish in front of it ...

The wish is older and kinder than the wager ... The wager needs the horse to finish first, the wish only needs it to be beautiful somewhere in the run ...

The house edge on a wish is zero ... The one number no house has ever moved, posted nowhere, honored at every window ...

A story, a stillness, and a wish with nothing riding on it ... Three fares for the same evening, and the gate takes them all without making change ...

And nobody has noticed ... The truest things on the property go unnoticed, kept safe by the crowd looking elsewhere ...

For horses and others who have never won anything with money on it ... A dedication with room in it for most of the standing world, us among them ...

The favorite is whoever leans on the rail first ... and the rail takes all of us at even money ... A picker's money is a picker's hope with a number on it ... We fold ours twice so the hope keeps its shape ...

There is no wire ... Nobody strung one ... And still their noses reach for it coming home ... Green light all the way home ... Ours is the porch lamp, lit before we left, and we crossed under it anyway, all our lives ...

The fish to bite tomorrow ... Already swimming tonight, every one of them ... circling a hook we haven't tied and a morning we haven't reached ...

Every wish in this index was on the losing half of a ticket ... The winning halves went through the window and came back as money ... ours stayed wishes, kept in the coat all winter ...

Hope offered, wanting nothing back ... Wanting without needing is the most stable ground there is ... Not a stake driven anywhere in it ... takes our full weight and gives back nothing but the holding ...

Between equals, each thanks the other ... Our hand on the dusty neck says it one way ... the mile they just gave says it back the other ... And neither of us wipes it off ... the dust staying on the palm the whole walk back ...

The board in the infield posts the odds ... this wall, the wishes ... One we read from across the track, the other from arm's length ... close enough to add ours in pencil ...

Somebody there before first light and after the last window closes ... presence in the stalls the way heat is in a body ...

Nobody wrote the arrangement down ... He gets fifteen minutes ... And the fifteen arrives on time every day ... kept in the same heads that never once forgot a feeding ...

She has watched races from here, on her feet, in rooms that were not hers, with the vacuum off for the two minutes of it ... The cord still in the wall the whole race ... two minutes lifted off the shift, and the carpet none the wiser ...

A horse coming off the track on three legs is a horse the groom runs toward ... Everyone else's afternoon stops still at the rail ... theirs breaks into a run before the announcer finds a word for it ...

A horse standing in its stall on three legs, with a hind foot cocked and the weight off it, is resting, and the groom walks past ... The same three legs read twice ... and the walking past is the deeper fluency, knowing rest from wreck at one glance down a shed row ...

There is always somebody at the barn ... The racing keeps dark days on its calendar ... the feeding has never heard of one ...

Every barn kid on earth has a hand on a handle ... And the wood already smooth when their hands arrived ... every grip that learned the chore before them kept in the grain ...

Every spiritual stall is a LAMP THAT CANNOT BE SEEN ... Burning tonight behind dark windows ... and not an animal in the barn standing cold ...

He went to sleep with the track lit, and tonight he could not with it dark, and so he came down to see ... And the dark held still for the looking ... sound all the way through, which took the walk down to know ...

A horse can only dream lying flat ... The dream waits for the legs to lie down first ... it wants the whole animal or nothing ...

When the barn has gone quiet after the last race and one lamp is left on in the aisle ... The straw settles under everything that has gone flat ... and somewhere down the row a dream starts running under a closed eye, first race of the night card ...

She learns a track on a dark night the way she learns another pronoun when he's not talking ... Not an ankle turned on either ground ... the dark and the not-talking both walked sure-footed by now ...

A horse made out of fog, and then no horse ... And the whole shape breathing back into the wetland by morning ... more animal in it than most fields hold ...

Some things aren't in the ledger because they aren't the kind of thing a ledger is for ... lit from behind by a lamp that cannot be seen ... Kept anyway, every one of them, in the older set of books ... the ones with no columns, entered by that same lamp ...

It is the building also dreaming ... Never off its feet since the boards went up ... the dreaming done standing, over every dreamer that had to go flat first ...

Slowness is also thrilling ... it only needs the room to be quiet enough ... And the room here is a whole wetland with the volume of frogs ... quiet the way deep water is quiet, loud all night and holding still underneath ...

Again ... a race relived at the speed of memory ... Run once at thirty or so ... and slower with every rerun, the stretch alone now taking most of an evening ... And an evening coming when it holds mid-stride ... every hoof off the ground at once, and us at the rail of it till morning ...

Next ... is the slowest light in the loudest room ... Every bulb around it at full gallop ... and the minutes walking down to post on a held gait, refusing to break ...

Heaven is that down to earth high ... The kind reachable in barn boots ... low enough to stand in, all the sky the standing needs ... And the gate of it a stall latch ... opened all day long, nobody made to die first ...

Not one date on the whole reckoning ... TIME MEASURED BY WHAT CONTINUES ... the feedings counting off the days and the foalings counting off the years ...

On a race night the glass is a window on the wire ... Every face at it pressed toward one lit stripe of finish ... the whole pane spent on a single second ...

Under snow ... the track keeps its oval quiet, and we walk the row remembering where the rail runs ...

Swans pair for life, and so do the cranes, and the geese come in lines ... And over the backstretch they cross in their lines, while down here we harness two by two and call it a morning ...

A mark is its fastest winning mile, standing in the books after the horse is gone ... We read them, two minutes standing for a whole life, and somewhere the oats still rattle ...

Every breath a horse head shaped cloud ... We stand in that weather of them, buckets frozen over, and the sky keeps creating horse heads ... A second herd assembling over the first, and we count them twice, once warm and once well you know ... And the word we don't finish rises with the rest of the steam, polite about where the breaths go ... Up past the barn lights they go, herd after herd of mornings, as we get on with the frozen buckets ... We crack the ice with the mallet, water under it all along, and the horses drink the temperature ...

Living is a crazy wild night at the track ... said at the rail by a person who has returned for eleven years or so ... Fridays will teach that, and we lean on the same rail they lean on, believing every word of it ...

The cranes were over this place before the ice that made the sand ... We rake footing they crossed while the glacier lay down, and their lines still know the route ...

A creature time barely carves on ground where everything else is written and erased nightly ... The program reprints, the chalk washes off the board, and overhead the crane keeps its unamended wings ... Same feathers as before the sand, and under them we tear our tickets nightly, confetti for the long memory ...

The newest picture medium on earth was born at this sport's gait ... Cameras on a wager settled the trot midair, and every movie since is horses coming home ...

The most widely sung secular song on this planet is a harness-racing song ... Camptown ladies sing it, doo-dah, doo-dah ... Goin' to run all night, Goin' to run all day, Bet my money on a bob-tailed nag, Somebody bet on the gray ... Five miles of track in everybody's mouth and nobody remembers the bay ... We hum it mucking stalls, and the race runs unfinished a hundred and seventy years on, nag and gray still nose and nose ...

They left for winter and came back ... The entry is worn ... Same book, same line, and we take the entry every spring, form unbroken since the sand was ice ...

Scarcity that is real does not need staging ... The gate holds thirty-some nights a year and no more, and we never once painted the moon scarce ...

Private work meeting its first public wall ... Three decades in the drawers, and now the lamps come up on it where the bettors pass, unhurried at last ...

The sounds are a chest full of ... HEARTS ... SPADES ... DIAMONDS ... CLUBS ... beating ... We shuffle and the whole deck walks, four suits to the stride, red and black taking turns at the rail ...

The sound in a barn at this hour is hay being pulled from a net and ground, a bucket knocked, a hoof shifted in shavings ... We pause with the wheelbarrow and it all keeps going, jaw and bucket and hoof, the barn talking in its sleep ...

They are the first to speak, and they speak for a long time before there is light ... Nickers past the row before the switches, and we walk in late to a conversation the dark had already started ...

A child asleep through a horse race ... Grandstand roaring past the eighth pole, and against a shoulder the small breathing keeps its own program, all favorites ...

Then ... again ... the sound of eight horses eating, when it starts, is one sound ... One jaw at first, then the row falls in, and we stand in the middle of a single animal chewing ...

A sound like rain on a roof if the roof were made of something soft ... Hay-rain steady over the stalls, and we stop sweeping to stand under it, dry as bones and getting rained on ... A person hearing their own two-beat again, which is the sport's gait, which is the heart's ... We walk the shed row and the pacer's stride hangs level, lub-dub down the rail, and we keep the entry ...

Harness racing is the only racing whose sound is a heartbeat ... Gallop scatters to a tumble, but the pace comes down the stretch in twos, and every chest at the rail answers it ...

The bells on a trotting harness, worn because a sleigh in snow is nearly silent ... Silent runners in the bleached road, so the law hung music on the horse, and winter learned to hear itself coming ...

The sounds arrive before the birds ... Buckets and bolts and the first nickering whin in the dark, and the sparrows wake into a morning already made ...

The sound a very old record makes in the second before the music starts, when the ear can hear that the room the music was recorded in was a very large one ... The needle finds its groove and a vast room clears its throat, and we wait small inside it for the band ...

The piece gives each person back their own two-beat gait ... As they leave the wall walking, and somewhere past the parking lot each stride finds its old level pair again ...

The MARK and LINE have a slight tremble ... The pen crossed living ground, and we keep the tremble in, proof the hand was breathing when it wrote ...

The rail comes up out of it, the top rail first, a line ... Fog giving the track back one piece at a time, and we watch the line remember the whole oval ...

Thousands of fine parallel marks in the direction the tractor went, which is the direction the horses will go, and the next race is run across them ... The harrow writes its marks and the field signs over them in hooves, all night the same handwriting twice ...

The sand stays combed until the first horse jogs in the morning ... The grooming holds under the lamps, and one warm-up mile ends it, first signature on a clean page ...

A deer crossing from the wetland, a crane walking the turn at first light, is there for the crew to read when they come in, and the harrow made none of it ... Horses go out in an hour ... Until then the ground stays theirs ...

Here the writing is the animal ... stabled with the rest then fed at the same time, startled at things the barn never hears ...

The strongest seam in leatherwork is the one only a hand can make, and it is slower, and that is why it survives ... Every bridle in this barn came through somebody's lap, an awl and an evening at a time ...

An old harness on its peg still holds the shape of the horse that wore it ... Nobody hangs anything else on that peg, and nobody in the shed row ever said why ...

Every housekeeper has a fold, and the fold is the one place in the room where the hand did not quite agree to disappear ... Towels on the tack shelf fold to a hand's width ... The barn knows whose ...

Hooves write in lime ... Rain takes most of it by noon, and the afternoon set walks out and makes more ...

The wall is made from the writing ... Horses lean on it where the morning sun lands, the boards take that too, tails swat flies against it all July, and that goes into the grain with everything else ...

A groom stands at the stall door doing nothing ... LET THEM LOOK ... the horse comes over anyway, nobody hurries either one ... Don't pull on him ... Whoever said it first is long gone, and it still gets said every spring to somebody new ...

A lovely horse at rest will not lie down where it does not feel safe ... One flat out in the straw at midday ... The whole shed row drops to a whisper going past ...

A girl carries a fish with both arms because it's heavier than a horse lick ... The barn cats know before she's halfway down the row ... Not one of them looks at the horses ...

The race gets clocked to a fifth of a second, and the forty minutes of circles after it, never ...

For the first few steps the sound of the room is all around her, and then it is behind her ... At the barn end it's a bucket bath and a haynet ... Whoever leads her hums or doesn't ...

The card room is a long way behind her and going on ... There is nothing to buy at the end of this hallway ... Steam off her back in the cold and the paper on her hasn't changed a thing ...

The way to the light should be paved with things that can be checked ... A boy holds the shank and counts laps by the same corner post his father used ...

The walk goes on all afternoon either way, nobody's business but the horse's ...

The horse cools out the same on a stakes day as a Tuesday, and the hallway never learns the difference ... The rest of it is the walk ... Forty minutes of nothing to see, and every good barn on the grounds does it in full ...

Elders sit still, and everyone comes to them ... The oldest pony on the grounds stands flat-footed at the gap, and every green two-year-old gets walked past him twice ...

So that a pulse standing in front of it settles, gently and for free ... He's calmed forty springs of them without a sound, and the barn keeps him in cheap peppermints for it ... A still place to stand, dignity while they stand there ... Nobody young understands what the old pony is for, and nobody old explains it ...

Every step is the customer and receiver agreeing to arrive, a good groom walks level with the throatlatch, never ahead, and six feet hit the gravel in one rhythm ...

Somewhere past the last slot bank the ear lets go of the noise, and nobody ever marks the spot ...

Somebody new turns left at the gap, and the whole line just swings wide around them ...

In the morning the horses go around this oval the wrong way ... Come racing it all turns back around, the horses treat both directions as somebody else's idea ...

Right way means go ... Wrong way means what day is it ...

Holding still is the whole of fishing and about half of horsing ... The other half is knowing which mornings not to, and no one writes down which is which ...

Who gets married at a racetrack? ... People who met at one ... Ask around the grounds, that's most of the marriages here, and nobody finds it strange except the caterer ...

Pole, bucket, scale, sign, bird, hose, bag, girl, fish ... Nine things standing in plain sight of each other on a Wednesday ...

The house's biggest night was a bet the house lost and everybody went home happy, the payout took two tellers and a supervisor, and the line behind clapped every bundle across the counter ...

Our canvas is clear sky light blue over the track and we're happy to be here ... You betcha ... Forty degrees and sunny gets called a beauty out here, and nobody's wrong about it ...

Do you remember the shovel we used to pick up horse manure? ... Still leaning in the same corner it always did, and every new kid gets handed it the first morning ...

This is the only casino we've been in where we could tell it was night ... The windows say so all evening, and the last race lets out under actual stars ... The card room can keep its forever noon, the barns run on the sun like always ...

People think the silks are for them ... They are ... Nobody asked us ... The horses would run naked as the day they were born, and the rail would still be three deep ... The colors get pressed and hung the night before anyway, the way things get done for a wedding ...

Release carried with dignity ... VICTORY'S OWN GESTURE ... is letting go ... The win photo takes one minute, and the shank comes off in the paddock same as any other night ... The flowers wilt in a bucket by the tack room door, and the horse gets his bath in the dark ...

The lost-and-found drawer runs deep in reading glasses, and the window envelope stays a window envelope ...

They wrote the time the one broke ... not the race time, the time of night ... Whoever kept that clock knew which number the barn would want to remember ... The chart keeps the other one ...
