That horse is walking to the van in his own clothes ... Blanket, halter, shipping wraps, everything on that walk is his, and the van waits till he's loaded easy ... The stall gets stripped by supper ... The name stays on the webbing till somebody needs the webbing ...

A race is the interval between a line letting go and a line arriving ... The gate crew owns the first line, the wire owns the second, and everything between belongs to nobody ... Two minutes nobody governs on a property that counts everything ... The crowd knows it in their feet ...

Walk a compass down the shed row and the needle makes the rounds ... Iron at all four corners of every horse, so the one instrument that holds its point through storm and grandstand goes swinging stall to stall on the way by ... Faithful to a single north everywhere on earth but here ... This aisle is a barn full of norths, and the needle, honest about it, greets every one ...

The barn owns one love song, a single note long ... The low nicker out of the chest, kept for the known few, sung at a familiar step on the gravel before the face ever shows ... Same verse before first light, same verse after the last race, answered in grain and a hand ...

Kindness here is measured in slack ... The good groom leads on a sagging line, a hand's width of nothing between fist and halter, and a thousand pounds keeps the bargain as though the rope were law ... A tight shank anywhere down the row draws the second look ... the loose ones pass unremarked, which is how a barn says grace ...

Trust that love will never die ... it paces, at a wondrous rate, the speed of breathing ... The night check goes by ear ... Stall to stall in the dark, no switches thrown, and a horse asleep on its feet draws about twelve breaths a minute ... Somebody stands at every door till the count arrives, thirty years of rounds and never a page to them ...

We search half the earth to discover what is there in our soul ... A claiming horse sees more country than most of the crowd ever will ... State to state a race at a time, half the map in one set of legs ... The van door drops at another strange barn, and the first business is a long drink and a roll in the fresh straw ... The old ones price that, a hundred dollars more every time a horse rolls clear over ...

1. THE PHYSICAL PROPERTY AND ARCHITECTURE

Master plan and architects. The racetrack complex was master-planned by LSE Architects of Minneapolis for Southwest Casino & Hotel Corporation. LSE's own project description reads: "Running Aces Harness Track was master planned for Southwest Casino & Hotel Corporation and includes a state-of-the-art 5/8 mile harness racing track with indoor and outdoor seating, barns with 300 stalls, paddock, and casino building. It also houses a 25 table full service poker room." LSE is a full-service Minneapolis firm specializing in casino complexes (it also designed Southland Park Gaming & Racing).

The track surface. Running Aces races on a 5/8-mile (0.625-mile) harness oval surfaced with agricultural lime and stone dust, billed as one of the newest harness tracks in the United States. Because it lacks an enclosed, climate-controlled grandstand, racing can only be held in summer; MinnPost reported that rain or excessive late-afternoon heat in the Twin Cities often depresses attendance.

No permanent grandstand. The original master plan called for a traditional grandstand, but co-owners Southwest Casino and MTR Gaming "ran out of money before it could be built" (former Racing Commission figure Dick Derus, via MinnPost). The track opened with small bleachers on a grassy area next to the apron; general manager Bob Farinella said they were removed after the first year because the track's liquor license limited beer sales to the apron. Seating is therefore benches on the apron. MinnPost's 2012 reporting noted racing insiders long believed the original co-owners — Southwest Casino (Minnesota) and MTR Gaming Group (West Virginia) — were primarily interested in slot machines ("racino") and used harness racing to establish a business presence.

The casino / card-club building. Per the April 2008 opening (SEC filing by Southwest Casino Corporation), the card room was planned with 50 gaming tables for non-banked casino card games and live poker, opened after completing the state-required 50 days of live racing. The building holds a Las Vegas-style card room, a poker room, a Race Book/simulcast area with a view of the finish line, and the "Trout Air Tavern" restaurant with a finish-line view. Running Aces bans smoking and has floor-to-ceiling/large windows — an unusual, deliberate departure from the "gaudy themes and dark interiors" of older casino design. [Verify current card-room specifics — 50-plus tables, five VIP rooms, poker-room high ceilings, 80-inch Race Book screens, "The Pocket," "Finish Line Plaza," outdoor food tent, tote/odds displays, ceiling heights, and any hallway/gallery spaces — with the client in person; these appear in the commission brief but could not all be independently sourced.]

The hotel (opened spring 2020). The five-story, 116-room hotel (the Star Tribune reported 115; trade press and Wyndham list 116) opened as a Trademark Collection by Wyndham property, at a cost of about $17 million (Star Tribune) to $18 million+ (CEO Taro Ito, via Twin Cities Business). It broke ground November 15, 2018.
- Architect: Cuningham Group (Minneapolis; frequently misspelled "Cunningham"). General contractor: Truline Construction. Multiple 2018–2020 sources (Twin Cities Business, Hospitality Net, Press Publications, Forest Lake Times/hometownsource) name both. Chairman Peter Frank said: "Rather than just use a cookie-cutter architecture, and have a regular 'hotel 6,' this will be a unique boutique hotel." A later (2022) hometownsource article links Truline Construction and Truman Howell Architects as affiliates of Apollo Development, noting Apollo "recently finished construction of the Running Aces Hotel" — so Truman Howell may have produced production drawings while Cuningham held the design concept. J-Berd Mechanical Contractors did the MEP/fire work.
- No separate interior-design firm was found; the "modern Scandinavian design with the 'Up North' feel of your get-away cabin" theme was executed by Cuningham Group working directly with Ito, who invoked the Scandinavian concept of hygge ("a state of coziness"): "We worked very hard to create this feeling … of a north cabin, more Scandinavian modern"; "the architects created tall ceilings and wide corridors and wood accents for a more airy and comfortable feel."
- Public art / interior. The hotel's official language: "Expansive corridors, high ceilings and the largest rooms in the category." Rooms have 65-inch flat-screens and Minnesota artwork. Minneapolis muralist Adam Turman created a large mural in the breakfast area/lobby (produced as a ~380"×90" vinyl wall mural via Haus of Print); Ito said it "symbolizes everything Running Aces has to offer: historic Trout Air, card tables and gambling, harness racing and an outdoors vibe with the Minneapolis skyline in the distance." A signature lobby piece is a glass-covered sand-drawing coffee table in the front lounge, described as Ito's personal highlight, plus a fireplace lobby. No design awards or architecture-press features were found; OTA "Art Deco" labels are erroneous auto-tags.
- Meeting/event space is three rooms named Jack, Queen and King; outside the banquet space is a private patio and fire pit with a full track view. It is the only smoke-free hotel of its kind in Minnesota, has an enclosed walkway to the casino, an indoor heated pool, hot tub, 24/7 gym, business center, guest laundry, room service, and a hotel bar. The hotel is a separate business/ownership from the casino under the same parent (operator GrandStay Hospitality LLC; operating entity RAHP LLC).

Barns, paddock, trout pond. The complex has barns (150 stalls in use, 300 planned per LSE), a paddock, and the Trout Air trout pond — a bubbler-aerated pond kept near 55°F, stocked a few times weekly from a local trout farm, with no catch-and-release (fish paid for by the pound; $10/pound in 2017). The experience is run in partnership with local Scouting (District F / Many Rivers Scouting, serving 1,110+ Scouts). Options in 2017: $2 pole rental, $3 on-site cleaning for take-home, $9 for chefs to clean and cook. Trout Air Tavern is the pond-to-plate restaurant (signature smoked trout dip, baked rainbow trout; open 24/7). The commission brief cites ceremonies of up to 120 guests at the pond.

Seasonal & outdoor amenities. In winter, Epic Kart Racing runs an ice-kart track (studded tires on ice; drivers 12+ and 4'10"+; Epic began ice karting in 2022 and rotates locations including Stillwater, Center City and Running Aces). "The Outskirts" is a new outdoor entertainment area in the main parking lot run with Hay Creek Entertainment — drive-in movies, concerts (the "Rocking the Racetrack" series), games and events. The property has about 1,200 parking spots and EV charging (per commission brief).

2. THE PATRON — TARO ITO AND OWNERSHIP

Taro Ito, President/CEO. Ito holds a BS in Investment Finance from California State University-Northridge (1979–1984). His career is in card-room operations: The Bicycle Club Casino (Bell Gardens — at the time billed as the largest table-games casino in the world) and Bay 101 (San Jose) as Vice President; then Vice President and COO of Hollywood Park Casino (Los Angeles); and consulting via iPursuit Consulting, Inc. / MMRC, Inc. He was announced as Running Aces President/CEO November 20, 2014, with "more than 25 years of direct experience in card room operations." At hiring, Chairman Peter Frank cited the property's 600 employees.

Ito's public voice and philosophy.
- Underdog identity (Star Tribune, 2020): "We were like the redheaded stepchild. Canterbury was the golden boy. And I think we had a little inferiority complex." "Nobody knew this place and they had been open for years." "We really don't even know what our ceiling is. We're still trying to find that."
- Recession-era struggle (Press Publications, 2015): "Opening during the second-largest economic meltdown (in the U.S.) was devastating to the property … The return on investment was certainly not there" (the card room's biggest jackpot had been $226,000 the prior year).
- Trout Air nostalgia (2015): "The place where thousands of Minnesotans once took their kids fishing — it was a fascinating idea to me… we decided Trout Air was far too important a landmark in Minnesota's history to pass up the opportunity, so went forward with a plan to recreate the nostalgia." And: "In 1964, Harmon Killebrew and Tony Oliva were hitting homers left and right — and Trout Air was born in Columbus, MN… Just as the Twins and Trout Air helped define Minnesota summers in the '60s, '70s and beyond, we know that Trout Air Tavern will define Minnesota summers for years to come… trout is king."
- Hotel design: hygge, tall ceilings, wide corridors, wood accents; "luxury and comfort."
- COVID closure (the hotel opened, then closed after five days): "Naturally we're disappointed, but we're not discouraged. In the five days we were open, we got a lot of positive feedback." "I think the hotel is going to be one bright spot in the whole sort of not very happy picture."
- RICO suit: "Obviously it's disappointing because we feel very strongly about our case. We'll take this to the Supreme Court if we have to." On competition: "If they're trying to put me out of business, I got to fight for my business. Why wouldn't anybody do that?"
- HHR (2024): historical horse racing "will enhance the tracks' self-sufficiency, create new jobs and produce additional revenue for state and local governments without burdening taxpayers."

Ownership chain. Founders were Southwest Casino & Hotel Corp. (Minneapolis, 50% membership interest at opening) and MTR Gaming Group (West Virginia). Running Aces opened April 11, 2008. After a $4M first-year loss, the venue defaulted on a $41.7 million loan from Black Diamond, which "eventually emerged as majority owner" of North Metro Harness Initiative, LLC — by 2012 the Star Tribune reported Black Diamond "already owns 56 percent of Running Aces." Peter Frank is chairman. (Confirm Black Diamond's current stake with the client.)

Key personnel. Racing Director Steve Carpenito (listed as "Director of Marketing" in some 2025 MRC minutes — verify title); track announcer Darin Gagne; track handicapper Steve Lake; spokesman/Director of Marketing Aaron Bedessem; CFO Tracie/Tracy Wilson; General Counsel Evan Nelson. For 2026, Frank Drucker — the on-air personality at New York's Yonkers Raceway for 25 years and a former New York Post copy editor/writer — joined the handicapping team, offering a free nightly tip sheet.

3. HISTORY OF THE GROUND

Trout-Air / Trout Air. The land was originally Preiner-family farmland; finding the lowland unsuitable for farming, the Preiners ran a construction company on it for 25 years, then started a fish farm. Sources conflict on the start year:
- 1964 — Anoka County Historical Society (via Press Publications) and Running Aces' own marketing ("In 1964, the Trout Air trout farm was born").
- mid-1960s/1965 — hometownsource Anoka County history column; one Minnesota Uncorked line says "1965-1998."
- 1968 — Jesse Preiner's own trout businesses (The Trout Monger): "In 1968, his family established Trout Air."

Bernard Preiner, a construction contractor, dug ponds and stocked them with rainbow trout to sell to restaurants; within a few years the public was invited to catch their own. The site had 38 man-made ponds (Press Publications/ACHS, hometownsource; The Trout Monger says "20+"). A Trout-Haus restaurant was added by the late 1970s (seated 300+; hosted company picnics/senior events of 3,000–5,000). A giant fiberglass fish and an I-35 billboard were landmarks; Preiner sons ran a skydiving club on the land. Trout Air closed in 1999 (some sources say 1998 / "late 1990s"); the property later became partly a retirement community before Running Aces.

Trout-Air Amphitheater (1980s–90s). By the 1980s the site added an outdoor grassy amphitheater (no fixed seating; attendees sat on the lawn) and hosted nationally known acts: per Press Publications, Iron Maiden, Ted Nugent, the Allman Brothers, the Marshall Tucker Band, Heart, Megadeth, Charlie Daniels and Korn. Setlist.fm and Concert Archives catalog the venue as "Trout-Air Amphitheater, Columbus, MN" (setlist.fm documents an Iron Maiden World Slavery Tour date of June 23, 1985). A Charlie Daniels Band show in 1985 is said to have drawn roughly 18,000 — but that figure appears only on a social-media music page (Metal Nexus) and could not be verified against a primary source; treat it as unconfirmed.

Licensing and modern timeline. North Metro Harness Initiative holds a Minnesota Racing Commission Class A license. Opened April 11, 2008 (Valentine Hotspur won the inaugural race); $4M loss first year, ~10% of staff laid off after five months; 2010 foreclosure threat; 2013 license revocation and MRC-ordered $400,000 purse-shortfall repayment; 2015 rebrand as an "entertainment center"; 2016 trout pond reopened; 2017 MRC approval of Interblock dealer-assist electronic table games; 2020 hotel; 2023 amended plan of operation (adding a dealer table and 11 player stations of electronic table games).

4. LEGAL AND POLITICAL LANDSCAPE (CURRENT)

Minnesota Supreme Court, dealer-assist electronic table games — A23-1738 (decided January 21, 2026). The Court was evenly divided 3–3 (Justice Theodora Gaitas recused), which by rule left the Court of Appeals ruling standing — a win for Running Aces. The decision permits the track to add one dealer table and 11 player stations of electronic table games. The games involve a live dealer shuffling physical cards, with images transmitted to player touch-screen stations where patrons make game decisions (distinguished from the "fully automated" game the courts had previously deemed a "gambling device"). The Shakopee Mdewakanton Sioux Community had challenged the MRC's approval of the 2023 amended plan of operation as unlawful gambling expansion and an encroachment on tribal exclusivity; it maintained, "We believe these games are a clear violation of Minnesota law." The Court held the Community had standing but upheld the MRC. The games were first approved by the MRC in 2017, and the amended floor plan does not exceed the statutory 80-table cap.

Federal RICO suit against tribal casino executives. Running Aces filed a federal RICO suit April 16, 2024 (amended May 2024 to add Mystic Lake and Little Six), naming 39 current/former executives and employees at Mystic Lake, Little Six, Grand Casino Hinckley, Grand Casino Mille Lacs and Treasure Island Resort & Casino. It alleged the casinos offer Class III games (Three Card Poker, Ultimate Texas Hold 'Em, Mississippi Stud, baccarat) not covered by the 1989 tribal-state compacts under IGRA, giving them an unfair advantage. Chief Judge Patrick Schiltz dismissed it without prejudice March 11, 2025, ruling the individual employee-defendants could not adequately represent the tribes' interests (a sovereign-immunity workaround problem): imposing "enormous financial liability on the individuals who operate (the) PIIC and (Mille Lacs) casinos — even the threat of such liability — is likely to severely hamper those Tribes' ability to operate their casinos, which are their economic lifeblood." Running Aces' motion to amend was denied in August 2025, ending the suit's federal-court journey for now; Ito said he was "surprised," called it "procedural," and threatened to take it "to the Supreme Court." The SMSC called the suit a "desperate stunt to attack the good reputation of tribes and tribal gaming," stating, "All gaming conducted at Mystic Lake and Little Six Casinos complies with tribal law, federal law, and the gaming compact that was executed in 1989." (One Tribal Business News line erroneously dates the case's start to April 2022; contemporaneous reporting confirms spring 2024. No evidence of a refiling as of September 2026 was found — verify.)

Historical horse racing (HHR) and the 2024 ban. On April 1, 2024, the MRC voted 5–1 to authorize HHR/ADW terminals (each track could install up to 500 beginning May 21). Within days, Rep. Zack Stephenson (DFL-Coon Rapids) introduced a bill to ban HHR, calling the MRC's move "unlawful." The Legislature passed the ban — House 71–58, Senate 36–25 — and, effective May 25, 2024 (HF2300/SF2219/Ch. 119, sponsored by Stephenson and Sen. Matt Klein), it became illegal for Canterbury Park and Running Aces to have HHR machines, and the MRC was barred from expanding gambling via rulemaking beyond horse racing and authorized card games. The Shakopee Mdewakanton Sioux Community also litigated the HHR approval. CFO Tracie Wilson called the direct-subsidy figures floated in sports-betting bills — $625,000 (House) to $3 million (Senate) annually for the tracks to share — "inadequate," and called HHR a "lifeline"; GLI had certified the product "100% parimutuel."

Sports betting, 2025 and 2026. Both sessions ended without a sports-betting bill. The core impasse is between Minnesota's 11 tribal nations (19 casinos; exclusive Class III gaming rights via the 1989 compacts, represented by MIGA) and the two tracks over whether tracks get retail sportsbooks or a revenue share. In 2026, Sen. Nick Frentz's SF 4139 (tribal-run mobile sportsbooks, ~22% tax) cleared its first committee but stalled after a March procedural vote and was never revived before the session adjourned (~May 18, 2026). The 2026 session also advanced a bill to classify prediction markets (Kalshi, Polymarket) as gambling.

Regulatory context. Minnesota card clubs operate under 18-and-over rules; the 80-table statutory limit applies; simulcast and ADW are MRC-regulated; parimutuel rules date to 1982.

5. THE RACING PROGRAM

2026 meet (the 19th). Live racing runs Sunday, May 17 through Saturday, September 19, 55 nights, three nights a week — Tuesday 7 p.m., Thursday and Sunday 6 p.m. (CDT). The final week is adjusted so Thursday Sept. 17 is dark ahead of the special Friday Sept. 18 and Saturday Sept. 19 Minnesota Night of Champions cards. The stable area opens Kentucky Derby Day, May 2; first qualifier May 7. Total purses are projected to top $6 million (≈$110,000/night average), as in the prior four seasons. Stall applications go to Racing Director Steve Carpenito (due March 16).

Night of Champions and stakes. Closing night showcases the Minnesota-sired program; in 2025 a record-equaling single-card total of $780,000 was distributed. Signature races: the Dan Patch (Free-For-All pacers), the Ron Banks Memorial (Fillies & Mares Free-For-All pacers), and the Steve Wiseman Memorial (Free-For-All trotters). The Minnesota Breeders' Fund and Minnesota-sired program underpin purses.

Star horse. Over The Moon For You ("Overthemoonforyou," barn name "Lilah"), a four-year-old pacing mare by Minnesota stallion Karpathian Kid, owned by QKS Racing/Quentin Schneider and driven by Aaron Lehman, was repeatedly in 2025 called the winningest Standardbred in North America — reaching 15 wins in 19 races (e.g., her 14th win of the season on June 12, 2025 in 1:54.1).

2026 driving colony is expected to include defending champion Brady Jenson, plus Nick Roland, Dean Magee, Rick Magee, Mooney Svendsen, Luke Plano, and newcomer/perennial Illinois champion Casey Leonard; trainers include Justin Anfinson, Dan Roland, Jenni King, Quentin Schneider, Nathalie Tremblay, Adam Hauser, and Justin Gould. Horses ship in from Minnesota, Iowa and Wisconsin. (The 250+-horses-per-season figure and specific trotter/pacer track records in the brief should be verified with the client/MRC.)

Aftercare. Running Aces created the North Metro Racehorse & Community Foundation (a 501(c)(3)), which partners with This Old Horse, Inc. (founded 2012; accredited by the Global Federation of Animal Sanctuaries and the Thoroughbred Aftercare Alliance; by 2026 had served 1,000+ horses of 60 breeds and cares for 200+ horses across Minnesota/Wisconsin), Minnesota Harness Racing, Inc., and the Racehorse Reimagined collaboration (led by This Old Horse; includes the Hugo-based program of Rebecca Turner). The MRC's Racehorse Aftercare Committee disburses grants — e.g., in 2025 it recommended $110,000 to This Old Horse (46 Thoroughbreds, 9 Standardbreds). The MRC's mandate explicitly includes "the safety and welfare of the human and equine participants."

Operational note (2025). Per MRC minutes, the 2025 meet ran 36 fewer races than 2024 at one point (three weather cancellations); per-race live handle was up 7%, export handle down 2%, against a national Standardbred handle down 10%. Running Aces/MRC also referenced a 2025 cyber incident that they said left core functions unaffected.

6. THE CASINO AND THE GAMBLING PSYCHOLOGY OF THIS HOUSE

No slot machines. Running Aces is a pari-mutuel track and a card club with no slot machines — its entire legal identity rests on parimutuel wagering and non-banked card games ("no commission or ante"). This is the crux of the tribal dispute: tribes hold exclusive rights to Class III slots/video games of chance.

Games offered: Blackjack, $1 Blackjack, Free Bet Blackjack, Face Up Pai Gow, EZ Baccarat, Mississippi Stud, Ultimate Texas Hold'em, Three- and Four-Card Poker, Aces Live Five-Card Draw (dealer-assist multi-deck, 50¢/hand on a touch-screen terminal), stadium gaming (individual terminals for 5-card draw and blackjack, 50¢ minimums), and a poker room (24/7 live tables; No-Limit Hold'em and Pot-Limit Omaha; spread-limit games). Blackjack side bets include TriLux, Lucky Lucky, Pot of Gold, and Blazing 7s progressive.

Poker tournaments. Weekly tournaments range from a free Monday tournament ($2,000 added) up to $200 buy-in Double Stacks; the property hosts MSPT (Mid-States Poker Tour) series (e.g., "MSPT '26 Winter Poker Classic"). The commission brief cites buy-ins up to $1,100 and prize pools $200,000+ for signature events. (As of mid-2026 the site indicated tournaments were "resuming in August 2026" after a pause — confirm current poker-room status.)

Aces Rewards is the free loyalty club (comps on food/beverage, merchandise, events, promotions, tournaments; 150 tier points = a $10 meal offer).

24/7, 18-and-over. Open 24/7, admitting patrons 18 and over (Minnesota card-club rule) — younger than tribal casinos' policies, which reviewers frequently note produces a young crowd.

Economic impact. Running Aces is "one of the largest employers and taxpayers" in Anoka County (Mayor Preiner, Star Tribune), with 600 employees cited in 2014. Card-club revenue is reported to the MRC and funds purses (a record $5M purse in 2019; $6M+ projected 2026). (Precise recent card-club revenue and pari-mutuel handle are in MRC packets — verify exact figures.)

7. THE ECOLOGY AND THE LAND

Geology. The site sits on the Anoka Sand Plain, a broad glacial-outwash sand plain — the very reason the Preiner lowland was too wet to farm but ideal for spring-fed trout ponds.

Carlos Avery Wildlife Management Area (adjacent). Per the Minnesota DNR's Carlos Avery WMA Master Plan 2025–2034 (implemented Dec. 31, 2024), it "is the largest WMA in the Twin Cities Metropolitan Area and provides about 25,000 acres of fish and wildlife habitat," including "two Wildlife Sanctuaries totaling 4,050 acres," across Anoka and Chisago counties. History: the Crex Carpet Company (originally American Grass Twine Company) bought 8,000+ acres of marsh to harvest wire-grass (Carex lasiocarpa) for grass rugs (c. 1895–1930; the St. Paul factory employed ~900 people in 1903). After tax forfeiture, the Minnesota Department of Conservation bought 8,478.73 acres from Crex in 1933 for a wildlife refuge, named for Carlos Avery (1868–1930), Minnesota's first conservation commissioner. The WPA built the game farm (construction began January 1936; dedicated October 16, 1938, ~9,000 acres; 11 buildings). Programs: bobwhite quail and chukar partridge early on; ring-necked pheasants (1947–1981); and later restoration of the Canada goose and the trumpeter swan. The WMA supports sandhill cranes, trumpeter swans, Blanding's turtles, hognose snakes, bald eagles and many rare species. The track itself rings a restored wetland; reviewers note "the racetrack circles a beautiful wetland." (The specific 1986 Alaska trumpeter-swan-egg detail and current rare-species counts should be confirmed via the DNR master plan/MNHS.)

8. THE VISITOR EXPERIENCE AND EMOTIONAL REGISTER

Synthesizing Google/Yelp/TripAdvisor/Facebook/Reddit and harness/poker forums, the recurring themes:

What people love: cleanliness and the smoke-free environment (rare and prized among casinos); friendly dealers; low-stakes tables ($1 blackjack, 50¢ minimums, "Tuesday dollar nights"); the tall ceilings and airy, window-lit rooms; track-view hotel rooms ("Racetrack view was neat… great to be able to tune in the live race feed on the TV. Hotel overall felt very clean with nice spaces" — TripAdvisor); free-admission, family-friendly racing (blankets/lawn chairs, races ~25 minutes apart); and the novelty of the Trout Air fishing experience.

What people dislike: the young (18+) crowd and attendant rowdiness; prices/promotions seen as inconsistent; the hotel breakfast ("subpar… toaster was also broken, no waffle maker… RA can do better here" — TripAdvisor); and occasional service/management-responsiveness complaints. (Yelp has posted a consumer alert on the business page re: review activity.)

Who uses it, and how: families (racing, trout pond, drive-in movies), low-stakes and social card players, serious poker players (tournaments/MSPT), horsemen (the backside community), hotel/event guests (weddings, conventions in Jack/Queen/King rooms), and locals seeking a night out.

Sensory environment: the wetland-ringed oval under night-racing lights; the announcer's call (Darin Gagne) building to the crowd at the wire; the sulkies and Standardbreds; the smell of the trout tavern and outdoor food; the low hum of a windowed, daylight-friendly card room rather than a dark, windowless casino.

Marketing voice: "Stay, Play & Party"; "the action is fast, the vibes are high, and the nights are anything but ordinary"; "Smoke-Free, Family Friendly, Open 24/7." It positions itself as the north metro's untapped entertainment destination and a "fun getaway" with a track view.

Charity/community: the Concert for a Cause / Invisible Wounds Project, Freedom Fest (the biggest event, held ~a week after Night of Champions, with the stage set on the track), charity race nights, Second Harvest food-donation poker events, and the Minnesota State Fair Parade of Breeds (This Old Horse brings retired racehorses). (Verify Invisible Wounds Project/Concert for a Cause specifics with the client.)

9. THE AESTHETIC CONTEXT

Existing art and design. The dominant existing art statement is Adam Turman's lobby/breakfast mural — a graphic, screen-print-style Minnesota montage fusing Trout Air, cards, harness racing, and the Minneapolis skyline (the property's own visual thesis of "everything Running Aces has to offer"). Rooms carry Turman prints and other Minnesota artists' work; the lobby has a glass-topped sand-drawing table and a fireplace. The hotel's vocabulary is "modern Scandinavian" with an "Up North cabin" feel — hygge, wood accents, tall ceilings, "expansive corridors," warm light — executed by Cuningham Group with Ito. The casino's aesthetic signature is daylight: floor-to-ceiling windows and a smoke-free, brighter room, a deliberate anti-"dark casino" stance.

Brand identity. Wordmark "Running Aces"; the "Aces" playing-card motif (Jack/Queen/King banquet rooms; "Aces Rewards"; "Aces Live"); harness-racing and Up-North/outdoors imagery; the trout as a recurring emblem ("trout is king"). Recent marketing leans on high-energy nightlife language ("Stay, Play & Party," "the vibes are high"). (Request the exact logo colors/typography and brand guidelines from the client to color-match a backlit piece.)

Comparable installations. Racino/racetrack art tends toward large equine photography/murals, finish-line imagery, and heritage-timeline walls; Canterbury Park (the in-state peer) is the obvious comparison. Treat Turman's mural as the incumbent visual reference to either harmonize with or deliberately counterpoint.

Photo/video presence. Running Aces maintains an active Facebook (22,900+ likes), a YouTube live-racing channel (Darin Gagne), and heavy event photography; visitors describe the grounds as "fantastically curated," with a lush lawn and the wetland-ringed oval as the signature exterior image.

10. WHAT IS NEW OR CHANGED SINCE AUGUST 2025

- Jan. 21, 2026: Minnesota Supreme Court's 3–3 deadlock (A23-1738) leaves the dealer-assist electronic table games standing — a significant legal win.
- 2026 racing dates announced Nov. 24, 2025 (May 17–Sept. 19; 55 nights; $6M+ purses); stall applications opened mid-January 2026.
- Frank Drucker (ex-Yonkers) added to the handicapping team for 2026 (announced ~May 8, 2026).
- Sports betting failed again in the 2026 session (SF 4139 stalled ~March; session adjourned ~May 18, 2026); a prediction-market bill advanced.
- Poker tournaments resuming August 2026 after a pause (per runaces.com), suggesting a card-room reset; a 2025 cyber incident was referenced in MRC minutes (core functions said unaffected).
- "The Outskirts" outdoor entertainment area (Hay Creek Entertainment) and the "Rocking the Racetrack" concert series are the newest amenities; ice-kart racing continues seasonally.
- This Old Horse surpassed 1,000 horses served in 2026.
- (No ownership or executive changes, closures, or major incidents beyond the above were found for Aug 2025–Sept 2026; ask the client for the very latest.)

FINAL SECTION

(a) Factual discrepancies found between sources
1. Trout Air start year: 1964 (ACHS, Running Aces marketing) vs. mid-1960s/1965 (hometownsource column, one Minnesota Uncorked line) vs. 1968 (Jesse Preiner / The Trout Monger). Running Aces officially uses 1964.
2. Trout Air closure: 1999 (Running Aces, Press Publications) vs. 1998 ("1965-1998," Minnesota Uncorked) vs. "late 1990s."
3. Number of Trout Air ponds: 38 (Press Publications/ACHS, hometownsource) vs. "20+" (The Trout Monger).
4. Hotel room count: 116 (trade press, Wyndham, TripAdvisor, 2018 groundbreaking) vs. 115 (Star Tribune). Five floors is consistent.
5. Hotel cost: $17 million (Star Tribune) vs. $18 million+ (Ito/TCB).
6. Hotel architect attribution: Cuningham Group (contemporaneous 2018–2020 press) with a later, conflicting reference to Truman Howell Architects as an Apollo/Truline affiliate that "finished construction."
7. Grandstand history: original plans called for a traditional grandstand (never built for lack of money); first-year grass bleachers removed due to liquor-license limits — consistent across MinnPost but worth in-person confirmation.
8. Stall counts: 300 stalls (LSE master plan / planned) vs. 150 in use (commission brief).
9. RICO filing date: most press says spring 2024 (filed April 16, 2024; amended May 2024); one Tribal Business News line erroneously says "began in April 2022." April 2024 is correct.
10. CFO name spelling: "Tracie Wilson" vs. "Tracy Wilson." Carpenito's title: "Racing Director" vs. "Director of Marketing" in some MRC minutes.
11. Columbus mayor: Star Tribune 2020 text names Jesse Preiner as mayor; a 2020 photo caption references "Mayor Jesse Sandell." Verify current mayor.
12. Carlos Avery WMA acreage & sanctuary: ~23,000–25,000 acres across sources; the DNR's own 2025–2034 master plan says ~25,000 acres with 4,050 acres of sanctuary (not the ~4,500 seen elsewhere).
13. Charlie Daniels 1985 attendance (~18,000): social-media figure only; unverified. Setlist.fm documents a June 23, 1985 Iron Maiden date at the venue.

(b) The ten most surprising or artistically potent facts
1. The ground remembers water, not horses: the site was a wet lowland "unsuitable for farming," which is exactly why it became a trout farm with 38 spring-fed ponds — and the track's oval today rings a restored wetland. Water is the land's oldest truth.
2. Trout is the property's secret totem — Ito literally says "trout is king," and the resurrected pond (bubbler-aerated, kept at 55°F) helped turn the failing track's fortunes.
3. A grassy field here once held huge rock crowds — Iron Maiden, Megadeth, Heart, Ted Nugent, Charlie Daniels, Korn — with no fixed seating, just lawn.
4. The casino is deliberately full of daylight — floor-to-ceiling windows and smoke-free — an intentional inversion of the windowless, timeless casino. A backlit artwork speaks directly to a house that already worships light and windows.
5. The whole enterprise runs on the absence of slot machines — its identity, lawsuits, and modest 50-cent tables all flow from being the anti-slots house in a state where tribes own the slots.
6. The neighboring Carlos Avery WMA is a resurrection story: land stripped for wire-grass rugs became the cradle of Minnesota's trumpeter-swan and Canada-goose comebacks — recovery and second chances literally border the property.
7. The aftercare ethos: Running Aces created a foundation so its retired Standardbreds get "from hardship to healing, from career to retirement" — a redemption narrative that rhymes with the track's own near-death-and-recovery history.
9. Adam Turman's existing mural is already a montage of Trout Air + cards + horses + skyline — the property has literally told the artist what its self-image is; the new work can extend, deepen, or quietly counterpoint it.
10. The five-day hotel opening before COVID lockdown (March 2020) — a brand-new building that opened and immediately went dark for months — is a haunting image of hope suspended, then recovered.

(c) Open questions the artist should verify in person or with the client
- The actual wall and hallway: exact dimensions, ceiling height, viewing distance, sightlines from the "high, wide hallway," ambient light day vs. night, and power/electrical for backlighting. Confirm whether the wall is in the hotel, the enclosed walkway, or the casino (the hotel's "expansive corridors, high ceilings" are the likely context).
- Brand assets: official logo files, exact color palette (hex), and typography.
- The incumbent Turman mural's location and relationship to the commissioned wall — echo or diverge?
- Current card-room floor specifics: number of tables, five VIP rooms, poker-room ceiling height, the Race Book's 80-inch screens/finish-line view, "The Pocket," "Finish Line Plaza," tote/odds displays, materials, and existing lighting design.
- Current poker-room status (post-2025 pause; tournaments resuming August 2026) and any 2024–2026 renovation plans.
- Exact stall counts (150 in use vs. 300 planned) and whether barn expansion is underway.
- Precise recent financials (card-club revenue, pari-mutuel handle, purse totals) from MRC packets, and current employee count (600 was a 2014 figure).
- Track records for trotters and pacers, and whether 250+ horses are stabled per season.
- RICO status as of Sept 2026 (any refiling) and any pending 2026 litigation.
- Current mayor of Columbus and current ownership structure of North Metro Harness Initiative (Black Diamond's present stake).
- The 1986 Alaska trumpeter-swan-egg detail and current rare-species counts at Carlos Avery (confirm via DNR master plan/MNHS).

What the Quarry would weigh for the parked batch, ranked

1. The dealer-assist ruling — a machine legal only because a human hand shuffles inside it. 2. No grandstand; the hotel is the grandstand. 3. The house built for machines that got horses instead (ledger, master only). 4. Scouts running the pond; nobody may release. 5. Ice karts on the winter oval; the drive-in in the lot. 6. Over The Moon For You; This Old Horse; Darin Gagne. 7. Night of Champions, September 19 — the research trip's date.

<<<PAGEBREAK>>>

Running Aces Worldwide Kin

Banked September 3, 2026, from the Advanced Research report of the same date and the Quarry's eight-search first pass of September 2. Nothing in this file is ruled. The quarry closed September 1 at 165 stones; every find here rides in the parked batch and changes nothing unless the Mason rules it in. Each section ends with what it offers the wall. The full citation list lives in the chat artifact.

Summary

The closest kin to Running Aces worldwide are not slot-driven racinos but the cooperative, folk-rooted trotting cultures of Scandinavia and Japan (Solvalla/ATG; Ban'ei at Obihiro) and the daylit, no-house-bank California card rooms that formed the patron. The single most transferable lesson: slowness, communal pari-mutuel betting, and honest natural light are the opposite of the machine zone — which is what the two-panel wall embodies.

Precedents converge: a single-continuous-line horse in the Picasso/Matisse/ensō tradition as the Wish; a slow text-that-surfaces field in the Holzer / Xu Bing / asemic lineage as the Dream; backlit in the Turrell/Eliasson slow-light manner; terminating an axial corridor like a shrine sandō or nave-and-apse.

No true twin exists. Running Aces is a rare hybrid — harness racino + hotel + card club with no slots + smoke-free + nature-adjacent + revived local heritage. The room the work enters is empty, and now measurably so.

1. Racinos and harness-track complexes, North America

- Canterbury Park (Shakopee): the in-state peer; 24-hour card casino under the same no-slots regime; card club opened April 2000; ~140-acre Canterbury Commons development; a 10-year, $71M purse-enhancement and marketing agreement with the Shakopee Mdewakanton Sioux Community; publicly traded (CPHC); returns up to 5% of profits to the community.
- Prairie Meadows (Altoona, Iowa): the first US racino by referendum (voters 62–38, May 17, 1994; slots opened April 1, 1995 to 15,000); bankrupt November 1991 first; owned by Polk County, run by a non-profit board — the opposite pole: it is its slot floor.
- Yonkers Raceway / Empire City: founded 1899; crowds of 50,000 in the 1960s; VLT racino 2006; MGM since 2019, 5,000+ slots, racing five nights a week; fans may, with permission, scatter ashes on the track.
- The Meadowlands (NJ): new grandstand 2013, no racino (Atlantic City monopoly) — competes on racing and hospitality alone; the closest US philosophical parallel.
- Others defined by machine floors: Pocono Downs, Scioto Downs, Hoosier Park, Harrah's Philadelphia, Northfield Park, Saratoga Casino Hotel, Dover Downs, Rosecroft. Cal Expo runs harness racing on fairgrounds with no slots. Woodbine Mohawk Park (Ontario); Hippodrome de Trois-Rivières; Century Mile (Alberta, 2019).
- The Harness Racing Museum & Hall of Fame (Goshen, NY; est. 1951; a 1913 Tudor stable beside Goshen Historic Track, est. 1838, National Historic Landmark): the world's largest collection of Currier & Ives trotting prints; 1,700+ paintings/lithographs/sculptures; 19,300+ photographs; 75 sulkies; 20,000+ visitors a year; free admission; a 3-D sulky simulator and a call-and-judge-a-race booth.

What this offers the wall: the kin prove the point by contrast. Nearly every North American relative leans on the machine and the Friedman logic; the two most like Running Aces survive on atmosphere, live sport and sociability. The wall is the anti-slot-floor — slow where the machine is fast, silent where the floor is loud. The Currier & Ives tradition gives the single-line horse a lineage inside the sport.

2. The California card-room lineage

- The Bicycle Club (Bell Gardens), Bay 101 (San Jose), Hollywood Park Casino (Inglewood, on the demolished racetrack's ground, now SoFi Stadium): 24/7, daylit, no slots; the house never banks the game — a rotating player-dealer position, often filled by licensed third-party proposition players, takes the bank. California Penal Code §330 has banned banked games since 1872; Prop 1A (2000) gave tribes exclusivity and card rooms the player-dealer compromise. Proposed rules: the player-dealer must be seated at all times and the position offered to all players before every hand; each table posts that any player can take it.
- SB 549 (2024): a one-time right for tribes to sue card rooms. Seven tribes (Agua Caliente, Rincon, Barona, Pechanga, Sycuan, Viejas, Yocha Dehe, San Manuel) sued ~90 defendants in January 2025 — lead case Agua Caliente v. Parkwest Bicycle Casino. Dismissed October 10, 2025 (Judge Lauri Damrell) as preempted by IGRA; "I may be wrong ... I expect there will be an appeal." Appeal docketed. The Gardens Casino spent $9.1M on lobbying in 2023. Attorney General Bonta's regulations (rotate the player-dealer every 40 minutes; alter blackjack rules) drew industry backlash.
- The mirror: Minnesota's track sued the tribes and lost on sovereign immunity; California's tribes sued the card rooms and lost on federal preemption — the same federal law closing the courthouse from both sides.

What this offers the wall: the patron's world is faces across a table in real light, and the refusal of the solitary machine. The communal form's rule is a moving seat; the house never holds the bank; the wall never holds a stake. Honesty — an unbroken line with nothing behind it — is the card room's virtue made art.

3. Scandinavian and Nordic trav

- Solvalla (Stockholm, 1927, capacity 35,000): Elitloppet (Group One, since 1952, last Sunday of May), billed as one of Sweden's largest sporting events, crowds 50,000+; ATG and the national pool games V75/V86/V85 — the whole country plays the same slip on Saturday; free admission under 18; an on-site trotting museum. Bjerke (Oslo) under cooperative Norsk Rikstoto; Vermo (Helsinki) and the cold-blood Finnhorse; Charlottenlund (Copenhagen). Nordic tracks (Biri, Jarlsberg) are owned by the national trotting association.
- Ice trotting: Trøndelag's first race on frozen Jonsvatnet (1904) drew 4,000; White Turf at St. Moritz since 1907 runs flat and trotting races on the frozen lake, blades replacing sulky wheels; skijoring. Russia's Orlov Trotter and troika sleigh racing. A traveler on Nordic ice racing: a tradition grandfathers shared with grandsons, oval laps carved through the snow on a frozen lake.
- Nordic light: winter darkness, precious light — the aurora, the low sun, the blue hour; artificial light treated as scarce and sacred, warm, low, layered; hygge and friluftsliv.

What this offers the wall: the ice karts on the Anoka oval are the Nordic winter of the hotel's own name; the blades-for-wheels sulky is a Wish image nobody has drawn. The Dream glowing at the end of a dim corridor is a Nordic winter window — the warm light across snow that friluftsliv walks toward. The cold-blood trotter authorizes the one-line horse as an everyman figure.

4. Japanese kin

- Ban'ei at Obihiro (Hokkaido): the world's only draft-horse sled race; one-ton banba horses drag iron sleds up two sand ramps on a 200-meter straight; races 1.5–2 minutes, the Ban'ei Kinen 3–5 minutes ("the world's longest one-furlong race"); the winner is decided when the rear of the sled crosses the line; jockeys stop their horses to rest mid-race; spectators walk alongside; the sound of breathing and hoofbeats reaches the seats; the horses were "treated as part of the family"; designated Hokkaido Heritage 2004; nearly closed 2006, saved by Softbank (the Odds Park Cup, 2007, "an expression of gratitude"), then Rakuten and Sapporo; three of four tracks closed in 2007, Obihiro alone remains, municipally run; internet betting took sales from ¥10.4B (FY2012) to ~¥21.9B (FY2017), quadrupling 2007's by 2021; individuals sponsor and name races from ¥10,000 a share.
- JRA racecourses with public art; ema ("picture-horse") votive tablets as substitutes for live horses once offered to shrines (a black horse for rain, a white to stop it); Fujinomori Shrine (Kyoto) the racehorse shrine; the Ōsugi/Bareki shrine near Miho with horseshoe ema. Tsuribori urban fishing ponds; Zauo, where diners fish from a boat in a moat and the staff beat a taiko drum at every catch — nothing thrown back.
- Emakimono; kitsune/Inari and fox-on-snow; ma; Miyajima's counting LEDs; teamLab; Sugimoto's theaters; hatsuyume and takarabune.

What this offers the wall: slowness is thrilling — a race walked beside and decided by the back of the load; the ema's root is the horse, so the wish rack recovers the votive's oldest meaning; ma and the number going dark model the Dream's dissolve; and the drum at Zauo proves the pond's silence is a law, not an accident.

5. European and world harness racing

- Vincennes (1863; 40,000; Bois de Vincennes): the Prix d'Amérique (since February 1, 1920, thanking the US for WWI aid; last Sunday of January; 2,700 m; €1M+; 30,000+), winter nocturnes Tuesday and Friday, the trotteur français, PMU; the "Amérique Races" rebrand. Italy, the Netherlands (kortebaan/harddraverij and Dutch trotter painting), Germany, Malta (trotting dominant), Australia/New Zealand (Addington, "the Meadowlands of New Zealand," NZ Trotting Cup since 1904, Cup Week the region's biggest social event; Menangle; the Inter Dominion since 1936; race-calling as oral art).
- Everywhere, pari-mutuel/totalisator betting is communal — a shared pool, a public board — against the solitary machine.

What this offers the wall: the wall as a civic, shared object — a tote board of wishes; the floodlit nocturne as cousin to a glowing panel at the end of a dark hall; the totalisator as a found precedent for surfacing, changing text.

6. Art made for racetracks, casinos, corridors

- Racetracks: Keeneland, Churchill Downs (Barbaro, Secretariat bronzes), Saratoga, Ascot, ParisLongchamp (Perrault, 2018 — golden for the October leaves, tilted toward the finish, "transparent shelves" letting gaze and light pass through), Meydan, Sha Tin/Happy Valley, Flemington, Del Mar. Casinos: Bellagio's Chihuly ceiling and gallery; Wynn's Roger Thomas playground (antique clocks, skylights, flowers); MGM's collection; Crown; Marina Bay Sands' ArtScience.
- Slow and backlit light: Turrell (Skyspaces, Roden Crater); Eliasson (The Weather Project); Jenny Holzer's LED text (the 1989 Guggenheim spiral, Bilbao, 7 WTC, Transbay) — text surfacing and dissolving as meditation; Miyajima; Jim Campbell's low-resolution LEDs; Villareal's Bay Lights; Lozano-Hemmer; Nauman's neon; Aitken and Viola's slow cinema; Jeff Wall's backlit transparencies; Ann Hamilton's text-and-thread works.
- The one-line horse: Picasso's single-line animals (1940s–50s), Matisse, Cocteau, the ensō and Zen ink horses, Native ledger-art horses, Lascaux. Text from marks: Xu Bing, Twombly, Darboven, Wool, asemic writing. Tactile relief translations for blind visitors (the Prado's "Touching the Prado"). Wish precedents: ema boards, Yoko Ono's Wish Trees, wishing walls, Candy Chang's Before I Die walls.

What this offers the wall: the direct toolkit — the Wish in the Picasso/ensō line; the Dream in the Holzer/Xu Bing family, engineered in the Turrell/Campbell manner so the change is felt more than seen; Holzer proves text can be contemplative light, the opposite of the slot's LEDs; the wish rack is a proven, low-cost civic gesture.

8. Places that resurrected their own past

- Prairie Meadows rebuilt from 1991 bankruptcy; Canterbury reopened May 1995 after going dark. Lost harness tracks: Roosevelt Raceway (closed 1988), Sportsman's Park (2003), Garden State (2001), Hazel Park (harness ended 2018), Freehold Raceway (the oldest half-mile track in the country, ceased racing 2024) — each a gathering place lost; the Goshen museum keeps a Roosevelt collection as deliberate memory.
- The county-fair circuit of the Midwest (Minnesota, Iowa, Wisconsin, Ohio): bleachers, simple tote boards, race callers, kettle corn, sweet feed, amateur drivers — the people's sport in its purest form. Aftercare worldwide (Standardbred Retirement Foundation, New Vocations, Ontario and Australian rehoming); Amish and road-horse afterlives.

What this offers the wall: resurrection is the deepest theme — trout farm, amphitheater, and sport all brought back; the Dream's surfacing and dissolving sentences are a mechanism of memory; the county-fair aesthetic argues for a visibly hand-drawn line and against a slick finish.

10. The hallway

- The sandō (shrine approach) and the roji (tea path) are deliberate decrescendos ending at a torii, a basin, the crawl-through door; the nave-and-apse puts the culminating image at the end of the long axis, drawn toward by light; museum vista terminations; hotel corridor art (21c, Dolder Grand, Hotel Okura, the Sagamore).
- Appleton's prospect-refuge; the Kaplans' "mystery" (a scene promising more if one moves deeper); wayfinding by light — a luminous beacon at the end of a corridor pulls the body forward. The home stretch as architecture. The decrescendo principle: stimulation and sound fall off from the loud room to the quiet one.

What this offers the wall: the hallway is the overture. The wall is a beacon — visible but not readable from the mouth, drawing the visitor down the home stretch; the corridor dims and quiets so the slow light and the surfacing text can work on arrival. The sandō gives the walk a name: a purification path with the shrine at its end.

The twenty most transferable insights, ranked

1. Slowness is thrilling — Ban'ei. 3. Text can be contemplative light — Holzer. 4. The single unbroken line is the sport's own heritage — Currier & Ives + Picasso. 5. Breathe the panel near six cycles a minute — resonance breathing. 7. The corridor is a purification path with a beacon — sandō, Kaplan. 8. The votive wish has a horse at its root — ema. 9. Betting here is communal — ATG/V75, PMU, the tote. 10. Honesty is the card room's virtue — the player-dealer model. 11. Light is scarce and sacred in the north — Nordic winter light. 12. Perceptual slow light makes viewers watch themselves seeing — Turrell, Eliasson. 13. Text emerging from marks is a mature form — Xu Bing, asemic writing, Twombly. 14. Memory rises and fades — the lost tracks, the trout farm. 15. Keep the hand visible — the county fair. 16. Nature views restore — ART, biophilic hospitality. 17. A distant warm glow pulls — prospect-refuge. 19. Provide a tactile relief — the Prado.

Kin venues, and what each did that Running Aces has not

Canterbury Park — a cooperative $71M purse agreement with the Shakopee Mdewakanton community. The Meadowlands — a landmark grandstand betting on architecture and hospitality. Obihiro — a race-sponsorship scheme and a distinctive slow-race identity. Solvalla/ATG — an on-site heritage museum and a cooperative funding model. Addington — a marquee civic week. Vincennes — a signature international race and a night identity. Prairie Meadows — public-benefit ownership.

Facts that correct or complicate the earlier research

1. The Meadowlands is a strong slot-less parallel omitted before. 3. The legal fight is mixed, not uniformly lost — the January 2026 dealer-assist win. 5. The California dismissal is on appeal; the mirror precedent is not final.

Verify seams from the first pass

Trout-Air's start year (1964/1968); stall count (150/300); the grandstand history from a single 2012 source.

Part Two
The Ore
Trial writing cut from the research. Each piece carries its own header saying what it is. Nothing in this part is finished work; it is shown as found.
