A WISH WITH NOTHING RIDING ON IT

The Running Aces telling, whole — one document — Quarry trial cut, September 3, 2026

Quarry ore ... nothing here is ruled or locked ... cut after every document in the project knowledge was read whole ... the technical specifics stay in the record and are left out of the telling ... one passage at a time, one strike per stone ... wording belongs to the Mason ...

1 · The loud room

There is a room in this building where the lights are fast ... where the cards turn and the odds climb a bright board and a voice comes down out of the air from a booth high up ... and there is a hallway leading out of that room ... high, wide, long ... and at the end of the hallway there is a wall ... and on the wall, two lights that are slow ...

That is the whole story ... the rest of it is the walk ...

Elders sit still ... and everyone comes to them ... and the coming is half the visit ... So walk ... nothing at the end of the hall will move until a body does ... nothing there hums, nothing calls, nothing breathes a scent into the air ... the visitor's body is the only moving part, and the piece was built that way on purpose ... every other thing in the house moves so the person doesn't have to ... this is the one thing in the house that only moves when a human does ...

Halfway down, the roar starts falling away behind the shoulders ... step by step ... the building performing a long fade-out with no hand on any dial ... and near the quiet end a sound comes back that carpet and roar had erased everywhere else ... footsteps ... breath ... a person hearing their own two-beat gait again ... which is the sport's gait ... which is the heart's ...

2 · The oldest memory is water

Whatever dream this building is, the water is its oldest memory ...

Before the horses there were ponds ... A family on a wet lowland too soft to farm dug holes in it and filled the holes with trout and invited the whole state to come and catch supper ... more than twenty ponds, every one a hole a person chose to dig ... a restaurant beside them, a giant fish by the highway, picnics of thousands on the grass ... The first business on this ground was a stocked game the player could not lose ... guaranteed luck ... a fish for an afternoon ... the benevolent casino ...

Then the same grass became a place for music ... no seats, nothing to sit on, everybody on the lawn ... and on the biggest night anyone remembers, the crowd came to sing along to a song that is, start to finish, the story of a wager with the devil which the human wins ... The house's biggest night was a bet the house lost, and everybody went home happy ...

Then the ponds closed ... and the ground sat quiet ...

3 · Resurrection is the neighborhood's habit

A racetrack came next ... and nearly died in its first year ... lost more than it could afford, faced the bank, lost its license once and earned it back ... a house that opened in the worst year to open anything, and kept opening ... We were like the redheaded stepchild ... says Taro Ito, who runs it now, of those years ... nobody knew this place ...

Then the house did the strangest thing a casino ever did ... it dug a pond ... stocked it with trout ... and gave the old name back to the ground, short one hyphen ... Trout-Air returned as Trout Air ... The water was called back ... the resurrection included resurrecting the fish ... and the one mark that stayed dead is the small horizontal stroke that used to stand between the two words ... which flies now somewhere off the edge of the picture, a distant bird to everyone and the loneliest bird in Minnesota to anyone who knows ...

Even the hotel practiced it ... opened, went dark five days in when the world shut, and lit again a season later ... Whatever burned here came back kinder ... ash-kindness, the tenderness that was not available before the burning ... a house that nearly died learned to pension its horses ...

And next door the whole neighborhood was already in the habit ... a marsh once cut for wire grass and woven into the floors of American parlors ... bought back, rested, and made into the place where the state called its geese home ... then its swans, the eggs carried down from Alaska, the whitest and most luminous bird on the continent brought back from the edge a few miles from the wall ... Trout, geese, swans, a racetrack ... resurrection is the neighborhood's habit, not the building's private miracle ... When the man who dreamed a dying place back to life says imagine a heavenly dream, that is a description of something already done ... The commission is not decoration for a wall ... It is the building dreaming ...

4 · Two floors, stacked

Columbus sits on a sand plain ... glacial outwash ... a page of sand ten thousand years deep ... old dunes holding still under dry prairie grass and ribbons of oak savanna ... and the dunes still move in a hard drought ... the page turns when the rain stops ...

The racing surface on top of it is crushed limestone and stone dust — ancient floor ground fine ... So two floors are stacked here ... the horses run on a spread over a glacier's leavings ... and every night the harrow drags the older floor smooth across the younger one ... and every night the horses write on it again ...

The racetrack circles a beautiful wetland ... a visitor wrote that, and it is the whole ground in six words ... the oval is a ring drawn around water ...

6 · Patrons here

Patrons here are warm, unpretentious, and better off for it ... They love storytelling ... and the one who runs the place described, without knowing there was a word for it, the oldest picture-scroll tradition on earth — where the writing was never a label beside the picture but the picture itself ... The favorite piece of art in the building is a fox alone on a snow gradient — white going to pale blue, and one small orange animal in the middle of nothing ... the beauty of the empty remainder, wearing Minnesota clothing ... a taste compass pointing one direction all along ...

Trout is king ... the patron says ...

7 · The pair

A wish and a dream ... two works side by side at the end of the hall ... and the sport itself supplies the law that divides them ... Harness racing runs on two gaits ... the trot in diagonal pairs, the pace in lateral pairs ... raced separately, never mixed ... So one work trots and the other paces — two works governed by two laws of repeated motion ...

And the leather truth divides them deeper ... pacers race in hopples, the loops that hold the lateral gait ... trotters go free-legged, held to their gait by breeding and training alone ... One gait is kept by a strap, the other by character ... The Dream paces — restraint worn as equipment ... the Wish trots — restraint carried in the body, still, calm, very fast, never breaking ...

8 · The Wish

The still work ... one constant steady glow, all night, all day ... the one lamp left on in a barn at night ... Its register is the shelter's quiet words and touch ... a lovely horse at rest ...

Its image is one figure — horse, sulky, driver — drawn as a single unbroken stroke ... never lifted ... all trembling kept within ... From the mouth of the hall, a body and a number ... at arrival, the discovery that it is one line ... and anyone can check that with their own eyes at close range ... the cheapest unforgettable thing there is ...

The job description is old ... Real horses were once given to shrines to carry prayers ... then statues of horses ... then pictures of horses ... and the word for the little wooden wish-plaques still hung by the thousand means picture-horse ... The oldest work a horse picture has is to carry a wish ... and a grandstand is a platform full of people wishing on horses ... the bet slip is a modern picture-horse ... And nobody has noticed ...

9 · The Dream

The transforming work ... the picture that causes the lucky dream, behaving like one ... Its register is the race wave ... It carries no figure, because here the writing is the animal ... a field of countless hand-drawn strides gathers out of blankness ... condenses ... until readable sentences surface — the water called back, the ruin, the rise, the horses holding their gait down the stretch — and stay legible for a long moment ... then loosen back into pure stride ... Language appearing out of the hand's pattern and dissolving back into it ... which is simply what dreams do with words ...

The light changes at the speed of breathing, or slower ... minutes, not seconds ... a trot that never breaks ... the slowest light in the house ... In a building of lights built to be fast, one light tuned to the calm rate of a resting chest ... so that a pulse standing in front of it settles, gently and for free ... On the far side of the world there is a race so slow the crowd walks beside it, decided when the back of the load crosses the line ... and it is the most suspenseful two minutes in racing ... Slowness is thrilling ... it only needs the room to be quiet enough ...

The layers have real depth ... so when a visitor moves, the field of strides slides and runs ... and when the visitor stops, it stops ... Their walking pace is the horses' gait ... powered by nothing ... sovereign even if every lamp in the building dies ...

Three distances to read it from ... far off, a glowing shape ... up close, countless strides ... at ten inches, the smallest text — a whisper, readable by one person at a time ... the barn whisper made literal ... the reward only intimacy collects ...

10 · The rail, and the grandstand that never came

The natural way to watch a race is leaning ... forearms on the rail, weight forward ... So put a real rail in front of the two works ... worn-smooth wood, the most touched surface in racing ... and no instruction will be needed ...

The grandstand here was never built ... the money ran out before it could be ... so the seats are benches on the apron, and blankets and lawn chairs on the grass with the races twenty-five minutes apart ... and the hotel's windows face the oval ... the hotel is the grandstand ... and the crowd watches the way the first crowds on this ground watched the music — sitting on the ground ... a house that could not afford the seats got the people's way of sitting instead ...

And nothing in the installation moves except the viewer ... which is why the layers, the line and the rail belong together ...

11 · A chest full of heartbeats

Listen for the wave ... far drumming felt before it is heard ... forty wheels hissing ... the voice from the booth climbing ... the rail trembling into the chest ... the break ... the whoosh ... then gone ... approach, break, recede — the shape the dissolve wears ...

A gallop is rolling thunder ... but the trot and the pace are two-beat gaits ... lub-dub ... harness racing is the only racing on earth whose sound is a heartbeat ... a field of eight is a chest full of heartbeats held at maximum speed ... forbidden to break rhythm ...

The break has a name in this sport — it is the sport's own word for a fault ... a horse that breaks stride, the trot collapsing into a gallop, must be pulled off the pace ... the whole breed is the refusal to break ... the gait held past what should break it ... So a light that never breaks puts a horse made of light on the room's oval ... and passes the same test in the same breath ...

The surreal move is dilation — what dreams do to peak moments ... the fifteen-second drive stretched across ten minutes of light ... a race relived at the speed of memory ... which is the elder's native speed ...

12 · Two tellers, one house

The house has one shared human voice ... the race caller, unseen, high up in a booth ... memorizing a new cast of silks every twenty minutes ... composing the race live in metered speech ... delivering to air what the hooves write in lime ... and every composition erased by the next race, the way the harrow erases the carves in the sand ...

At the far turn the open air smears the call toward pure tone ... and it gathers back into words as the field comes home ... and at the wire the crowd swallows the voice whole ... the biggest word of the night is the one nobody hears ... The crowd finishes the poem ... oh no, oh no, oh no, yes, yes, yes ...

So the house has two tellers ... the spoken one from above, loud, live, erased each race ... and the written one at the end of the hall, silent and permanent ... The wall is the caller's quiet sibling ... a race call at the speed of memory ... And the house has two boards ... the fast one posts the odds ... the slow one posts the wishes ... One sentence and any visitor understands the work ...

And somewhere in the surfacing text, if the tests let it through, the number six named Hope Your Lucky is trotting a little slower than usual and wandering off the groove ... the caller's comedy secretly carrying the wish ...

13 · The lines

Harness drivers call the reins the lines ... The fisherman reads the trout through the pole ... the driver reads the horse's mouth through the lines ... and the pen is the third practice of the same gesture — fingers, tension, careful steady movement, then a living mark ... And the driver's body at speed states the sport's whole law ... lying back ... pulling ... not thrust, tension ... velocity achieved through restraint ...

Every line is fastened to its living end by a knot ... and the knot is the weakest point, the place the line admits a hand touched it ... the imperfection at the connection is the signature ... which is why the tremble is left in ...

14 · The mark

The mark is harness racing's word for a record ... a Standardbred's mark is its fastest winning mile ... standing in the books after the horse is gone ... Like a line cut in stone, a mark is a real thing — the best time of a horse no longer on this earth ... a kauai, the last living copy of a speed ... And the old word underneath the word record meant to pass a thing back through the heart ... which is what a mark is for ... nobody noticing the mark of a horse long gone means somebody is noticing ... passing a stone-gone horse's fastest mile back through an enlarged heart ...

The wishbone knows the other half of the law ... a bone only pays by breaking ... one hand wins the longer piece ... A Wish held unbroken stays whole ... both ends kept ...

15 · Proof

An old word for what the work asks of itself is proof — to test ... Before proof meant evidence it meant ordeal ... A barrel is fired past anything a sane hand would fire ... and if it refuses to fail it is stamped with a proof mark — steel that behaved ... marked for life ...

That is the family every test in this house belongs to ... Nothing passes by being admired ... things pass by refusing to fail ... A joke passes only if it grew inside the house and nobody outside the house pays for it ... a light passes only if it tells the truth at every state ... a line passes only if a stranger can check it with their own eyes ... And the elder test asks the plainest question of all ... would the elder say it this plainly ... notice this smallness ... laugh this gently ... wait this long ... offer this freely ...

16 · The honest air

Smell the air through words ... liniment, warm harness leather, cut hay, cold loam after rain ... The complex has an olfactory geography running from engineered to honest ... the concession register first — popcorn, grill, fryer, beer, the sweet county-fair smell — then the apron's lime dust and sand after rain ... the rail's leather and hide ... the backstretch's manure and hay and shavings — the one air on the property nobody engineered and nobody deploys ... then the pond ... then the plain ... And the wall at the end of the hall adds nothing to the air ... no perfume, no speaker ... the one place in the building where neither the sound nor the air is working on anybody ...

Summer sweat ... horses and humans are nearly alone among the animals in cooling by profuse sweat ... a July grandstand is two species doing the same ancient thing ... and horse sweat whips into white foam where the harness rubs, so the straps write on the horse in foam ... the harness traces itself on the body ... hard effort made visible ... gone by morning ... A lathered horse cannot be faked ... sweat is the body's honest ledger ... the opposite of every disguised loss inside ...

Cold-night racing, the Minnesota gift ... every breath a cloud ... and the hottest horse's steam is sweat becoming a bigger cloud ... And first frost is the season's harrow ... one cold night and the whole smell-field goes silent ... the last races of the year run in first-frost air — the densest sound of the year inside the emptiest air of it ... the Dream's breathing performed by the calendar ...

And the shovel ... do you remember the shovel we used to pick up horse manure ... is the childhood door into all of it ... every barn kid on earth has a hand on that handle ...

17 · Inside the animal

A trout turns into a current and holds its place ... a little effort and a lot of grace ... near a rock it rides the river's own contradictions ... A trotter at speed runs the same discipline ... form held against the urge to break ... One law, two elements ...

The horse has the largest eyes of any land mammal ... nearly wrap-around sight, with two blind spots — directly behind, and directly in front of its own nose ... A horse at the wire cannot see the wire ... it runs flat out toward the one place its eyes cannot go ... trusting the hands through the lines ... The shadow roll on its nose is there to keep it from seeing shadows on the track and breaking over them — tack whose one job is to keep an animal from spooking at dream-shapes on the ground ... The reds of the silks live in a color the horse does not have ... and from inside the animal, the floodlit oval is a held world with darkness at its rim ... Nobody has made a wall carry what the horse senses ... this one tries ...

18 · The cord

The elder's nature is the cord ... patience, plain truth, mercy, memory, and the gift with no stake ... and these are its fibers ...

Fine harness is hand-sewn with two needles crossing one waxed thread ... every stitch locked on its own ... needle-patience made into a seam ... a machine cannot make it ... a machine's stitch unravels from one cut, the hand-sewn seam holds even severed ... The strongest seam in leatherwork is the one only a hand can make ... and it is slower ... and that is why it survives ...

The horseman's quick-release knot holds fast against any pull from the horse ... and frees with one pull of the tail-end by a hand ... because a tied horse in a panic can kill itself, the holding and the letting-go are built into the same knot ... restraint with mercy folded inside ... the elder's temperament in a piece of rope ...

An old harness on its peg still holds the shape of the horse that wore it ... ghost-weight in leather ... and the humblest named part of the whole rig — the little loop that holds a strap-end flat — is called the keeper ...

Sea speed is knots because it was ... a line knotted at intervals, paid out while a sandglass ran ... speed once read from rope and sand ... and a racetrack still reads it from sand, the fractions called from the booth ...

And baling twine ... the leftover string off every bale, saved in coffee cans, mending half of rural Minnesota ... the most-used cordage in any barn is the packaging the feed came in ... kept because an elder never throws away good string ... Wonderful Garbage in orange polypropylene ...

19 · The words the reader came in carrying

Every horse in tonight's parade is the far end of a cord nearly two centuries long ... one stud book, one founding sire ... and the word for the tracing is lineage — a book of knotted strings ... with the elder the eldest living point on the line ...

Down to the wire ... the finish was a real cord strung across the track for the judges' eye ... and the cord escaped into the language ... with its gentle sibling, winning hands down — so far clear the hands drop and the lines go soft ... victory's own gesture is a letting-go ... The start is a released line too, the folding wings of the moving gate ... so a race is the interval between a line letting go and a line arriving ...

Home stretch ... neck and neck ... by a nose ... dead heat ... front-runner ... dark horse ... across the board ... hold your horses ... down to the wire ... Every visitor arrives already fluent in racetrack ... we have all spoken it our whole lives without knowing where it came from ... So let the surfacing sentences lean on the track's own words, and every reader owns them on sight ... No wall text is more universal than the words the reader came in carrying ...

20 · The house with no machines

Here is the contradiction the whole house stands on, and it is a happy one ... This is a casino with no slot machines ... The house was drawn for them ... and the machines never came ... so the horses stayed, and the cards, and the trout ... and the windows go to the floor and the smoke is gone and the room fills with daylight — every dark-casino law inverted by a house that could not have the machine and made a virtue of the lack ...

The machine the house does not have has an animal's name — the one-armed bandit ... needing a second arm to come and feed it ... and slop, misheard, is the bucket carried to the stable stall ... the only slop in this house goes to the horses ...

At the rail, bettors wager into one pool against each other ... the track takes a fixed cut and has no interest in which horse wins ... The card room is players against players ... a whole house of people betting against each other and nobody betting against the house ... And the one screen the house was allowed to keep has a dealer's hands inside it — a live person shuffling real cards for the picture — a machine permitted only because a hand is at work in it ... the saddle-stitch law, upheld by people who had never heard of it ...

Minnesota keeps small kin for this humane form ... pull-tabs in every bar ... the meat raffle, where the prize is supper ... and the property's first business, a stocked pond nobody could lose at ... Scouts run that pond now ... a fish caught is paid for by the pound, cooked, and eaten ... nobody may throw one back ... a rule that a restaurant on the other side of the world, where the diners fish their supper out of a moat, enforces with a drum ...

21 · The paper harrow

The track floor holds a ritual nobody designed ... losing tickets torn and dropped ... the floor covered in paper wishes by night's end ... swept before morning — the paper harrow ... and the stoopers walk it first, hunting a dropped winner ... The picture-horse hangs its wish up ... the bet slip drops its wish down ... both floors get swept ...

22 · The quiet end of the hallway

The building's product is a trance ... the zone, where time and worry dissolve ... and some play not to win but to just keep playing ... The practice has a name for the room the fast lights brick over — flow-theft, the breath between stitches called laziness and walled up — and the wall at the end of the hall is where the breath is handed back ... The work answers the trance with a trance ... same territory, opposite gift ... The machine's dissolve takes hours and gives nothing back ... the wall's dissolve costs nothing, asks nothing, and returns the viewer to their calmer senses ... stillness full instead of empty ... presence where the machine sells oblivion ... the one trance in the building with no meter running ... a fire-cave at the end of a casino hallway — sanctified by condition, not by location, which the practice holds can be set up almost anywhere ... even here ...

And the research brought back one sharp thing, and the record keeps it ... calm, open, daylit rooms restore people — and in a machine casino that same calm keeps them at the machines longer ... a restful artwork is safe only where there is nothing to buy at the end of the hallway ... There is nothing to buy at the end of this one ... So the wall can pursue calm honestly ... which very few walls in very few casinos on earth are permitted to do ...

Luck's house takes as well as gives ... and some of the people walking that hallway carry more than they came in with ... No lecture ... the work's answer is built in — the held gait against the break ... the slowest light in the loud house ... the honest state ... and the Wish itself, carrying hope without a stake ... the wish is older and kinder than the wager ... The work does not judge the person at the end of the hallway ... It gives them a still place to stand ... and dignity while they stand there ... the elder's moral act, and not a small one ... Really ... tell the truth ... celebrate nothing falsely ... and remember that the house edge on a wish is zero ...

23 · The elder of the ground

The cranes were over this place before the ice that made the sand ... and they still come ... the oldest voice in the neighborhood by a margin of millions of years ... Its call arrives before the bird is seen — the wave's law in feathers — and its chest is an instrument, the windpipe coiled into the breastbone ... crane, horse, spectator ... chest to chest to chest, one law ... And in the tradition that folds paper into birds, the crane is the wish-bird ... fold a thousand and the wish is granted ... so the local bird and the wish-carrier are the same animal, standing in the wetland the whole time ...

The sand plain holds a bestiary ... swans, eagles, badgers, bobolinks ... a turtle that shows almost no sign of aging, active into its ninth decade — the resident still-calendar, a creature time barely writes on, walking ground where everything else is written and erased nightly ... the track keeps the passing-calendar, the turtle keeps the other one ... And the hognose snake, the resident bluffer — hoods like a cobra, strikes with a closed mouth, plays dead when the bluff fails ... the casino's reptile is a bluffer who never bites ... hidden small in the Wish's field for lookers to find and tell newcomers about forever ...

And the aurora ... the oldest slow backlit picture over this land ... the light in the Dream moves like northern lights, not like a sign ... every Minnesotan understands that without a number ...

24 · The people's horse

The breed itself is a piece of writing ... a horse became a Standardbred by trotting a mile inside a standard time — admission by the clock, performance not pedigree — against the aristocrat's closed book and its three founding sires ... The people's sport got the people's forms ... seven hundred trotting prints in parlors and saloons and barns, never museums ... the gilded trotter on the weathervane pointing where the weather came from ... the buggy horse on the plain road when the racing is over ... The aristocrat's horse retires to pasture ... the standard horse keeps its job ... and here, by the track's own foundation, gets a pension too ... a horse finished with racing goes to a place called This Old Horse ... I know it is the right thing to do ... the patron said of the old ones ... And the winningest horse on the continent one recent season was a mare from this barn named Over The Moon For You ... a wish with four legs ...

A Minnesota town renamed itself for a trotter ... whose record stood thirty-three years ... and the horse and the man who dreamed on that horse died thirty hours apart ...

And the crown ... the birth of the moving image had a trotter in it, pulling a sulky through a row of cameras ... the newest picture medium on earth was born at this sport's gait ... so when the patron jokes about new technology, the answer is already on the wall ... And the sulky itself, for the colophon ... a cart named for those who ride alone ... which is right for a practice built alone across thirty years ...

The finding under all of it ... there is no Degas of harness racing ... the painters who made the racehorse famous painted the rich man's animal ... The commoner's house never received its major fine-art wall, anywhere, in a hundred and fifty years ... so the room this work enters is not just quiet ... it is empty ... and the tradition already taught us the beauty of the empty remainder ...

25 · The winter song

The most widely sung secular song on the planet is a harness-racing song ... born of street sleigh races in a Massachusetts town ... and its last verse is a horseman's counsel — get a bobtailed bay ... two forty as his speed ... a mile in two minutes forty at the trot, the speed of the horse later called Standardbred ... The tune's rhythm is the bells on a trotting harness, worn because a sleigh in snow is nearly silent ... Almost nobody on earth knows they have been singing the sport their whole lives ... Let that line stand in the winter text unglossed ... and detonate quietly for anyone who stops to count ...

And winter here is not empty ... karts run on the frozen oval where the trot was ... a drive-in lights the lot where the lawn was ... the season is plural ... and the Dream glowing at the end of a dim corridor is a northern winter window — the warm light across snow that a walker in the dark heads toward ...

26 · The crack

The moon jar loved for its seam ... the mended break shown in gold ... Wonderful Garbage, the practice's own name for the law ... For this commission it lands in one place ... the line stays hand-drawn and keeps its human tremble ... nothing machine-clean anywhere ... no pastiche, no costume ... the function governs and the line stays ours ... The imperfection of the stroke is quiet kinship with every house at the table ... and the knot is where the line admits a hand ...

27 · A circle drawn by animals

The track is one closed line drawn in sand ... drawn nightly, by horses, at heartbeat rhythm ... erased by the harrow ... drawn again ... The one-line circle prized for its imperfection and its tremble ... the Wish holds that law at the scale of a figure ... the track holds it at the scale of the ground ... and the nightly erasure makes it the only such circle on earth redrawn every day by running animals ... When the moment comes to tell Taro what the track is, this is the sentence ... and it needs no foreign word to land ...

28 · Method, said plainly and never headlined

Say the eighth ... submerge the seven-eighths ... A person fishes in flat true sentences with the war underneath never mentioned once, and the omitted weight is what makes the story tremble ... The fox on the snow is the same law in paint ... the prose law and the picture law agree ...

Nothing here was found by admiring ... A machine was asked for ore by the hundred sheet ... most of it was thrown away ... what stood was cut by hand ... one passage at a time ... one strike per stone ... The practice keeps five witnesses for when the work is stuck ... four with addresses, asked about the opening, the overthinking, the middle, and the ending — and the one for endings, Jean, says end on what is not there ... and one witness with no address, Hahnu, who gives no advice and asks the only three questions that matter ... what is the piece for ... whose land does it live on ... and what does it owe ...

Every nobody in this telling implies a somebody ... nobody looks down ... nobody calls the animal by its oldest names ... nobody notices the one mark that stayed dead ... The somebody behind every nobody is the elder ... and the elder is not a figure placed in the work ... the elder is the temperament running through every stone the way the single line runs through the Wish ...

29 · What is not there

Nobody has measured the wall ... nobody has counted the steps from the roar to the quiet ... nobody has stood at the rail at the wire with their eyes closed through one wave ... yet ... A plain wooden rack for blank wishes, hung the way the picture-horses are hung, stays a question for Taro and is never assumed ...

And the work is built so that a stranger, a bettor, a child, a widow, and a skeptic can each walk one hallway ... arrive at one quiet wall ... and be given the same three things without a word of instruction ... a story ... a stillness ... and a wish with nothing riding on it ...

Down to the wire ... hands down ... the house edge on a wish is zero ... the slowest light in the loudest room ... one still place to stand, and dignity while standing there ...

And nobody has noticed ...

A Rumba for a House With No Slot Machines

Lyric ore, September 2, 2026. Quarry trial in the hybrid form ruled for on the Mason's word: one picture per line, no joints, a refrain that does not move (the method of "Jockey Full of Bourbon," no line of it borrowed), in the elder's grammar — no I, no you outside quoted speech, ellipsis breath, terminal ellipses, the mercy test on every line. Every line is a fact from the Running Aces research or the record; nothing invented. Nothing here is ruled or locked. Strike by strike from the title, or feed a verse back carved. The quarry remains closed; the form is a second cut of the same ore.

one.

Sand ten thousand years down and a pond dug in it ...
thirty-eight ponds and a fiberglass fish on the interstate ...
Trout-Air with the hyphen ... Trout Air without ...
the one mark that stayed dead, flying far off like a bird ...
Iron Maiden on the grass in eighty-five ...
nobody sat down, there was nothing to sit on ...

refrain

Down to the wire ... hands down ...
the house edge on a wish is zero ...
the slowest light in the loudest room ...
and nobody has noticed ...

two.

A house built for slot machines that never came ...
so the horses stayed, and the cards, and the trout ...
fifty tables and no arm to pull ...
two-dollar blackjack, fifty cents to sit ...
eighteen years old and the card comes up ...
no smoke in the room and the windows go to the floor ...
a dealer shuffling real cards for a screen ...
three judges said yes, three said no ... the cards kept turning ...

refrain

three.

Fifty-five nights ... Tuesday at seven, Thursday and Sunday at six ...
the barn opens on Derby Day ...
a mare called Over The Moon For You wins fifteen of nineteen ...
the old horse goes to This Old Horse ...
Darin in the booth delivering to air what the hooves write in lime ...
the harrow behind them, erasing the carves ...
seven hundred and eighty thousand on the last night ...
then frost, and the smell-field goes silent ...

refrain

four.

Scouts holding the poles and nobody throws one back ...
fifty-five degrees in the bubbler ... paid by the pound ...
swan eggs carried down from Alaska in eighty-six ...
karts on the ice where the trot was ...
a drive-in in the parking lot where the lawn was ...
a hotel open five days, then dark, then lit ...
a rail with nobody leaning yet ...
two forty as his speed ... and nobody counts ...

refrain

five — the founders

Southwest and a firm from West Virginia, waiting on a law ...
fifty days of racing before the cards could be dealt ...
Valentine Hotspur wins the first race ever run here ...
four million gone the first year, one in ten sent home ...
a forty-one-million-dollar note, and Black Diamond holding it ...
the license pulled in twenty-thirteen, four hundred thousand paid back ...
the sign changed to entertainment in twenty-fifteen ...
and the fish came back the year after ...

refrain

six — the patron, in quoted speech only

A card-room man from Los Angeles, thirty years of tables ...
the Bicycle Club, Bay 101, Hollywood Park ...
we were like the redheaded stepchild ...
nobody knew this place ...
we really don't even know what our ceiling is ...
trout is king ...
a post shared once, about the man who grooms the track like a child ...
I know it is the right thing to do ... said of the old horses ...

seven — the hotel

Ground broken November of eighteen ... seventeen million, or eighteen ...
Cuningham drew it, Truline built it ...
a hundred and sixteen rooms on five floors, every one facing the oval ...
hygge, the patron said, and the architects gave it wide halls ...
a fireplace in the lobby ... a sand drawing under glass on the coffee table ...
Turman's mural of everything: the ponds, the cards, the horses, the skyline ...
Jack, Queen and King for the weddings ... a fire pit with the finish line in view ...
open five days in March of twenty ... then dark ... then lit ...

refrain

eight — the fight, told as weather

Five hundred machines approved on the first of April ...
five to one at the commission ... then seventy-one to fifty-eight, thirty-six to twenty-five ...
gone by the twenty-fifth of May ...
thirty-nine names in a federal suit, and a judge who would not let it stand ...
economic lifeblood, the judge wrote, of the nations down the road ...
three justices one way, three the other, one stepped aside ...
a dealer's hands on real cards, and that was the whole case ...
two sessions since, and no bill crossed the wire ...

ten — the floor, any night

Bingo Tuesday, two-dollar beer and a five-dollar dog ...
trivia Thursday, comedy once a month ...
twenty new screens and two eighty-inch ones, all pointed at the wire ...
three percent back on every wager with the card in hand ...
Frank from Yonkers with a free tip sheet at the program stand ...
a dollar blackjack, Blazing Sevens, Lucky Lucky, Pot of Gold ...
a chair kept twenty minutes, then it goes to the room ...
a toaster broken at breakfast ... RA can do better here ...

eleven — the barns and the lot

Two barns, a hundred and fifty stalls, three hundred drawn ...
farmhouse feel in the off-season, forty-six thousand square feet ...
horses shipped in from Iowa and Wisconsin ...
Brady Jenson defending, Casey Leonard up from Illinois ...
the Dan Patch, the Ron Banks, the Steve Wiseman, all free-for-alls ...
twelve hundred parking spots ... Finding Nemo on the fourteenth of August ...
a Seger tribute band closing the summer on the fifteenth ...
Concert for a Cause, for the wounds nobody can see ...

refrain

twelve — the water, all of it

A lowland too wet to farm ... so a contractor dug ...
a restaurant that sat three hundred ... picnics of five thousand ...
the sons ran a skydiving club off the same field ...
a Preiner still working every job, down to the reservations ...
closed in ninety-nine, making way ...
a pond that looks like a fountain, and is full of fish ...
the same farm stocks the pond and the kitchen ...

thirteen — the ledger of small things

A cyber incident in twenty-five ... core functions unaffected ...
thirty-six fewer races that year, three nights lost to rain ...
per-race handle up seven ... the country down ten ...
poker paused, back in August ...
the mayor's last name is the trout family's name ...
a hundred and ten thousand to This Old Horse, forty-six thoroughbreds, nine standardbreds ...
three horses in the Parade of Breeds at the State Fair ...
a thousand horses served by twenty-twenty-six ...

refrain

fourteen — the approach

Google and MapQuest are incorrect ... the sign says use the directions on this page ...
just south of Forest Lake, before the interstate splits ...
exit one twenty-nine, right at the top of the ramp ...
three-quarters of a mile west on Lake Drive ...
thirty-two miles from the airport, twenty minutes from either downtown ...
a truck stop three minutes off ... Culver's five ...
the road used to be called Zurich Street ... same place, new name ...
and a service that drives the jockey home, car and all ... the number is on the wall ...

refrain

fifteen — the plan on paper

Five-eighths of a mile, drawn in Minneapolis for a company that is gone ...
indoor and outdoor seating on the drawing ... benches on the apron in the world ...
twenty-five poker tables drawn ... a room with a ceiling to the sky built ...
the newest harness track in the country, and no roof over the seats ...
so the season is summer ... rain in the afternoon and half the crowd stays home ...
a general manager who ran the Army's slot machines overseas for ten years ...
half owned by a firm that filed it with the government ...
fifty-six percent to the lender by twenty-twelve ...

sixteen — the rooms

Sixty-five inches of screen and a rainfall showerhead ...
three hundred ninety-two square feet ... the suite twice that ...
blackout drapes, a down duvet, a safe the size of a laptop ...
check-in at four, until two in the morning ... eighteen to sign ...
no cribs ... no pets ... service animals welcome ...
a card tapped to the elevator before the floor lights up ...
coffee only at breakfast, said the one who drinks it all day ...
local culture and ecosystem education on the list of amenities ... nobody knows what it means ...

refrain

seventeen — the cards, every one

Free Bet Blackjack ... Face Up Pai Gow ... EZ Baccarat ...
Mississippi Stud, Ultimate Texas Hold'em, three-card, four-card ...
Aces Live Five-Card Draw, fifty cents a hand on a screen ...
no commission, no ante ... the house has no hand in it ...
a free tournament Monday with two thousand added ...
Double Stacks for two hundred ... eleven hundred to sit at the big one ...
a hundred fifty points for a ten-dollar meal ...
the second-largest poker room in the state, under the tallest ceiling ...

eighteen — the horses by name

Karpathian Kid the sire ... Lilah in the barn, Over The Moon on the program ...
a mile in one fifty-four and one, the fourteenth win of the year ...
Aaron Lehman in the bike ... Quentin Schneider signing the checks ...
Nick Roland, Dean and Rick Magee, Mooney Svendsen, Luke Plano ...
Jenni King, Nathalie Tremblay, Adam Hauser, Justin Gould ...
a qualifier on the seventh of May ... stalls due the sixteenth of March ...
a hundred and ten thousand a night, on average, for the purse ...
WCCO came out for the winningest horse in America, a Minnesota mare ...

refrain

nineteen — the wetland's ledger

Carex lasiocarpa, the sedge they wove into floors ...
American Grass Twine, then Crex ... the rug company's marsh ...
eight thousand four hundred seventy-eight acres and a fraction, bought in thirty-three ...
Carlos Avery, eighteen sixty-eight to nineteen thirty ...
quail and chukar first, then the pheasants ...
four thousand fifty acres where nothing is hunted ...
the largest wild ground in the metro, and the track circles a piece of it ...
the racetrack circles a beautiful wetland, a visitor wrote ...

twenty-two — the lot, the lawn, the years

Ted Nugent ... the Allman Brothers ... Marshall Tucker ... Heart ... Korn ...
June twenty-third of eighty-five, the World Slavery Tour on the grass ...
a retirement village on the land for a while, between the fish and the horses ...
a construction company twenty-five years before the first pond ...
a billboard on the interstate ... a fish the size of a car ...
Tuesday and Friday four to eight, weekends eleven to eight, no license needed ...
one thousand one hundred and ten Scouts in thirty-two units ...
trout is king ...

refrain

twenty-three — what the visitors said, kept

fantastically curated ... the lawn lush, the oval ringed with water ...
not ready for prime time ... the band outdoors, small and nice ...
five-and-ten tables easy on a Sunday ... I won LOL ...
after Mystic with all the smoke, this was a great place ...
friends of horse owners, invited when the other trip fell through ...
Tuesday is the night, bets and concessions cut ...
Stay, Play and Party ... poolside after hours ...
Laugh your Ace off once a month ...

twenty-four — the seams the record keeps open

A hundred sixteen rooms, or a hundred fifteen ...
seventeen million, or eighteen ...
thirty-eight ponds, or twenty and some ...
sixty-four, or sixty-eight ... ninety-nine, or ninety-eight ...
Cuningham drew it, or Truman Howell finished it ...
Preiner is mayor, or Sandell ...
eighteen thousand on the lawn for Charlie Daniels, said nobody who was there ...
and the record keeps every pair until one is ruled ...

refrain

twenty-five — what is not there yet

Nobody has measured the wall ...
nobody has said how high the hallway is, or how long the walk ...
nobody has counted the steps from the roar to the quiet ...
nobody knows what the light does there at noon, or at ten at night ...
nobody has stood at the rail at the wire with their eyes closed through one wave ...
the mural in the lobby is there ... the wall at the end of the hall is not ...
the rack for the wishes is a question ... the answer is Taro's ...
and the wish itself has nothing riding on it ...

refrain, last

Down to the wire ... hands down ...
the house edge on a wish is zero ...
one still place to stand ... and dignity while standing there ...
and nobody has noticed ...

Verify seams carried inside the lyric

- The 1985 crowd number left out on purpose (unverified).
- Thirty-eight ponds against twenty.
- Seventeen million or eighteen kept as both because both were said.
- Two of the three yous in the house's own slogans are kept as quoted speech in italics; if the lyric is to be clean of them entirely, both lines go and nothing is lost.
- Two forty as his speed is the winter song's own words (Jingle Bells, 1857), quoted — the one his in the piece, inside the exception.

Where each research find would go in the record, if ruled

The dealer-assist ruling beside the saddle stitch (master). No grandstand / the hotel as the grandstand beside the rail stone. The house built for machines that got horses — ledger, master only. Scouts running the pond; nobody may release — the benevolent casino's two new rules. Ice karts and the drive-in — the season stone's two halves. Over The Moon For You, This Old Horse, Darin Gagne — names inside the exception. Night of Champions, September 19 — the research trip's date.

Sick Writing for Running Aces

Raw ore, kept as it came up — the register the cull throws away, shown for the yow: how much gets thrown away.

Oh no, oh no, oh no, yes, yes, yes, Yay! Yeah!!

Junkie in the Trunk(y) ... Junky in the Trunky ... Number [ 6 ] named "Hope Your Lucky" is trotting slower than usual ... and wandering off the side sand grooves a little ... Do you remember the shovel we used to pick up horse manure? ...

Do you remember that song? ... We heard a rumor that came from the Tower of Marginal Power ...

The canvas is clear sky light blue ... over the track ... and we're happy to be here ... You betcha ... In Columbus ... Minnesota ...

Fish broil ashes to funky lanky ... Heaven is that down to earth high ... felt here ... experiencing the invigorating win ... while not worshiping the almighty dollar so much that we never spend a greenback ... Make your bet ... before the horses line up and run as fast as they can ...

Stay clean ... let the music follow you to the bleachers bench ... Big Dark Eyes is fortunately stuck with the most valuable standardbred ... Human's best friend ... That's what we say ...

The Writers' Record

A research document: portraits of writers drawn from the public record, each imagined at Running Aces, each with the frame they would bring and where each would stop. Five portraits were drawn; four are shown here — the fifth stays on the bench.

Jay Cronley (November 9, 1943 – February 26, 2017) was an American newspaper columnist for the Tulsa World and the author of many works of humorous fiction, including Fall Guy, Good Vibes, Quick Change, and Funny Farm. He worked for the Tulsa Tribune for 22 years before moving to the Tulsa World in 1992, and also wrote for Playboy and ESPN.com. Good Vibes was the basis for the 1989 movie "Let It Ride," starring Richard Dreyfuss as a horseplayer who couldn't lose; Quick Change became the Bill Murray heist comedy; Funny Farm became the Chevy Chase film. His father, John, was the sports editor of The Daily Oklahoman in Oklahoma City for 35 years. He died in February 2017 — so he never saw the hotel, the lawsuits, the Supreme Court deadlock, the ice karts, or Frank Drucker's tip sheet. He would have known Running Aces as the track that opened in 2008, nearly folded, lost its license once, rebranded as an "entertainment center," and dug a trout pond. Everything after that is material he'd have had to be handed.

The voice. ESPN's remembrance describes it exactly: a cool, aloof, smart-alecky voice that feels like a very clever friend telling you the story sitting next to you on a barstool. His daughter said "He would find humor in the most mundane parts of life," and his Tulsa columns often focused on his pets and observations about life in Tulsa. Every spring, he would write stinging columns about Tulsa's TV meteorologists and their interruptions for severe weather coverage. A man who spent decades mocking weather coverage would have a great deal to say about a racetrack that built no grandstand and races only in summer, three nights a week, with rain in the forecast.

What he'd make of the no-slots house. This is where accuracy matters most, because a lazy guess would have him cheering the purity of a machine-free track. He wouldn't. As an Oklahoma horseplayer he watched slots save his home tracks, and he was unsentimental about it: "Whatever the reason for some horse racing plants to get slot machine profit: Purses can almost double overnight. Plugs can run for a purse that is worth more than their entire bloodline." He'd already written the Running Aces situation from the other side — "But now the Cherokees own Will Rogers Downs, and every horse player knows what means: a stampede of slot machines is a featured attraction." So the Minnesota arrangement — tribes with the slots, a track legally forbidden them, the track suing everybody and losing — would strike him as the racino that never got its sure thing, and he'd narrate the legislative fight the way he narrated everything institutional: from a bench on the apron, with a losing ticket in his shirt pocket, faintly amused that grown men were fighting in court over who gets to be the house. He'd also have the Minnesota joke ready-made; he once wrote that Breeder's Cup weekend makes Lake Wobegone look like Siberia.

The book itself. His novels are about one ordinary schemer and one impossible day, so the Running Aces book would be that: a losing horseplayer — in Good Vibes it was a cab driver — who drives past Forest Lake, takes the exit the property's own website says the maps get wrong, and has the day of his life at a five-eighths-mile harness oval he'd never heard of. The set pieces write themselves from the property's real inventory, and he'd use every one:

- The trout pond, where the sign says catch-your-own and you pay by the pound, as the only pari-mutuel in the state a man cannot lose. He'd have his hero try to bet on the fish.
- Harness racing through a thoroughbred player's eyes: the same eight horses every week, the favorite that keeps winning, and the one rule he'd find hilarious — that in this sport your horse loses by running. The break, the horse pulled off the pace for galloping, would be a running gag and probably the climax.
- The rail. He wrote a whole late column about it: The rail was where imperfect strangers quickly became like close friends, where a person might spill his guts out to somebody he had never met, explaining who he liked in the next race, why he liked it and why he needed the money so desperately. Running Aces has benches, a rail, and no grandstand — a Cronley paradise, and a chapter.
- The hotel with the oval out the window, so a man can lose from bed without getting dressed; the banquet rooms named Jack, Queen and King, and a wedding party drifting across the walkway to the card room between the toast and the cake.
- The 18-and-over rule and the dollar blackjack at 3 a.m.; the stoopers on the floor after the last race; the announcer high in the booth composing a new poem every twenty minutes for people who are not listening.
- The Charlie Daniels night — thousands on the grass singing along to a bet the devil lost — which he'd treat as the property's founding legend and the last time the house came out behind.

He'd be gentle with the marks and merciless with the experts, as always: The Picker's Pledge is to bet two bucks on what you print. Messing with more money than that is too serious and gets too personal. The tip-sheet man would get it worse than anyone. And the ending would be a win — his books end with the win, the getaway, the house sold — probably a Minnesota-sired pacer named something like the real mare Over The Moon For You, paying just enough to cover the trout.

What he wouldn't do. He wouldn't write about harm, policy, or the tribes' side of the ledger; he was a horseplayer's humorist, not a moralist, and "Horseplayers are a private lot. Sometimes sharing is like publishing the transcript of a therapy session." He'd also almost certainly never mention a slow backlit artwork at the end of a hallway, except to have his hero walk past it on the way to the window, look at it for one paragraph, and decide it was a sign.

Gloria Steinem died September 2, 2026, at her home in New York City. She was 92. Her foundation's statement said "Gloria's greatest gift was her ability to listen to others, to make others feel seen and heard." So what follows is inference from the record she left — and, as with the others, she never wrote a line about Running Aces or, as far as I can find, about gambling as a subject.

Where she would start — and it is uncanny how close it is. Her father, Leo, ran a summer dance pavilion on a Michigan lake. Though there was no ocean within hundreds of miles, he had named it Ocean Beach Pier, and given it the grandiose slogan "Dancing Over the Water and Under the Stars." On weekends, he booked the big dance bands of the 1930s and 1940s into this remote spot. And then, in her own words, "there was always a sense of gambling. I think my father loved that." When the season ended, the family drove south buying and then selling junk or antiques or whatever, earning enough of a profit to make it to the next town. A Steinem essay on this ground would open on the Trout-Air lawn — the family ponds, the giant fish by the highway, the big acts on the grass, the summer-only racing where rain empties the apron — because she had lived that exact economy as a child. She would be tender toward the small dreamers, the Preiners and a card-room man from Los Angeles who renamed a failing track an entertainment center and dug a trout pond, and considerably less tender toward the lender that emerged as majority owner when the dream missed a payment. Reviewers noted the memoir credited her father's ability to live with and even love insecurity as what let her become a freelance writer. She'd see him at the rail.

The horses. She would love that the breed is admitted by the clock and not by pedigree — performance over bloodline is her whole politics — and that the winningest horse on the continent one season was a Minnesota mare. She would ask who drives her, who owns her, and who holds the lines.

How it would end. Steinem wrote the way she organized: by listening first, in circles, and by refusing the sentence "it is what it is." She would want to sit at the rail with the Tuesday regulars and in the card room at closing, and she would close on the practice she took from Native women — the circle rather than the pyramid — and on hope as a plan rather than a mood. The wall at the end of the hallway would get a paragraph, and probably a kind one: an object that asks nothing of the people standing in front of it, in a building that asks a great deal. Whether she'd have thought a wish with nothing riding on it was enough — she'd have said no, and then said it was a start.

Tom Waits is the one of these four who has actually written about Minnesota — twice — and he is alive and working, so this inference has more to stand on. Status first: the now 76-year-old Waits released "Boots on the Ground" with Massive Attack this April, marking his first original material since 2011's Bad As Me; a Small Change 50th-anniversary reissue is due October 2, and an Italian docuseries about homelessness in the American South features Waits reading one of his poems. He has never written a word about Running Aces.

The Minnesota he already has. "Christmas Card from a Hooker in Minneapolis" (1978) is a woman's letter full of good news that turns out to be lies, ending with her parole date on Valentine's Day — and the first race ever run at Running Aces was won by a horse named Valentine Hotspur. He would not let that go. "9th & Hennepin" (1985) came from a night in a downtown Minneapolis donut shop: a twelve-year-old pimp, forks hurled into the street, automatic weapon fire cracking the mirror, and "Our Day Will Come" on the jukebox. Introducing it in 1999 he complained that the corner had been cleaned up — "It went from scary... to kinda fun! And it really kinda upset me." That sentence is the key to how he'd treat a smoke-free, family-friendly, 18-and-over, 24-hour casino: a scary thing made fun, which is the one transformation he distrusts.

The form it would take. Not an essay in paragraphs. His only long prose "interview" is the one he did with himself — Anti Records' 2008 "Tom Waits' True Confessions," which opens by pretending to fear his gambling debts, his animal magnetism, coupled with his disregard for the feelings of others and proceeds as Q&A, lists and tall tales. So the Running Aces piece would be a self-interrogation, a rumors-and-corrections sheet (he just posted a "Sets the Rumors Straight" notice on his own site), an inventory of objects, and a few invented residents — the way he peopled Frank's Wild Years and the Black Rider. He would present true facts as if he'd made them up, and his standard would be the one he gave Vanity Fair: "bad writing is destroying the quality of our suffering."

What he'd inventory. He writes in nouns, and this property is a junkyard of the right nouns. The fiberglass fish by the interstate. A pond kept at 55 degrees by a bubbler where a Scout hands you a pole and nobody may throw one back. A house drawn for slot machines that never arrived, so the horses stayed — a casino with the machines removed is, to him, a bar with the ashtrays removed, and he'd say which loss he minds more. The one-armed bandit's absence and the slop bucket's presence. Banquet rooms named Jack, Queen and King; a comedy night called Laugh Your Ace Off, which he'd quote with a wince. Ice karts on the frozen oval; a drive-in in the parking lot; a hotel that opened for five days and went dark. The announcer high in a booth composing a new poem every twenty minutes — the auctioneer-preacher voice he has imitated his whole career, from "Step Right Up" on. The paper: losing tickets torn and dropped, a floor of wishes swept before morning, and the stoopers who walk it first — those are his people, and they'd get the tender treatment, never the joke.

The found instruments. He hunts sounds the way other men hunt game, so the essay would stop for the two-beat gait (harness racing as a rhythm section), the hiss of forty wheels, the bells on a trotting harness worn because a sleigh in snow is silent — and the fact that the most-sung song on earth is a harness-racing song with a trotter's mile time in its last verse would delight him past reason. The sandhill crane, older than the ice, whose windpipe coils into its breastbone so the bird is its own violin, would get a full page. So would the hognose snake that plays dead when the bluff fails.

The devil, who belongs to him. The Black Rider is his Faust — a clerk, a bargain, magic bullets — and this ground's biggest night was thousands on the grass singing the story of a wager with the devil that the human wins. He'd write it as local history: the last time the devil lost in Anoka County, and the house lost with him, and everybody went home happy.

The casino itself. He is the man who sued a chip company for imitating his voice in an ad, so "Stay, Play & Party" and a loyalty card that pays out in meal vouchers would draw his dry contempt, and he'd be sharpest about the daylight — a windowed casino, to him, is a magician showing the trapdoor. But he'd also refuse the sermon. The 2011 record about bailing out millionaires shows where his sympathy sits: with the people at the dollar table at 3 a.m., not with anyone's policy.

The wall. In the 2008 self-interview he describes owning a Marcel Marceau record — forty minutes of silence followed by applause — and adds, "It really bothers me, though, when people talk through it." A silent, slow, hand-drawn artwork at the quiet end of a casino hallway is that record hung on a wall, and I think he'd say so, in about that many words, and then walk down to the rail to see what the horses were writing in the lime.

Richard O'Brien wouldn't write it as an essay, and the honest answer to "how far would he go" is: all the way to a stage, with one hard stop I'll get to. But first, what's true about him right now, because it shapes the piece.

Where he is. He's 84, Tauranga-based, and still writing: two years ago he announced a new satirical musical, The Kingdom of Bling, inspired by the madness of American politics — a cautionary tale with the wacky hallmarks of Rocky Horror while featuring giants, a menacing fox, trolls, and the king of the land, the obnoxious 'The He', set in the style of a radio play, starring O'Brien as The Narrator, with a second act Q&A, and 100% of all royalties going to the Starship Children's Hospital in perpetuity. The Rocky Horror Show returns to New Zealand for the first time in 15 years this year — including Christchurch, which happens to be the trotting capital of his adopted country. And a week ago he buried his collaborator: Tim Curry died aged 80, and O'Brien called him "a good egg and class act." Anything he wrote this month would carry that.

Why this place would grab him. Three biographical facts do the work. He was picked on at school for his small size, but became proficient on a horse, and his horse skills got him employment as a stuntman in Carry On Cowboy, 1966, and Casino Royale, 1967 — so a jockey-sized man who got into show business riding horses for a film called Casino Royale has a first paragraph handed to him. His accountant father had purchased a sheep farm in Tauranga: he knows barns, manure shovels and the honest end of a property. And he is a New Zealander of the trout country, where trout cannot legally be bought or sold — a restaurant called Trout Air Tavern serving pond-to-plate rainbow trout at so much a pound would strike him as a small, delicious American scandal.

The form. Not prose. Rocky Horror was built from comics, science fiction movies and rock'n'roll, and the new show is a narrated radio play; so the Running Aces piece would be a Narrator's script with a lectern and a bow tie, in the manner of the film's Criminologist — a "case" laid out with exhibits. Exhibit A: a couple with a flat tire in the summer rain that empties the apron, walking up a long, wide corridor toward a light at the end. He has already written that arrival once. The castle is a casino, the corridor is the hallway, and the thing at the end is the floor show.

What he'd say, exhibit by exhibit. The house drawn for slot machines that never came, so the horses stayed — he'd play that as the castle's secret laboratory with the monster missing from the slab. The legislature and the lawsuits would be Denton: in Shock Treatment he turned a wholesome American town into a television station where everyone lives on camera, so "Stay, Play & Party," the loyalty card, the odds board, the 24-hour screens, and the marketing slogan about high vibes would get the Denton treatment, and the state's guardians of morality — the ones who banned the machines but keep the takeout — would get the Dr. Everett Scott treatment. He'd notice the banquet rooms named Jack, Queen and King and cast the Queen as the only one worth talking to. He'd notice the patron's favorite painting is a fox alone on snow, and since his current villain's court features a menacing fox, the fox would get a musical number. He'd notice the wishbone, the devil, and the night on the lawn when thousands sang a song about a wager the devil lost — and this is where the costume comes out: by the late 1980s, Richard was concentrating on music again and created his alter ego, Mephistopheles Smith, a devil from Club Inferno, otherwise known as Hell, and one critic remembered him as resplendent in pointed tail and cloven hooves. Mephistopheles Smith would narrate the Trout-Air chapter in person, standing on the grass where the house's biggest crowd watched the devil lose, and he'd lose again, graciously, in hooves.

How far, theatrically. Farther than an essay, short of a spectacle. The Crystal Maze gives him the second half: the casino as a maze of zones, the timed games, and the Crystal Dome finale, where contestants grab flying gold tokens in a wind, restaged with the night's torn losing tickets blowing off the track floor before the sweepers come. He'd want audience participation at the rail, callbacks to the announcer's race call, a Time Warp on the apron. And he'd want to narrate it himself, with the Q&A after, because that's what he does now.

The hard stop. His targets are always the pompous — The He, the Dentons, the Everett Scotts — never the people Brad and Janet become. He would not put the losers in the piece as a joke; the 18-year-olds at the dollar table and the stoopers on the floor would be treated as the creatures who get to stay for the party, which is the whole moral of his most famous show. The tender note would come from a barber from Hamilton who knows what it is to be small in a room of big men, and from a week of grief for the man who made his monster human. And at the end of the hallway he would do something surprising for a man who invented the floor show: he'd bring the lights down, turn the Narrator to face the slow wall, and give it the silence — the one thing in his career he's never staged. Then he'd give the royalties away.
