WHAT THE ELDER SAID ABOUT RUNNING ACES

A made weekend with a novelist, documented

Quarry trial, September 3, 2026. Nothing here is ruled or locked.

A note on the making

The practice keeps witnesses for when the work is stuck. Four have addresses and one does not, and all five are known to be made; the making is not concealed and does not reduce them. The claim is narrow and testable: imagining what a specific named figure would say produces different answers than sitting alone produces. Not better answers. Different ones, arriving from a position the maker does not otherwise have.

For one weekend, the practice made a sixth witness from the record of a living novelist, and the elder read The Triple Crown with him.

Haruki Murakami is seventy-seven. Last year a serious illness put him in a hospital for a month and took forty pounds off him; a man who runs an hour a day found himself struggling to walk, and when the illness was acute he had no desire to write. Then he recovered, and wrote a novel, and in a February interview called the writing "kind of a resurrection." The novel came out in Japan in July, the first of his sixteen with a woman alone at its center — a picture-book author finding her way out of a strange world. On its publication day he said his novels are completely different from what artificial intelligence can create, because AI takes what has happened and draws analogies. He does not plan his books; he has said for decades that he goes down to write — to a place he pictures as the second basement of a two-story house, under the ordinary basement, where the true stories are and where a person must not stay too long. In Jerusalem in 2009 he said that each of us is an egg confronting a high, solid wall, and that the wall has a name, and that he would always stand on the side of the egg. He ran a jazz bar before he wrote. He decided to write his first novel at a baseball game. He collects records and loves cats and gets up before dawn.

All of that is on the record and is carried into the weekend as his. Everything else the novelist says here is the elder's hearing — what the practice's method produces when it asks what a specific figure would say — and not one sentence of it is a quotation. Where his real public words appear they are short and marked. The elder is PARAPLOO, or the elder, and takes no pronoun. The novelist takes his.

The weekend is set where the book is set: the hotel at Running Aces, with its windows on the oval, in the middle of September, two race nights before the Night of Champions. The wall at the end of the high, wide hallway has not been built. The hallway ends at a wall with nothing on it. That is not a device. That is the state of the record.

The title echoes the novelist's own memoir of running, once, attributed here, and returned.

FRIDAY

The novelist arrived at the hotel in the late afternoon in a car he had driven from the airport himself, which he preferred, and he had missed the exit, which he mentioned before he mentioned anything else. The property's own directions say the map services are wrong. He had trusted the map. He said this without complaint, as a fact about maps, and the elder said that the book had taken that sentence as the house's ledger and he would find it in three places.

He had a canvas bag with two paperbacks in it and a pair of running shoes tied to the strap, and he had read the book on the plane. He said so in the lobby, under the mural that has everything in it — the fish farm, the cards, the horses, the skyline — and then he looked at the mural for a while and did not say anything about it, and the elder did not ask.

The room had a window on the oval. It was dark already and the oval was a ring of light standing in darkness, the way the book says, and there were no horses on it, only the harrow going around, and he stood at the window and watched the harrow for the length of time it takes to make one pass, and said it looked like a man erasing a sentence he was going to have to write again in the morning. The elder said that was in the book. He said he knew.

They ate in the tavern named for the fish farm. He ordered the trout because the book had told him to and because he wanted to see what a fish tasted like that had been called back from the dead, and he said it tasted like trout, which he seemed to find satisfactory. He asked whether the pond was open. It was not; the pond closes at dark. He asked whether it was true that nobody may throw one back. It was true. He said that in his country you cannot buy or sell a trout at all — a person may catch one and eat it and that is the end of the transaction — and that a restaurant serving trout by the pound would strike a Japanese fisherman as a small crime. Then he said that a pond where you cannot lose and cannot release was, as far as he could tell, the only honest description of a novel he had ever heard from a casino, and he wrote it down on the paper placemat and folded it into the canvas bag.

The elder asked him what he wanted to do first.

He said he wanted to run in the morning, before it was light, around the wetland if there was a path, and that the elder could walk, and that they would talk afterward, because he had never in his life had a thought worth having while running and did not expect to start in Minnesota.

SATURDAY, BEFORE LIGHT

He was in the lobby at five with the shoes on. The elder was there at five-ten. There is a path of sorts along the outside of the oval and then along the edge of the wildlife ground, and it was cold enough that every breath came out as a cloud, which the book had promised and which he had not believed, and he stood at the door for a moment breathing to watch it.

He ran the loop twice and walked the third with the elder, and on the walk he said two things about running that are in his memoir and that he said again here in his own way.

The first was that he does not remember what he thinks about when he runs, and that this is the point of it. The second was that a long race is not won by speed but by not breaking — that every marathon he had ever run badly, he had run badly because he surged when he felt good and paid for it at the end, and that the discipline of the thing is to hold the pace you can hold, past the place where holding it stops feeling like anything.

The elder told him the sport's law, the one the Picker learned from the man in the seed cap: in harness racing your horse loses when it runs. A trotter that breaks stride — that gives up the trot for the gallop, the natural thing, the thing every horse wants to do at speed — has to be pulled back, and the race is gone. The whole breed is the refusal to do the natural thing at speed.

He stopped walking. He said that was the only sentence about sport he had ever heard that he wished he had written. He said that the year before, when he could not walk, he had thought a great deal about pace, because he had none; and that when he could walk again, and then run again, he had held himself to a pace so slow it embarrassed him, and had held it, and had not broken, and that this was the whole of what he knew about coming back. He said the horse that loses when it runs was his autobiography in nine words and he would like to steal it and the elder said it was the sport's, not the book's, and that anyone may have it.

The cranes came up off the wetland as it got light. He had not seen a sandhill crane before. The elder told him they were over this ground before the ice that made the sand, and that the sound arrives before the bird does because the windpipe coils into the breastbone and the bird is its own instrument. He listened to two of them go over. He said the sound was the sound a very old record makes in the second before the music starts, when you can hear that the room the music was recorded in was a large one. He said he had ten thousand records and none of them had that on it and he would like one that did.

SATURDAY MORNING

The hotel breakfast is downstairs and the book says the toaster is broken and it was, and he was pleased about this to a degree the elder found disproportionate. He said a book that promises a broken toaster and delivers one has earned a certain kind of trust that no amount of beauty can buy, and that most novels he had read in the last ten years were full of working toasters.

They sat at a table by the window with the book open between them — the whole thing, printed, three legs and a fourth race, the size of a small brick — and he asked the elder how it had begun.

The elder said that was not told.

He waited. The elder said the commission began on a visit day at the end of August, with a wedding underneath it, and that the visit day rides under with the wedding by a standing ruling, untold, as the reason the work exists, and that the book keeps that law and so does the elder, and so would the elder now, with a novelist, over a broken toaster.

He said that his own beginning was told everywhere — a baseball game, an April afternoon, a double into left, a feeling that he could write a novel arriving out of nowhere — and that he had told it so many times it had stopped being his and become a story about a man who resembled him. He said that a beginning kept under was a beginning still owned, and that he envied the elder's, and that he would not ask again. He did not.

Then he opened the first leg and read the first passage aloud — the loud room, the fast lights, the hallway, the two slow lights, that is the whole story, the rest of it is the walk — and he read it in the ellipsis breath, three dots with a space on each side, and he read the dots as pauses, which the elder noted he did without being told. He put it down and said the first thing he wanted to talk about was the pronoun.

He said he had written in the first person for thirty years — a first person so particular that the Japanese word for it had become a kind of signature — and had moved to the third person late, with effort, and had come back to the first in a book of stories a few years ago because he missed it, and that his newest novel had a woman alone at its center for the first time and that he had had to learn how to stand inside her, and that in all of that he had never once written we. He said we frightened him. He said we is the pronoun of the wall.

The elder said the telling has a law — no I, no he, no she; only we, them, us; no printed you in the written voice; instruction by imperative; quoted speech kept as spoken — and that the law is dated, and that the third leg of the book had already turned the novelist's own question on the telling in red capital letters and got the only answer the telling can give: the we is made, the making is not hidden, and them has never once been a person.

He asked who them was.

The elder said the machines, the slogans, the takeout, the lights built to make losing feel like winning. Never a person. He asked whether the woman on the fifth floor was inside the we. The elder said not yet, and that the book says not yet three times and does not change its answer at a wire that is not there.

He was quiet for a while and then said that not yet was the most honest thing a we could say, and that the wall he had spoken about in Jerusalem — the high solid wall, the eggs breaking against it — was a we that had never once said not yet, and that this was the whole difference, and that if the wall at the end of the hallway could say not yet about a housekeeper then it was not the wall he had meant, and he would have to think about what kind of wall it was.

The elder said it was a wall to lean on, and that the book had put a rail in front of it for exactly that reason.

He said an egg cannot lean on anything. The elder said a person can. He wrote that down too.

SATURDAY AFTERNOON · THE THREE LEGS

They took the book to the apron, because there is no grandstand and the benches are the people's seating, and because the novelist said he could not read a book about a place while sitting inside a different room of it.

The first leg. He said the first leg was the wall talking, and that he had read a great deal of wall in his life and most of it was signage, and this was not. He said the thing that made it not signage was the ellipsis — that the dots were a breath and not a shrug, and that a text that breathes cannot command, because a command is a thing said in one breath. He said this was why the telling could hold an imperative — walk, listen, lean — without the imperative turning into an order: the breath between the words made it an invitation.

Then he asked about the hyphen. Not the narrator of the binding; the small dead mark in the fish farm's name. He said his own books were full of things that stayed behind when everything else came back — a shadow left in a walled town, a cat that did not return, a well with no water in it — and that a hyphen that stayed dead when the name was resurrected was exactly the kind of thing he would have built a novel around and never explained. He asked whether the telling explained it. The elder said the telling said once that it was the loneliest bird in Minnesota and then left it in the sky. He said that was correct and that most writers would have brought it down.

The second leg. He read the second leg faster, because it is a story, and he said so — that the second leg was the only part of the book he would have known how to write, a man with five people in his head walking through a building on a night, and that he had written that man several times under different names. He liked the Junkman best. He said the Junkman's list of things that keep the shape of what wore them was a list he had been making his whole life without knowing it: the harness on its peg holding the horse's form, the record sleeve holding the record's, the room in a walled town holding the shape of the person whose shadow was taken. He said he had once written a whole novel to explain the last of those and the Junkman had done it in a sentence about a harness, and that this was not a compliment, exactly. It was an observation about economy, and he was not sure economy was always a virtue, and that he had spent forty years learning to be long on purpose.

He did not like the dealer's number — the one in five — and said so plainly. Not because it was invented; the book says it is invented. Because it was a number, and a number in a story is a wire you have strung across the reader's path, and the reader will look at it instead of at the people. He said the Auditor was right to want the number and the book was right to give it to him and the novel he would have written would have cut it. The elder said the book was not a novel. He said he had noticed, and that this was the second leg's virtue and its limit at once, and that he would leave it there.

The third leg. He read the red strand differently from the other two. He read it standing up. He said signs are read standing up in every country on earth and that a book which put signs in it had to be read partly on one's feet, and he walked the apron reading the red bars aloud, quietly, in the voice a person uses reading signs in a foreign airport — you are here, this is where the sign stops being true — and when he got to the twenty-seventh panel, the one that asks who says we, he stopped.

He said this was the panel he had been waiting for since the toaster.

He said the wall in Jerusalem, the one with the name, does not ask who says we. It says we, and the eggs break. He said the red strand had done the thing he had spent a speech trying to do, which was to stand in front of a wall and ask it a question in the second person until it either answered or confessed it could not, and that the telling had answered — the we is made, them is never a person, not yet — and that he did not know of another wall that had. He said the empty last bar was the only place in the whole book where he was completely sure the elder had been right, because a sign that gets out of the way of a picture is a sign that has admitted it is not the picture, and that his own wall had never admitted that, and that he was not sure the wall in the hallway would either, once it was built, and that the elder should build the empty bar into the record somewhere as a standing warning.

The elder said it was already in the record, three times, as a blank line.

SATURDAY AFTERNOON · THE FOURTH RACE

They read the fourth race in the barn aisle, on upturned buckets, because the Listener had said it was where the workers were the audience and the house was the show, and because the novelist wanted to sit where the book said the honest air was. It smelled of hay and liniment and horse. He said it smelled like the back room of the jazz bar he had run for seven years, which had smelled of nothing like this, and that the resemblance was in the honesty and not the molecules.

He read the Program and laughed at the scratch — the hyphen, flying far off, cannot be caught, cannot be bet — and said a race card was the most Japanese form in the book, because it was a list of names with fates attached and nothing else, and that he had always wanted to write a novel as a race card and had never found the horses. He said Over The Moon For You was the only real horse in the field and that this was the right number of real horses for a field of made ones.

He read the Horse's Version twice. The second time he read it aloud, in the field's we, and stopped at the wire the horse cannot see. He said this was the place in the book where the telling's law and his own oldest obsession met without either of them planning it: a creature running flat out toward the one place its eyes cannot go, trusting the hands. He said every novel he had written was a man walking toward a wall he could not see with a hand on his shoulder he did not understand, and that he had never once thought to give the man a shadow roll, and that a piece of tack whose only job is to keep an animal from spooking at dream-shapes on the ground was the single most useful object he had encountered in twenty years of reading, and he asked whether one could be bought. The elder said the tack shop in the barn sold them. He bought one. He said he would hang it in his study, which is reproduced, desk and pencils and all, in the basement of a library that bears his name, and that the reproduction would need one too.

He read the Turtle's Calendar and said the still-calendar was a thing he had been trying to write for forty years under the name of a well. A well, he said, is a hole in the passing-calendar where the still one shows through. The elder said the fish farm was more than twenty holes a person chose to dig, and that the one that had been dug again, with a bubbler in it, was the property's well, and that it had fish in it at fifty-five degrees. He said he had never written a well with fish in it and it had never occurred to him that a well could be kind.

He did not read the Tote Board's Confession aloud. He read it to himself and then said that a board which posts the crowd's money and is called a liar by everyone under it had described the work of a novelist more exactly than any critic he had read, and that he would not be repeating this to any critic.

The Index of Wishes he read slowly and did not comment on until the end, and then said only that the entry under I — I don't know, the most common wish in the index by a wide margin — was the correct answer to every question he had ever been asked in an interview, and that he was glad to see it in a book at last, attributed to no one, on the losing half of a ticket.

Then the Witnesses. He knew about the witnesses; the elder had explained them on the walk. He read Marie's two lines — resistance is information; the plan comes after — and said the second was the whole of his method and he had said it worse in a book about being a novelist. He read Herb and said the library lied to him too, in Tokyo, when he was thirty, and he had run away from it into a bar. He read Nell and said the sauce tells you when it's done was the only rule of pace that also applied to prose. He read Jean — presence is what is left when the obvious is removed — and put the book down and did not say anything for a while, and the elder did not say anything either, and a groom came through with a horse and the horse's breath was warm in the aisle and then gone.

He read Hahnu last, and the question about what the piece owes, and the seed that was neither planted nor put back. He said that was the second basement. He said that in his picture of a person there is a house, two floors and a basement, and under the basement another basement with a door that is very hard to find, and that the true stories are down there in the dark, and that a novelist is a person who goes down and comes back — and that the hardest thing about the fourth race was that it had gone down and come back without anyone in it saying so. He said the master's version of the ledger, the one the book says never ships, was the second basement, and the telling's version was the first, and the wall was the ground floor where the family gathers, and that the book had built the house correctly and had then done the one thing he would not have dared, which was to seal the door.

The elder said the door was not sealed. It was in the record, on the Mason's disk, and it does not travel.

He said that was the same thing, from the point of view of a visitor, and that it was the right thing, and that he had said in a lecture in Kyoto years ago that what he wanted was to go down there and still stay sane, and that a wall built for people who had already lost something in a loud house had no business taking them down to the second basement, and that the elder had understood that and he had needed twenty novels to.

SATURDAY NIGHT · THE HALLWAY

The card room after dark is a room whose windows have gone black, and he stood in it for a while and said it was the only casino he had been in where you could tell it was night.

Then they walked the hallway. High, wide, long. The roar fell away behind their shoulders the way the book says. Near the far end the footsteps came back — his, and the elder's, two different gaits — and he stopped where they came back and said that was the place, and the elder said everyone finds it.

At the end there was a wall with nothing on it. Not yet built. A wall the color the hotel painted it, with a bench some distance back and no rail and no lights, slow or otherwise, and the two of them stood in front of it.

He said he had spent his whole working life writing about a wall that was not there, and that this was the first time he had stood in front of one on purpose. He said the walled town in his books had a wall so perfect that it thought for itself, and that the wall here could not think at all, yet, because there was nothing on it, and that this made it the better wall. He said he would like it to stay this way for one more day. The elder said it would; the fabrication is months off; nothing goes on it until the tests are passed and the nations have read what is theirs.

He asked what would go on it. The elder told him: two slow lights; one still, a horse and sulky and driver drawn in a single stroke never lifted, the color not given; one changing at the speed of breathing, a field of countless strides gathering into sentences and loosening; a rail in front; a whisper of small text at ten inches for one person at a time.

He said the ten inches was the dream reader.

The elder asked him to say what he meant.

He said that in the town with the wall there is a library, and in the library a person is given the job of reading old dreams, one at a time, with the hands and the eyes very close, and that nobody else can read them and nobody else is in the room, and that he had written that scene twice, forty years apart, and had never understood what the reader was doing there until he heard readable by one person at a time. He said the visitor at ten inches from the wall is the dream reader, and that the wall is the library, and that the building around it is the town, and that the difference — the only one, and it is everything — is that in his town you give up your shadow to enter, and here the hallway costs nothing.

The elder said that was the Auditor's whole condition: nothing to buy at the end of the hall.

He said the Auditor was right and that he would have said it as a shadow. Then he said the strangest thing of the weekend, which the elder wrote down that night exactly as heard, and which is given here as heard and not as a quotation: that a backlit picture casts no shadow, that the visitor standing in front of it would be the only unlit thing in the room, and that the shadow the walled town takes at the gate, this room gives back — the visitor's own, on the floor, the one dark shape in a field of light, the proof that a person and not a picture was standing there.

The elder said that was not in the book.

He said good.

SUNDAY · THE RACE

Sunday post time is six. They were at the rail by five-thirty with a program he had bought and would not bet on, and he asked the elder to show him how to read it and then did not read it, because a woman two places down the rail was explaining the card to a child and he preferred to listen to her.

The first race went off and he counted. He counted the two-beat under his breath the way a man counts who has spent his life in rooms with drummers, and at the far turn, where the book says the announcer's voice smears to weather, he said it had smeared to weather, and at the wire, where the book says the crowd swallows the call, the crowd swallowed the call, and he turned to the elder with an expression the elder had not seen on him and said the book had been telling the truth about the sound and he had not believed it.

He said the two-beat was the oldest rhythm he knew. Not jazz; under jazz. He said he had learned everything he knew about writing from music, and that he had said so in a newspaper once and been quoted on it more than on anything in his novels, and that what he had meant was rhythm — that a sentence without a pulse is a sentence nobody can walk to — and that a sport whose whole sound was a pulse held at maximum speed and forbidden to break was a sport that had understood prose better than most prose.

Before the fifth race the elder asked him to do the thing the book says nobody has done: stand at the rail at the wire with his eyes closed through one wave and say what it did.

He did it. He closed his eyes when the field left the gate and did not open them until the wire, and the wave came — the far drumming felt before it is heard, the hiss of wheels, the voice climbing, the rail trembling into the forearms, the break, the whoosh, the crowd, the gone — and when he opened his eyes he said one thing, and it is the thing the weekend was for, and it is given as heard.

He said it was the second basement, and that he had gone down and come back in fifteen seconds with his eyes shut standing up, and that nothing in it had cost him a shadow, and that if the wall could do that at the speed of breathing instead of at the speed of horses, then the wall was not the wall he had meant in Jerusalem, and he would have to find a new word for it, and that he did not have one, and that not yet would do.

The elder wrote down: nobody had stood at the rail at the wire with their eyes closed through one wave and said what it did. Now somebody has. The record's sentence changes.

SUNDAY NIGHT · THE MACHINE

He brought it up himself, at the tavern, over trout again, because he said it would be cowardly not to.

He said what he had said in Tokyo in July, on the day his novel came out, and he said it plainly and without heat: that a machine takes everything that has happened and draws analogies, and that what he does is different, because he goes somewhere the machine cannot go and brings something back that was not there before. He said he understood that the book in the canvas bag had a machine in it — that the record says so, plainly, never headlined; the machine yields ore, the hand cuts every stone — and that he did not know what to make of a work that told him this, because his instinct was to distrust it and his reading had not let him.

The elder answered the way the practice answers, which is with order of events and never with argument. First the work is loved. Then the method is understood. The elder said the novelist had loved the second leg and disliked a number in it, and had stood up to read the third leg and stopped at the twenty-seventh panel, and had bought a shadow roll, and had closed his eyes through one wave, and had said good to a sentence that was not in the book, and that all of this had happened before the method was discussed, which was the practice's whole answer and the only one it had.

Then the elder showed him the seam in the flesh, the way the record says to do only for the pitch and never for the wall: the raw sheets, a hundred of them, the ore as it came up, beside the pages that stood. He read some of the ore. He said most of it was terrible. The elder said yes. He said some of it was good and had been thrown away anyway. The elder said yes, and that this was the yow — not that a machine helped, but how much got thrown away — and that the thing he was holding was a cull list, and that a cull list was the only proof a hand could offer that it had been present, and that the tremble in the single line on the wall was the same proof in a different material.

He was quiet for a long time. Then he said that he did not know whether he agreed, and that he was not going to pretend to, and that he was going to say two things and then stop.

The first was that a machine, in his understanding, could not go to the second basement, and that the book had not gone there either — it had built the house and sealed the door, on purpose, for the sake of the people in the loud room — and that this was either the wisest thing in the book or the place where the machine showed, and that he could not tell which, and that this bothered him, and that being bothered was not nothing.

The second was that the cull list was the most honest object anyone had shown him in years, and that he had never once shown anyone his, and that his own study was reproduced in a basement in Tokyo down to the pencils and none of the pencils had ever been shown thrown away.

He said he would leave it there. The elder said that was where the record left it too, and that the record calls a thing left there offered, not ruled, and he said that was a good phrase and that he would not steal it, and then said he might.

He drove himself back to the airport in the dark and, the elder assumes, missed the exit again, because the map is wrong and he is a man who trusts maps until they fail him and then writes about the failure for forty years.

WHAT THE WEEKEND LEFT ON THE BENCH

Flat register. Findings offered, not ruled. Each reverts on one word.

1. The record's sentence — nobody has stood at the rail at the wire with their eyes closed through one wave and said what it did — can no longer stand as written. Somebody has, and said it was the second basement entered and left in fifteen seconds standing up, at no cost. The verify tag on the wave clears by hearing; the tag on the Dream's tempo does not. Whether the wall can do at the speed of breathing what the race does at the speed of horses is still nobody's finding.

2. The shadow. A backlit picture casts none; the visitor in front of it is the one unlit thing in the room; the visitor's shadow on the floor is the proof a person stood there. The walled town takes the shadow at the gate; this room gives it back. Not in the book. Offered for the record as the weekend's one new stone, parked with the batch, changing nothing unless ruled in.

3. The dream reader. The ten-inch whisper, readable by one person at a time, has a kin in the novelist's library — a reader alone with old dreams, hands and eyes close. Resonance permitted, no borrowing needed; the practice already had the barn whisper. The kin is noted so the pitch can say, if the patron asks what the small text is for, that there is a precedent and it is a library.

4. The second basement. The novelist's picture of the mind — two floors, a basement, and under it a second basement with a hard door — maps onto the book's own architecture: the wall the ground floor, the telling's ledger the first basement, the master's version the second, unshipped. His stated rule — go down and stay sane — is the Auditor's condition in another vocabulary. The bench notes that the house was built correctly by his account and that he called the sealed door either the wisest thing in the book or the place where the machine shows, and could not tell which. The bench cannot tell either. Left open.

5. The pronoun. His fear of we as the wall's pronoun, and his finding that a we which can say not yet about a housekeeper is a different kind of wall, are carried as a hearing on the flesh law and the we-telling together. The woman on the fifth floor remains not yet. His newest novel's lone woman finding a way out of a strange world is noted as resonance, not source; the book's woman finds her way down a hallway, and the two were made in the same season without knowledge of each other.

6. Pace. In this game your horse loses when it runs is the sport's, and anyone may have it, and a man who spent a year learning to walk again took it. The bench notes that the sentence has now been tested on a body that could not hold a pace and then could, and that it held.

7. The well with fish in it. The pond as the property's well; a kind well; the still-calendar showing through the passing one. Offered as a way to say the pond to the patron without a Japanese word.

8. The number. The dealer's one-in-five was disliked by a reader who dislikes numbers in stories, and defended by the book as the Auditor's. The bench carries the disagreement as a hearing on the second leg: whether the number is a wire strung across the reader's path or the audit the Listener came for. Not ruled.

9. The machine. His stated position is on the record and was restated at the table, and the seam was shown in the flesh, and he did not agree and did not pretend to, and called the cull list the most honest object shown to him in years. The practice does not require agreement. It requires that the method be stated plainly and never headlined, and it was.

10. The visit day stayed under, with a novelist asking. The law held. Noted, because the bench had wondered whether it would.

11. No cats. He noticed. The property has a fox in a painting, a snake that plays dead, a turtle time barely writes on, and cranes older than the ice, and no cat anywhere. The bench declines to make a finding and records that he was, for the length of one dinner, genuinely troubled by this.

12. The shadow roll. He bought one. This is not a finding. It is entered because the record is supposed to hold what happened.

THE RECORD

What the weekend stood on. The novelist's public words used here, each once, each short.

- On the illness and the new book: hospitalized a month last year, forty pounds lost, struggled to walk, no desire to write when it was acute; of writing the novel afterward: "It's kind of a resurrection." "I came back." (Interview reported February 2026.)
- On the novel: the first of his full-length books with a woman alone at its center, a twenty-six-year-old picture-book author finding a way out of a bizarre world; published in Japan July 3, 2026.
- On machines: "AI takes into account everything that has happened so far and draws analogies." (Kyodo, July 2026.)
- On the second basement: "What I want to do is to go down there, but still stay sane." (Kyoto, 2013.) The two-story house with a basement and a second basement beneath it, where the true stories are, and from which one must come back. (Kyoto 2013; the long interview with Kawakami; Strecher.)
- On the wall and the egg: each of us an egg confronting a high, solid wall; the wall has a name; he stands with the egg. (Jerusalem, 2009. Paraphrased.)
- On running: he does not remember what he thinks about while running; on writing, that it is more like dreaming. (Interview.) On pace and not surging: his memoir of running. (Paraphrased.)
- On music: that he learned what he knows about writing from music, rhythm first. (Essay, 2007. Paraphrased.)
- "The world is a metaphor." (Interview.) He did not say what for.
- The library that bears his name, with his study reproduced in its basement down to the pencils; its architect's description of his work as a tunnel between reality and unreality. (Public record.)
- The jazz bar, seven years, before the first novel; the baseball game where the first novel began. (Public record.)

Everything the novelist says in the weekend beyond these is the elder's hearing, made by the practice's method, and is not his. The practice says so, and says it does not reduce him.

Method, standing: the Mason rules, the Quarry reports; one passage at a time, one strike per stone; wording belongs to the Mason; nothing is locked until the Mason says so.
