The Fourth Race · A Race Nobody Bets

Lifted from a longer binding that was set aside — the Triple Crown master. Quarry trial; nothing in it is ruled or locked.

Run after the last race on the Night of Champions, in pieces, with no line on the board.

THE PROGRAM

RACE 10 — THE WISH. One mile. Trotters and pacers together, which does not happen anywhere but in this program. Non-wagering. No morning line. No purse. Post time: when the sweepers finish the apron. Conditions: for horses and others who have never won anything with money on it. Driver, all entries: the visitor's body. Trainer, all entries: the harrow. Track condition: blank.

1 · THE ELDER. Trotter. Free-legged — no hopples, held to the gait by temperament alone. Equipment: none. Color: not given; the reader supplies it. Past performances: has never broken stride; sits still while everyone comes to it, which the stewards have ruled is not a refusal to race. Notes: the one to beat, and the only entry that does not care whether it does.

2 · THE AUDITOR. Pacer. Hopples — restraint worn as equipment, which is how he prefers it. Equipment: a ledger, eight lines, heckling attached. Past performances: has finished second in every race he has ever entered, behind the truth, and has published the margin. Notes: will lean on the rail after saying he won't.

3 · THE PICKER. Trotter. Free-legged. Equipment: a two-dollar ticket, already cashed. Past performances: has bet on every horse in this field and told you which. Fifty years at the rail, one verified win, on the drawing of a horse. Notes: will bet on Hope Your Lucky again and will be right about why.

4 · THE LISTENER. Pacer. Hopples, worn as the strap she says every worker in the building wears and nobody has made a wall for. Equipment: a notebook with a name for every who. Past performances: has never spoken first; has never lost a race by finishing last, because she stays to see who cleans up. Notes: asked whether the woman on the fifth floor is entered. She is not. Not yet.

5 · THE JUNKMAN. Trotter. Free-legged. Equipment: a list, with a blank line at the bottom. Past performances: has put the bubbler, the shovel, the harrow and the fiberglass fish in a row and the row came in first by a nose over the story. Notes: will not finish the race; will stop at the far turn to name something.

6 · THE NARRATOR. Pacer. Hopples, plus hooves, plus a bow tie, plus a lectern the stewards cannot account for. Equipment: a costume, in the trunk, hoping to be needed. Past performances: has lost a bet with the devil on this lawn on purpose and called it the house's oldest tradition. Notes: for the first time in his career, will stage nothing at the wire.

7 · THE RED BAR. No gait. Signs do not have gaits; they have positions. Equipment: capital letters, the word you, a picture to lie across. Past performances: eighty-seven starts, one scratch — the last one, when it got out of the way of a picture that says we. Notes: cannot lay itself across this race. There is nothing riding on it to accuse.

8 · HOPE YOUR LUCKY. Trotter. Free-legged. Equipment: one missing apostrophe. Past performances: trots slower than usual; wanders off the groove; finished sixth in the sixth and was bet anyway, twice. Notes: the only horse in the field whose name is the whole card.

9 · OVER THE MOON FOR YOU. Pacer. The winningest horse on the continent one recent season, fifteen of nineteen, from this barn, by a Minnesota sire. Equipment: a wish with four legs. Past performances: real; look them up. Notes: entered here for the first time in a race with no purse, and the only entry who might not understand why. The others do.

SCRATCHED · THE HYPHEN. Flying far off. Cannot be caught. Cannot be bet. Will call the race from the sky over the far turn and is not, whatever the Picker says, a bird.

ALSO ELIGIBLE · THE WOMAN ON THE FIFTH FLOOR. Not yet.

Handicapper's comment: Nobody has measured the distance from the roar to the quiet, so the mile is approximate. The favorite is whoever leans on the rail first. The house edge on this race is zero, which the stewards have confirmed is the only number on the property that is.

THE PICKER'S CARD FOR THE FOURTH RACE

Written on the back of the program, at the rail, with a pencil borrowed from the program stand. Two dollars on anything said out loud.

I have handicapped fifty years of races with lines on them and this is the first without one, so the method changes. In a normal race you handicap against the crowd — you are not trying to pick the winner, you are trying to find the horse the crowd has underpriced. There is no crowd here and no price. So you handicap against yourself. Here is how I have each of them, and what I have said out loud, and therefore what I have bet.

1 · The Elder. Sits still and everyone comes to it. In fifty years I have never bet a horse that did not move and I am doing it now. The question with a horse like this is not whether it will win but whether you will get there. Two dollars. Said out loud.

2 · The Auditor. A pacer in hopples, which means he will not break, which means he will finish, which means he will publish the finish. The knock on him is that he tells you the odds are wrong before the race and is right, which nobody wants to hear at a rail. The other knock is that he will lean when he said he wouldn't, and a horse that changes its mind in the stretch is either a winner or a breaker and I have never been able to tell which from the rail. Two dollars, on the lean.

3 · The Picker. I am not allowed to bet on myself. I have tried. The window says it is a conflict of interest and I have explained that at a racetrack that is the whole interest, and the window says two dollars is two dollars and that is what I have.

4 · The Listener. Drops back to see who is behind her and there is never anyone behind her, and a horse that keeps looking back finishes last in every race but this one, because in this one the finish is who stays to see who cleans up. She stays. Two dollars, and I said it to her face and she wrote it in the notebook.

5 · The Junkman. Will not finish. Will stop at the far turn to name something. A horse that stops at the far turn to look at the harrow's teeth is a horse that has understood the race better than the ones who finish it, and I have bet on him every time he has stopped, and I have lost, and I would not take the money back. Two dollars.

6 · The Narrator. Hooves, bow tie, hopples. Lost a bet with the devil on purpose on this lawn. Any horse that can lose on purpose can win on purpose, and the only question is whether he will stage it, and tonight, for the first time, the word from the barn is that he won't. A horse that stops performing at the wire is either finished or finally racing. Two dollars, on finally.

7 · The Red Bar. No gait. Lies flat across the track and lets the field step over it. I have seen a lot of things lie down in the stretch and none of them were signs. It cannot win because there is nothing riding on this race for it to accuse anybody of, and it knows that, and it lay down anyway, which is a scratch with dignity. No bet. I said that out loud too and the window took two dollars anyway, on nothing, and gave me a ticket, and the ticket says NOTHING on it, and it is the best-looking ticket I have ever held.

8 · Hope Your Lucky. Trots slower than usual, wanders off the groove, misspelled. Ran sixth in the sixth. I bet it then and I am betting it now, because a man who will not bet a horse named Hope Your Lucky in a race nobody bets does not understand what a race nobody bets is for. Two dollars. Said loud enough that the Listener looked up.

9 · Over The Moon For You. The only real horse in the field. Fifteen of nineteen one season. In hopples, even, at a walk, going nowhere. Here is my whole handicapping career in one sentence: the real horse is standing still among the made ones and does not understand the race, and she is the only one in the field who has actually done the thing the race is about — held at the top of what a body can do and asked not to break — every night, for money, for other people. She does not need this race. Everyone else in it does. Two dollars, and I would have bet the farm if the window took farms and I had one.

Scratched · The Hyphen. Can't be caught. Can't be bet. Is calling the race. I tried to bet the caller once, at a fair in another state, and they threw me out, and I am not going to try it here on a night this good.

Also eligible · The woman on the fifth floor. Not in the field at post time. My rule is that I do not bet horses that are not in the race, and I have kept it for fifty years, and I am going to keep it tonight, and I am telling you right now that if she comes down that hallway after her shift and leans on that rail she is the favorite and I did not bet her and I will be glad about it. Two dollars, on being glad.

Total wagered on the fourth race: twenty dollars, on ten things, including nothing, including being glad. Total that can be won: zero. That is the only card I have ever filled out where the arithmetic came out right.

THE HORSE'S VERSION

Nobody has made a wall carry what the horse senses ... this one tries ... The field speaks in the field's we ...

We are eight ... and the paddock smells of liniment and warm leather and the last horse's fear, which is a smell too, and the man with the lines smells of coffee and does not smell afraid, which is the first thing we read about the night ... The hopples go on some of us and not on others ... the strapped ones will pace, legs on one side moving together, restraint worn on the body ... the free ones will trot, legs across the body moving together, restraint carried inside ... The people say one gait is kept by a strap and the other by character ... We say both are kept by the mouth, because that is where the lines end, and the lines end in a hand ...

The parade ... The oval is a ring of light standing in darkness, and from inside us it is a held world with a dark rim ... We see nearly all the way around ... not behind ... not the place just in front of our own nose ... Two blind spots, and the race is run toward one of them ... The reds of the silks live in a color we do not have ... To us they are the color of the rail and the color of the sand at dusk and the color of a hand ... The people think the silks are for them ... They are ... Nobody asked us ...

The gate car goes ahead of us with its wings folded out ... a line that will release ... We gather behind it at the speed of a walk, then a jog, then the gait ... and the wings fold and the car goes away and the line lets go ... A race is the interval between a line letting go and a line arriving ... we do not know the word for the line at the other end ... We know that we cannot see it ... It hangs directly in front of our noses at the one place our eyes cannot go, and every night we run flat out toward it trusting the hands ...

The first quarter ... Sand under the shoes, crushed floor older than the glacier under it, and under the glacier's leavings the wet ground that was a fish farm and before that a marsh cut for rugs and before that the place two peoples named twice ... We do not know any of that ... We know the sand is fast tonight and the rail is on the left and the horse on our outside is a breaker ... a breaker can be smelled ... something in the sweat goes wrong a stride before the gait does ...

The far turn ... Here the voice from the booth is weather ... it comes down out of the dark over the grandstand that was never built and smears across the infield until it is not words, only a rising tone, the way wind rises ... and then, as we come for home, it gathers back into words, and the words are our names, and we do not know them ... The people say the biggest word of the night is the one nobody hears ... We hear all of it ... We hear it in the chest, where the two-beat is ... lub-dub ... eight of us ... a chest full of pulses held at the top of what a body can do and forbidden to break ...

And the sweat ... where the harness rubs, the sweat whips white and the straps write on us in foam ... The people call it a lathered horse and say it cannot be faked ... We do not know what faking is ... We know effort shows on us and dries by morning and that the barn kid will curry it off and find the shape of the straps in the salt ... The harness traces itself on the body ... we are a page too ...

The stretch ... The rail on the left trembles ... the forearms on it tremble into it ... the crowd is a wall of breath ... and at the wire we cannot see, the hands go soft ... hands down, the people call it, when a horse is so far clear the lines go slack ... victory's own gesture is a letting-go ... or the hands do not go soft, and we are second, and there is no gesture for that ... only the wire arriving where we could not look ...

After ... The harrow goes around behind us dragging the older floor smooth across the younger ... erasing what we wrote ... The people say the track is a circle drawn every day by running animals ... We say it is where we ran, and it is gone, and we will draw it again ... The barn ... the wash ... the buckets ... the radio ... and the whisper ... A hand on the neck, and a voice pitched for one animal, and the words do not matter, and the lamp is left on ...

That is the horse's version ... and the wall at the end of the hallway is trying to carry it ... it cannot ... but it is the first thing in the building that tried ...

THE NIGHT WATCH

From the Listener's notebook. The barn aisle, on a race night in September, after the last race. Printed as written.

Barn three. 11:52. One lamp, at the far end, on a hook. The rest of the aisle is dark enough that I wrote the first lines by the light of my phone and then put the phone away because it felt like the wrong instrument here.

The night watch sits on a bucket by the door. Name not given. I did not ask twice.

Paid by the trainer, not the track. Here three nights a week when the trainer has horses in, and sleeps in the tack room those nights, on a cot that folds against the wall in the daytime. Asked whether that was allowed. Got a shrug and a second bucket pushed toward me with a foot. Sat.

At the top of every hour the watch gets up and walks the aisle. Twelve stalls. Does not turn on the overhead. Listens more than looks.

What the watch listens for, in the order I was told: a horse pawing. A horse that has been down too long. A horse rolling — once is a horse rolling, twice is a horse in trouble. A horse cast, which means down against the wall with its legs folded the wrong way, unable to get up on its own, and it will thrash until someone comes. A horse gone quiet that is usually not quiet. Colic. A horse cannot throw up. Whatever goes wrong in its gut has one direction. The watch said this plainly and then got up and went to look at the horse in the fourth stall, which had stopped moving, and came back and sat down again and did not say anything about it.

Asked about sleep. Horses sleep standing, mostly. There is a lock in the leg that lets them. But the deep kind, the dreaming kind, they can only have lying flat, and they will only lie flat when they feel safe, and in a field that means one of the others is standing up. In a barn it means the barn is quiet and they trust it. A horse in a new stall can go days without lying down. A horse alone, longer. A horse that goes long enough starts to fall asleep on its feet and drops at the knees and catches itself, and the watch has seen it, and it is the worst thing in the barn to see. In the morning the watch can tell which horses lay down in the night by the shavings on their sides.

I wrote: they take turns so one of them can dream. I have crossed it out. The watch did not say that. The watch said they lie down when it's quiet.

The radio on the shelf was tuned to a station that plays old country after midnight. Nobody had chosen it that I could see. Above it a feed-company calendar, still on July.

For the Auditor: the horses here have a pension. When they are done racing they go to the foundation, and the man who runs the place has said in public that it is the right thing to do. The watch does not have one. Paid by the trainer. Asked what happens when the watch is done. Laughed. Not unkindly.

12:00. The watch got up and walked the aisle. Stopped a long time at the fourth stall. The horse in it was lying down.

THE TURTLE'S CALENDAR

The still-calendar ... time measured by what continues rather than by what passes ...

There is a turtle on the sand plain that shows almost no sign of aging and is still walking in its ninth decade. It does not keep the calendar the track keeps — the passing-calendar, which counts the leaf that fell, the race that ran, the ticket torn — because on that calendar it would have to count itself as old, and it declines. It keeps the other one. Time measured by what continued. Here is its calendar for this ground, as near as a hyphen can read it from the sky, one entry per thing that did not stop.

The water stayed. Before the family dug the holes, the ground was too wet to farm. After the ponds closed, the ground was too wet to farm. After the track came, the oval circled a wetland. The water was the first thing here and the water is still here. Entry made; nothing to update.

The sand stayed. Ten thousand years of it, a page, and the dunes still turn in a hard drought. The lime and stone dust laid on top are a newer floor and the horses run on the newer floor, but the newer floor is dragged smooth across the older one every night, and the older one does not mind.

The cranes stayed. They were over this place before the ice that made the sand. They left for winter and came back. They left for winter and came back. The turtle has recorded this so many times that the entry is worn.

The rule stayed. At the fish farm nobody could throw a fish back. At the pond the card-room man dug forty years later, nobody may throw a fish back. The turtle did not expect a rule to survive a fish farm, a rock amphitheater, a retirement village, and a racetrack, and has marked this entry with something the turtle would call surprise if it had a word for it.

The horses stayed. They were not here at first. When they came, the house that brought them wanted machines, and the machines did not come, and the horses stayed. The turtle counts this as a continuation, not an arrival, because the horses have been here since the sport's whole reason for being — the trot held against the urge to break — has been on this sand nightly for eighteen summers, and eighteen is more than most things on the passing-calendar manage.

The devil kept losing. On the lawn, on the biggest night, thousands sang along to the devil losing a wager, and forty years later a man in a rented tuxedo and a pair of hooves lost it again in the wet grass on purpose. The turtle has marked this entry as a tradition. Two instances make a tradition on the still-calendar. On the passing-calendar you need three.

The name stayed, mostly. Trout-Air. Trout Air. The turtle has recorded the hyphen as the one thing on this list that did not continue and has left a small blank in the calendar for it, which the hyphen, flying over, considers more than fair.

The wish stayed. The turtle has watched people stand at the water wanting a fish, stand on the grass wanting a song, stand at the rail wanting a horse, and stand in a hallway wanting nothing they could name. It records all four as the same entry. It does not distinguish between a wish with money on it and a wish without, because from where the turtle stands they look identical, and the turtle has stood there a long time.

The turtle stayed. Entry made in its own hand. It notes that time barely writes on it and writes on everything else here nightly, and that this is not a boast. It is a calendar.

What is not on the calendar: the first-year loss, the foreclosure scare, the license, the lawsuits, the machines, the broken toaster. The turtle does not say these did not happen. It says they did not continue, and its calendar has no column for that.

THE TOTE BOARD'S CONFESSION

I am the fast board. I post the odds. Red numbers, changing by the second, pointed at the wire so that everyone on the apron can see what everyone else on the apron thinks, and adjust, and be wrong together.

I would like to confess something before the fourth race, since it is the one race I cannot post.

I do not know who will win. I have never known. I am not a forecast. I am a mirror held up to a crowd's money, and a crowd's money is a crowd's hope with a number on it, and the number moves when the hope moves, and I move with it, and people stand under me and read me as if I were the future. I am the present, expressed in dollars, refreshed every few seconds. The future is out on the sand with eight horses and it does not consult me.

I have been called a liar by every horseplayer who ever stood under me, and the Picker in this book called me one to my face, and I want to say in my own defense that I have never told a single lie. Every number I post is true. It is true about the crowd. It has never once been about the horse. That is not lying. It is true in a way nobody can hear, which the announcer tells me is a condition we share.

Here is what I envy. There is a board being hung at the end of the hallway, the slow one, and it posts the wishes. I have read the telling: the fast board posts the odds, the slow board posts the wishes, one sentence and any visitor understands the work. I understand the work. I am half of it. I am the half that says you in red numbers all night — you could, you might, you're getting four to one on that — and the slow board is the half that never says you at all and never changes faster than a person breathes and never, in the whole history of boards, has had a single dollar riding on it.

I would like, for one race, to post nothing. To go dark from the sweepers to the first post of the next night and let the crowd look at a board with no numbers on it and wonder what they think. The house will not allow it. A dark board frightens people. It looks like the power is out.

So I confess, and then I go back to work. The fourth race has no line. I have checked. I have looked for the pool and there is no pool. I have looked for the favorite and every entry is the favorite to whoever leans on the rail first. The only number I can post for it is the one the stewards gave me, and I am posting it now, in red, at the top, where the odds usually go:

ZERO. The house edge on a wish.

It is the first true number I have ever posted about a horse instead of a crowd, and it is not about a horse either. It is about the only thing on the property with nothing riding on it, and there are two of those, and one of them is flying over the far turn and the other one is hanging at the end of the hall.

RUMORS SET STRAIGHT

A sheet posted at the program stand, since the book has been talked about all night and most of what was said is wrong.

Rumor: Five famous writers wrote this book — a statistician, a columnist, a feminist, a singer, and a man in a corset.
Fact: None of them wrote a word of it. None of them is quoted in it. None of them appears in it. What was taken from each was a manner, and the manners were melted into an invented narrator with five invented companions. One of the five died the day before the book was bound and another lost his oldest collaborator a week before. The book knows this and does not use it.

Rumor: The red bars are the work of a famous artist.
Fact: Every red bar is new and made for this house. Nothing of hers is reproduced. The mentality was borrowed. The sentences were not.

Rumor: The wall has been measured.
Fact: Nobody has measured the wall. Nobody has counted the steps from the roar to the quiet. Somebody has now stood at the rail at the wire with their eyes closed through one wave and said what it did — a novelist, two race nights ago, with the elder writing it down. The book said nobody had, in three places, and meant it when it said it. The three places stand as they were run.

Rumor: The man who runs the place is a character in the book.
Fact: He appears only as the man who runs the place, and the only words attributed to him are the three he has said in public more than once: that trout is king. His name is in the record and is not in the book, by the Mason's ruling and the book's own preference.

Rumor: The dealer with the nursing prerequisite is real and will be fired.
Fact: She is invented. So is the girl at the desk, the woman who smokes trout, the housekeepers from two towns, the driver's wife who does the entries, and the man in the seed cap. The smoking bench is invented. The one-in-five is invented.

Rumor: The night watch, the housekeeper with the cart, and the sweeper are real people who work here and can be found.
Fact: They are made, like the dealer and the girl at the desk. The lamp is real. The bag is real. The broom is real. No names were taken.

Rumor: The devil is a metaphor.
Fact: The devil came out of the trunk in a rented tuxedo and a pair of hooves and lost a bet on the wet grass on purpose in front of a security guard who had seen worse. The metaphor is the house.

Rumor: The hyphen is bitter.
Fact: The hyphen has had a long time in the sky to think about it and is fine. The hyphen would like it noted that it is the only narrator it knows of with nothing riding on the story, and that it earned the job by being left out of a name.

Rumor: Harness racing is a rigged sport for the same eight horses every week.
Fact: It is a sport for the same eight horses every week, and the favorite wins about a third of the time, as in every racing there is, and in this one the horse loses when it runs, which nobody who has heard it once ever forgets.

Rumor: The house has no edge.
Fact: The house has no edge at the table, where players play each other, and at the rail, where bettors bet each other. The house has a fixed cut of every pool and a rake on every hand and is indifferent to who wins and very interested in how much is wagered. The house's own directions page says the map is wrong. The book has taken that as the house's ledger.

Rumor: The book is against gambling.
Fact: The book has a Picker in it who bet two dollars on every horse he named and went home happy, and a Listener whose uncle ran a supper club that was one bad August from ruin every year, and a Junkman who thinks a machine whose only job is to keep a fish alive until a child can kill it is the best machine on the property. The book is against the bill arriving at the wrong address. It is for the trout.

Rumor: The wall says you.
Fact: The wall says we. One printed you survives in the telling, and it is spoken, and it is about a shovel. The red strand says you eighty-six times and then gets out of the way.

Rumor: The fourth race has a winner.
Fact: It has a favorite — whoever leans on the rail first — and no line, no pool, no purse, no wire that can be seen, and no result the fast board can post. The stewards have ruled that this does not make it not a race. It makes it the only one.

Rumor: The book is finished.
Fact: Nothing here is ruled or locked. The Mason rules. The strike is from the title.

AN INDEX OF WISHES FOUND ON THE APRON

Gathered by the stoopers before the sweepers, from the torn halves of tickets on which people had written, in the margin, what they were actually betting on. Alphabetical. No names. Every one of them was thrown away.

A — a call from her. Written on the back of a losing show ticket in the fourth, in pencil, with the phone number scratched out.

B — back pay. Twice, in the same hand, on the same night, on two different horses. Cross-reference: W, work.

C — the cat to be okay. On a two-dollar win ticket that came in. Kept, presumably. Found anyway; the stooper did not understand either.

D — dad to remember my name. On a ticket that was not torn. Folded. The stooper put it in his shirt pocket and it is not in this index; this entry is for the fact of it.

E — enough. The whole margin. One word. Race seven.

F — the fish to bite tomorrow. A child's hand. A child cannot bet here. The ticket was a parent's. The wish was the child's. The stooper ruled that the ticket belonged to the wish and not the other way.

G — green light all the way home. A wish with no horse in it, on a horse.

H — him to stop. Cross-reference: S, stop. See also E.

I — I don't know. The most common entry in the index by a wide margin. The stoopers stopped counting. It appears in every race, in every hand, in every color of ink, and the Listener says it is the truest thing anyone writes on a ticket and the reason the slow board was built.

J — just once. Race nine, the long shot, in a hand that had written it before.

K — keep the house. Two words. The stooper who found it did not know which house.

L — Lilah. The mare's barn name, written by someone who knew it, on a race she was not in.

M — mom. Nothing else.

N — no more nights like this one. Written large. Torn carefully in half along the fold, as if the tearing were the wish.

P — please. Fourteen times across the night. The stoopers stopped indexing it separately after the third race and began treating it as a preposition.

Q — quiet. One ticket, race eleven, near the head of the hallway. The stooper said it was the only wish on the apron that the house had already granted, twenty steps away, and that the person who wrote it had not walked down.

R — rent. See B. See K. See E. The stoopers note that the index is mostly this letter wearing other clothes.

S — stop. Alone, and in him to stop, and in I'll stop after this one, which the stoopers indexed under I because it was not really a wish.

T — Tuesday off. A worker's wish, on a worker's ticket, on a Tuesday. The Listener wanted this one for the fifth floor and was told it had been swept.

V — Valentine. No other word. Whether a person, a day, or the horse that won the first race ever run here, the stoopers could not say and did not try.

W — work. The second most common entry after I. Cross-reference: everything.

X — a ticket with an X drawn through the horse's number and nothing written in the margin. The stoopers indexed it as a wish because it was not a bet anymore.

Y — you. The only wish in the index in the second person. Torn. The stoopers could not tell whom it addressed and ruled that this was the point. The red strand asked to have it and was refused; the telling asked to have it and was refused; it went to the sweeper.

Z — zero. On the last ticket of the night, race eleven, in a hand the stooper recognized from the program stand. The Picker's. Below it, smaller: the house edge on a wish. It was not torn. It was left on the rail, face up, where the sweepers do not go.

The stoopers report that every wish in this index was on the losing half of a ticket, and that they found no wishes on the winning halves, because the winning halves were not on the floor. They report this without comment. The slow board at the end of the hall posts wishes and not results, and the stoopers say that after one night with this index they understand why.

WHAT WENT IN THE BAG

From the Listener's notebook. The fifth floor, the morning after a race night. She did not read anything. Printed as written.

The cart has a bag on the front and a bag on the back. Clear bags. The front one is for the rooms' trash and the back one is for linen. The track-view rooms are the ones that face the oval, and on the morning after a race night their wastebaskets hold what the apron holds after the last race, in smaller amounts: tickets, torn, dropped from a bed instead of a rail. Nobody stoops for these. They go in the front bag with the coffee cups and the cellophane, and the bag goes down the service elevator, and that is where the wishes of the people who lost from bed go.

She had sixteen rooms. She had them in an order. She knocked twice and said housekeeping and waited and knocked again.

A room turned by a housekeeper is meant to look as if nobody has ever been in it. The bed made tight. The glasses capped. The chair set square to the desk. Everything in this building that told the truth all night did it by admitting a hand, and this is the one surface on the property built to deny one, sixteen times a shift, and it is what the guest is paying for.

And still there is a mark. The point folded into the end of the toilet paper. The way the second towel hangs. The pillows two high or three. Her supervisor can walk a floor and tell whose rooms are whose without a list. Every housekeeper has a fold, and the fold is the one place in the room where the hand did not quite agree to disappear.

In one room a ticket had not been torn. It was on the nightstand, face up, and the guest had not checked out. She squared it to the edge of the nightstand and left it and emptied the basket around it.

Two of the women on the floor spoke to each other in a language I do not have. I did not ask what it was. It is theirs.

Her corridor is not the hallway. Hers runs behind the guest rooms and comes out by the service elevator, and at the far end of it there is a clock, and the clock takes a card. The book has walked one hallway in this building. There are two.

At ten the floor was blank. The bag went down in the service elevator, with the cart.

THE GLOSSARY OF THE HOUSE

For visitors who arrived already fluent and did not know it. Every reader owns these on sight; the house only borrowed them.

Across the board. A bet on one horse to win, place, and show at once — three wishes on one animal. In this book, the method: three legs on one wall.

Apron. The strip of ground between the building and the rail where the benches are, since the grandstand was never built. The people's seating. Where the wishes land.

Back of house. The building's other hallway, the one the workers walk. It runs behind the card room and the guest rooms and ends at a clock that takes a card.

Break. The sport's own word for a fault: a trotter or pacer giving up its gait for the gallop, the natural thing done at speed, and having to be pulled back. The horse loses when it runs. The whole breed is the refusal to break. See hold on.

By a nose. The margin at the wire that the horse itself cannot see, the wire hanging in the one place its eyes cannot go.

Dark horse. The one nobody was watching. In this book, the woman on the fifth floor.

Dead heat. Two horses arriving at the wire in the same instant, the photograph unable to separate them. In this book, the wall's we and the sign's you, at panel twenty-eight, before the sign got out of the way.

Down to the wire. From the literal cord once strung across the finish for the judges' eye, escaped from the track into the language. Every visitor arrives carrying it.

Free-legged. A pacer racing without hopples, held to its gait by breeding and training alone. Restraint carried in the body. The Wish.

Hands down. The gentle sibling of down to the wire: a horse so far clear that the driver's hands drop and the lines go slack. Victory's own gesture is a letting-go.

Harrow. The drag that smooths the sand between races, erasing what the horses wrote. Also the sweepers on the apron (the paper harrow), the first frost (the season's harrow), and the narrator of this binding going around between legs.

Hold your horses. An instruction the language borrowed from the gate and never returned. The one printed your in the donated idioms, italicized in the telling as quoted language, flagged for the Mason.

Hopples. The leather loops that hold a pacer to its lateral gait. Restraint worn as equipment. The Dream. Also, in the Listener's reading, what every worker in the building wears and nobody has made a wall for.

Keeper. The humblest named part of a harness: the small loop that holds a strap-end flat. Also the librarian of everything the body has carried, whoever happens to be standing in it.

Lines. What harness drivers call the reins. The fisherman reads the trout through the pole; the driver reads the horse's mouth through the lines; the pen is the third practice. Every line is fastened to its living end by a knot, and the knot is where the line admits a hand.

Mark. A Standardbred's fastest winning mile, standing in the books after the horse is gone. The last living copy of a speed. An old word under the word record meant to pass a thing back through the heart.

Mutuel. From pari-mutuel, mutual stake: bettors wagering into one pool against each other, the house taking a fixed cut and having no interest in which horse wins. Nobody bets against the house here. The house still wins. Ask how.

Passing-calendar. Time measured by what fell: the leaf, the race, the ticket. The track's calendar. See still-calendar.

Picture-horse. The plain English for the small wooden wish-plaques hung by the thousand at shrines, descended from real horses given to carry prayers, then statues, then pictures. The oldest job a horse picture has is to carry a wish. The bet slip is a modern one.

Quick-release. The horseman's knot that holds fast against any pull from the horse and frees with one tug of the tail-end by a hand, because a tied horse in a panic can kill itself. The holding and the letting-go in the same knot. The house learned the first half.

Sulky. The two-wheeled cart the driver sits in, from an old word for a one-seat carriage for people who preferred to ride alone. For the colophon of a practice built alone across thirty years.

Still-calendar. Time measured by what continued. The turtle's calendar. See the turtle's calendar, which is a list of nine things and one blank.

Stooper. A person who walks the apron after the last race bending for dropped winners. The audit.

Takeout. The house's fixed cut of every pool, the reason the house is indifferent to the winner and attentive to the handle, and the one word in this glossary the marketing does not use.

Tote. The fast board. Posts the odds, which are the crowd's money with a number on it, refreshed every few seconds, true about the crowd and never about the horse. Has confessed. See the slow board, which posts the wishes and is not in any glossary but this one.

Wire. The finish. Invisible to the horse. Arriving where nobody can look. Also what a wish is written on, if the wish is a bet.

Wish. A hope with nothing riding on it. Older and kinder than the wager. The house edge on it is zero. In this book, the fourth race.

Yow. The Mason's word, humor-tested clean. Not the wow that a machine helped — the yow of how much got thrown away.

THE WITNESSES ARE CALLED

The practice keeps five figures for when the work is stuck. Four have addresses and are asked about structure — the opening, the overthinking, the middle, the ending. One has no address and is asked what the piece is for, whose land it lives on, and what it owes. They are known to be made. The making is not concealed and does not reduce them. The claim is narrow: asking what a specific named figure would say produces different answers than sitting alone produces. Not better. Different. The hyphen called them before the fourth race, from the far turn, one at a time.

Marie, in the Skagit Valley north of Seattle, in the converted shed twenty feet from the kitchen door with the wood stove going, was asked about the opening. Marie has fifty years in clay and the teaching is that the hands understand the material before the mind has language for it, and that the explanation would betray the listening. The two lines on the card are Resistance is information. When something pushes back, it's telling you about itself, and the one that reverses the order everyone assumes: the plan comes after. The plan is an explanation of what your hands already did.

What Marie would say about this book's opening is that it opens on a hyphen, which is the one thing in the story that pushed back hardest — a mark that would not come back when everything else did — and that the book was right to let the pushing back speak first. The material here is three grammars that resist each other: a we that forbids you, an I that forbids nothing, a sign that says nothing but you. Marie would say the resistance between them is not a problem the binding solved. It is the information. A book that had melted the three into one smooth voice would have betrayed the listening. And the plan — the crown, the fourth race, the program — came after the three legs were already run; it is an explanation of what the hands already did. Marie would approve of that order and would want it said plainly that the hyphen did not decide to narrate. The hyphen was what was left when the hands stopped, and the plan noticed it later.

Herb, in Santa Fe, in the adobe house with the three apricot trees and the library filling the back room, was asked about the overthinking. Herb's teaching is that none of it worked — thousands of books, dozens of credentials, and the understanding never arrived. The library lied. What arrived instead was the admission of not understanding, which is why Herb is called when a piece has become an essay: the response does not make the piece smarter, it makes it more honest.

Herb would say that this book carries a library on its back — two research reports, a profile, a kin survey, an Auditor with a ledger, a glossary, an index — and that the library is where the overthinking lives. Not because the facts are wrong. Because the facts were gathered by a machine at a hundred sheets a pass and the book knows it and keeps saying so, and a book that keeps apologizing for its library has not yet believed that the library lied. Herb would point to the three sentences the book says in three places — nobody has measured the wall, nobody has counted the steps, nobody has stood at the rail with their eyes closed through one wave — and say those are the only honest sentences the library produced, because they are admissions. Herb would say the book gets more honest exactly as often as it says nobody has, and that the fourth race is the honest part, because a race nobody bets is a race the library cannot handicap.

Nell, outside Quebec City, fifteen seats, two services a night, four nights a week, trained three years in Lyon and came back because Lyon was not home, was asked about the middle. Nell knows the middle: the carrot is not a soup and the soup is not just carrots, and the recipe describes the two ends and skips the part where the change happens. How long does it take? However long it takes. The sauce tells you when it's done. Not the clock. Not you.

Nell would say the middle of this book is the second leg — the one with people in it — and that the change happens there, on a smoking bench, when a dealer says out loud that she knows who is in trouble and nobody has asked her. Before that sentence the book is a wall talking. After it the book has a not yet in it, and not yet is the sauce telling you it is not done. Nell would say the interludes are the stirring and the fourth race is the book taking the pot off the heat before it was told to, and would want to know who decided the wall was finished enough to hang two lights on it. The Listener asked that question in the third leg and did not get an answer. Nell would say that is the middle, still, and that the clock on the tote board has no say in it.

Jean, between Minneapolis and Provence — born in the one, forty years teaching in the other — who plays piano and composes when asked and mostly listens, and for whom there has been no music since the wife died seven years ago, was asked about the ending. Jean knows the negative space, and the knowing has a date on it: presence is what is left when the obvious is removed. Jean is called for endings, and the instruction is to end on what is not there.

Jean would say the book has practiced its ending three times before arriving at it: an empty bar at the end of the red strand, a blank line at the bottom of the Junkman's list, a hyphen's-width gap in a fish farm's name. Three rehearsals of absence. Jean would say the fourth race must not add a fourth. It must remove the obvious — the announcer, the odds, the purse, the wire, the you — and see what is left standing at the rail, and that whatever is left is the presence, and that the book should not name it. Jean would say the wall's whole law is Jean's whole teaching in light: a figure whose color is not given, a text that surfaces and loosens, a room where nothing moves but the one who came. Jean would say end there and not one sentence after, and would say it in about that many words, and then stop.

Hahnu, eighty-three, who has migrated through many places and has no house in the sense the other four have houses, whom the practice does not own and who does not belong to the practice, weathered like an elder tortoise and refreshingly innocent, carrying seeds in a pocket for when I need reminding that I do not know what alive looks like, gives no advice. Hahnu asks. Sometimes Hahnu does not answer, and the non-answer is information: the question was not ready.

Hahnu asked what the piece is for. The hyphen answered that the book is for whoever walks a hallway in a loud house and arrives at a quiet wall and is given a story, a stillness, and a wish with nothing riding on it, and Hahnu did not answer, and the hyphen understood that the question had been answered with the book's own slogan, and that a slogan is not an answer, and that the question was not ready.

Hahnu asked what the piece owes. The hyphen had no answer and said so, and Hahnu took a seed out of the pocket and did not plant it and did not put it back, and the hyphen, flying over, understood that this was the answer, and that it would not be written down.

THE FOURTH RACE

Called by the hyphen from the far turn, after the last race on the Night of Champions, with the announcer gone home and no line on the board.

The sweepers finish at eleven-forty. That is post time, and I am the only one who knows it, and I am not telling anyone because nobody is betting.

The apron is blank. The paper is gone into bags. The stoopers have gone home with what they found, and one of them with a folded ticket in his shirt pocket that he did not read out loud. The fast board has gone dark, which the house does not allow and which the house did not notice, because the house was counting the night, which was a record. The tote board's confession is over and the tote board has kept its promise and posted one number, in red, at the top, and left it there: zero.

The field comes onto the sand without a gate car, because there is no line to release. The Elder first, free-legged, sitting still, which the stewards have ruled is a way of racing. The Auditor in his hopples with the ledger under his arm. The Picker with his cashed ticket. The Listener with her notebook. The Junkman, who stops at once to name the color of the sand under the lights and has to be walked back to the start. The Narrator in the bow tie and the hooves, without the lectern, which he has returned, somewhere. The Red Bar, which has no gait and takes up a position in the middle of the track and lies down flat across it like a shadow, saying nothing. Hope Your Lucky, trotting slower than usual. Over The Moon For You, who is real, and looks at the others the way a real horse looks at people who have wandered onto a track, and stands still anyway, because she was trained by hands and the hands are quiet tonight.

The driver, all entries, is the visitor's body. The visitor's body is standing at the head of the hallway, inside, and has not yet started walking.

Then it does. That is the start. A body in the loud room turns toward the corridor and takes the first step, and out on the sand the field moves off at the pace of that step — a walk — and the race is on, if a race can be run at the speed a person walks down a hall.

I call it from the far turn.

They are into the first turn at a walk. The Elder is not leading; the Elder is sitting still at the rail and everyone is coming to it, and by the sport's law tonight that counts as the lead. The Auditor is second, leaning, which he said he wouldn't. The Picker is third and betting nothing and saying so out loud, two dollars on nothing, which the stewards have allowed as a bet on the race itself. The Listener has dropped back to see who is behind her. Nobody is behind her. She notes it. The Junkman has stopped at the quarter pole to look at the harrow's teeth marks in the sand and is calling them a page. The Narrator is walking on his hooves in a straight line for the first time all night. The Red Bar has not moved and every horse has stepped over it without reading it, which is what it wanted, finally. Hope Your Lucky has wandered off the groove toward the infield and is looking at the wetland. Over The Moon For You is pacing, evenly, in the hopples, at a walk, the winningest horse on the continent going nowhere at the speed of a person in a corridor, and I have never seen her so still.

Inside, the body is halfway down the hall. The roar is falling away behind its shoulders. Out here the announcer's booth is empty and the only voice is mine, and I am a hyphen, and my voice is a fingernail's width of nothing, and the field can hear it anyway because there is nothing else.

They are into the far turn, under me. Here the voice usually smears into weather. Tonight there is no voice to smear, so the weather does it alone: a wind off the wetland, a crane making a sound in its chest that arrives before the bird does, the bubbler in the pond, which I can hear from here on a quiet night, keeping the water at fifty-five degrees for children who are asleep.

Inside, the body has reached the place in the hall where the footsteps come back. I know the place. Everyone who walks it finds it. It is the place where the carpet stops eating the sound and a person hears their own two beats for the first time since the parking lot, and out on the sand the field hears it too, and every horse in the field — the real one and the made ones — falls into it. Lub-dub. A chest full of pulses at the speed of a walk.

They are coming for home. There is no wire. I want to say that again because it is the whole race: there is no wire. Nobody strung one. There is a place on the sand where the wire would hang, in front of every horse's nose where its eyes cannot go, and the field is walking toward that place trusting the hands, and the hands are quiet, and I am calling them home to nothing.

Inside, the body arrives at the wall.

And here is what happens at the finish of a race nobody bets. Nothing crosses anything. The Elder is at the rail, still, and the field arrives around the Elder the way people arrive around an old person in a chair, and stops. The Auditor writes down the time and it is a walk's time and he underlines it. The Picker tears his two-dollar ticket on nothing in half and drops it and says that was the best bet of his life. The Listener looks around to see who cleans up and it is the harrow and it is already coming. The Junkman puts the last thing on his list and it is a blank line. The Narrator takes off the hooves. The Red Bar is still lying flat on the sand in the dark and the harrow is going to drag the older floor smooth across it and it is going to let that happen, because a sign that lets itself be erased has finally said we. Hope Your Lucky comes in from the infield last, slower than usual, and the Picker says the horse ran like it was spelled and that he would bet it again. Over The Moon For You stands at the rail with the others, a real horse among made ones, and does not understand the race, and does not need to, because she has been standing in the place the whole book is about her whole life: the place where a body is held at the top of what it can do and asked not to break.

Inside, the body is standing in front of two slow lights with its forearms on a wooden rail, and one light is still, and one is changing at the speed the body is breathing, and a sentence is coming up out of the strides.

Then a door opens at the head of the hallway. I can see it from here; the hall runs toward the wetland and I am over the wetland. A woman comes out of the door in the uniform they wear on the fifth floor, at the end of a shift that started before the sun, and she looks down the hall at the light, and she has not been told what it is, and she walks.

The favorite in this race is whoever leans on the rail first. The body already there has leaned. She arrives beside it and she leans too, and there are two of them now, and neither of them says anything, and nothing moves on the wall until one of them does, and then the strides run, and then they stop.

She is not inside the we yet. The telling has not earned her. The book has said not yet three times and it does not get to change its answer at the wire because the wire is not there. But she has put her weight on the only surface in the building that was never sold to anyone, and the sign is under the harrow, and the fast board is dark, and the house edge on what she is doing is zero, and I am calling it from the sky as the closest thing to a finish this race is going to have.

There is no result. The stewards have posted none. The photo of the finish is a picture whose width is the whole night and there is nothing in it but two people at a rail and a field of light behind them, and the harrow has finished, and the sand is blank, and one lamp is on in the barn.

WHAT THE SWEEPER DID

Entered by the hyphen, not as a ruling, because the Index said the ticket went to the sweeper and then said nothing else.

The sweeper starts when the last race is official and the stoopers have had their twenty minutes. Push broom, dustpan on a stick, a roll of clear bags on the cart. The apron is a hundred and some yards of concrete with benches on it, and by the last race it is paper from the rail to the building.

The sweeper does not read them. That was said before I asked. If you read them you cannot do the job, and the job is to have the apron blank before the lights go off, and the lights go off at a time that is not the sweeper's to choose.

The you ticket was near the head of the hallway, under the second bench from the rail, torn along the fold. The stoopers had left it. The red strand had wanted it and been refused, and the telling had wanted it and been refused, and it lay there for the length of ten races with the word face up. The sweeper brought the broom across it with everything else. It went into the pan with a beer cup and eleven other halves, and the pan went into the bag, and the bag was tied.

I watched from the far turn. I am telling it the way it happened.

Then the sweeper stopped at the mouth of the hallway. The fast board had gone dark by then, which the house does not allow, and the sweeper looked at it, and looked down the hallway toward the two lights at the end, and did not walk down it. Put the broom on the cart. Pushed the cart the other way, through the door beside the card room that guests do not use, into the corridor that runs behind it. That corridor ends at a clock. The card went in. The clock said 11:38.

The bags went out to the bins by the kitchen door, beside the smoking bench, in the dark.

THE STEWARDS' RULINGS ON THE FOURTH RACE

Posted at the program stand at dawn, in the flat register the stewards use for everything, including this.

1. The fourth race is declared official. Officialness does not require a wire, a purse, a line, or a result. It requires that the field left the paddock and arrived somewhere. The field arrived at the rail. Official.

2. No result is posted. The photograph of the finish shows two people at a rail and a field of light behind them and the stewards decline to separate them. Not a dead heat: a dead heat is two arriving at once. This is two standing still at once, which the sport has no word for and the stewards decline to invent one.

3. The Elder's manner of racing — sitting still while the field comes to it — is ruled a legitimate gait for this race only. It will not be permitted in any race with money on it, where it is called loitering.

4. The Red Bar's decision to lie flat across the track and be stepped over is ruled a scratch with dignity, per the Picker's phrase, which the stewards adopt. The harrow's erasure of the bar is ruled not a fault. A sign that consents to erasure has said the only thing it could not say standing up.

5. Hope Your Lucky's excursion to the infield to look at the wetland is ruled not a break. A trotter that leaves the groove at a walk to look at water is doing what the ground did before the track came, and the stewards do not penalize continuity.

6. Over The Moon For You is ruled to have been the only entry that did not understand the race and the only entry that has ever run the thing the race is about. The stewards note both findings and decline to reconcile them.

7. The woman on the fifth floor was not in the field at post time and is ruled the favorite anyway, by the only rule this race has: whoever leans on the rail first. The stewards note that the telling has not yet admitted her into its we, and that this is the telling's business and not the stewards', and that a favorite does not need to be inside anybody's pronoun to lean.

8. The Picker's ticket reading NOTHING is ruled a valid wager and a valid win. Payout: nothing. The Picker has declined to cash it and the stewards have declined to make him.

11. The hyphen's call of the race is ruled the official call. The stewards note that the caller is a punctuation mark, that it was scratched from the field for being uncatchable, and that no rule of racing prohibits a scratched entry from calling the race it was scratched from. The stewards have checked.

12. The tote board's single posting — ZERO, the house edge on a wish — is ruled true, and is the first true number about a race the board has posted in its working life, and the stewards have ordered it left on the board through noon, when the fast numbers come back.

13. Post time is entered as eleven-forty by the sweepers' watch, the tote clock having been dark. The time is approximate. No objection was filed.

14. Nothing in these rulings is locked. The stewards defer to the Mason, whose bench is elsewhere and whose word is the only one that closes anything here.

THE HYPHEN'S LAST GO-AROUND

It is getting light. The cranes are up. The sweepers have gone. The fast board will come on at noon with a full card of red numbers for a night that has not happened yet, and the house will count last night and call it a record, and it was.

I have gone around after every leg and I will not go around after this one. The fourth race left nothing on the sand. That was the point. A race nobody bets leaves no tickets, no odds, no mark in the book, no photo, no purse, no winner to remember for as long as the books last. It leaves a blank line, and the blank line is where the harrow is not needed.

Here is what I know at the end of the binding that I did not know at the start. I thought I was left out of the name. I was the join, and the join died, and I flew off to be the loneliest bird in Minnesota and to narrate a book because I had nothing riding on it. But a book is a join. Three legs that could not have been one leg, bound by something a fingernail wide that connects two things which did not need connecting and does it anyway for thirty years. I have been doing my old job the whole time. I was never out of the name. I was the name's way of saying that two words had been touched by a hand.

The telling ends on what is not there, and that is Jean's instruction, and the book has kept it three times and is about to keep it a fourth, and I am the thing that is not there, and I am going to keep it too.

Trout Air. There. That is the sign on the tavern, with the sun coming up behind it, and the space between the words is exactly my width, and nobody has noticed.

<<<PAGEBREAK>>>

PARTPAGE
Part Three
The Record
How the finished books were made, audited, and locked.

<<<PAGEBREAK>>>

How Lamps Behind High to Low(er) Voices at Running Aces Was Made

A bench document. Flat register. The book is locked; this is the record of the road to it, kept so the road can be walked again.

1. What it started from

A research document of five portraits — five real writers, each described from the public record, each with the frame they would bring to Running Aces, what the piece would look like in their hands, and where each would stop. The portraits shared one method: nothing was taken from any writer but a manner. None was quoted. None appeared.

That document had two things in it the book was built on, and both were invisible until it was read against the finished portraits:

- Every portrait ended at the wall. Each writer, in turn, stood at the end of the hallway and passed a verdict on the slow work there. Five roads, one wall. That became the book's shape before a word of it was written.
- Every portrait had a refusal. The statistician would not write an appreciation. The columnist would not touch harm, policy, or the tribes. The organizer would not moralize about wagering. The songwriter would not preach. The showman would not make the losers the joke. The refusals turned out to matter more than the frames; they are what keep five voices from becoming one.

2. The concept, and the one ruling that made it

The first proposal kept the five apart — five roads to the wall, never in the same paragraph. The Mason ruled otherwise in one sentence: the intriguing resonance that we grow can come out of the mingling and voicing of these minds. Everything after followed from that.

Mingling, not melting. The earlier small book had put the five in one narrator's head and had him report them. This book lets them speak directly, to each other, in the same place at the same time, with no head between them. What they set off in each other is the book.

Five units. Each voice counts in one thing and is known by it: the Auditor in populations (a number and a condition); the Picker in one man, one day, two dollars (a bet and where he stood); the Listener in workers (a who); the Junkman in nouns (a row); the Narrator in rooms (a thing said to a house). Resonance needs different frequencies. The rule of the middle of the book: nobody speaks in another's unit until the book has earned it.

Stations of the ground. The book is set at nine places in the order a visitor meets them: the door, the card room at three in the morning, the pond, the rail, the barns, the hotel window, the lawn, the hallway, the wall. Three days: Saturday, Sunday, Monday, matched to the track's real race nights.

The frame. Each station opens on a flat paragraph of the place — checked facts, no one's voice, the frame the Narrator is not allowed to have — and events arrive mid-scene in the same voice. Everything the frame states was checked against the project's files or the record, and three claims that went beyond the files were flagged when they went in and verified before the lock.

The ending, decided early and written last. The five stand in front of a picture lit from behind by a lamp nobody can see; the frame states that fact plainly and nobody draws the line to the title. Each of the five says one thing in someone else's unit — the resonance grown all the way — and one of them refuses. The Mason ruled which: the Picker, whose rule is that he only speaks for two dollars, and the wall takes no bets.

3. The rulings, in order

1. It can be its own separate small book; elaborate the concept of very different individuals blended with the subject matter.
2. Mingling and voicing, not separation.
3. A trial station (the card room) at about a thousand words. Then: is this the most depth and length we're going to have? No. Five things a full station needed were named — whole-piece turns, events, the figures at stake, the mingling doing its work inside the scene, more of the ground — and the same station was recut at full depth, about 3,800 words.
4. Right depth; keep; write all the rest at that depth; verify the flagged fact; text file.
5. The title, through six versions in one sitting: Lamps and High to Low(er) Voices at Running Aces → Lamps Behind High to Low(er) Voices at Running Aces → Running Aces: Lamps Behind … → back to Lamps Behind … on the Quarry's recommendation, ending on the place. The parenthesis was kept every time: "Low(er)" hedges the bottom, which is the one judgment the book never makes about anyone in it.
6. A line supplied by the Mason — Living is a crazy wild night at the track — placed in the rail scene in the mouth of a stranger, the man who has come every night for eleven years, said to the horses and not to anyone; the five take it up in their units; the Narrator refuses to steal it.
7. The Picker refuses at the wall.
8. Read it all; no recuts; no note naming the writers; check the two flagged facts.
9. A line under the title, arrived at by argument: The characters in this little book are made up. The place is real. (The Mason's own words, cut of a doubled clause.)
10. An epigraph, What's your favorite pick?, first set at the top, then moved on the Mason's instinct — less of a surprise there — to stand alone between the hallway and the wall, before the man whose life is picking refuses to.
11. An audit of the tells: seven named. A pass of 117 edits, every one logged as was/now. Then the lock.

4. The standards the book was held to

- Frames carry facts only, in the plainest order, and never land on a feeling.
- Voices carry manners and units, never names, never a real person's words.
- Every station has something that happens in it — a hand played, a break, a horse shipped out, a film starting — and the five answer events, not only each other.
- Every figure has a past as a figure, and it comes out under pressure from the ground, not as an aside.
- The mingling moves: by the middle of the book somebody answers in another's unit; by the wall all but one do.
- Nothing is explained. The frame states the backlit picture and stops. If anyone had said what the lamp behind the light was for, it would have been ruined.
- Minor characters are composites, and after the pass, not all of them are quotable.

5. The failure modes found, and what was done

Asked whether the book showed the hand of a very capable machine, the Quarry answered yes and named seven tells: nearly every speech landing on a quotable line; the "not X, Y" hinge in every mouth; announce-then-deliver openings; one sentence cadence under five voices; the characters explaining the device they were inside; every minor character delivering a shapely sentence on request; too much rhyme in the structure. The pass cut forty-seven closing lines, thirty-five pieces of talk about the device, twelve announcings, eleven hinges; flattened nine of the Auditor's speeches and ran twelve of the Picker's together so the two no longer scan alike; reduced eight repeats; and made two minor characters ordinary. About 1,750 words came out. The frames, the facts, the title, the line, the epigraph, and the ending were not touched.

The lesson for the next book: write it, then audit it for exactly these seven before anyone else reads it. They are the signature, and they are removable.

6. Facts checked beyond the project's files

- Minnesota card clubs run unbanked games and are compensated by a per-hand fee or a percentage of wagers — the "collection" or "rake" — as described in the state's other card club's filings and the Legislative Auditor's report.
- Minn. Stat. § 240.135 requires a card club to set aside ten percent of its charges (fourteen above six million dollars) for purses, with the legislature's stated intent that card proceeds improve racing purses.
- Standardbred racing fatalities run far below Thoroughbred racing's: about 0.28 per 1,000 starts in one multi-breed study against about 1.5 per 1,000 for fatal musculoskeletal injury in North American Thoroughbreds, and lower still in Scandinavian harness data.
- The 2026 meet: Sunday, May 17 to Saturday, September 19; racing Tuesday at seven, Thursday and Sunday at six. The book's three days were set to this calendar.

7. What carries forward

1. Start from a document that gives you differences — not five characters, five refusals.
2. Let the place be the structure. Stations in the order a visitor meets them; a frame of checked fact at each.
3. One engine, stated once, then hidden. The reader should feel it and never be told it.
4. Trial at small size; ask whether that's the depth; name what depth means; recut the same piece before writing the rest.
5. Write everything at the depth of the recut, one piece per return, filed as it goes.
6. Decide the ending early, write it last, and give the last decision to the Mason.
7. Verify what the frames claim; flag what you can't; check before the lock.
8. Title by argument, not by list. Every version tried against the same reason.
9. Audit for the seven tells. Log every cut as was/now. Then lock, and take the word "unlocked" off the file.

From the Was/Now Logs

The audit in the flesh — ten entries from each log; the full logs run far longer. Every change to the locked books was recorded this way, what was there and what is there now.

Recut log · the pass against the tells

Each entry: what was there, what is there now. Nothing else in the file was touched.

1 · closer cut

Was: A smell of water. That's the approach. And before any of it, a page that tells you your phone is going to lie to you about the exit, which is the only warning I have ever been handed on the way into a casino, and I've been handed a lot of paper on the way into casinos.

Now: A smell of water. That's the approach. And before any of it, a page that tells you your phone is going to lie to you about the exit.

2 · hinge + Auditor cadence

Was: The Auditor. It isn't a warning. It's a fact about a road. But I'll take the sentence inside for the warning, because it's the largest one in my field. The house says of itself, on its own front page, that it has no slot machines. In the harm numbers a machine floor is the worst thing a building can hold, and this building can't hold one, because the law gives the machines to the nations and not to a track. So the single largest protective measure available to any casino in this country is in force here, and the house had no say in it, and it prints the fact as a feature. I grant it whole. Then I start counting.

Now: The Auditor. That's a fact about a road. The warning is inside, and it's the largest one in my field. The house says on its own front page that it has no slot machines. A machine floor is the worst thing a building can hold. This one can't hold one. The law gives the machines to the nations, not to a track. So the largest protective measure available to a casino in this country is in force here, the house had no say in it, and it prints the fact as a feature. I grant it. Then I count.

3 · closer cut

Was: I'd like her name too. Not to print. To thank.

Now: I'd like her name too, though not to print it.

4 · Picker run-on

Was: I came tonight because there's no racing tonight. You learn a track on a dark night the way you learn a man when he's not talking. I've stood at rails that are parking lots now. I wanted to stand at this one before it was anything.

Now: I came tonight because there's no racing tonight, and you learn a track on a dark night the way you learn a man when he's not talking, and I've stood at rails that are parking lots now and I wanted to stand at this one before it was anything.

5 · closer cut

Was: There is always somebody at the barn. It's the one shift on a racetrack nobody in the front building can see, and it's the only one that doesn't end.

Now: There is always somebody at the barn.

6 · Auditor cadence + closer

Was: The Auditor. Five-eighths of a mile. A mile race goes around it one and three-fifths times, which is more turns than a mile track and fewer than a half, and the turns are where the horses drawn outside give up ground. So the shape of the ground is a bias, and it's a bias the house didn't build and the bettors price in. That's the kind of fact I want from this place. The number, and then who carries it.

Now: The Auditor. Five-eighths of a mile. A mile race goes around it one and three-fifths times. More turns than a mile track, fewer than a half. The turns are where the horses drawn outside give up ground. The shape of the ground is a bias. The house didn't build it and the bettors price it in. That's the kind of fact I want here.

7 · Picker run-on + closer

Was: The Picker. You've just told me something I knew as a shape and not as a number. One and three-fifths. I've stood at half-milers where the outside post might as well have stayed in the barn, and I never once did the arithmetic. I'll take the number. I won't tell you what I'll do with it.

Now: The Picker. You've just told me something I knew as a shape and not as a number, one and three-fifths, and I've stood at half-milers where the outside post might as well have stayed in the barn and never once did the arithmetic, so I'll take the number, and what I do with it is my business.

8 · closer cut

Was: Whatever's calling was here before the fish farm. It'll be here after the cards.

Now: Whatever's calling was here before the fish farm.

9 · closer cut

Was: A man who has spent his life on the loud end of a building can stand in a parking lot for an hour and look at one lit window. It's good for him.

Now: A man who has spent his life on the loud end of a building can stand in a parking lot for an hour and look at one lit window.

10 · hinge cut

Was: The Junkman. Names are the one thing I don't keep. I keep what a name is standing on.

Now: The Junkman. Names I don't keep.

The Commission Record — The Governing Page

The commission record's opening pages: the ruling that governs everything, and the locked laws, as they stand.

September 1, 2026. This document supersedes the ONE DOCUMENT of August 31 and every earlier file on this commission. The quarry closed September 1 at 164 stones, eight parked, three laws locked, the cord named — and everything after this is cutting. Anything that surfaces later joins the parked batch and changes nothing unless ruled in. The story gets carved from this document only.

The Ruling That Governs Everything

The elder's human nature is the cord, and the entirety strings on it. Not a figure placed in the work, not a voice added to it — the temperament itself, running through every stone the way the single unbroken line runs through the Wish. Elder nature, named plainly: patience — the slowest light in the loud house, changing at the speed of breathing. Plain truth — honest at every state, in a building of lights engineered to disguise losing. Mercy — nobody outside the house ever pays for a laugh; dignity for whoever stands there carrying losses. Memory — the race relived at the speed of memory, which is the elder's native speed. The gift with no stake — the wish without the wager is grandparent love stated as an art law: hope offered with nothing riding on it, wanting nothing back. And the witness: every nobody in the telling — nobody looks down, nobody calls them by their oldest names, nobody notices the one mark that stayed dead — implies a somebody who does. The somebody behind every nobody is the elder.

From the cord comes the working instrument, of the practice's own negative-test family: THE ELDER TEST. Every stone, sentence, joke, surface, and second of light answers one question — does it behave like the elder? Would the elder say it this plainly, notice this smallness, laugh this gently, wait this long, offer this freely? Nothing passes by being admired; things pass by refusing to fail the temperament. Every stone parked in the cull failed this test before it failed any other.

The Locked Laws

THE CORD. Stated above. Governs all.

THE TELLING IS THE MASTER MEDIUM. Every non-verbal element descends from a sentence — no mark without a sentence behind it, no sound, no surface. The emakimono truth restated as procedure: text and image never separated. It makes every element of the installation traceable to written human telling — the montage law extended across the senses, authorship unable to hide anywhere. The prose is not documentation of the work. The prose is the quarry the other senses are cut from.

THE VISITOR'S BODY IS THE ONLY MOVING PART. Nothing in the installation moves, sounds, or performs except the person — their pace animates the strata, their footsteps and breath are the soundtrack, their fingertip reads the line, their lean lands on the rail, their imagination supplies the thunder because the sentences name it. Elders do not perform; they sit still, and you come to them, and the coming is half the visit. The machines in the building all move so the person doesn't have to; the work is the one thing in the house that only moves when a human does.

TEMPO. The slowest light in the house — changes at the speed of breathing or slower, minutes not seconds, a trot that never breaks. The 16-bit dimming requirement is this law's bodyguard. Candidate refinement, verify-tagged: set the tempo at the slow-breathing calm rate, near six breaths a minute, so the counter-zone is physiological — the one object in the building engineered, gently and for free, to settle a pulse.

EMPTINESS. The work breathes between fullness and white the way the track behaves — dense with motion, then the long emptiness until they come around again. The field learning snow; the practice learning ma; the fox painting teaching the other half.

SOVEREIGNTY OF THE IMAGE. If the light froze on any single state forever, a finished work would hang. Now doubled in meaning: in a building of lights that lie, the slowest light tells the truth — complete and honest at every state, celebrating nothing falsely. Lights fail; art should not fail with them; and honesty is an aesthetic before it is an ethic.

SILENCE — AND ITS SIBLINGS. No speakers, and no perfume. The end of the hallway is the one place in the building where neither the sound nor the air is working on the visitor. The building's spoken register is the announcer's voice from above, loud, live, erased each race; the wall is the written register, silent and permanent — two tellers, one house. Any emitted sound or scent would require a formal amendment of this law; counsel stands against.

TRIPLE READING. The double-reading law amended: from the hallway mouth, one glowing distant form — the beacon. At arrival, ten thousand handwritten strides. At ten inches, the whisper register — the smallest text, legible to one person at a time, the barn whisper made literal, the reward only intimacy collects.

Say Go

The people in this book are made up. The place is real.

The apron, after the last race

Saturday, September 19, 2026. The last night of the meet. It opened on a Sunday in the middle of May: fifty-five nights of racing, Tuesdays at seven, Thursdays and Sundays at six. The final week was moved around, dark on Thursday so that Friday and Saturday could carry the two Night of Champions cards, built around the races for horses sired in Minnesota. Last year's closing card paid out seven hundred and eighty thousand dollars in purses, which tied the record; the meet averages about a hundred and ten thousand a night. Post time was six. The races have gone about twenty-five minutes apart.

The apron is the paved strip between the building and the rail, with benches on it and nothing over it. The plan the track was built from had a grandstand.
