The money ran out before it went up, and the bleachers that stood on the grass beside the apron the first summer came out after a year, because the liquor license allowed beer to be sold on the apron and not on the grass. People bring lawn chairs and blankets and sit on the grass without the beer. Behind the apron the building's windows run to the floor. The tavern, which carries the name of the fish farm that was on this ground before the track, looks at the finish line through the glass, and so does the room where other tracks run on screens all year. The hotel's windows face the oval. In the infield there is a video board that shows the odds until the gate car pulls away from the field and folds its wings, and after the wire shows the replay, the order, and the prices. A camera at the wire photographs the finish line and nothing else. When two horses reach it together the judges separate them from that picture, and until they have, the board says PHOTO.

A ticket is a slip of paper from a window or a machine. It carries the track, the date, the race, the kind of bet, the horses' numbers, the amount, the time it was sold, a barcode, and a number under the barcode. It pays whoever hands it in. It does not carry the odds. By the state's rule a torn ticket is not paid unless the bet can be verified some other way. A losing ticket is worth the paper. Most people tear them, and some drop them, and there are cans at the doors that the tickets do not go into, and by the end of a card the apron is white with them.

The twelfth race is the last one. The field comes up behind the car at a jog, the car pulls away and its wings fold, the board freezes, and the call comes down from the booth. On the apron people get up off the benches for the stretch and put their forearms on the rail. Two horses come to the wire together, the four on the rail and the seven outside it, and the board says PHOTO.

Most of the apron stays on its feet. A man in a canvas work jacket at the fourth bench does not. He looks at his ticket, looks at the board, tears the ticket across, lets the halves go at his feet, and walks toward the doors with his hands in his pockets. He is through them before the numbers come up. The photo puts the seven in front by a nose. Then OFFICIAL, and the prices under it. The seven pays nine dollars and forty cents to win on two.

On another night the apron would be half empty by now. Tonight it is not. People stay at the rail after the numbers are up and look at the track with nothing on it. A woman takes a picture of the board with the prices on it. A man folds his program in half and puts it inside his coat instead of leaving it on the bench. The girl of about eight who goes past the last bench with her father has a ticket in her hand that she has not torn, a two-dollar show ticket on the eight, which finished sixth, and the father has his program under his arm and is not looking at the ticket.

The winner's circle is busy. The seven's driver has come off the bike. The horse stands in the lights in its harness with the sweat dark on its neck and a blanket over its back with the name of the race on it, and people in good coats are arranged around it for the picture. The horse is breathing hard and looking at nothing. When the picture is taken it is led off through the gap in the rail toward the paddock, and the blanket comes off at the gap, because the blanket is for the picture.

The man with the canvas bag has been on the last bench, the one nearest the mouth of the hallway, since the tenth race. He is here most race nights. He bets the early races and stops at the sixth, and after that he watches, and when the last race goes official and the apron starts to empty he gets up and walks it, bending. The sport's word for it is stooper, and he uses it about himself. He has a rule about when to start. The windows stay open for cashing after the last race, and he does not bend for anything until they have been open ten minutes, because people come back for what they dropped.

The board changes. The replay runs once more and stops, and the prices stay, and a line of type crosses the bottom thanking everyone for the season. The caller says the same thing from the booth, and says good night, and the speakers click. Through the glass of the tavern the last tables are still seated, and a server is turning the chairs up onto the empty ones. At the far end of the apron the beer stand has its shutter down. On another night the two people who run it would be restocking for Tuesday. Tonight they carry the tubs out to the grass and tip the ice, and one of them wheels the kegs in through the doors on a hand truck, and the other one puts a padlock on the shutter and pulls on it twice.

In the ten minutes two people come back. A man finds his program on the second bench and takes it. A woman looks under the third bench and along the foot of the rail below it, and does not find what she is looking for, and does not take the scarf, and goes.

At the ten-minute mark by his watch the man with the bag stands up.

He works the apron the way it was filled, from the mouth of the hallway toward the beer stand, along the benches first and then along the rail. He does not use a stick. He bends from the knees and turns each piece of paper over with two fingers and reads it and lets it go. What he lets go he leaves face down. Near the rail the paving has a film of dust on it that he takes for lime off the track, and the paper there is grey on the side that was up.

On the apron there are halves of tickets, whole tickets folded once, whole tickets flat, fifty-cent tickets from the last two races, programs with every race marked in pen but the last one, a child's shoe, plastic cups, and a scarf on the third bench, which he leaves where it is, because a scarf gets come back for. The tickets are almost all from the windows here. A few are from the machines inside, on other tracks, printed in the afternoon and carried out and dropped with the rest. Most of the tickets are two dollars. Some are twenty. One is two hundred, to win, on the one horse in the ninth, which finished fourth, and he reads it twice and lets it go face down.

He finds the two halves of the canvas jacket's ticket at the fourth bench, where they landed, one on the bench and one under it. He fits them together on his knee. Race twelve, the seven, two dollars to win. The tear goes across the barcode.

"That's a — that's the nine-forty," he says, to nobody, and puts the two halves in the breast pocket of his shirt, apart from the bag.

The bag is for the whole ones he will take to the window. By the time he has reached the beer stand it holds two small ones, a show ticket on the winner of the eleventh and a place ticket on the second horse in the eighth. There is one more, and he cannot read the result of it. It is from the machines inside, a two-dollar exacta on a race in California that does not go off until after midnight here. He looks at it under the light for a while. It goes in the shirt pocket with the halves.

One window is still open for cashing. The clerk in it has counted this drawer since June and does not look at the keys. There are four people at the window, and then two, and then him.

"Two?" she says, before he has the bag open.

"Two."

He puts them on the counter. She runs them under the reader, which beeps twice, and counts five dollars and eighty cents onto the counter without looking at the drawer.

He puts down the halves.

She fits them together the way he did, on the counter, with a finger on each half. She does not run them under the reader, which will not take a torn code. She turns the halves over, finds the number printed under the code, and types it. The terminal finds the ticket. It is a live winner, uncashed, nine dollars and forty cents.

"Tore it before the photo," she says.

"Fourth bench. Fourth or fifth."

She takes an envelope from under the counter and a form from a clip. She writes the ticket number on the form, and the amount, and the reason, which is torn, and puts the halves in the envelope without sealing it. "Manager has to — a torn one, the manager signs for it. He's counting the night. It'll be a few minutes."

"I've got a few minutes."

"You want to wait or you want it mailed."

"I'll wait."

She nods and turns to the drawer. He stands off to the side of the window under the light with the bag over his shoulder and takes the California ticket out and reads it again.

"Race book open tomorrow?"

"Yeah." She is counting twenties. "I won't be. Tonight's my last."

"I know it," he says, and puts the ticket back in the pocket.

The sweeper comes out through the doors a little after eleven with a push broom, a dustpan on a stick, and a cart with a roll of clear bags on it. She has swept this apron every summer the track has run, the first of them under the bleachers. She is not tall. She starts at the far end, by the beer stand, where he has finished. Nobody wrote the arrangement down. He gets fifteen minutes.

She does not read them. The broom goes in long strokes toward the rail and back along it. The paper comes together in a ridge. The ridge goes into the pan, the pan goes into a bag, the bag is tied and set on the cart. On a Tuesday the cart takes it all. Tonight the cart is full by the fourth bench and she goes in for a second one.

She picks up the scarf and hangs it over the back of the bench and sweeps under it.

While she is at the second bench the track lights go off. They go in banks, the backstretch first, then the far turn, then the stretch, and the oval is a dark shape with the wetland behind it. The wetland is loud. From somewhere beyond the track a diesel starts and idles, and then another. The apron lights stay on. They are on a separate switch, inside.

The manager comes to the window from inside with a clipboard. He is a man in a shirt with the house's name on it, and he has been in the money room since the twelfth went official. He looks at the envelope. He fits the halves together himself on the counter, and signs the form. The clerk counts nine dollars and forty cents onto the counter. The man with the bag puts it in his pants pocket with the five-eighty and says thank you.

"Have a good winter," the manager says to him, and then, to her, "See you in May."

"Yeah, we'll see," she says, and he goes back in.

"Fifteen-twenty," the man with the bag says.

"Fifteen-twenty." She pulls the drawer. The shutter comes down over the window from the inside.

The board in the infield goes dark. The prices go first, and the line about the season stays a moment longer, and then it is gone too, and the infield is as dark as the track. Through the glass the screens in the room behind the tavern are still lit, six or seven of them, and on one of them horses are walking in a paddock under lights in a place where it is two hours earlier. In the hotel a few of the windows that face the oval are lit.

The man with the bag sits back down on the last bench, at the mouth of the hallway, for the length of time it takes the sweeper to finish, and she does not hurry for him. She has thought before that he stays to see it bare, and has never asked him. When she comes past the bench with the broom she says, "Twelve," and he nods. She sweeps to the doors. Then she goes back to the third bench, takes the scarf off the back of it, folds it once, and puts it on the cart with the bags. When the last bag is on the cart the apron is bare from the rail to the building, the way it is bare on a morning in April before the meet opens. He stands up. He does not go down the hallway. He goes out along the building toward the lot the way everyone else went, with the bag, which has nothing in it now, and the shirt pocket, which has a ticket in it on a race that has not been run.

The sweeper pushes the cart in through the doors, and the apron lights go off a few minutes after she is in. Behind the glass the card room is lit. The building is open twenty-four hours a day, every day of the year.

The barns, the same hour

The barns are on the far side of the grounds from the building, past a gate with a guard in it. Nobody brings a horse through the gate without three papers: a health certificate less than ten days old, a negative Coggins test less than a year old, and proof of a vaccination against the equine herpes virus within six months. The stalls were allotted in the spring, by application, in blocks to each trainer, and a diagram of who has which is posted at the end of every barn. The backstretch opened on the second of May. The people who work the barns are hired by the trainers and paid by them. For the summer some of them live in trailers on the grounds, assigned like the stalls, and some in motel rooms the house arranges.

A horse that has raced is not put away. It comes back from the track with its harness on, and the winner, and any other horse the judges pick, goes first to the test barn, where the state takes blood and urine before the horse is allowed home. A horse will not urinate on command. The people in the test barn whistle at it, one long note, over and over, because horses come to associate the whistle with urinating, and most of them eventually do. Then the horse is walked. The harness comes off, the hopples come off a pacer, the horse is hosed and scraped and walked in the shedrow under a cooler until its breathing has come down and its coat is dry, which takes twenty minutes on a warm night and longer on a cold one, and it is given water a few swallows at a time. Then its legs are done up and it is put in its stall with hay. A horse sleeps mostly standing. For the deep sleep it has to lie flat, and it will not lie down where it does not feel safe, and it needs a little of that sleep, in short pieces. A horse that lies down too close to a wall and cannot get its legs under it to rise is said to be cast. A horse cannot vomit, so trouble in its gut has one direction to go. These are the two things a night watch walks the barns for.

At ten to eleven the horses from the twelfth race come off the track through the gap in the rail, at a walk, with their drivers still on the bikes. The seven turns off toward the test barn with a groom at its head and the state's man walking behind. The four, which lost the photo, comes down the row to its own barn with a woman walking beside it who has one hand flat on its neck.

The four is a pacer. She unhooks the bike and a man takes it away by the shafts. She takes off the hopples and hangs them on the nail by the stall. The harness comes off, and the hood, and the shadow roll. She leads the horse to the wash stall at the end of the row. She hoses it from the neck back with water that steams a little and scrapes it off in long pulls that throw the water onto the concrete. The horse stands for this with its head down, the way they do. She puts a cooler on it, a wool one, dark green, with the trainer's initials on the corner, and walks it away down the shedrow, and the two of them go out of the light at the far end and turn and come back into it.

The trainer is stripping stalls. He has six horses in this barn and four of them leave tonight. The stalls of the four that leave are being emptied to the floor. The shavings are forked into a cart and the cart is run out to the pile. The water buckets are tipped and stacked upside down in the aisle. The hay nets are taken down and rolled. A boy of about seventeen who has worked for him since June is doing the forking. The stall cards with the horses' names on them are still on the doors. The trainer takes them down as each stall is finished and puts them in his shirt pocket.

"Leave the two on the end," he says. "They're not — leave those two. They're not going till Tuesday."

In the paddock, the long building the horses are held in before they race, the house's own crew is finishing the year. The numbered saddle pads the house puts on every horse in every race are collected from the stalls and counted against a list and dropped in a canvas hamper for the laundry. The paddock stalls are hosed. A woman in a house shirt walks the length of the building with a broom, pushing the water toward the drains, and the paddock judge's office at the end has its blinds down and its light off.

The trailer is backed up to the end of the barn with its ramp down and its lights on inside, a six-horse with a dressing room in the nose, and the truck that pulls it is running. Two horses are already in it, standing with their heads toward the front, hay nets in front of them. The third horse comes out of its stall in a cooler and a shipping halter and walks to the foot of the ramp and stops.

"Walk on," the boy says. "Walk on. Come on, then." The horse does not. The lead rope goes tight and the horse's head goes up and its front feet stay where they are, on the gravel, an inch short of the rubber. The boy waits and the horse waits. Then the boy turns the horse in a circle, away from the ramp and back to it, and walks on again, and the horse puts one front foot on the ramp and takes it off again.

"Don't pull on him," the trainer says from inside the barn, without looking out. "Let him look."

The boy does not pull him. He stands at the horse's shoulder with the rope slack and looks up the ramp himself, and after a while the horse puts a foot on the rubber, and then the other one, and then it goes up in two strides and into its slot, and the boy ties it and the partition swings shut behind it. The fourth horse loads behind it without stopping. The ramp comes up on its springs and the boy pins it.

The trainer comes out of the barn with a tack trunk on a dolly and runs it up into the dressing room. He comes back for the bikes, two of them, which go on a rack on the trailer's roof with their wheels off. He takes an envelope from his back pocket and hands it to the boy without saying anything about it. The boy puts it inside his jacket without looking in it and gets in the truck on the far side.

In the next barn over, when the truck moves, a horse calls after it, and a horse in the trailer answers, and then another horse somewhere down the row, and then it stops. The truck goes slowly up the gravel toward the gate with the trailer rocking behind it. It stops at the gate. The guard comes out of the booth with a clipboard, looks at the paper the driver hands down, writes on the clipboard, and says something short, and the truck goes out onto the road.

In the test barn the seven is standing in a stall with the groom holding it, and a man in a state jacket is whistling. It is one note, held, and then held again. The horse has been there a long time. Its blanket from the picture is folded over the stall door. The people in good coats who stood around it for the picture are in the tavern. The groom shifts his weight and does not sit down, because there is nothing to sit on, and the man whistles, and the horse stands with its ears turned back toward the sound.

The woman stops the four under the light and puts the back of her hand flat against its chest, between the front legs, and holds it there. The heart under her hand has come down. She does the same under the cooler along the flank, and takes the cooler off. The coat under it is dry and stands up in places where the scraper went. She leads the horse to its stall, the second from the end, one of the two that are staying, and it goes in and puts its head straight into the hay.

She does its legs sitting on an upturned bucket in the doorway: a wet cotton under a bandage on each front leg, wound from the knee down, the bandage pinned. It takes her the length of time it takes the horse to eat a flake of hay. Then she stands and picks up the bucket, and stands in the doorway a moment longer, and turns off the light over the stall, and goes down the row past the four bare doors to the tack room, where there is a chair and a coffee maker and not much else now, and sits down. Her trailer is outside, unhitched. She is here until Tuesday with the two on the end.

The seven comes back from the test barn after an hour with the state's man behind it. It is washed and walked in another barn, by a different groom, and put up, and the blanket goes over a rail.

The night watch has come on. He is a man of sixty or so in a canvas coat who has walked these barns for this trainer for the whole meet, from eleven at night until the first grooms come in, and he does the walk with a flashlight he does not turn on unless he needs it, because the shedrow lights are enough and a light in a horse's face wakes it. He looks into each stall as he passes and says the stall number under his breath, not the horse's name. At the end of the row he stops at the diagram on the wall, which has the trainer's stalls marked in pencil, and draws a line through four of them.

There are two horses left in this barn on this trainer's side, and nine on the other side belonging to a trainer whose truck is not leaving until morning. The night watch walks the other side too. Those nine are eating. The sound in a barn at this hour is hay being pulled from a net and ground, a bucket knocked, a hoof shifted in shavings. The pile of shavings outside is high. The stacked buckets in the aisle catch the light.

Around midnight the last truck of the night goes up toward the gate, a two-horse trailer behind a pickup, and the guard comes out and writes, and it goes. After that there are no engines. The wetland is as loud here as it was behind the track.

Some time after that the horse in the second stall from the end lies down. It goes down front end first, onto its knees, and then over onto its side, and then it lies flat, with its legs out straight and its neck along the shavings and its head on the ground, and breathes so that the shavings move in front of its nose. The bandages on its front legs are white against the shavings. The night watch, coming down the row, sees the legs through the stall gate and stops. He does not turn on the flashlight. He stands where he is, a stall away, long enough to see the ribs rise and fall three times, and then he goes on.

At the end of the row he looks at the diagram again and does not write anything.

The two stall cards that are still on the doors say the horses' names. The four other doors on that side are bare. In the tack room the woman has fallen asleep in the chair with her coat on. The shedrow lights stay on all night. In the off-season the barns are offered for events: two barns with a hundred and fifty stalls and more than forty-six thousand square feet, the listing says, and a seven-thousand-square-foot paddock for musical guests, food and beverage outlets, or meeting locations, with a farmhouse feel.

The tavern, after midnight

The tavern is inside the building, on the ground floor, with its glass on the finish line. It is named for the fish farm that was on this ground before the track. The fish farm wrote its name with a hyphen, and the sign over the tavern's door writes it as two words. A family dug the ponds here in the sixties, more than twenty of them, and raised rainbow trout in them to sell to restaurants, and then let the public in to catch their own. There was a picnic ground where people cleaned and grilled what they caught, and in the seventies a restaurant, and in the eighties a stage on the lawn, and by the end of the nineties it was all closed. The track dug one pond back. It is aerated with a bubbler and kept near fifty-five degrees and stocked from a local farm, and there is no catch-and-release: a fish that comes out is paid for by the pound, and can be cleaned for a fee, and cooked for a fee. The tavern's menu has a smoked trout dip on it and a baked rainbow trout.

The tavern does not close. The card room it opens off does not close either, and the building is eighteen and over inside the doors and smoke-free everywhere. On a race night the glass is a window on the wire, and the rest of the year, and under snow, it is a window on a track with nothing running on it.

At eleven the long table by the glass is the loudest thing in the building. There are eight at it, in the good coats from the winner's circle, and the horse they own has won the last race of the meet by a nose and paid nine-forty. Behind them the track lights are still on and the board is still lit with the prices, and they have not looked at the glass since they sat down. The server, who is young and has been on since four, has brought two rounds and is bringing a third.

"He said don't watch the photo. He said you can't tell a thing from it, I'm telling you, that's what he said to me. Don't watch it."

"You watched it."

"I watched it."

"Everybody watched it."

The driver comes in at twenty past, in a jacket over a shirt he has changed into, with his hair wet. He does not sit down. He stands at the end of the table with one hand on the back of a chair and lets them tell him what he did, and says the horse did it, and says the four had him at the eighth pole and he wasn't sure, and one of the women at the table gets up and holds his face in both hands and says something to him that the server, coming with the third round, does not hear. A driver's share of a purse is five percent, and a trainer's is five, and the owners have the rest. He says he has to get back, and goes, and the server puts the third round down where he was standing.

In the kitchen the cook has come on. She works the overnight and has since before there was a hotel. The owners' table has ordered eight of the trout. It was the trout they wanted, most of them, because of the name of the place, and two of them do not like fish. The walk-in has six. She sends the six and tells the server to tell them, and he tells them, and the two order something else, and the eight plates go out on two trays.

At half past eleven the track lights go off in banks, and the long table looks at the glass. With the track dark the glass gives back the room: the eight coats, the plates, the server standing back with the tray on his hip. One of the men gets up and goes to the window and puts his hand flat on it and looks out through the shape of his hand, and sees the board still lit in the infield, and then the board goes dark too. "Well, there I am," he says, and sits down.

By half past twelve the owners have gone up to the hotel or out to the lot, and the table is stripped and reset and stands by the glass with the settings on it and nobody at it, and the print of the man's hand is on the glass above it, the whole of it, fingers up, and the room has gone back to what it is at that hour. Four men from the card room are eating at the bar with their jackets on. A woman in the corner has a book and a coffee and has had the coffee since the tenth race. The bartender is wiping down. Through the archway the card room is the same as it was at noon: the chips, the shuffle, a voice on the speaker calling a seat open on a table.

"Was that the last one," the bartender says to the server. "Race."

"That was it."

"They settle?"

"The one in the red coat. Cash."

He puts a glass up.

The four men at the bar are talking about a hand. One of them had a pair and one of them had a better pair, and the one with the better pair is telling it, and the one who lost it is eating and saying "yep" at the right places. Their food is a burger and three of the fish sandwich.

In the kitchen the cook filters the fryer oil, the oil pumped through the filter and back and the crumb caught in the paper, which is done at this hour every night of the year. She wipes the flattop with a rag she has folded to a pad. The dish machine runs through its cycle and stops. She takes the clipboard off the walk-in door and goes in and counts, and comes out, and writes down what the morning will need.

At one the server takes his break in the chair by the walk-in with his shoes off. He can see the window from there, down the length of the room, and in it the room, and in the room the woman with the book. He has been here since four in the afternoon, when the glass showed the field for the first race warming up, going the wrong way of the track. He is not on again until Thursday, and Thursday there is nothing on the other side of the glass, and he has known that all summer, and it is strange to him now. He puts his shoes back on and counts what is in his apron, without taking it all the way out, and it is more than a Tuesday.

At half past one a dealer from the card room comes through the archway on her break, in the house's vest, and eats a sandwich standing at the end of the bar, and asks the bartender for a coffee to take back with her, and goes back through the archway with it.

At two a man comes down from the hotel in a hooded sweatshirt and the pants he was going to sleep in and sits at the glass. He orders a beer and the dip. He tips his head to get past his own reflection, and with his hand up he can see the shape of the rail, and beyond it nothing. He is on the fifth floor with the curtains open, he tells the server, and last night he went to sleep with the track lit, and tonight he could not with it dark, and he came down to see. He eats the dip and leaves the beer half done and goes back up, and the server wipes the table and takes the bowl and the glass back to the bar.

At three the cook comes out of the kitchen to the end of the bar with her own plate and sits on the stool nearest the kitchen door, with her back to the window, and eats. She has cooked on nights when the room was full to the glass and on nights when it was the bartender and her. Tonight it is the four men, finishing, and the woman with the book, who has closed it, and the server, polishing a glass. She tells the bartender the trout is gone, all six, and he writes it on the pad by the register for the morning.

The one who lost the hand pays for all four, and they go. The server turns the chairs up at the empty tables so the floor can be done under them, and the bartender runs the machine. The woman with the book leaves a bill under her cup and goes out through the card room.

The far turn, first light

The track is five-eighths of a mile around, surfaced with agricultural lime and stone dust, with no roof over any part of it, and the racing here is done in summer. In a mile race the field comes through the far turn twice. The ground under the surface is the Anoka Sand Plain, glacial outwash, sand over water, a lowland that would not take a crop, and a family dug it into trout ponds, and the track circles a wetland that was put back after the ponds were gone. Next door is the state's wildlife management area, about twenty-five thousand acres of marsh, lakes, and swamp. A hundred years ago the marsh was cut for wire-grass, which a company in St. Paul wove into rugs. The trumpeter swan, which had been gone from the state, was brought back there, and the sandhill crane nests there, and both come and go over the track.

The sun on the twentieth of September comes up a little before seven. First light is about half an hour before that.

At six there is the building, lit, and the lot lights, and the hotel with two windows lit on the upper floors, and past all of it the oval is a darkness with a slightly different quality from the darkness around it, because the lime is pale and holds what light there is. The board in the infield is off. Under everything is the interstate, which does not stop for the hour. Over it, water, or the things in it: a frog, and another, spaced far apart now that the nights have gone cold, and a duck somewhere shifting.

From the wetland, a rattle with a rise in it, once, and then answered. The cranes are on the water. They stand in it at night, in a group, where it is a few inches deep, and they are the first to speak, and they speak for a long time before there is light to see them by, and they are answered from farther off, from the marsh next door, by more of them.

By twenty past six the fog is visible because there is enough light to see it against. It lies on the water and on the low grass and on the track, thickest over the water, and it moves a little, in no direction. The rail comes up out of it, the top rail first, a line, and the posts under it, and then the surface of the track, which is pale and combed in fine lines, the crew's pass after the twelfth.

The infield comes up next: the grass, the water in the low places, the board on its stand with its screen the grey of a screen that is off. The cranes are still calling, and now their shapes can be made out, standing, tall, in the shallows, thirty or forty of them, and a heron at the edge of them that does not move. Something walks along the outside rail in the fog at the height of a dog, a fox, and goes on along the rail into the fog.

At twenty to seven the light has color in it, and the fog is going from the high ground first, and the track comes clear, the whole oval of it, a pale ring with the wetland inside it and the marsh beyond, and on the far side the building, and the apron with the benches on it, and the tavern's glass lit from inside. In the hotel the two lit windows have gone dark and three others have their curtains open.

The cranes go up a few at a time. They run in the shallow water and lift, and their legs hang and then straighten out behind them, and they call the whole time, and they circle once low over the far turn, over the surface, close enough that the fog moves under them, and go over the rail and over the marsh, and the calling goes on after them for a while. It takes a quarter of an hour for the water to be nearly empty of them. Two walk in the shallows with their heads down.

The sun comes up. It hits the board first, because the board is the tallest thing in the infield, and the screen goes from grey to white and stays white while the sun is low. The top rail takes it next, all the way around, and then the surface, which goes from grey to something nearer white, with the combed lines plain in it now the whole way round. The fog is gone from the track and is still on the marsh next door in a layer.

At ten past seven a pickup comes onto the track through the gap in the rail by the paddock and drives the oval once, slowly, on the inside, with its windows up. It stops at the far turn, and goes on, and comes off where it came on, and the surface behind it shows its tires across the lines. In the lot, which has room for twelve hundred cars, there are a few dozen, near the doors, and a line of geese goes over the lot low and calling.

In the hotel most of the curtains are open, and in some of the windows there are people standing, looking at the track. The two cranes are still in the shallows. A pair of swans is on the water at the far end of the infield, and the sun on them makes them hard to look at.

Next door, of the twenty-five thousand acres, four thousand and fifty are sanctuary, closed to people the whole year.

The fifth floor, the morning after

The hotel is five stories and a hundred and sixteen rooms, and the rooms on the track side have windows on the oval. It broke ground in November of 2018, cost more than eighteen million dollars, and opened in March of 2020. Its own description of itself is expansive corridors, high ceilings, and the largest rooms in the category.

On the fifth floor the corridor runs the length of the building with the track-side rooms on one hand and the lot-side rooms on the other, and it is wide enough for two carts to pass. A housekeeper is given a list in the morning with the rooms on it in two kinds: the ones where the guest is leaving, which are stripped and done from the bare mattress up, and the ones where the guest is staying, which are made and wiped and left. The list changes as the desk calls up. A room that is done is done the same way every time, down to the point folded into the end of the paper roll and the way the towels are hung.

At nine the carts are at the far end, by the ice machine, and the two women who work the fifth floor have divided the list, one taking the track side and one the lot side, and they are talking to each other across the corridor, low, and one of them laughs. The corridor smells of the coffee from the breakfast room, which comes up the stairwell.

The first room on the track side has its curtains open and has had them open all night, and the bed has been slept in, and the window shows the whole of the oval at once, pale, with the sun on it and nothing moving on it, the far turn, the infield with the water in it, the benches on the apron below. On the nightstand there is a program folded back to the twelfth race and a ticket under it, whole. This one is staying another night. The woman who works the track side empties the wastebasket, which has four torn tickets in it and a coffee cup, and makes the bed, and wipes around the program and the ticket without moving them.

The corner room is the one the owners had. Glasses from the tavern are on the desk and the dresser and the windowsill, and a bottle in the wastebasket, and the beds have been sat on more than slept in. A copy of the program is on the bed with the twelfth race circled in pen and a number written in the margin, and under it a second number that is much larger and has a question mark after it. The desk has called up: this one is a checkout. She strips it to the mattress, and the program goes in the bag with the rest, and the glasses go on the cart, on the tray under the top shelf.

Two rooms have the sign on the door. She passes them and comes back to them later. In one of them, at ten, a man opens the door in a T-shirt, barefoot, and says sorry, sorry, and asks what time it is. She tells him. He says he'll be out by eleven, and she says that's fine, and moves the cart down one, and the door closes.

The lot-side rooms show the lot. There are thirty cars in it, and a lawn beyond, and the road, and trees. In one of them a family has left early and left the television on, a game on it with the sound down, and the woman who works that side turns it off with the remote and puts the remote on the nightstand by the phone.

The radio on the supervisor's belt says a room number and then says late check, and the supervisor, who is walking the corridor with the list, crosses a room off one column and writes it in the other. She has been here since the hotel opened the first time, in March of 2020, and was here the five days it was open before it closed with the rest of the state, and was here when it opened again. The list for next Saturday is already on her clipboard under this one. It is a wedding block, most of the fifth floor, track side and lot side.

At half past ten the woman on the track side is in a room whose guest has gone, running the vacuum, and turns it off, and stands at the window. From the fifth floor the whole oval fits in the window, and the apron with its benches is directly below, and the far turn is across the water. She has watched races from here, on her feet, in rooms that were not hers, with the vacuum off for the two minutes of it. There is nothing on the track, and she turns the vacuum back on and does the floor along the window.

The stairwell door opens at the end of the corridor and a man in the kitchen's whites comes through with a case of the small bottles of water and leaves it by the ice machine and goes back down.

By eleven the two carts have met in the middle of the corridor, and the women stop for a minute with the carts nose to nose and drink water from the small bottles and talk, and one of them takes off a glove and shows the other a cut on her thumb, and the other one looks at it, and they go on.

The corner room is done: the bed made to the tightness of every bed on the floor, the glasses gone, a new liner in the wastebasket, the curtains open, as the track-side rooms are left. The supervisor radios it down as ready.

At half past eleven the man with the sign on his door is gone, and his room is a checkout, and the woman who works the track side goes in. His curtains are open. The bed has been slept in on the side nearest the window, with the pillow moved over to that side from the other. She strips it and makes it.

Downstairs, in the breakfast room, the mural on the wall has the fish farm in it, and the card tables, and the horses, and the skyline of Minneapolis.

The hallway

The hallway takes the long building from the card room to the far end of it. It is high, and wide enough that people pass in it without turning, and it is brightest where it begins, by day from the room's windows and by night from the tables, and it darkens as it goes, and the one light at the far end is the light of what hangs there. Down one wall, at the height a person reads at, there are paragraphs, short and plain, set one after another the length of the hallway, about the sport and about the ground. Under them, at the height of a hand let down at the side, a band of the track's own surface, lime and stone dust, combed, runs behind glass from one end to the other.

The hallway ends at two lit panels set side by side. One holds a single dark figure drawn in one line, without the pen lifting, and its light does not change. The other holds a band of handwriting the width of the wall, and its light changes, but slower than a person standing in front of it can see it change; now and then a sentence comes up in it and stays long enough to be read twice, and goes. One line of writing near the panels is set so small that it has to be read from ten inches. Before the panels, at standing distance, there is a rail of wood at the height of the rails outside. There is no speaker in the hallway, and the lamps behind the panels are hidden from every place a person can stand.

Sunday, the twentieth of September, half past two in the afternoon. The card room is as full as it is on any Sunday afternoon, and as loud, and through its windows the track is empty and the board in the infield is dark. Two dealers are coming on shift through the far door of the room. A woman in the fifth floor's uniform, with her bag over her shoulder and her shift over, comes through the card room from the hotel side, past the tables, and turns into the mouth of the hallway.

She walks it some days, after a long shift or a short one, and nobody in the card room looks up. For the first few steps the sound of the room is all around her, and then it is behind her.

The writing on the wall goes past at her shoulder. She has read some of it, on other days, a paragraph at a time, and she knows where the ones she has read are, and passes them. The carpet ends and the floor is harder. The light from the card room end no longer reaches the floor in front of her, and the light from the far end does not reach it yet, and for a few steps she is walking on a floor she can hear and not see, and then the far light is on it, low, and getting stronger, and the two panels at the end grow as she comes on.

There is a paper cup on the floor against the wall, empty, and she picks it up without stopping.

At the rail she stops. She puts the bag down between her feet. She puts her forearms on the rail, and the wood under them is worn smooth, and she leans, and the weight goes forward onto the wood.

The figure in the left-hand panel is dark against the light and is one line, and the line goes around and comes back to where it started, and she follows it with her eyes and loses it and finds it again. The panel on the right is a different color than it was when she came into the far light, if it is; the change, if there is one, is too slow to catch. The band of handwriting in it goes from one edge to the other, and from the rail it is a band.

A sentence comes up in the right-hand panel, and she reads it twice before it goes.

She takes her forearms off the rail and goes closer, to the small line, and bends to it from the waist with her hands behind her back, and reads it at ten inches, and straightens up.

She puts a fingertip on the figure in the left-hand panel, where the line is raised, and follows it. The line has a tremble in it in places. She follows it all the way around to where she put her finger down, and takes her hand off it.

The card room is a long way behind her and going on, and the hallway between is empty.

She picks up the bag and the cup and walks back up the hallway toward the sound, with the far light on her back and then not, and the paragraphs go past at her other shoulder now, and the carpet begins, and the light from the room reaches the floor in front of her, and then she is in the room, and a man at the first table is laughing at something, and she goes past him and through the room and out the door she came in by, and puts the cup in the can by the door.

The hallway is empty for a while, and then two people come into the mouth of it from the room, talking, with the far light on the floor a long way in front of them.

The wall

A horse coming off the track on three legs is a horse the groom runs toward. A horse standing in its stall on three legs, with a hind foot cocked and the weight off it, is resting, and the groom walks past.

A harrow runs at a set depth and never reaches the base, which is packed hard under the cushion and carries every race; the few inches above it are combed smooth between races, thousands of fine parallel lines in the direction the tractor went, which is the direction the horses will go, and the next race is run across them.

Most weeks the sport runs a race with nothing on it, in the morning, with the windows shut and nobody on the apron: a qualifier, no purse, no line, a mile against the clock, which a horse that has broken stride too often or been away too long has to go without breaking before the judges will let it back into a race that pays.

On this property hands are mostly a measure, four inches each from the ground to the withers, and a Standardbred stands fifteen or sixteen of them, and cannot hold a ticket, and is bet on at the window by its program number.

The wetland next door is where the state brought its swans back: eggs collected in Alaska in three summers in the eighties, flown down, hatched and raised there, and the young released at two years old. Swans pair for life, and so do the cranes, and the geese come in lines.

The morning line is the handicapper's odds, printed in the program before a ticket is sold, and the caller and the crowd measure the board against it all night; the program's line stays as it was printed while the board's moves.

When fog comes off the wetland onto the backstretch, a race is called in pieces: the start and the first turn, then quiet while the field is a sound, then the horses picked up one at a time as they come out of it into the lights of the stretch.

A driver looks for cover: another horse to sit behind and let break the wind. The hard trip is first over, parked out, with nobody in front; a horse with cover sits, and the driver waits, and pulls when the cover tires.

Post positions on this oval are drawn, and on five-eighths of a mile the draw matters, the rail and the two hole giving a horse a short way around and the outside posts giving the driver a decision in the first eighth, leave hard or tuck in and wait; the same horse from a different post runs a different race, and the program prints the win percentage by post.

The board posts the order the horses made at the wire, then the order the judges made and the rule under it, a horse still breaking at the wire placed, if the judges choose to place it, behind any horse on its gait that was lapped on it there, and then one word, official, and the tellers pay on it. A ruling afterward can move the purse; the tickets were paid at official.

Just south of the house the two interstates from the two cities become one road again, and the house is at the first exit after they do, the one the property's own directions give with a warning that the map services are wrong, left at the top of the ramp and a mile west; a driver who misses it is carried on, on the one road, to the next exit, which is Forest Lake.

The trot and the pace are gaits held on purpose, and a horse that breaks into the gallop, the gait it was born with, has to be taken back until it is on stride again, and the lengths lost coming back are usually the race, and in the program's record of it a break is marked with an x.

In the morning the horses go around this oval the wrong way: jog miles, most of a horse's work, are done clockwise, slow and untimed, and a horse turns to go the right way of the track, counterclockwise, to train or to race.

Some races here are claiming races, and every horse in one is for sale at the price the race is named for: a claim is written on a slip and dropped in a locked box before the race, it cannot be taken back, and from the moment the gate car pulls away the horse belongs to the new owner, while whatever it earns in that race is paid to the owner who sold it.

The breed is named for a rule: when the registry opened a horse was a Standardbred if it could trot a mile inside a standard time; the standard has since given way to pedigree, and a horse is a Standardbred now because its sire and dam were, back through the registry to the ones that made the time.

Most pacers race in hopples, a strap on each side that loops the front leg and the hind leg behind it together, long enough to let the two swing as a pair, which is the pace, and short enough that they cannot swing apart; the length is measured to the horse in inches, and a horse that holds the gait on its own is raced without them, free-legged.

Nearly every race in this sport is a mile, whatever the size of the oval, and the oval decides the shape of the mile: on a mile track it is one lap and two turns, on this five-eighths a lap and part of a second and three turns, on a half-mile track two laps and four, and a horse's time for the mile is read against the size of the track it was run on.

The field is started from a car. The starter rides in the back of it, facing the horses, with the field coming up behind on the two wings of the gate, and he has them until he gives the word. He brings them up slowly at first and then at speed, and at the point on the track where the mile begins he says go, and the wings fold and the car pulls away. Until the word he can call them back, for a horse in trouble behind the gate or a gate that fails, and the field goes around and comes again. From the word every horse in the field is a starter, and there is no recall after it.

How Lamps Behind High to Low(er) Voices at Running Aces Was Made

A bench document. Flat register. The book is locked; this is the record of the road to it, kept so the road can be walked again.

1. What it started from

A research document of five portraits — five real writers, each described from the public record, each with the frame they would bring to Running Aces, what the piece would look like in their hands, and where each would stop. The portraits shared one method: nothing was taken from any writer but a manner. None was quoted. None appeared.

That document had two things in it the book was built on, and both were invisible until it was read against the finished portraits:

- Every portrait ended at the wall. Each writer, in turn, stood at the end of the hallway and passed a verdict on the slow work there. Five roads, one wall. That became the book's shape before a word of it was written.
- Every portrait had a refusal. The statistician would not write an appreciation. The columnist would not touch harm, policy, or the tribes. The organizer would not moralize about wagering. The songwriter would not preach. The showman would not make the losers the joke. The refusals turned out to matter more than the frames; they are what keep five voices from becoming one.

2. The concept, and the one ruling that made it

The first proposal kept the five apart — five roads to the wall, never in the same paragraph. The Mason ruled otherwise in one sentence: the intriguing resonance that we grow can come out of the mingling and voicing of these minds. Everything after followed from that.

Mingling, not melting. The earlier small book had put the five in one narrator's head and had him report them. This book lets them speak directly, to each other, in the same place at the same time, with no head between them. What they set off in each other is the book.

Five units. Each voice counts in one thing and is known by it: the Auditor in populations (a number and a condition); the Picker in one man, one day, two dollars (a bet and where he stood); the Listener in workers (a who); the Junkman in nouns (a row); the Narrator in rooms (a thing said to a house). Resonance needs different frequencies. The rule of the middle of the book: nobody speaks in another's unit until the book has earned it.

Stations of the ground. The book is set at nine places in the order a visitor meets them: the door, the card room at three in the morning, the pond, the rail, the barns, the hotel window, the lawn, the hallway, the wall. Three days: Saturday, Sunday, Monday, matched to the track's real race nights.

The frame. Each station opens on a flat paragraph of the place — checked facts, no one's voice, the frame the Narrator is not allowed to have — and events arrive mid-scene in the same voice. Everything the frame states was checked against the project's files or the record, and three claims that went beyond the files were flagged when they went in and verified before the lock.

The ending, decided early and written last. The five stand in front of a picture lit from behind by a lamp nobody can see; the frame states that fact plainly and nobody draws the line to the title. Each of the five says one thing in someone else's unit — the resonance grown all the way — and one of them refuses. The Mason ruled which: the Picker, whose rule is that he only speaks for two dollars, and the wall takes no bets.

3. The rulings, in order

1. It can be its own separate small book; elaborate the concept of very different individuals blended with the subject matter.
2. Mingling and voicing, not separation.
3. A trial station (the card room) at about a thousand words. Then: is this the most depth and length we're going to have? No. Five things a full station needed were named — whole-piece turns, events, the figures at stake, the mingling doing its work inside the scene, more of the ground — and the same station was recut at full depth, about 3,800 words.
4. Right depth; keep; write all the rest at that depth; verify the flagged fact; text file.
5. The title, through six versions in one sitting: Lamps and High to Low(er) Voices at Running Aces → Lamps Behind High to Low(er) Voices at Running Aces → Running Aces: Lamps Behind … → back to Lamps Behind … on the Quarry's recommendation, ending on the place. The parenthesis was kept every time: "Low(er)" hedges the bottom, which is the one judgment the book never makes about anyone in it.
6. A line supplied by the Mason — Living is a crazy wild night at the track — placed in the rail scene in the mouth of a stranger, the man who has come every night for eleven years, said to the horses and not to anyone; the five take it up in their units; the Narrator refuses to steal it.
7. The Picker refuses at the wall.
8. Read it all; no recuts; no note naming the writers; check the two flagged facts.
9. A line under the title, arrived at by argument: The characters in this little book are made up. The place is real. (The Mason's own words, cut of a doubled clause.)
10. An epigraph, What's your favorite pick?, first set at the top, then moved on the Mason's instinct — less of a surprise there — to stand alone between the hallway and the wall, before the man whose life is picking refuses to.
11. An audit of the tells: seven named. A pass of 117 edits, every one logged as was/now. Then the lock.

4. The standards the book was held to

- Frames carry facts only, in the plainest order, and never land on a feeling.
- Voices carry manners and units, never names, never a real person's words.
- Every station has something that happens in it — a hand played, a break, a horse shipped out, a film starting — and the five answer events, not only each other.
- Every figure has a past as a figure, and it comes out under pressure from the ground, not as an aside.
- The mingling moves: by the middle of the book somebody answers in another's unit; by the wall all but one do.
- Nothing is explained. The frame states the backlit picture and stops. If anyone had said what the lamp behind the light was for, it would have been ruined.
- Minor characters are composites, and after the pass, not all of them are quotable.

5. The failure modes found, and what was done

Asked whether the book showed the hand of a very capable machine, the Quarry answered yes and named seven tells: nearly every speech landing on a quotable line; the "not X, Y" hinge in every mouth; announce-then-deliver openings; one sentence cadence under five voices; the characters explaining the device they were inside; every minor character delivering a shapely sentence on request; too much rhyme in the structure. The pass cut forty-seven closing lines, thirty-five pieces of talk about the device, twelve announcings, eleven hinges; flattened nine of the Auditor's speeches and ran twelve of the Picker's together so the two no longer scan alike; reduced eight repeats; and made two minor characters ordinary. About 1,750 words came out. The frames, the facts, the title, the line, the epigraph, and the ending were not touched.

Professor Matthew Browne is a quantitative social-scientist whose work focuses on the psychological and public-health aspects of gambling at CQUniversity's Experimental Gambling Research Laboratory. He is co-host of Decoding the Gurus with Chris Kavanagh, Associate Professor of Psychology at Rikkyo University, where they analyse public intellectuals and online influencers — which matters, because his temperament is that of a statistician allergic to grand claims.

The frame he would bring. His signature move is to refuse the "problem gambler" as the unit of analysis. The study found that low and moderate-risk gamblers account for a majority of the aggregate years of health life lost in Victoria — 50.24 per cent of years lost to disability were from low-risk gamblers · 34.52 per cent of years lost to disability were from moderate-risk gamblers · 15.24 per cent of years lost to disability were from problem gamblers. When all risk categories are combined, gambling accounts for years lost to disability that equates to about two-thirds of the years lost to disability for alcohol use and dependence and major depression. His later work found the majority of harms in the 72-item list, including serious harms such as needing temporary accommodation, emergency welfare assistance, experiencing separation or end of a relationship, loss of a job, needing to sell personal items, and experiencing domestic violence from gambling, were more commonly associated with lower risk gamblers. And he keeps the mechanism blunt: in an interview this spring he said "What causes it is excessive losses", and that the providers are actually incentivized for you to lose as much as possible because your loss is their gain. His newest paper estimates that approximately 4.7 people are affected for every person with problem gambling, and that low- and moderate-risk gamblers still account for the majority (70%) of all vectors of harm transmission in the population.

What that frame does to Running Aces. He would likely start by granting the house its one real point and then taking most of it back. No slot machines removes the single worst product — pooled Australian prevalence work found EGM was the major contributor to the problem gambling burden — so he would probably read Minnesota's ban on historical-horse-racing terminals as a genuine public-health protection rather than a business injustice. But the same literature holds that EGMs, casino table games, race betting and sports betting continued to be high-risk activities in the Victorian population using a variety of criteria, with a dose-response on frequency. So his essay would say: a card club open around the clock, with fifty-cent electronic hands, a 24-hour poker room and 18-year-olds admitted, is a lower-intensity venue than a machine floor, not a harmless one. Low minimums lower the door, not the total lost. He would note that young adults represented a new vulnerable group as they reach the legal gambling age, and that the interventions with measurable effect on losses were structural — the smoking bans, removal of ATMs from venues and decreases in bet size — which is why he would credit the smoke-free room more than any amount of daylight.

The prevention paradox applied to the property is his most uncomfortable point, and he would make it: the harm here is mostly not the stereotyped addict but the Tuesday regular who is fine, the couple who lose a little more than they meant to, the eighteen-year-old at the dollar table — and each of them, on his numbers, carries harm home to four or five other people. Responsible-gambling signage and self-exclusion address the fifteen percent; nothing on the property addresses the eighty-five. He would ask the operator's least favorite question, the one he asks of every provider — what share of revenue comes from patrons who are being harmed — and he would notice that a fixed pari-mutuel takeout makes the track indifferent to which horse wins but not to how much is wagered.

On the tribal fight he would be, I think, deliberately unimpressed by both sides' framing: exclusivity is a question about who captures the losses, not whether they occur. On the wall, expect the Decoding the Gurus reflex. He would probably find a slow, silent, hand-drawn artwork at the end of a hallway more decent than most things in a casino, and would then say plainly that it is not a harm-reduction measure, that "settles a pulse" and "returns the viewer to their senses" are claims requiring evidence nobody has collected, and that a venue should never be allowed to count decoration as duty of care. His recommendations would be dry: measure harm among patrons with a validated screen, publish the revenue concentration, put limit-setting into the loyalty card, raise the age to twenty-one, keep ATMs away from the tables, keep the terminals out, and fund the research independently.

The honest caveat: he weighs harm above nearly everything, and an essay by him would be a public-health audit with a skeptic's edge, not an appreciation. Anyone else named Matt Browne — if a different one was meant — would write a different piece.

Jay Cronley (November 9, 1943 – February 26, 2017) was an American newspaper columnist for the Tulsa World and the author of many works of humorous fiction, including Fall Guy, Good Vibes, Quick Change, and Funny Farm. He worked for the Tulsa Tribune for 22 years before moving to the Tulsa World in 1992, and also wrote for Playboy and ESPN.com. Good Vibes was the basis for the 1989 movie "Let It Ride," starring Richard Dreyfuss as a horseplayer who couldn't lose; Quick Change became the Bill Murray heist comedy; Funny Farm became the Chevy Chase film. His father, John, was the sports editor of The Daily Oklahoman in Oklahoma City for 35 years. He died in February 2017 — so he never saw the hotel, the lawsuits, the Supreme Court deadlock, the ice karts, or Frank Drucker's tip sheet. He would have known Running Aces as the track that opened in 2008, nearly folded, lost its license once, rebranded as an "entertainment center," and dug a trout pond. Everything after that is material he'd have had to be handed.

The voice. ESPN's remembrance describes it exactly: a cool, aloof, smart-alecky voice that feels like a very clever friend telling you the story sitting next to you on a barstool. His daughter said "He would find humor in the most mundane parts of life," and his Tulsa columns often focused on his pets and observations about life in Tulsa. Every spring, he would write stinging columns about Tulsa's TV meteorologists and their interruptions for severe weather coverage. A man who spent decades mocking weather coverage would have a great deal to say about a racetrack that built no grandstand and races only in summer, three nights a week, with rain in the forecast.

What he'd make of the no-slots house. This is where accuracy matters most, because a lazy guess would have him cheering the purity of a machine-free track. He wouldn't. As an Oklahoma horseplayer he watched slots save his home tracks, and he was unsentimental about it: "Whatever the reason for some horse racing plants to get slot machine profit: Purses can almost double overnight. Plugs can run for a purse that is worth more than their entire bloodline." He'd already written the Running Aces situation from the other side — "But now the Cherokees own Will Rogers Downs, and every horse player knows what means: a stampede of slot machines is a featured attraction." So the Minnesota arrangement — tribes with the slots, a track legally forbidden them, the track suing everybody and losing — would strike him as the racino that never got its sure thing, and he'd narrate the legislative fight the way he narrated everything institutional: from a bench on the apron, with a losing ticket in his shirt pocket, faintly amused that grown men were fighting in court over who gets to be the house. He'd also have the Minnesota joke ready-made; he once wrote that Breeder's Cup weekend makes Lake Wobegone look like Siberia.

The book itself. His novels are about one ordinary schemer and one impossible day, so the Running Aces book would be that: a losing horseplayer — in Good Vibes it was a cab driver — who drives past Forest Lake, takes the exit the property's own website says the maps get wrong, and has the day of his life at a five-eighths-mile harness oval he'd never heard of. The set pieces write themselves from the property's real inventory, and he'd use every one:

- The trout pond, where the sign says catch-your-own and you pay by the pound, as the only pari-mutuel in the state a man cannot lose. He'd have his hero try to bet on the fish.
- Harness racing through a thoroughbred player's eyes: the same eight horses every week, the favorite that keeps winning, and the one rule he'd find hilarious — that in this sport your horse loses by running. The break, the horse pulled off the pace for galloping, would be a running gag and probably the climax.
- The rail. He wrote a whole late column about it: The rail was where imperfect strangers quickly became like close friends, where a person might spill his guts out to somebody he had never met, explaining who he liked in the next race, why he liked it and why he needed the money so desperately. Running Aces has benches, a rail, and no grandstand — a Cronley paradise, and a chapter.
- The hotel with the oval out the window, so a man can lose from bed without getting dressed; the banquet rooms named Jack, Queen and King, and a wedding party drifting across the walkway to the card room between the toast and the cake.
- The 18-and-over rule and the dollar blackjack at 3 a.m.; the stoopers on the floor after the last race; the announcer high in the booth composing a new poem every twenty minutes for people who are not listening.
- The Charlie Daniels night — thousands on the grass singing along to a bet the devil lost — which he'd treat as the property's founding legend and the last time the house came out behind.

He'd be gentle with the marks and merciless with the experts, as always: The Picker's Pledge is to bet two bucks on what you print. Messing with more money than that is too serious and gets too personal. The tip-sheet man would get it worse than anyone. And the ending would be a win — his books end with the win, the getaway, the house sold — probably a Minnesota-sired pacer named something like the real mare Over The Moon For You, paying just enough to cover the trout.

What he wouldn't do. He wouldn't write about harm, policy, or the tribes' side of the ledger; he was a horseplayer's humorist, not a moralist, and "Horseplayers are a private lot. Sometimes sharing is like publishing the transcript of a therapy session." He'd also almost certainly never mention a slow backlit artwork at the end of a hallway, except to have his hero walk past it on the way to the window, look at it for one paragraph, and decide it was a sign.

Gloria Steinem died Wednesday at her home in New York City. She was 92. Her foundation's statement said "Gloria's greatest gift was her ability to listen to others, to make others feel seen and heard." So what follows is inference from the record she left — and, as with the others, she never wrote a line about Running Aces or, as far as I can find, about gambling as a subject.

Where she would start — and it is uncanny how close it is. Her father, Leo, ran a summer dance pavilion on a Michigan lake. Though there was no ocean within hundreds of miles, he had named it Ocean Beach Pier, and given it the grandiose slogan "Dancing Over the Water and Under the Stars." On weekends, he booked the big dance bands of the 1930s and 1940s into this remote spot. And then, in her own words, "there was always a sense of gambling. I think my father loved that." When the season ended, the family drove south buying and then selling junk or antiques or whatever, earning enough of a profit to make it to the next town. A Steinem essay on this ground would open on the Trout-Air lawn — the family ponds, the giant fish by the highway, the big acts on the grass, the summer-only racing where rain empties the apron — because she had lived that exact economy as a child. She would be tender toward the small dreamers, the Preiners and a card-room man from Los Angeles who renamed a failing track an entertainment center and dug a trout pond, and considerably less tender toward the lender that emerged as majority owner when the dream missed a payment. Reviewers noted the memoir credited her father's ability to live with and even love insecurity as what let her become a freelance writer. She'd see him at the rail.

Then she would go to the women who work there. This is the part of her career that maps most directly onto a casino: in 1963 she went undercover as a Playboy Bunny and wrote "A Bunny's Tale," which was about labor dressed as glamour — costumes, demerits, tip-sharing, the men with keys. She would interview the cocktail servers on the 24-hour card-room floor, the housekeepers in a hotel whose banquet rooms are named Jack, Queen and King (she would note, in passing, that the Queen outranks nobody in a deck), the dealers at the dollar tables at 3 a.m., the grooms on the backstretch, the women trainers whose names are on the program. She would ask what "Stay, Play & Party" and "the vibes are high" cost the staff who have to produce the vibes, who pays for the uniforms, who handles the 18-year-olds, whether anyone is organized, and what a dealer's pension looks like next to a horse's — because she would notice that the track pensions its Standardbreds through its own foundation and would want the comparison on the record.

Then the land, through Wilma Mankiller. Her closest friend for two decades was the first female Principal Chief of the Cherokee Nation; Mankiller's funeral was attended by women's rights activist and close friend Gloria Steinem, and Steinem was by her side when she died. Mankiller, in turn, had learned the practical applications for exercising tribal sovereignty and treaty rights from the Pit River Tribe's fight for its land. So Steinem would read Running Aces' suits against the Minnesota tribal casinos and its lobbying for slot-like machines the only way she could: as an attack on a sovereignty the nations spent a century recovering, and she would say plainly that the exclusivity in the compacts is not a market distortion but a treaty right in modern form. She would also — this was her habit — ask the tribes' casinos the same labor questions she asked the track. And she would go looking, as she always did, for the buried history: a county named "on both sides" in Dakota and "working waters" in Ojibwe, the treaties that priced the ground in pennies, and the fact that the property has published no acknowledgment of any of it. Her essay would end that section with the Native artist's companion piece as the obvious answer and the Dakota and Ojibwe words as theirs to bring, not anyone's to borrow.

Who loses. She would not moralize about wagering — she was her father's daughter — but she would insist on asking who pays, and her answer would be the one she always gave about money: women and children, as the "affected others" of a loss that arrives at the kitchen table. She would note that a state which will not raise a tax will happily license a takeout, and that a "family-friendly, 18-and-over, 24/7" casino is a sentence with a contradiction in every clause.

The horses. She would love that the breed is admitted by the clock and not by pedigree — performance over bloodline is her whole politics — and that the winningest horse on the continent one season was a Minnesota mare. She would ask who drives her, who owns her, and who holds the lines.

How it would end. Steinem wrote the way she organized: by listening first, in circles, and by refusing the sentence "it is what it is." She would want to sit at the rail with the Tuesday regulars and in the card room at closing, and she would close on the practice she took from Native women — the circle rather than the pyramid — and on hope as a plan rather than a mood. The wall at the end of the hallway would get a paragraph, and probably a kind one: an object that asks nothing of the people standing in front of it, in a building that asks a great deal. Whether she'd have thought a wish with nothing riding on it was enough — she'd have said no, and then said it was a start.

Tom Waits is the one of these four who is alive, working, and has actually written about Minnesota — twice — so this inference has more to stand on. Status first: the now 76-year-old Waits released "Boots on the Ground" with Massive Attack this April, marking his first original material since 2011's Bad As Me; a Small Change 50th-anniversary reissue is due October 2, and an Italian docuseries about homelessness in the American South features Waits reading one of his poems. He has never written a word about Running Aces.

The Minnesota he already has. "Christmas Card from a Hooker in Minneapolis" (1978) is a woman's letter full of good news that turns out to be lies, ending with her parole date on Valentine's Day — and the first race ever run at Running Aces was won by a horse named Valentine Hotspur. He would not let that go. "9th & Hennepin" (1985) came from a night in a downtown Minneapolis donut shop: a twelve-year-old pimp, forks hurled into the street, automatic weapon fire cracking the mirror, and "Our Day Will Come" on the jukebox. Introducing it in 1999 he complained that the corner had been cleaned up — "It went from scary... to kinda fun! And it really kinda upset me." That sentence is the key to how he'd treat a smoke-free, family-friendly, 18-and-over, 24-hour casino: a scary thing made fun, which is the one transformation he distrusts.

The form it would take. Not an essay in paragraphs. His only long prose "interview" is the one he did with himself — Anti Records' 2008 "Tom Waits' True Confessions," which opens by pretending to fear his gambling debts, his animal magnetism, coupled with his disregard for the feelings of others and proceeds as Q&A, lists and tall tales. So the Running Aces piece would be a self-interrogation, a rumors-and-corrections sheet (he just posted a "Sets the Rumors Straight" notice on his own site), an inventory of objects, and a few invented residents — the way he peopled Frank's Wild Years and the Black Rider. He would present true facts as if he'd made them up, and his standard would be the one he gave Vanity Fair: "bad writing is destroying the quality of our suffering."

What he'd inventory. He writes in nouns, and this property is a junkyard of the right nouns. The fiberglass fish by the interstate. A pond kept at 55 degrees by a bubbler where a Scout hands you a pole and nobody may throw one back. A house drawn for slot machines that never arrived, so the horses stayed — a casino with the machines removed is, to him, a bar with the ashtrays removed, and he'd say which loss he minds more. The one-armed bandit's absence and the slop bucket's presence. Banquet rooms named Jack, Queen and King; a comedy night called Laugh Your Ace Off, which he'd quote with a wince. Ice karts on the frozen oval; a drive-in in the parking lot; a hotel that opened for five days and went dark. The announcer high in a booth composing a new poem every twenty minutes — the auctioneer-preacher voice he has imitated his whole career, from "Step Right Up" on. The paper: losing tickets torn and dropped, a floor of wishes swept before morning, and the stoopers who walk it first — those are his people, and they'd get the tender treatment, never the joke.

The found instruments. He hunts sounds the way other men hunt game, so the essay would stop for the two-beat gait (harness racing as a rhythm section), the hiss of forty wheels, the bells on a trotting harness worn because a sleigh in snow is silent — and the fact that the most-sung song on earth is a harness-racing song with a trotter's mile time in its last verse would delight him past reason. The sandhill crane, older than the ice, whose windpipe coils into its breastbone so the bird is its own violin, would get a full page. So would the hognose snake that plays dead when the bluff fails.

The devil, who belongs to him. The Black Rider is his Faust — a clerk, a bargain, magic bullets — and this ground's biggest night was thousands on the grass singing the story of a wager with the devil that the human wins. He'd write it as local history: the last time the devil lost in Anoka County, and the house lost with him, and everybody went home happy.

The casino itself. He is the man who sued a chip company for imitating his voice in an ad, so "Stay, Play & Party" and a loyalty card that pays out in meal vouchers would draw his dry contempt, and he'd be sharpest about the daylight — a windowed casino, to him, is a magician showing the trapdoor. But he'd also refuse the sermon. The 2011 record about bailing out millionaires shows where his sympathy sits: with the people at the dollar table at 3 a.m., not with anyone's policy.

The wall. In the 2008 self-interview he describes owning a Marcel Marceau record — forty minutes of silence followed by applause — and adds, "It really bothers me, though, when people talk through it." A silent, slow, hand-drawn artwork at the quiet end of a casino hallway is that record hung on a wall, and I think he'd say so, in about that many words, and then walk down to the rail to see what the horses were writing in the lime.

Richard O'Brien wouldn't write it as an essay, and the honest answer to "how far would he go" is: all the way to a stage, with one hard stop I'll get to. But first, what's true about him right now, because it shapes the piece.

Where he is.
